Our freedom to doubt was born of a struggle against
authority in the early days of science. It was a very
deep and strong struggle. Permit us to question—to
doubt, that’s all—not to be sure.
RICHARD FEYNMAN
1
He believed in the primacy of doubt, not as a blemish
on our ability to know, but as the essence of knowing.
FROM JAMES GLEICK’S BIOGRAPHY OF RICHARD FEYNMAN
2
...
Author’s Note
A
S A READER, I SUPPOSE IT’S NOT UNUSUAL TO SKIP OVER
THE 
preface 
to 
a 
book 
and 
get 
straight 
down 
to 
the
business end of things. At least, that’s what I tend to do.
If you do the same, it won’t stop you understanding the
key theme of this book: how the science of uncertainty can
help us make sense of our very uncertain and unpredictable
world. But you may wonder, since pretty much everything
in life is uncertain, why I focus on the particular, seemingly
disparate topics singled out in this book—climate change,
economics, 
quantum 
physics 
and 
cosmology, 
human
creativity and consciousness, to name a few? Why not
earthquakes, drug design or cyberattacks? The answer lies
in my scientiﬁc background.
I studied for a PhD in the 1970s, researching Einstein’s
theory of general relativity—Einstein had become a hero of
mine by the time I was a teenager. I was lucky, the 1970s
are considered to have been a golden age for general
relativity theory. At the ﬁrst scientiﬁc conference I ever
attended, in 1974, I literally heard Stephen Hawking—he
hadn’t then lost his voice—announce his most important
and famous result: that black holes aren’t truly black, but
radiate quantum particles.
My research supervisor
1
, Dennis Sciama, Hawking’s PhD
supervisor 
some 
years 
earlier, 
was 
ecstatic 
about 
this
result. Sciama was optimistic that we were on the verge of
understanding how to unify Einstein’s theory of general
relativity 
with 
quantum 
mechanics, 
the 
theory 
that
successfully 
describes 
atoms 
and 
elementary 
particles.
There 
were 
problems, 
however. 
One 
problem 
which
frustrated Sciama was that Hawking’s calculations were a
little arcane. Sciama was convinced that there must be a
more 
physically 
transparent 
way 
of 
deriving 
such 
an
important result. He started to look at alternative ideas
based on concepts from a ﬁeld of physics called non-
equilibrium thermodynamics. Sciama asked me to look at
what 
is 
called 
the 
principle 
of 
maximum 
entropy
production, something I hadn’t heard of at the time.
Towards the end of my PhD research
2
, I had become an
expert on black holes and was successful in securing a
postdoctoral position in Hawking’s group at Cambridge. All
seemed 
set 
for 
a 
career 
as 
a 
theoretical 
gravitational
physicist.
But the closer I came to starting this new position, the
less sure I became that this was what I wanted to do. For a
start, the work I was doing had absolutely no relevance to
the well-being of the person in the street. On top of that, I
realised 
that 
there 
were 
actually 
only 
a 
handful 
of
professional people in the world who were truly interested
in the nitty-gritty details of my work.
Perhaps none of this would have mattered if I felt I was
going to be the person to unify quantum mechanics and
general relativity. This was a holy grail for physics in the
1970s, and indeed it still is today; it remains an unsolved
problem. But the more I thought about it, the more I found
myself concluding that at a conceptual level these two
theories were quite incompatible and weren’t going to be
uniﬁed—at least not by me.
The prevailing view at the time was to ignore these
conceptual 
problems 
and 
to 
press 
ahead 
with 
the
mathematics hoping something miraculous would work out
in 
the 
end 
(dubbed 
“Shut 
up 
and 
calculate” 
by 
the
American physicist David Mermin). However, not everyone
felt like this. I began thinking back to that ﬁrst conference I
attended 
in 
1974. 
The 
conference 
was 
opened 
by 
the
renowned British theoretical physicist Chris Isham. In his
introductory talk
3
, Isham remarked,
“It has become unfashionable these days for much notice
to be taken of these conceptual problems, most people
preferring to work on the more ‘respectable’ technical
diﬀiculties. However, in quantum gravity, the conceptual
and technical problems frequently go hand in hand and it is
possible that by neglecting the former one is rendering
irrelevant the latter.”
These words began to resonate. I started to worry about
embarking 
on 
a 
career 
working 
on 
things 
that 
would
ultimately turn out to be irrelevant.
By 
chance, 
I 
met 
a 
world-famous 
meteorologist,
Raymond Hide. Hide had interests in both astronomy and
meteorology; he was the only person to be president of
both 
the 
Royal 
Astronomical 
Society 
and 
the 
Royal
Meteorological 
Society. 
I 
asked 
him 
what 
was 
new 
in
climate physics. He proceeded to describe a paper by an
Australian colleague
4
showing how properties of Earth’s
climate can be derived from the principle of maximum
entropy production. It was as if a bolt of lightning had just
hit me. Here was some obscure concept in physics whose
existence I had only recently become aware of which could
seemingly unify everything from the quantum evaporation
of black holes to the structure of Earth’s climate system!
At 
the 
time, 
the 
United 
Kingdom’s 
Met 
Oﬀice 
was
looking for scientists. Almost on a whim, I decided to apply,
not sure if this was what I really wanted to do. At the
interview stage I blathered incoherently about the critical
importance of the principle of maximum entropy production
for understanding climate. To my great surprise I was
oﬀered a job.
But should I turn down an oﬀer to work with one of the
world’s top theoretical physicists, and instead move into
the 
more 
prosaic 
world 
of 
the 
scientiﬁc 
civil 
service,
working on a ﬁeld about which I knew next to nothing? My
parents would be disappointed with me if I turned down the
job oﬀer from Hawking. I didn’t know what to do and
walked round in a complete state of indecision for day after
day. I decided to shelve the problem for a few days and get
back to writing my thesis. And then a week or so later, I
walked into my oﬀice and it suddenly became crystal clear
to me what to do. Before I started to have second thoughts,
I 
wrote 
to 
the 
head 
of 
the 
Department 
of 
Applied
Mathematics and Theoretical Physics at the University of
Cambridge, declining the oﬀer.
What on earth was going on in my brain that suddenly I
knew what to do, when earlier I was like Buridan’s donkey,
unable to move towards either the pile of hay or the pail of
water? I became deeply interested in the process by which
the brain makes decisions.
For ten years, I didn’t think about fundamental physics. I
immersed myself in weather and climate science, and was
able, 
with 
colleagues, 
to 
make 
some 
exciting
breakthroughs. 
Michael 
McIntyre 
and 
I 
discovered 
the
world’s largest breaking waves in Earth’s stratosphere—a
process fundamental to understanding why the ozone hole
was 
discovered 
unexpectedly 
over 
the 
Antarctic. 
Chris
Folland, David Parker and I showed that the decade-long
drought 
in 
the 
sub-Saharan 
Sahel—the 
drought 
which
motivated the famous Live Aid concert—was caused by
ﬂuctuations in tropical Atlantic sea temperatures. Glenn
Shutts and I developed a way of representing small-scale
atmospheric gravity waves (not the gravitational waves of
general relativity that I had previously been researching) in
weather 
forecast 
models, 
and 
showed 
how 
this 
helped
airlines improve their fuel eﬀiciency. And James Murphy
and I developed the world’s ﬁrst ensemble forecast system,
about which I shall be writing in detail. It was during this
time that I met Ed Lorenz, a key protagonist in this book,
and became deeply interested in chaos theory.
In fact, in the 1980s, “chaos” was becoming a buzzword
around the world thanks to James Gleick’s masterful book
Chaos: 
Making 
a 
New 
Science
5
. 
Working 
with 
chaos
pioneer Bob May, I began to meet scientists from other
ﬁelds 
where 
chaos 
theory 
was 
relevant, 
and 
started
thinking about whether ensemble prediction methods could
be applied in these other areas. This aroused my interest in
ensemble prediction in ﬁelds like economics.
My career was developing well and I was happy and felt
fulﬁlled in what I was doing. I had pretty much forgotten
about my earlier life in fundamental physics.
But then, sometime in the late 1980s, I was in one of the
main 
bookshops 
in 
Oxford. 
It 
was 
1987, 
the 
three
hundredth 
anniversary 
of 
Newton’s 
famous 
Principia
Mathematica
, and a new book celebrating the anniversary,
edited by Stephen Hawking, had just appeared. I ﬂicked
through the pages. It brought my old world back to me in
an instant. The distinguished physicist Roger Penrose had
contributed 
an 
article 
in 
which 
he 
mentioned 
the 
key
experiment—the Bell experiment—which has led physicists
to reject Einstein’s views about quantum reality.
And then, some weeks later, completely out of the blue, I
had an idea based on chaos theory—a possible way to
explain how this key experimental result might after all be
consistent with Einstein’s views. I wrote a paper on this
6
and then tried to forget about it and get back to my post-
PhD ﬁelds of research. I found I couldn’t. I began to devour
papers on the foundations of quantum physics, made easier
with the rapidly developing internet, and tried to develop
my ideas on quantum physics further. I was being sucked
back into the type of physics I thought I had left behind, in
the evenings at least.
For this type of work, you simply need pencil and paper.
However, to solve the equations that were part of my day
job in weather and climate physics, I needed some of the
largest supercomputers available. But the more I got to use
these computers, the more frustrated I became by how
much of a limitation they were on what could be studied.
For all their impressive capabilities, being able to perform
millions of billions of calculations per second for example,
computers 
nevertheless 
put 
severe 
limitations 
on 
the
amount of detail that can be coded into a global weather or
climate model and on the number of ensemble members
that could be run at any time.
Ultimately, the processing power of a supercomputer is
limited by the electrical power used to run it. It takes a lot
of electrical power—some tens of megawatts—to run a
modern 
supercomputer. 
I 
became 
interested 
in 
the
possibility that you could reduce the amount of energy a
computer uses by turning the voltage down across the
transistors
7
. This might allow you to add more chips to your
supercomputer for the same amount of input energy, and
that would enable you to increase the amount of detail in
your model, or the number of ensemble members you could
run. 
But 
reducing 
the 
voltage 
would 
make 
the 
chips
unreliable—the calculations would be corrupted by thermal
noise from the uncontrollable jiggling of atoms inside these
chips and would no longer be precisely correct. With such
noise, computers would no longer be infallible. However, as
I 
will 
discuss, 
in 
chaotic 
systems 
noise 
can 
be 
a
constructive 
resource, 
amplifying 
signals 
rather 
than
obscuring them. I proposed
8
that we should be developing
low-energy, 
noisy 
supercomputers 
for 
problems 
like
weather and climate prediction. This is starting to happen.
To try to argue my point to colleagues, I began to
wonder if there are examples of systems in nature that
make use of low-energy noisy computation. I began to
realise the human brain, which needs only 20W to ﬁre its
80 billion neurons, may provide an example. And this got
me thinking about what actually it is that makes us the
creative species we are.
All these topics have in one way or another fascinated
me and have become interwoven in my research career
over the years. This is the reason why they appear together
in this book.
...
Acknowledgements
T
HE DEVELOPMENT OF ENSEMBLE WEATHER AND CLIMATE
PREDICTION systems—a concept that lies at the heart of
this book—involved the hard work of many dedicated and
inspiring colleagues, ﬁrst at the Met Oﬀice and then at the
European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts. I
want to start by acknowledging these colleagues. My deep
thanks and gratitude to Dave Anderson, Jan Barkmeijer,
Roberto Buizza, Philippe Chapelet, Francisco Doblas-Reyes,
Dennis Hartmann, Renate Hagedorn, Martin Leutbecher,
Jean-Francois 
Mahfouf, 
Doug 
Mansﬁeld, 
Martin 
Miller,
Franco Molteni, Robert Mureau, James Murphy, Thomas
Petroliagis, Kamal Puri, David Richardson, Glenn Shutts,
Tim 
Stockdale, 
Stefano 
Tibaldi, 
Joe 
Tribbia 
and 
Antje
Weisheimer. In addition, I want to thank Jagadish Shukla,
now at George Mason University, who made me realise the
potential power of physics-based models for exploiting the
predictability of the atmosphere. His work kickstarted my
passion for ensemble forecasting. I also want to thank Peter
Webster, from the Georgia Institute of Technology, who
played such an important role showing how probabilistic
ensemble forecasts could be used for disaster preparedness
in the developing world. His work is the forerunner of the
anticipatory action programmes which are now changing
the 
way 
disaster 
relief 
is 
enacted 
by 
humanitarian
organisations.
In my career I have learned from many great scientists.
From my undergraduate days at Bristol University, I thank
my tutors Michael Berry, Brian Pollard and Ruth Williams.
From my graduate days at Oxford University, I thank my
supervisor, Dennis Sciama, and my teacher and my internal
doctoral examiner, Roger Penrose. Dennis was the most
inspiring speaker I have ever heard. It was impossible to
come away from a Sciama lecture without somehow feeling
good about the world. And of course Roger, now a Nobel
laureate, was something else. When I participated in small
discussion 
meetings 
in 
Roger’s 
oﬀice, 
I 
felt 
that 
the
Copernican principle was being violated; I was manifestly
in a preferred frame of reference at the very centre of the
universe. Importantly, Dennis and Roger together taught
me not to be afraid to think outside of the box, to think in
terms of geometric pictures and simple models, and not to
be intimidated by complicated-looking mathematics—things
which I believe have served me well throughout my career.
Raymond Hide taught me all I needed to know about
geophysical ﬂuid dynamics to make a start in my new
career, and I am so grateful to him for this. I was soon
lucky to be working with three of the most outstanding
scientists in meteorology, Jim Holton, from the University of
Washington; 
Michael 
McIntyre, 
from 
the 
University 
of
Cambridge; and then Ed Lorenz, from the Massachusetts
Institute of Technology. Ed was shy and diﬀicult to engage
in 
conversation, 
but 
eloquent 
on 
stage 
when 
giving 
a
lecture. He reminded me of Dennis’s own supervisor, Paul
Dirac. Like Penrose, Lorenz was a geometer at heart—by
forming simple pictures he ﬁnally discovered the geometry
of chaos. He gave me great support in developing the
ensemble forecast system when others around me were
expressing scepticism. Only later did I realise how lucky I
was to have interacted with him.
Like Dennis Sciama and Raymond Hide before him, Bob
May showed boundless enthusiasm, and from him I learned
not 
only 
ideas 
from 
chaos 
theory, 
but 
also 
how 
to
communicate these ideas eﬀectively, of which he was a
master. Through him I met economists like Mervyn King,
governor of the Bank of England, something I would have
hardly imagined possible when I started my career. I was
elected to the Royal Society in 2003, when Bob was its
president. A highlight of my career was sitting next to him
and his wife at the special banquet for the new inductees. I
still get goosebumps thinking about this. I am grateful to
Bob for all he did for me.
I need to thank the Royal Society in another way. I have
been able to pursue the interdisciplinary topics described
in this book through an award of a Royal Society Research
Professorship 
(in 
2010, 
the 
Royal 
Society’s 
350th
anniversary year). This, I think, is the closest one can come
to being given money and being told to get on and do
whatever you like! I suppose a position like this could be
abused. However, in my case, it allowed me to do research
on many of the diverse topics discussed in this book. As
such, I am profoundly grateful to the Royal Society for this
position. It occurs to me that philanthropic money should
be used to fund many more such very light-touch, open-
ended research positions. It would be a good way for
billionaires to give something back to society and I suspect
the bang per buck would be enormous.
I also want to acknowledge the kindness of two of the
world’s leading philosophers of physics, Harvey Brown and
Jeremy Butterﬁeld, in carefully explaining to me the ﬁner
points 
of 
Bell’s 
inequality 
in 
quantum 
mechanics—
discussed in detail in this book—and in welcoming me into
their fold as one of their own. The Philosophy of Physics
seminars at Oxford University were one of the main ways I
managed to get up to speed on the foundations of physics,
whilst continuing my day job in meteorology.
And my thanks to Krishna Palem, now at Rice University,
for teaching me about low-energy noisy transistors. He,
more than anyone, has pioneered the concept of imprecise
computers.
I have been lucky that a number of colleagues have
given me extremely helpful comments on early drafts of
this book, correcting things where they needed correcting.
In particular I would like to thank Harvey Brown, Martha
Buckley, 
Mark 
Cane, 
Peter 
Coveney, 
Clara 
Deser, 
Josh
Dorrington, Simant Dube, Kerry Emanuel, Doyne Farmer,

Godfray, 
Weisi 
Guo, 
Dennis 
Hartmann, 
Sabine
Hossenfelder, Saleh Kouhen, John Krebs, Beatriz Monge-
Sanz, 
Sebastian 
Poledna, 
Juan 
Sabuco, 
Chris 
Shaw,
Jagadish 
Shukla, 
Nick 
Stern, 
Bjorn 
Stevens, 
Nick
Trefethen, 
Joe 
Tribbia 
and 
Peter 
Webster. 
I 
thank 
my
editors, Latha Menon (Oxford University Press) and Eric
Henney, 
Emily 
Andrukaitis 
and 
Thomas 
Kelleher 
(Basic
Books), for extensive comments which improved the book
enormously. As ever, remaining errors are my own.
My thanks to my son Brendan Palmer for drawing and
preparing many of the ﬁgures throughout this book; to my
students Josh Dorrington and Milan Klouwer for many of
the diagrams showing chaos and turbulence in Chapters 1
to 3; to my weather forecast colleagues Martin Leutbecher,
Fernando Prates and Cihan Sahin for the ensemble weather
forecast ﬁgures shown in Chapter 5; and to Paul Lowe and
Alex Rattan at the Bank of England for providing the fan
chart ﬁgure in Chapter 8.
Finally, I need to thank my family for their love, and for
their patience and understanding when I would disappear
for hours and hours (indeed months and months) in my
study: to my wife, Gill; my sons, Sam, Greg and Brendan,
who are now all doing so well in their own careers; and to
my brother, John, and sister, Rosalyn, for their boundless
encouragement and enthusiasm for this project.

Introduction
U
NCERTAINTY 
IS 
AN 
ESSENTIAL 
PART 
OF 
THE 
HUMAN
CONDITION. We don’t know if we will be run over by a bus
in the coming week or win some stupendous prize in the
national lottery, should we have bought a ticket. Looking
farther into the future, we can’t be sure if our investments
will be rendered worthless by the next global ﬁnancial
crash, or whether we will succumb to a new pandemic, to
the next world war, or indeed to climate change. Perhaps
we might wish for supernatural powers to predict the
future and relieve us from the stress of living under such
uncertainties. But what sort of people would we be if we
had these powers? If we knew for certain what was heading
our way, would we still be the creative, get-up-and-go
species we are?
Apparently, it’s not just 
our
lives that are uncertain.
According 
to 
our 
most 
successful 
theory 
of 
physics—
quantum mechanics—uncertainty is also an essential part
of the life of the elementary particles from which both we
and the world around us are made. How would these
elementary particles behave if we could excise uncertainty
from the basic laws of physics? Would they also end up as
indolent good-for-nothings? Would the universe be a very
diﬀerent 
place 
if 
the 
laws 
of 
physics 
were 
themselves
certain?
These are some of the questions I will try to answer in
this book. I will be elevating the notion of uncertainty, or
doubt, to a status it is not usually given: not as an “Oh, I
suppose we’d better do a risk analysis” afterthought, but as
a matter of primal importance and focus. There are two
reasons for doing this. First, there is the practical reason
that we are liable to make lousy decisions if we base them
on predictions with unreliable estimates of uncertainty. But
just as important, at least for me as a scientist, we may be
able 
to 
understand 
better 
the 
way 
systems 
work 
by
focusing on the ways in which they are or can become
uncertain. These reasons form the two themes of my book:
the science of uncertainty to predict our uncertain world
and to understand our uncertain world.
A 
well-known 
saying, 
variously 
attributed 
to 
Danish
quantum physicist Niels Bohr and baseball philosopher Yogi
Berra, is that prediction is very diﬀicult, especially if it’s
about the future. However, even tougher than making a
prediction is reliably estimating the uncertainty in that
prediction. You never hear an astrologer put error bars on
their forecast that you’ll meet a tall dark stranger. The
reason 
predicting 
uncertainty 
is 
so 
tough 
is 
that 
the
underlying 
causes 
of 
uncertainty 
can 
be 
small 
and
seemingly insigniﬁcant. Recall the famous proverb:
For want of a nail the shoe was lost.
For want of a shoe the horse was lost.
For want of a horse the rider was lost.
For want of a rider the message was lost.
For want of a message the battle was lost.
For want of a battle the kingdom was lost.
And all for the want of a horseshoe nail.
But this is only the half of it. Most of the time, kingdoms
weren’t lost for want of a single horseshoe nail. It’s only
occasionally that they were. Most of the time, kingdoms
ticked along in a reasonably steady and predictable way,
even 
though 
horseshoes 
frequently 
lost 
nails. 
The 
real
challenge is to know 
when
the kingdom is at risk of a
missing nail that results in huge repercussions, and when a
missing nail is irrelevant and doesn’t matter.
Many will have heard of the butterﬂy eﬀect: the idea that
a butterﬂy ﬂapping or not ﬂapping its wings in the jungle
can make the diﬀerence between a storm forming or not
forming over a remote part of the world. Whether or not
this is literally true, our weather forecast models cannot
represent all the tiny gusts of air on every part of the
planet. 
Like 
the 
lost 
horseshoe 
nail, 
tiny 
errors 
in
representing 
small-scale 
gusts 
of 
wind 
can

grow 
very
rapidly 
to 
make 
huge 
errors 
in 
large-scale 
weather
patterns. But most of the time they don’t. Most of the time
it doesn’t matter that we don’t know about these tiny gusts
of air when we are forecasting the weather for tomorrow or
the next day.
But sometimes it does matter.
Michael Fish was a respected weather forecaster on BBC
TV. Although his forecasts weren’t spot on all the time, by
and large they were reliable and the British public trusted
what 
he 
said—at 
least 
until 
October 
16, 
1987. 
Then,
overnight, 
he 
became 
an 
eponym 
for 
a 
failed 
and
discredited forecast system.
In his TV weather forecast on October 15, Fish famously
said: “Earlier on today, apparently, a woman
1
rang the BBC
and said she heard there was a hurricane on the way. Well,
if you’re watching, don’t worry, there isn’t!” Yet in the early
hours of the sixteenth, hurricane-strength winds battered
the south of England in what was the worst storm to hit the
country in over three hundred years. Fifteen million trees
were blown down, twenty-two people died and damages
exceeded $3 billion. Of course, if people had known that
there was indeed a hurricane on the way, they would have
moved cars from under trees and secured boats, cranes
and 
aircraft. 
They 
would 
have 
cancelled 
or 
postponed
inessential trips. But they trusted Fish’s assessment that
there would be no hurricane.
There was little else on the morning news. The BBC
anchorman harangued the weather forecaster on duty:
“Well, you chaps were a fat lot of good last night.… If
you can’t forecast the worst storms for several centuries
three hours before they happen, what are you doing?!”
The nation was incensed and there were calls for Sir
John Houghton, the director general of the UK’s national
weather service—the Met Oﬀice—to resign. For all their
supercomputers and satellite data, the government agency
which provided Michael Fish with the weather forecast had
failed woefully in its most fundamental duty: to warn the
public of life-threatening weather. Why was this? As we will
see, it was the intermittent butterﬂy eﬀect.
Economists 
had 
their 
Michael 
Fish 
moment 
in 
2008
when 
the 
global 
ﬁnancial 
markets 
collapsed 
without
forewarning. So said the Bank of England’s chief economist
Andy 
Haldane
2

in 
a 
speech 
to 
the 
UK 
Institute 
for
Government in 2017. According to Haldane the problem
with 
economic 
modelling 
was 
much 
worse 
than 
with
weather 
modelling: 
the 
economic 
models 
were 
“simply
unable to function properly when the world was tipped
upside 
down”. 
Why 
should 
this 
be? 
Is 
the 
economy
fundamentally 
more 
unpredictable 
than 
weather 
and
climate, as some leading economists believe
3
? Or was the
2008 crash an example of when, like the 1987 storm, the
conditions 
were 
right 
for 
small 
uncertainties 
in 
the
economic system to cascade up and create complete loss of
predictability 
at 
the 
macroeconomic 
scale? 
Does 
the
intermittent butterﬂy eﬀect apply to the economy as well?
In Part I of this book, I will explain why the butterﬂy
eﬀect is profoundly intermittent—and hence why we can be
lulled into a false sense of security about the predictability
of systems like the weather and perhaps the economy too.
Indeed, I will discuss how even planets, whose motion is
the very epitome of clockwork predictability, might have
their own Michael Fish moments. Underpinning this is
something I call “the geometry of chaos”: a type of fractal
geometry discovered by meteorologist Ed Lorenz, which I
rate as important as Einstein’s theories of relativity and
Schrödinger 
and 
Heisenberg’s 
theory 
of 
quantum
mechanics. 
“The 
Geometry 
of 
Chaos” 
vied 
with 
“The
Primacy of Doubt” as the title of this book.
The 1987 storm made it clear that we need to know
ahead of time when the butterﬂy eﬀect is going to be
particularly virulent and when it isn’t. In Part II of this
book, 
I 
discuss 
practical 
methods 
for 
dealing 
with
uncertainty in a number of diﬀerent areas where having
reliable 
predictions 
is 
critically 
important 
for 
our 
own
personal well-being and that of society more generally. For
example, weather services around the world no longer run
their model once when they make a weather forecast.
Instead, they run it perhaps ﬁfty times in what is called an
“ensemble 
prediction”. 
The 
ﬁfty 
diﬀerent 
starting
conditions 
for 
these 
forecasts 
are 
virtually 
identical.
However, they diﬀer by small “ﬂaps of butterﬂies’ wings”—
uncertain disturbances on the smallest scales resolvable by
the models. Not only that, some randomness, or noise, is
introduced into the computer-based weather equations to
represent 
the 
fact 
that 
the 
computer 
models 
are 
also
uncertain. When the atmosphere is especially unstable, as
it was in October 1987, the members of the ensemble
diverge from one another very rapidly. On other occasions,
they may only diverge very slowly. By studying how quickly
the 
small 
diﬀerences 
in 
the 
ensemble 
members 
grow
during 
the 
forecast 
period, 
forecasters 
should 
not 
be
caught out again by an explosively unpredictable situation
such as occurred in October 1987.
A consequence of the development of ensemble forecast
systems, one that many of us are familiar with on our
weather 
apps, 
is 
that 
the 
primary 
weather 
forecast
variables 
are 
now 
expressed 
as 
probabilities, 
the
probability 
of 
rain 
for 
example. 
This 
probability 
is
determined by the fraction of the ﬁfty ensemble members
in which it is raining. You may curse these probabilistic
forecasts for their imprecision—after all you just want to
know if it’s going to rain or not. However, probabilistic
forecasts now provide an objective basis for disaster relief
agencies to act pre-emptively ahead of a possible extreme
weather event, rather than simply wait for the event to
happen as they did in the past. This is called anticipatory
action. And these probabilistic ensemble techniques are
vital 
tools 
for 
trying 
to 
predict 
climate 
change 
on
timescales of decades and longer.
In Part II I also discuss the development of ensemble
prediction techniques for pandemic, economic and conﬂict
prediction, areas of enormous societal importance. Indeed,
COVID-19 has seen a rapid development of so-called multi-
model ensemble methods for predicting hospitalisations
and 
deaths 
from 
the 
pandemic. 
The 
Intergovernmental
Panel 
on 
Climate 
Change 
uses 
a 
similar 
multi-model
technique to estimate future levels of climate change. Our
experience in using these types of ensembles for climate
prediction can help assess their strengths and weaknesses
for pandemic prediction.
But 
what 
about 
those 
elementary 
particles 
and 
the
uncertain lives that they seem to lead? Einstein thought the
idea that the laws of physics are uncertain was nonsense.
He believed that the uncertainty in quantum physics was
no diﬀerent, in principle, from uncertainty in forecasting
the weather. That is to say, it’s not the elementary particles
that are uncertain, it’s our knowledge of the elementary
particles that is uncertain. As I discuss in Part I, most
contemporary 
physicists 
reject 
Einstein’s 
view, 
largely
because there are lab experiments which cast doubt on this
view.
In Part III, I challenge this consensus view of quantum
uncertainty 
using 
ideas 
based 
around 
the 
geometry 
of
chaos. The implications for fundamental physics—currently
in crisis given the ongoing failure to understand the nature
of 
the 
dark 
universe 
and 
a 
seeming 
inability 
to 
unify
Einstein’s theory of gravity with quantum mechanics—are
potentially massive.
One of the key results discussed in Part I is that noise
can be a constructive resource, and not the nuisance it is
often 
considered 
to 
be, 
when 
representing 
complex
systems. For example, when modelling a turbulent ﬂuid, it
makes sense to use noise to represent eddies and whirls
which are too small-scale to be modelled explicitly. In Part
III, I claim that, in a similar way, the brain makes use of
noise in modelling the world around us and that this is an
essential part of what makes us the creative species we
are.
But creativity is just one of our deﬁning characteristics.
We also have a very visceral sense of having a free will and
of being conscious of ourselves and the world around us.
But what is free will and consciousness? In Part III, I will
suggest that the geometry of chaos can also provide new
insights into these age-old conundrums.
In navigating through this book, one can read Part III
independently of Part II, and indeed one doesn’t need to
absorb all of Part I to read the later chapters. Although I
will 
be 
discussing 
mathematically 
sophisticated 
ideas
throughout the book, I have tried hard to avoid using
mathematics in the main text. I have drawn the Lorenz
equations, the Navier-Stokes equation and the Schrödinger
equation—the three key sets of equations in this book—
simply as cartoons. Along with the works of the great
artists, these are products of remarkable human creativity.
I have written a few equations in the endnotes.
The title of this book is taken from Gleick’s biography of
Richard Feynman, one of the greatest physicists of the
twentieth century. But what do we mean by “doubt”? If a
friend asserts something and you reply, “Hmm, I have my
doubts”, then you are expressing uncertainty about the
assertion. Indeed, “to feel uncertain about something” is
the ﬁrst deﬁnition in the Oxford Learner’s Dictionary
4
for
the word “doubt”. However, if you instead reply to your
friend, “I really doubt that”, then you are expressing more
than uncertainty, you are eﬀectively saying the assertion is
unlikely
to be correct. In this book, I am using the word
“doubt” in the former sense. I do not mean it to imply that
something is unlikely to be true. Indeed, as I shall discuss,
it is important to neither overestimate nor underestimate
uncertainty. 
Both 
can, 
and 
indeed 
have, 
led 
to 
poor
decisions.
In short, the scope of this book is quite unique. On the
one hand we cover the loftiest questions that philosophy
has ever addressed and attempt to answer them in novel
ways. On the other we’ll describe practical techniques that
have transformed the way we go about predicting how our
world will evolve over the coming days, years and decades
ahead. Some readers, I hope, will be exhilarated by the
discussion of long-standing conceptual problems like free
will, consciousness and the puzzling nature of quantum
physics. Others may be thrilled to see the science of chaos
applied for the beneﬁt of society (and some of the poorest
parts of society in particular). For yet others, reading this
book may help them understand themselves better. Not
least they may come to realise that some of our apparent
shortcomings are not signs of irrationality or failure, but
manifestations 
of 
our 
unique 
ability 
to 
cope 
with 
the
enormous uncertainties of life. There is, I hope, something
for everyone.
...
PART I
The Science of Uncertainty
The greater the doubt, the greater the awakening.
ATTRIBUTED TO ALBERT EINSTEIN
When I read those words, I get a prickling at the back
of my neck and my hair stands on end. He knew!
Thirty-four years ago, he knew!
IAN STEWART, 
DOES GOD PLAY DICE?
1
DESCRIBING ED
LORENZ’S DISCOVERY OF THE FRACTAL GEOMETRY OF
CHAOS IN 1963
Three important ideas are discussed in Part I. The ﬁrst is
that a type of geometry—what I call the geometry of chaos
—explains why some systems can be stable and predictable
for 
much 
of 
the 
time, 
and 
yet 
their 
future 
behaviour
becomes completely uncertain on occasion. The second
idea applies to systems that are so complex, we will never
be able to model them exactly. In such situations, adding
noise to a model can be a good way to represent some of
the missing complexity. In this way, noise is often a positive
constructive resource, and not the nuisance we typically
think 
it 
to 
be. 
I’ll 
give 
a 
number 
of 
examples 
of 
the
constructive value of noise. Finally, I discuss uncertainty in
quantum 
mechanics 
and 
explain 
why 
most 
physicists
believe it is diﬀerent to the uncertainty which characterises
the other chaotic systems discussed in this part of the
book. The ideas developed in this part of the book are
applied in Parts II and III.
...
1
Chaos, Chaos Everywhere
M
ANY OF US WOULD DESCRIBE OUR LIVES AS CHAOTIC—
DISORDERED and confused. Indeed, the science of chaos
describes systems whose behaviour is so unpredictable as
to appear disordered and confused. Strange, then, that the
science of chaos was initially developed by studying what
most 
of 
us 
believe 
to 
be 
the 
epitome 
of 
order 
and
predictability: the motion of the planets. We take it for
granted that the sun will rise in the east, not only tomorrow
but every day for the rest of our lives. And because we can
predict with great precision the movement of the Earth
about the sun, and the moon about the Earth, we know with
conﬁdence the time of high tide and the date of an eclipse,
not only days ahead, but for the rest of our lives.
And yet, for all that, the future motion of the planets in
the solar system is profoundly unpredictable and hence
uncertain.
The 
story 
starts 
with 
the 
scientiﬁc 
renaissance, 
the
series of events which marks the emergence of modern
science. 
The 
renaissance 
was 
kickstarted 
by 
Nicolaus
Copernicus’s 
De 
Revolutionibus 
Orbium 
Coelestium
,
published in 1543, and completed with the publication of
Isaac Newton’s 
Principia Mathematica
, in 1687.
In his book, Newton uses his famous three laws of
motion, together with his law of gravity, to deduce the
formula found empirically by German astronomer Johannes
Kepler—that the motion of a planet around the sun is an
ellipse with the sun at one focus
1
. To make this prediction,
Newton ignored the complications of the real solar system
and assumed it comprised just two gravitating bodies: the
sun and the planet in question.
There 
is 
nothing 
random 
or 
indeterminate 
about
Newton’s laws. If you know a planet’s position and velocity
right now, and you know the forces acting on the planet in
the future, you can calculate the position and velocity of
the planet at all times in the future. Those of us who
studied science in high school may recall the rather tedious
homework problems in which one had to calculate the
precise distance a projectile would travel if thrown with a
given initial speed and angle of elevation. The calculation
provides 
an 
example 
of 
how 
the 
future 
is 
seemingly
determined by applying Newton’s laws to a given set of
initial conditions. Because of this, Newton’s laws are said
to be deterministic. In 1814, the French philosopher and
mathematician Simon Laplace wrote about a hypothetical
daemon
2

who 
could 
exploit 
Newtonian 
determinism 
to
predict the future perfectly. Laplace said:
“An intellect which at a certain moment would know all
forces that set nature in motion, and all positions of all
items of which nature is composed, if this intellect were
also vast enough to submit these data to analysis, it would
embrace in a single formula the movements of the greatest
bodies of the universe and those of the tiniest atom; for
such an intellect nothing would be uncertain and the future
just like the past would be present before its eyes.”
This reference to “a single formula” is relevant here,
because 
Laplace 
was 
one 
of 
the 
many 
scientists 
from
Newton onwards who tried hard to generalise the formula
for 
an 
ellipse
3

in 
order 
to 
describe 
the 
orbits 
of 
a
hypothetical 
solar 
system 
with 
three 
or 
more
gravitationally bound bodies, e.g., the sun, the Earth and
the moon—or the sun, the Earth, the moon and Jupiter. One
would input the time into the formula, and the formula
would output the positions of the planets at that time.
The problem became known as the 
n
-body gravitational
problem. Newton had solved the problem for two bodies
(the sun and the Earth for example). Now the race was on
to ﬁnd the formula for 
n
= 3 or more. Laplace and his
contemporaries failed to ﬁnd this formula. Finding the
formula 
became 
a 
cause 
célèbre, 
and 
to 
celebrate 
his
sixtieth birthday, King Oscar II of Sweden oﬀered a prize to
anyone who could ﬁnd it.
The problem was ﬁnally solved in the late nineteenth
century by the French physicist and mathematician Henri
Poincaré, cousin of French president Raymond Poincaré.
Poincaré’s 
solution 
took 
the 
scientiﬁc 
community 
by
surprise. Whilst all the previous giants of mathematics had
assumed 
from 
the 
outset 
that 
such 
a 
formula 
exists,
Poincaré showed that when 
n
= 3 or more, no formula
exists.
What does it mean that no formula exists? Using a laptop
computer, which of course did not exist in Poincaré’s time,
we can solve Newton’s laws to plot the orbits of the three
bodies on the laptop screen, let’s say over a million Earth
years of simulated time. In principle we—or better, perhaps
an artiﬁcial intelligence (AI) system—could ﬁnd a single
mathematical formula, more complicated than that of an
ellipse, that describes these orbits very accurately.
However, if we extend the computer solution to two
million Earth years, then we will ﬁnd that our formula fails
to describe the orbits over the second million years. Our AI
system can perhaps ﬁnd a yet more complicated formula
which describes the orbits of the three bodies over two
million years. However, this formula will again fail if the
simulation is extended to three million years. Indeed, no
matter how complex a formula we construct to describe the
motion of the three bodies over any given ﬁnite period of
time, 
the 
formula 
will 
eventually 
fail 
if 
the 
motion 
is
extended to longer periods of time. There is no single
formula that can describe the orbits of these bodies over
arbitrarily 
long 
periods 
of 
time. 
This 
is 
what 
Poincaré
found.
A consequence of this is that the orbits of the three
bodies never repeat; if they did repeat, then there would
exist a formula for the orbits which would apply over
arbitrarily long periods of time. We say that the orbits of
the three bodies are non-periodic. Poincaré realised that
this meant the motion of planets in the solar system is
ultimately 
unpredictable—a 
property 
shared 
with 
the
weather. 
By 
studying 
planetary 
motion, 
Poincaré 
had
discovered a phenomenon we now call “chaos”. Because of
chaos, there is no formula that Laplace’s daemon can use
to see arbitrarily far into the future.
We can get some understanding of this planetary chaos
by looking at snapshots from a computer animation for 
n
=
4 gravitating bodies (Fig. 1)
4
. This example has been set up
to 
illustrate 
explicitly 
and 
dramatically 
what 
Poincaré
understood could happen from his mathematical analysis.
For a limited period of time, the orbits of these four bodies
resemble ellipses, and based on data from this limited
period, an AI system would likely conclude that the motion
would continue indeﬁnitely on these near-elliptical orbits.
However, very suddenly and with little warning, the planets
decide to spiral oﬀ to inﬁnity! The simple near-elliptical
formula that describes the orbits of the four bodies in the
early period fails completely in the later period.
FIG. 1. Snapshots of the orbits of four gravitationally bound objects. For
quite a long time, illustrated by snapshots 1–3, the bodies orbit around one
another in paths that approximate well to ellipses. However, suddenly,
seemingly out of nowhere, the objects spiral out to spatial inﬁnity. The
animation of this—see endnote 4—is well worth watching.
Fig. 1 illustrates a feature of chaotic systems which
occurs also for weather, and perhaps the economy and
many 
other 
systems. 
Like 
the 
Michael 
Fish 
moment
described 
in 
the 
introduction, 
the 
system 
appears
reasonably predictable until, out of the blue, it behaves
unpredictably diﬀerently.
Could the Earth ever get ejected from the solar system
in this way? That, were it to occur, would make global
warming, ﬁnancial crashes and everything else discussed in
this book pale into insigniﬁcance. How would we ﬁnd out?
The answer is to make a computer model of the solar
system and run it into the future.
But can our computer model be relied on to predict such
an event? Perhaps, like Michael Fish’s weather forecast,
the model will tell us not to worry. Perhaps the model will
predict that the simulated Earth will continue to orbit
periodically around the sun—only for us to ﬁnd the real
Earth getting ejected from the solar system in ﬁve years’
time.
The 
way 
to 
address 
this 
legitimate 
concern 
is 
to
understand the key uncertainties in predicting the future
orbits of the planets. In this case, the key uncertainty
relates to the precise position of the planets. To deal with
this, we can run our model hundreds of times with the
planets having very slightly diﬀerent starting positions,
consistent with this uncertainty. This is an example of an
ensemble forecast, a concept we will meet many times in
this book.
We 
can 
be 
relieved. 
Researchers 
from 
Princeton
University have run such an ensemble
5
, and in none of the
ensemble members is the Earth ejected from the solar
system over the coming billions of years. So we can assign
this potential crisis event a probability that is extremely
close to (but, strictly, not identical to) zero. The only crisis
event of note from these ensembles is that in about 1
percent of the ensemble members, the orbit of Mercury
becomes suﬀiciently eccentric that it collides with Venus
some billions of years in the future.
Here 
is 
our 
ﬁrst 
example 
of 
the 
application 
of 
the
ensemble prediction method. It allows us to conclude that
it’s extremely unlikely that the Earth will be ejected from
the 
solar 
system. 
However, 
such 
ensemble 
forecasts
indicate that the chance of the Earth being hit by a minor
asteroid, one that could easily destroy a large city such as
London, is not so completely negligible. This is why we
monitor threats from such asteroids as best we can
6
.
« »
AFTER THE DEATH OF Henri Poincaré, a Harvard University
mathematician, George Birkhoﬀ, took over the mantle as
the 
world’s 
leading 
expert 
on 
the 
gravitational 
n
-body
problem. In the 1930s, Birkhoﬀ took on a bright student,
Ed 
Lorenz, 
who 
was 
setting 
out 
on 
his 
postgraduate
studies, 
having 
studied 
mathematics 
at 
a 
nearby 
New
England Ivy League college. The mathematical topic that
Lorenz worked on under Birkhoﬀ was not the gravitational
n
-body problem, but was in an area called Riemannian
geometry. However, it would seem that some of Birkhoﬀ’s
thinking rubbed oﬀ on the young Lorenz, as, in the early
1960s, Lorenz was to make one of the greatest discoveries
of chaos theory, indeed of science in general I will argue.
Lorenz discovered the fractal geometry of chaos.
Lorenz’s 
plans 
to 
become 
a 
mathematician 
were
disrupted by the Second World War, and as a result he had
to decide how to best use his mathematical talents for the
war eﬀort. Since he had been rather fascinated by the
weather as a child, Lorenz signed on for a wartime training
course for meteorologists and eventually went out into the
Paciﬁc arena as an operational weather forecaster.
After the war, Lorenz had the option to continue his
mathematics research at Harvard. However, his wartime
work had rekindled his interest in weather, and he switched
ﬁelds and worked for a PhD at the Massachusetts Institute
of Technology (universally known as MIT) on the new,
physics-based weather forecast models that were starting
to be developed in the immediate postwar years. Having
completed his PhD at MIT and a postdoctoral appointment
at the University of California, in 1956 Lorenz was oﬀered
a permanent job back at MIT. His duties included leading a
group of researchers who were exploring the possibility of
predicting weather a month or more into the future.
In 
those 
days, 
little 
was 
understood 
about 
the
predictability of weather more than a day or two into the
future. Some of the leading statisticians of the day told
Lorenz 
that 
long-range 
weather 
forecasting 
was 
in
principle 
an 
easy 
problem 
to 
crack. 
Say 
you 
want 
to
forecast the weather a month ahead. Then you simply go to
the weather archives and ﬁnd a weather map that looks
close enough to today’s weather map—this would be called
an analogue. The forecast for a month hence would simply
be the weather map in the archive a month following the
date of the analogue map.
The method didn’t seem to work in practice. However,
the statistical experts argued that this was simply because
the archives were not large enough, and hence did not
contain close-enough analogues. Wait for the archives to
grow, they would say, and then you will ﬁnd that the
analogue method will start to work. Lorenz was sceptical
about this. For analogue forecasting to work in this way,
Lorenz argued, the weather would have to be periodic,
repeating itself over and over, as in the movie 
Groundhog
Day
. Lorenz’s intuition was that the weather equations
would not support such periodic behaviour. Indeed, this is
exactly what Poincaré had shown in the case of the 3-body
gravitational problem.
But there are two important diﬀerences between the
motion of the planets and the evolution of weather. Firstly,
unlike the solar system, with a relatively small number of
planets and moons, the atmosphere is a turbulent ﬂuid
7
with literally trillions upon trillions of whirls or eddies with
all sorts of diﬀerent sizes. If you had asked most of the
meteorological experts of the day, they, like Lorenz, would
also have been sceptical that the atmosphere ever repeated
itself. However, the experts would have said that such non-
periodicity 
arose 
because 
the 
atmosphere 
is 
such 
a
complex 
multiscale 
system, 
with 
numerous 
interacting
whirls from the planetary scale down to the microscale. In
other 
words, 
conventional 
wisdom 
had 
it 
that 
the
complexity 
of 
the 
atmosphere 
was 
the 
origin 
of 
its
unpredictability. 
The 
more 
the 
weather 
equations 
are
simpliﬁed, the experts of the time would have conjectured,
the more the simpliﬁed weather equations would produce
periodic and hence predictable behaviour.
Lorenz had the idea of trying to use the computer-based
weather forecast models he had helped develop as a PhD
student to demonstrate his intuition that weather did not
repeat 
itself. 
However, 
the 
very 
fact 
that 
these 
were
complex models posed a practical problem. You might run a
model for a hundred years and conclude that the simulated
weather did not repeat itself. But perhaps years 101–200
were exact repeats
8
of years 1–100. Who’s to say?
In thinking about this, Lorenz began to wonder whether
non-periodicity could be found in simpliﬁed systems which
could be described with a very small number of equations.
Where 
did 
he 
get 
this 
idea? 
Was 
it 
from 
practical
experience, 
running 
simpliﬁed 
weather 
models 
on 
the
computer? Or did he get the idea from the fact that the 3-
body gravitational problem was not periodic? In his semi-
biographical book
9
, 
The Essence of Chaos
, Lorenz writes
that, despite working with Birkhoﬀ, he was not familiar
with Poincaré’s work. And so we have to assume it was a
hypothesis 
he 
formed 
from 
his 
experience 
in 
running
weather models.
In any case, Lorenz was right and the meteorological
experts 
were 
wrong. 
Lorenz 
kept 
simplifying 
and
simplifying until he devised a mathematical model of ﬂuid
motion based on just three equations for three variables,
conventionally 
labelled 
X
, 
Y

and 
Z
. 
This 
is 
actually 
a
simpler set of equations than for the 
n
= 3 gravitational
problem, since each planet in the gravitational problem
needs six variables to represent it: three spatial coordinate
positions and three components of velocity.
Lorenz’s model is so idealised it no longer makes much
sense 
to 
relate 
these 
three 
variables 
to 
speciﬁc 
ﬂuid
quantities 
(though 
it 
is 
possible 
to 
make 
a 
chaotic
waterwheel 
whose 
motion 
is 
described 
by 
the 
Lorenz
equations
10
). So, let’s just think of 
X
, 
Y
and 
Z
as abstract
variables whose values change with time. At a particular
instant in time, these variables can be described by three
numbers, e.g., 
X
= 3.327, 
Y
= 5.674, 
Z
= 0.485. Lorenz’s
equations describe how these numbers evolve in time. The
equations make use of the mathematical technique called
calculus, discovered by Newton and independently by the
German mathematician, philosopher and engineer Gottfried
Leibniz. Using the calculus, the time rate of change of 
X
, 
Y
and 
Z
can be described in terms of the current values of 
X
,
Y
and 
Z
and various ﬁxed parameters (see Fig. 2). Lorenz’s
equations 
would 
have 
been 
quite 
understandable 
and
recognisable 
to 
Newton 
or 
Leibniz 
as 
three 
so-called
nonlinear diﬀerential equations.
The 
word 
“nonlinear” 
is 
of 
crucial 
importance 
here.
Nonlinear systems are those whose outputs do not change
in direct proportion to their inputs. Assuming you are not a
wealthy person, you would be very happy indeed to win $1
million in the lottery. You would be even happier to win $2
million, but likely not twice as happy. You would very likely
not be ten times as happy to win $10 million (substitute
billions for millions if you are very wealthy). That’s an
example of nonlinearity—your happiness (output) does not
vary in direct proportion to your lottery winnings (input). In
the case of the Lorenz system, if you double the size of the
X
, 
Y
and 
Z
variables, the time rate of change of the 
X
, 
Y
and
Z

variables 
does 
not 
double. 
You, 
dear 
reader, 
are 
a
nonlinear system, just as Lorenz’s equations are. Lorenz’s
system would be predictable if its governing equations
were linear.
FIG. 2. THE LORENZ EQUATIONS AS A WORK OF ART.
Solving these equations on a computer of the early
1960s was a challenging task. However, with just three
variables, Lorenz could run the equations for long enough
periods of time for him to ﬁnally realise that the model
state was never going to repeat itself. How exactly he came
to this realisation will be discussed in the next chapter—it
is one of the great discoveries of twentieth-century science.
Published in the 
Journal of the Atmospheric Sciences
in
1963, Lorenz’s most famous paper announces the discovery
of “Deterministic Non-periodic Flow”
11
. As before, “non-
periodic” means non-repeating. Because it was published in
such a “niche” journal, it took another decade before the
paper became known to the mathematical community at
large, and this delayed the explosion of chaos science by a
similar period of time.
However, Lorenz’s model is not best known for this
property 
of 
non-periodicity. 
Instead, 
it’s 
known 
for 
a
property which is a consequence of non-periodicity, though
Lorenz himself did not fully understand this at ﬁrst.
Lorenz used to tell the story about how he wanted to
repeat a simulation on his computer in order to get the
computer to output data more frequently. He took some
values which the computer had previously outputted onto
printer paper, and restarted the computer initialising the
model with these printed values. After he had returned
from a coﬀee break, Lorenz noticed that the repeated
values of 
X, Y
and 
Z
bore no relation to the original values
that the computer had earlier outputted. An example of
what Lorenz saw is shown in Fig. 3. At ﬁrst, he thought that
the computer had become faulty (valves, or “tubes”, often
failed 
in 
these 
early 
computers). 
However, 
he 
quickly
realised that this wasn’t the problem. Rather the problem
was related to the fact that the printer did not record the
variables at “machine precision” but instead printed only
the leading digits of the variables. For example, if the
precise value of a variable was 
X
= 0.506127, the printer
printed only 
X
= 0.506. Starting the model with 
X
= 0.506
instead of 0.506127 eventually led to big diﬀerences in the
future values of 
X, Y
and 
Z.
This 
“sensitive 
dependence 
on 
initial 
conditions”
eventually became known as the butterﬂy eﬀect. However,
in some ways this is a misnomer. Butterﬂies are very small
creatures, certainly much smaller than weather systems.
The 
Lorenz 
model, 
with 
only 
three 
variables, 
has 
no
concept 
of 
spatial 
scale. 
It 
is 
too 
simple 
to 
describe
weather, never mind butterﬂies. In particular, the Lorenz
model cannot describe how a 
small-scale
error can aﬀect a
large-scale weather system. All it can do is describe how a
small-amplitude

error 
can 
grow 
into 
a 
large-amplitude
error. We will look at the butterﬂy eﬀect in terms of small-
scale errors growing into large-scale errors in Chapter 3.
And we will ﬁnd something even more mind-boggling than
the type of chaos shown here.
FIG. 3. This is the classic illustration of chaos in the Lorenz model. Two
almost but not quite identical initial conditions eventually lead to very
diﬀerent evolutions. This is the way chaos is traditionally described.
However, there is something deeper underpinning this behaviour.
In some ways, the illustration of chaos in Fig. 3 seems
not so diﬀerent to that in Fig. 1. In Fig. 1, the position of
the planets is unpredictable, whilst in Fig. 3, the more
abstract variables in Lorenz’s equations are seen to be
unpredictable. We might well ask whether Lorenz’s chaos is
somehow described by the earlier theory of Poincaré.
The answer is no. There is one very important diﬀerence
between Lorenz’s chaos and Poincaré’s chaos. Real-world
ﬂuids are viscous. If a ﬂuid’s viscosity is large, the ﬂuid is
sticky like treacle. Viscosity is a kind of internal friction in
the ﬂuid which makes it resistant to small-scale gradients
in ﬂuid velocity. In practice, viscosity converts such small-
scale changes in ﬂuid velocity into the random motion of
molecules 
and 
hence 
into 
heat. 
This 
process 
is 
called
energy dissipation. All realistic ﬂuids, water or air for
example, are viscous to a greater or lesser extent.
Imagine a tank of water at rest that is suddenly stirred
vigorously with a paddle. Whilst the paddle is moving, it
excites turbulent whirls that spread out from the paddle.
However, as soon as one stops stirring, the whirls die out
due to the eﬀects of viscosity. The ﬂuid returns to a state of
rest but is very slightly warmer than it was, due to the
eﬀects 
of 
energy 
dissipation 
by 
viscosity. 
Now, 
if 
we
videoed this, and ran the video backwards, we would see
something completely unrealistic: small eddies growing in
the ﬂuid far from the paddle and converging onto the
paddle. In this backwards-in-time video, it would seem the
paddle’s 
motion 
is 
being 
powered 
by 
a 
slight 
uniform
cooling 
of 
the 
ﬂuid, 
contradicting 
the 
second 
law 
of
thermodynamics, which says that uniform low-grade heat
can’t be converted into work in such a way. Because the
backwards-in-time motion is unrealistic, we say that the
equations that govern the motion of viscous ﬂuids are
irreversible 
in 
time—the 
video 
and 
hence 
the 
ﬂuid
equations can realistically be run in only one direction in
time.
By contrast the equations that govern the motion of
planets are time reversible. If we had a video of the planets
moving around the sun and reversed it, the motion would
be consistent with the laws of physics. In some sense, it is
only chance that makes the planets rotate the way they do
about the sun.
Lorenz 
felt 
that 
it 
was 
vital 
to 
include 
this 
time
irreversibility 
into 
his 
equations. 
He 
was 
right. 
Time
irreversibility is crucial for generating the geometry of
chaos, a geometry that will form a central part of this book.
The irreversibility we see in ﬂuid motions is much more
commonplace than the reversibility in time that we see in
planetary motion. For example, when we drop a glass, it
smashes into hundreds of pieces. We never see the reverse.
A baby grows into an adult. We never see the reverse. We
can easily scramble eggs. We can’t unscramble them into
whites and yolks. We can think of many similar types of
examples. What is the origin of such irreversibility? This
has perplexed scientists for centuries because Newton’s
laws 
of 
motion, 
or 
indeed 
Schrödinger’s 
equation 
of
quantum mechanics, are themselves time reversible; the
same equation describes a system in a movie, irrespective
of whether the movie is run forwards or backwards. The
way most scientists deal with this problem is to say that the
universe must have begun at the time of the Big Bang in an
exceptionally 
ordered 
state. 
That 
is 
to 
say, 
the
irreversibility 
we 
see 
arises 
from 
the 
fact 
that 
the
cosmological initial conditions were special. I will attempt a
diﬀerent 
explanation, 
using 
the 
geometry 
of 
chaos, 
in
Chapter 11.
Now, although the type of chaos studied by Poincaré and
Lorenz made use of Newton’s calculus, calculus is not itself
an 
essential 
ingredient 
of 
chaos. 
Before 
ﬁnishing 
this
chapter, I want to give a very simple example of chaos—
popularised by physicist-ecologist Bob May and applied to
population growth—which doesn’t use calculus.
If Adam and Eve had four children, two boys and two
girls, and in the next generation, each pair of adult males
and females in turn produced four children, then the size of
the population for each generation would double (2, 4, 8,
16… ). Prior to May, it was assumed that the population
would grow until it eventually either came into some kind
of steady state consistent with the ﬁnite environmental
resources available to the population or, at most, would
oscillate 
periodically. 
As 
a 
result, 
when 
irregular
ﬂuctuations in some species’ populations were observed,
ecologists assumed this must be because the environmental
resources 
were 
changing 
because 
of 
external 
factors—
maybe because of irregular ﬂuctuations in the climate, for
example.
However, with a very simple equation, May
12
showed
that this was not necessarily the case. May’s equation
13
determines the size of a population at the next generation,
given the population at this generation. The size of the
population at each generation can be computed using a
simple calculator—the equation is that simple. In May’s
equation, there is a free parameter called 
a
. It describes
the number of oﬀspring each single adult produces on
average, at least in the initial phases of growth. In Adam
and Eve’s example, 
a
= 2. However, once the population
becomes 
large 
enough, 
its 
growth 
becomes 
limited 
by
environmental 
resources. 
In 
such 
circumstances, 
the
population 
can 
suddenly 
crash 
to 
small 
values, 
before
starting to grow again. What May showed was that if 
a
exceeds about 3.57, the evolution of the population over
multiple generations is chaotic—the periods of boom and
bust start to occur irregularly. This means two things. Like
the Lorenz-model variables, the population ﬂuctuates non-
periodically without any external forcing in environmental
resources. And like in the Lorenz model, it is very diﬀicult
to 
predict 
these 
ﬂuctuations—they 
are 
sensitive 
to 
the
starting conditions for the population size.
In this way, chaos theory has had a signiﬁcant impact in
the science of ecology. Indeed, over the years, chaos theory
has impacted on almost all branches of science: not only
astronomy, 
meteorology 
and 
ecology, 
but 
chemistry,
engineering, biology and social science; you name it! But
perhaps one ﬁeld where chaos theory hasn’t had so much
impact is the foundations of quantum physics
14
. The reason
is simple: the basic equation which governs the evolution of
quantum mechanical systems, the Schrödinger equation, is
a 
linear 
equation. 
As 
such, 
the 
governing 
equation 
of
quantum mechanics doesn’t exhibit the type of sensitive
dependence on initial conditions that we discussed earlier.
It is sometimes argued that because we expect planetary
motion, the weather and indeed population dynamics to
somehow follow from quantum mechanics (not that anyone
has 
actually 
shown 
this), 
chaos 
is 
somehow 
only 
an
approximate 
notion 
and 
uncertainty 
in 
the 
weather 
is
ultimately due to quantum uncertainty, not chaos. My view
is 180° diﬀerent. I believe chaos actually underpins the
dynamical 
equations 
of 
quantum 
physics; 
chaos 
is 
a
property of the planets and the weather, precisely because
chaos is a property of the fundamental dynamics of the
universe. I will discuss in the next chapter why such a view
is in fact consistent with the linearity of the Schrödinger
equation.
...
2
The Geometry of Chaos
A
LTHOUGH ISAAC NEWTON WOULD HAVE UNDERSTOOD
LORENZ’S equations, not least because these equations are
expressed 
using 
the 
calculus 
which 
he 
himself 
had
discovered, 
Newton 
would 
not 
have 
had 
the 
slightest
inkling of the remarkable type of geometry that emerges
from them.
For Newton, the word “geometry” was a shorthand for
the geometry of Euclid, the Greek mathematician from
Alexandria born around 300 BCE. As part of his training to
be 
a 
natural 
philosopher, 
Newton 
would 
have 
studied
Euclid’s 
seminal 
work, 
The Elements
. 
Here 
Euclid, 
for
example, introduces the cross-sections of a cone. One of
these, the ellipse, describes well the motion of a planet
around the sun.
The ellipse characterises an important aspect of many of
the geometric ﬁgures studied by Euclid. Draw an ellipse on
a piece of paper and zoom into any portion of it with a
magnifying glass. With a powerful enough glass, any small
portion will become indistinguishable from a straight line.
Similarly, zoom into the surface of an ellipsoid—an egg-
shaped 
two-dimensional 
ﬁgure. 
Again, 
with 
a 
powerful
enough magnifying glass, any small portion of the ellipsoid
will be indistinguishable from a ﬂat plane. In Einstein’s
theory of general relativity, space-time is a curved four-
dimensional space. The curvature of space-time manifests
itself by the fact that the internal angles of a triangle do not
sum to 180°. However, if you zoom suﬀiciently into any
region of space-time, it looks ﬂat—the internal angles of
small-enough triangles sum as close as you like to 180°.
In 
all 
these 
examples, 
the 
special 
properties 
of 
the
geometric object that make it an ellipse, or an ellipsoid or a
solution 
of 
Einstein’s 
equations 
of 
general 
relativity,
somehow get washed out if you zoom in enough with your
magnifying glass. Considered as spaces in their own right,
these geometric objects are called “locally Euclidean”. By
contrast, 
the 
properties 
associated 
with 
the 
geometry
produced 
by 
Lorenz’s 
equations 
never 
get 
washed 
out
under such a zoom, no matter which part of the geometry
you zoom into. This is a concept which would have been
alien to Newton and to the generations of mathematicians
and scientists brought up studying Euclid’s 
Elements
. The
idea that such a strange geometry could be the product of a
simple set of equations based on calculus was also mind-
boggling to Lorenz, and it took him a long time to realise
what he had actually discovered.
In my view, Lorenz’s really great discovery was not so
much the butterﬂy eﬀect—this was, after all, implicit in the
work of Poincaré—as the discovery of the fractal geometry
of 
chaos. 
As 
we 
shall 
see, 
the 
consequences 
of 
this
geometry permeate everything in this book, from modern
mathematics, to climate change, to quantum physics, to
free will and to consciousness.
« »
IT 
IS 
RATHER 
CONVENIENT 
that 
there 
are 
only 
three
variables, 
X
, 
Y
and 
Z
, in Lorenz’s model of chaos. It means
we can represent a point in Lorenz’s “state space” (relative
to three right-angled axes labelled 
X
, 
Y
and 
Z
) in the same
way we would plot a point in ordinary three-dimensional
physical 
space 
(relative 
to 
three 
right-angled 
axes
conventionally labelled 
x, y
and 
z
, in lower case).
The notion of state space is really important in this book.
So I want to give a simple example of state space. Suppose
you are buying a pair of trousers. The ﬁrst thing to do is
decide what leg length, what waist size and what colour of
material to buy. Let’s suppose the only choice of colour is a
shade of grey. Then a particular pair of trousers can be
represented as a point in an abstract space where one
direction 
describes 
variations 
in 
leg 
length, 
a 
second
describes variations in waist size and a third the shade of
grey. We can call this three-dimensional space “trouser
state space” (see Fig. 4). There is nothing special about the
fact that trouser state space is three dimensional. Indeed, if
we also had the possibility to choose the type of material
used (e.g., wool, polyester or some mixture thereof), then
we would need an extra, fourth dimension to additionally
account for the choice of type of material, and trouser state
space would then be four dimensional (and impossible to
illustrate in a single ﬁgure in this physical book). In this
way, the dimension of state space is equal to the number of
independent variables needed to specify the object you are
interested in (such as a pair of trousers).
Compared with the theory of physics known as string
theory, which posits that the physical space in which we
live is not three dimensional but ten or eleven dimensional,
there is nothing weird, unusual or incomprehensible about
a multidimensional state space. If you get confused about
the diﬀerence between physical space and state space, just
think trousers!
FIG. 4. You want to buy a pair of trousers. You have to know what leg
length, waist size and shade of grey to choose. Your choice corresponds to a
point in three-dimensional “trouser state space”. If you also had the option
of a type of material, then this would correspond to a fourth dimension in
trouser state space, making the state space impossible to illustrate but
nevertheless easy to understand conceptually.
Let’s think of another example: the state space of 3
gravitationally bound bodies. Each body can be speciﬁed by
a position and a velocity. In physical 3-dimensional space
we need 3 numbers to specify a position and 3 more
numbers to specify velocity (the speed of the body relative
to the 
x
, 
y
and 
z
axes). This means that the number of
independent numbers needed to specify the position and
velocity of the 3 bodies is 3 lots of 3 + 3, which is 18.
Hence, the dimension of the state space of the 3 bodies is
18
1
. 
Although 
it 
is 
impossible 
to 
visualise 
an 
18-
dimensional space, the concept of an 18-dimensional state
space is perhaps not especially diﬀicult in principle.
The dimension of the state space of weather is enormous
by comparison. If we consider all the whirls that exist in the
atmosphere at any time, the state space of weather is
bigger 
than 
anything 
we 
could 
ever 
represent 
in 
a
computer—not 
just 
today’s 
computers, 
but 
ones 
in 
the
foreseeable 
future 
too. 
The 
state 
space 
of 
a 
modern
weather 
prediction 
model 
has 
more 
than 
a 
billion
dimensions. That’s a large number of course, and one that
a modern supercomputer can handle, but it’s still a tiny
fraction 
of 
the 
dimension 
of 
the 
actual 
state 
space 
of
weather
2
.
Try to imagine the state space of the whole universe,
comprising all the atoms that make all the stars, galaxies
and clusters of galaxies. The dimension of such a state
space is overwhelmingly, unbelievably large. But as long as
it is a ﬁnite number, it is in principle no more diﬀicult to
comprehend than 4-dimensional trouser state space.
Let’s 
return 
to 
Lorenz’s 
3-dimensional 
state 
space.
Choose a point in this state space. This can be considered
as an initial state for Lorenz’s equations. Using a computer,
we can solve Lorenz’s equations over a ﬁnite period of
time. This solution describes a set of points in state space,
one for each moment in time. We can represent the overall
time evolution of the values of 
X
, 
Y
and 
Z
as a curve in
Lorenz’s 3-dimensional state space (see Fig. 5). We call this
curve a state-space trajectory, or simply a “trajectory”.
If 
Lorenz’s 
model 
did 
have 
periodic 
(i.e., 
repeating)
behaviour, and if the state-space trajectory were continued
for a long-enough time, it would eventually join up with the
starting point, represented by the dot. The states described
by such a closed loop would then repeat over and over.
FIG. 5. A short trajectory segment in Lorenz’s state space. The initial point
is marked with a dot. This can be chosen arbitrarily. The trajectory gives the
values of the 
X
, 
Y
and 
Z
variables at future times and is obtained by solving
the Lorenz equations on a computer.
Fig. 6 shows a picture of what the trajectories of the
Lorenz model look like when you solve the equations over a
long period of time. It is now quite a famous diagram. To
produce such a picture, it doesn’t matter from what state
you start. Eventually, the trajectories start following this
strange shape with the two lobes. It is as if the trajectories
are attracted to this geometric object in state space. As a
result, 
this 
geometric 
object 
is 
called 
an 
“attractor”.
Curiously, it looks a bit like a butterﬂy, but that is a
coincidence.
The question Lorenz asked himself was this: What is the
underlying geometry of this attractor? Is it some kind of
Euclidean 
geometry? 
One 
possibility 
is 
that, 
if 
the
computer could continue running Lorenz’s model forever,
all the tiny gaps that appear in Fig. 6 would be ﬁlled in,
leaving a solid 3-dimensional object, one that we could
perhaps carve from a piece of solid wood.
FIG. 6. An approximation of the famous Lorenz attractor as represented by
a computer. No matter where you start in state space, the Lorenz equations
will take you closer and closer to this attractor. One can say that the
attractor is an emergent property of the Lorenz equations. The key question
Lorenz asked himself is this: What is the geometry of this attractor? Is it a
solid object or a complex folded surface? Lorenz showed it could be neither
of these; it had to be a fractal.
However, Lorenz realised this could not be so. This is
where the time irreversibility of Lorenz’s model is crucial.
As we saw in the last chapter, Lorenz included a very
simple representation of the irreversible eﬀects of energy
dissipation in his model. Hence Lorenz’s equations are time
irreversible. And this makes these equations fundamentally
diﬀerent to those for planetary motion, studied by Poincaré.
Under the eﬀect of the irreversible energy dissipation, a
solid 3-dimensional ball of initial conditions in state space
would eventually shrink to an object with zero volume,
under the action of the Lorenz equations. On the other
hand, because of the chaotic unpredictability of the Lorenz
equations, the diﬀerent points of the initial 3-dimensional
ball 
will 
eventually 
uniformly 
cover 
the 
whole 
Lorenz
attractor. As such, the volume of the attractor has to be
strictly zero.
Now, a 2-dimensional surface has zero volume. So could
the attractor be 2-dimensional? For example, instead of
carving 
it 
from 
a 
block 
of 
wood, 
perhaps 
we 
could
construct the attractor by carefully folding a 2-dimensional
sheet, like a piece of origami. Well, Lorenz could rule this
out too, by a mathematical theorem proved by Poincaré
himself. The Poincaré-Bendixson theorem says that if the
attractor 
were 
2-dimensional 
and 
had 
the 
property 
of
sensitive 
dependence 
on 
initial 
conditions 
(which 
we
showed in the last chapter was the case) and lay in some
bounded region of state space (which the Lorenz attractor
does), then somewhere the state-space trajectories would
cross 
over 
themselves. 
However, 
if 
the 
equations 
are
deterministic, 
then 
such 
a 
crossing 
point 
would 
be
impossible: determinism implies that an initial point in
state space can evolve in time only into another single
point, not into two diﬀerent points.
Well, 
perhaps 
the 
attractor 
is 
just 
some 
closed 
1-
dimensional curve where a trajectory eventually joins up
with itself, like a convoluted circle? But if this were the
case, it would imply that the model is periodic, and the
computer output from the model suggests otherwise
3
.
So, what is the geometry of the attractor if it is neither a
3-dimensional volume nor a 2-dimensional sheet nor a 1-
dimensional line? Lorenz agonised about this for quite
some time. I can easily understand his frustration. He knew
he was onto something important, but he couldn’t quite put
his ﬁnger on what it was.
The solution came from the work of the nineteenth-
century German mathematician Georg Cantor. Cantor was
the founder of a type of mathematics known as set theory.
At one level, sets are fairly trivial mathematical things.
They simply denote collections of objects. The set {1, 2, 3,
4} is the collection of whole numbers between 1 and 4
inclusive. The collection of countries in the world is a set
whose elements are individual countries. However, things
get 
mathematically 
interesting 
when 
considering 
sets
which contain unlimited numbers of objects, such as the set
of all positive whole numbers, which we can write as {1, 2,
3… }. Now although this set is inﬁnite, it is, as Cantor was
able to show in a precise way, not nearly as big as the set of
all numbers, which includes not only whole numbers and
fractions like 2.4 and 6.97, but also numbers like √2, the
square 
root 
of 
2, 
and 
π, 
the 
ratio 
between 
the
circumference of a circle and its diameter. Numbers like
2.4 
and 
6.97 
can 
be 
written 
as 
fractions 
(24/10 
and
697/100), but √2 and π cannot be written in this way and
are called “irrational”. Such irrational numbers cannot be
represented on a computer with a ﬁnite number of bits. We
call the set of all numbers, which includes such irrational
numbers, “real numbers”. It is the complete dominance of
the 
irrational 
numbers 
over 
the 
rational 
numbers 
that
makes the set of real numbers so much bigger than the set
of whole or, indeed, rational numbers.
There is an intimate relationship between sets of real
numbers and Euclidean geometry. If you draw a line, say of
length 10 cm, then each point on the line can represent a
real number, say between 0 and 10. The whole numbers—0,
1, 2,… 10—can be represented by points at a distance 0 cm,
1 cm… 10 cm from one end. The number π corresponds to
a point roughly 3.14 cm from one end of the line.
Now, a line is a one-dimensional set. One might imagine
that all sets which contain as many points as the set of real
numbers are at least one dimensional. However, Cantor
constructed a set, and in turn a geometry, in which this
intuitive 
idea 
is 
false. 
Cantor 
was 
criticised 
by 
his
colleagues for his ideas. The great Henri Poincaré said that
in the future people would look on Cantor’s work as “a
disease 
from 
which 
one 
has 
recovered”
4
. 
Ironically,
Poincaré had no idea how important Cantor’s work would
be to understanding the subject that he, Poincaré, had kick-
started: 
chaos 
theory. 
Ultimately 
these 
criticisms 
sent
Cantor 
into 
a 
depression 
from 
which 
he 
never 
fully
recovered. Forty years after Cantor’s death, Lorenz ﬁnally
understood the importance of Cantor’s ideas. Cantor is now
rightly seen as a mathematical pioneer.
Cantor’s set, shown in Fig. 7, can be described by a set
of 
steps. 
These 
steps 
are 
repeated 
applications, 
or
iterations, of a simple rule. Start with the real-number line
between 0 and 1. In the ﬁrst application, throw away the
middle third of this line (technically, one keeps the end
points of the two remaining smaller lines). Then, in the
second application, throw away the middle thirds of the two
smaller lines. Then, in the third application, throw away the
middle thirds of the four remaining lines. One can carry on
like this indeﬁnitely, removing at each step the middle
thirds of the remaining lines. Cantor’s set is the set of
points that are common to all the iterates. What Cantor
showed was that his eponymous set had as many points as
the points of the original line—even though we have thrown
away an inﬁnite set of points. In this sense, Cantor’s set is
very large (much larger than the set of whole numbers).
However, in a diﬀerent sense, Cantor’s set is also rather
small. One way of seeing this is to imagine randomly
choosing a point from the original line (between 0 and 1).
The probability that this randomly chosen point belongs to
Cantor’s set is equal to zero. Mathematicians say that
Cantor’s set has “measure zero”.
FIG. 7. The Cantor set is created by a series of iterations. Start with the
line between 0 and 1 and remove the middle third. Then remove the middle
thirds of the remaining two pieces and continue on and on as shown. The
Cantor set is the set of points which belong to all the iterates.
The Cantor set (as I shall now call it) reminds me of the
time traveller Doctor Who’s spacecraft from the BBC TV
