science ﬁction series—apologies to readers who have not
encountered this TV programme. From the outside, Doctor
Who’s spacecraft looks like a 1950s police telephone box—
small and insubstantial. However, from the inside it looks
palatial, with rooms everywhere! From the outside, the
measure-zero property of the Cantor set makes it appear
tiny. However, it has as many points as the real line and so,
looked 
at 
internally, 
it 
seems 
enormous. 
This 
has 
big
consequences.
We can easily see that the geometry of the Cantor set is
diﬀerent to the Euclidean geometries discussed earlier. No
matter how much we zoom into the Cantor set we ﬁnd the
same structure as we started with—small intervals with
pieces missing. Whatever the geometry of the Cantor set is,
it is not locally Euclidean.
So how can we describe the Cantor set in a way that
distinguishes it from the real-number line? It turns out that
one way of doing this is through the concept of dimension.
Now, a set containing a ﬁnite collection of points is a 0-
dimensional set. Lines are 1-dimensional sets. Surfaces are
2-dimensional 
sets, 
and 
solid 
objects 
are 
3-dimensional
sets. Space-time in Einstein’s theory of relativity is a 4-
dimensional 
set, 
and, 
as 
we 
have 
discussed, 
the
unrestricted 
state 
space 
for 
the 
gravitational 
3-body
problem 
is 
an 
18-dimensional 
set. 
It 
is 
possible 
to
generalise this notion of dimension in order to describe the
Cantor set. With this deﬁnition it turns out that the Cantor
set has a dimension which lies between 0 and 1—neither 0
nor 1, but approximately 0.631
5
. The type of geometry
associated 
with 
the 
Cantor 
set 
is 
known 
as 
fractal
geometry, 
so 
named 
by 
the 
French 
American
mathematician Benoit Mandelbrot
6
. The word “fractal” is in
fact short for “fractionally dimensioned”.
The Cantor set lies at the heart of the Lorenz attractor.
To explain this, I am going to actually describe the fractal
attractor of a set of equations similar to but slightly simpler
than the Lorenz equations. These are known as the Rössler
equations.
The German biochemist Otto Rössler discovered his set
of three equations
7
as a direct result of Lorenz’s work. This
is the simplest set of equations which uses calculus to
produce a fractal attractor. The Rössler attractor is shown
in Fig. 8 (I have labelled the axes 
X
, 
Y
and 
Z
as before, but
now 
these 
variables 
satisfy 
equations 
diﬀerent 
to 
the
Lorenz equations).
To understand how the Rössler equations generate a
fractal 
attractor, 
let’s 
start 
by 
imagining 
part 
of 
the
attractor as a simple 2-dimensional sheet. The action of the
Rössler equations is to fold the sheet over as in the left-
hand picture of Fig. 9. But we can’t join the two ends of the
sheet because one end is folded and the other end not.
Also, because of dissipation, the total area of the folded end
is a little smaller than the original, unfolded end. We could
imagine starting with a folded-over sheet, as in the middle
panel of Fig. 9. But now the action of the Rössler equations
is to create a doubly folded-over sheet. Again, we can’t join
the two ends together. We could imagine starting with a
doubly folded-over sheet, as in the right-hand panel of Fig.
9. But now the action of the Rössler equations is to create a
four-times folded-over sheet. Again, we can’t join the ends
together. We can repeat this over and over and over. In this
way, what started as a simple 2-dimensional sheet becomes
in fact like a piece of inﬁnitely layered ﬁlo pastry! The
Rössler attractor is like this ﬁlo pastry in the limit where
there are an inﬁnite number of folds, in fact where there is
a Cantor set’s worth of layered ﬁlo pastry.
FIG. 8. Like the Lorenz attractor, the Rössler attractor is also a fractal
geometry. Its relationship to the Cantor set is a little easier to understand
than the Lorenz attractor’s.
FIG. 9. Constructing the Rössler attractor. From Abraham and Shaw (1984).
Lorenz was the ﬁrst to have the insight that the Cantor
set lies at the heart of his attractor. Here are Lorenz’s exact
words from his famous 1963 paper:
“We see that each surface is really a pair of surfaces, so
that, where they appear to merge, there are really four
surfaces. Continuing this process for another circuit, we
see that there are really eight surfaces, etc., and we ﬁnally
conclude that there is an inﬁnite complex of surfaces, each
extremely 
close 
to 
one 
or 
the 
other 
of 
two 
merging
surfaces.”
Lorenz realised that his attractor, as with the Rössler
attractor, 
can 
be 
considered 
a 
Cantor 
set’s 
worth 
of
surfaces on which the state-space trajectories reside. It was
this discovery that ﬁnally made Lorenz realise that the
states of his model would never repeat themselves, that the
trajectories would simply be moving from one part of the
Cantor 
set 
to 
a 
diﬀerent 
part, 
without 
repetition. 
The
fractal 
dimension 
of 
the 
Lorenz 
attractor 
is 
2.06 
with
typical parameter values. That is to say, it is just a little bit
more than a 2-dimensional surface. However, the extra bit
is 
absolutely 
crucial 
to 
understanding 
the 
non-periodic
properties of the Lorenz equations.
It’s 
hard 
to 
overestimate 
the 
signiﬁcance 
of 
this
discovery. Before Lorenz, no one
8
imagined that a set of
three coupled equations based on Newton’s calculus could
generate 
such 
an 
extraordinary 
geometry. 
As
mathematician Ian Stewart wrote in 1997
9
of Lorenz’s 1963
paper:
“When I read those words, I get a prickling at the back
of my neck and my hair stands on end. He knew! Thirty-
four years ago, he knew!… In a mere 12 pages Lorenz
anticipated 
several 
major 
ideas 
of 
nonlinear 
dynamics,
before 
it 
became 
fashionable, 
before 
anyone 
else 
had
realised that new and baﬄing phenomena such as chaos
existed.”
It actually took another forty years before the Lorenz
attractor was shown rigorously to be fractal, i.e., according
to the exacting standards of mathematics
10
.
« »
LET 
US 
RETURN 
TO 
the 
serendipitous 
experiment 
that
Lorenz performed: starting a run of his model with an
approximate value for the starting conditions (
X
= 0.506)
rather than the model’s exact value (
X
= 0.506127). Would
we expect some initial error to grow at the same rate,
regardless of the starting conditions? Fig. 10 shows that
the answer is certainly no. Here is our ﬁrst application of
the geometry of chaos, one that has big consequences, as
discussed in Part II of this book.
In Fig. 10 we imagine that we know the initial conditions
only approximately. Let us suppose that the actual initial
state lies somewhere inside a small ring of points—but we
don’t know where exactly. Fig. 10 shows how this initial
ring evolves on the Lorenz attractor over a ﬁxed period of
time, 
for 
three 
diﬀerent 
positions 
of 
the 
ring 
on 
the
attractor.
With the initial ring in the position on the attractor
shown in the top left of Fig. 10, there is no growth of
uncertainty, even though the ring evolves from one lobe of
the attractor to the other. In this case we can be certain
that the actual future state will make a transition from the
left-hand lobe of the attractor to the right-hand lobe.
With 
the 
initial 
ring 
in 
the 
top 
right 
of 
Fig. 
10,
uncertainty grows with time. We see that the ring evolves
into a distorted banana/boomerang shape. If we wanted to
know whether the actual state will make a transition to the
right-hand 
lobe 
of 
the 
attractor, 
then 
all 
we 
could
reasonably say is that there is roughly a 40 percent chance
of a transition, and hence a 60 percent chance the true
state will stay on the left-hand lobe.
However, when the initial uncertainty ring is in the
position on the attractor shown in the bottom of Fig. 10,
then the uncertainty grows explosively. Here we have no
idea whether the actual state will lie on the left- or right-
hand lobe of the attractor. With a deterministic prediction
system, there’s a good chance we will forecast the outcome
completely incorrectly.
Let’s return to the parable of the missing horseshoe nail,
as discussed in the introduction. Let’s suppose we can
model 
the 
dynamic 
state 
of 
kingdoms 
using 
chaotic
dynamics. (Can we? This I discuss later.) Let’s imagine the
initial 
uncertainty 
refers 
to 
possible 
missing 
horseshoe
nails. Let’s also imagine that for states on the left-hand
lobe of the Lorenz attractor, the king has retained his
kingdom, whilst for states on the right-hand lobe of the
attractor, the king has lost his kingdom.
Then, for the example on the top left of Fig. 10, the king
will 
certainly 
lose 
his 
kingdom. 
Here 
it 
doesn’t 
really
matter whether some horseshoe nails are missing or not,
the kingdom is sure to be lost (perhaps the king is totally
incompetent). By contrast, in the example at the bottom of
Fig. 10, whether the king loses his kingdom or not is
critically dependent on the uncertain state of horseshoe
nails. It is this parlous state to which the parable refers.
Fig. 10 is an important ﬁgure for this book. The fact that
the evolution of uncertainty depends so strongly on the
position of the initial ring of uncertainty on the attractor is
a 
consequence 
of 
the 
nonlinearity 
of 
the 
underlying
equations
11
. It explains why there are occasions—like the
October 1987 storm, and possibly the 2008 ﬁnancial crash
—which are so diﬀicult to predict in a precise manner.
FIG. 10. The initial ring of points represents the uncertainty in our
knowledge of the actual initial state. How the ring evolves depends on
where it is located on the attractor. In the top left, the ring of uncertainty
does not grow at all. In the top right, the ring is distorted into a thin
banana/boomerang shape, indicating that it is not certain whether the time
evolution of the actual state makes a transition from the left-hand lobe to
the right-hand lobe. In the bottom ﬁgure, the position of the time evolution
of the actual state on the attractor is very uncertain.
However, an ensemble system can warn us when the
system is transitioning into a very unstable situation where
dramatic things might, but not necessarily will, happen.
A mathematician might ask whether there is a single
equation which describes how these banana/boomerang
shapes evolve from simple rings, as shown in Fig. 10. There
is indeed. It is called the Liouville equation
12
, named after
the 
nineteenth-century 
French 
mathematician 
Joseph
Liouville. 
It 
turns 
out 
that 
the 
Liouville 
equation 
is
remarkably 
similar 
to 
the 
Schrödinger 
equation, 
the
fundamental equation of quantum mechanics.
Indeed, 
like 
the 
Schrödinger 
equation, 
the 
Liouville
equation is a linear equation. What does this mean? Earlier,
we asserted that it was 100 percent certain that the “true”
state lay inside the initial ring of uncertainty. However,
suppose instead it was only 80 percent certain that the true
state lay inside the initial ring. If this was so, then there
would be only an 80 percent probability that the future true
state 
lay 
inside 
the 
evolved 
banana/boomerang-shaped
ring. We can rescale probabilities in this way because the
Liouville equation is linear.
In early papers on probabilistic weather prediction, it
was suggested
13
that meteorologists should try to solve the
Liouville 
equation 
to 
forecast 
uncertainty 
in 
weather
predictions. 
However, 
it 
turned 
out 
that 
this 
is 
totally
impractical. In fact, even for a simple model based on the
Lorenz equations, it is hard to solve the Liouville equation
directly. 
This 
is 
because 
the 
shape 
of 
the 
probability
contours becomes more and more convoluted and distorted
the longer the prediction time. It is much easier to estimate
probabilities using ensembles.
Let’s look at another feature of the Lorenz attractor. The
long time series at the top of Fig. 11 shows the evolution of
the 
X
variable in the Lorenz model over a long period of
time. The 
X
variable moves irregularly between positive
and negative values (corresponding to irregular chaotic
oscillations between the left- and right-hand lobes of the
Lorenz 
attractor). 
These 
irregular 
oscillations 
are
completely unpredictable for most of the timescales shown.
However, irrespective of the starting condition, overall the
value of the 
X
variable is positive as often as it is negative.
This is because of a symmetry in the geometry of the
Lorenz 
attractor: 
the 
left- 
and 
right-hand 
lobes 
of 
the
attractor (c.f. Fig. 6) are of exactly the same shape and
size.
FIG. 11. Top: time series of the
X
variable in Lorenz’s original model.
Bottom: time series of the
X
variable from the forced Lorenz model, where
an extra constant term is added to the equations. The likelihood of being in
one of the lobes of the attractor is itself predictable, even if the precise
ﬂuctuations in either time series are not.
The long time series at the bottom of Fig. 11 shows the
evolution of the 
X
variable in a modiﬁed version of Lorenz’s
equations. Here I have added a constant term to the right-
hand side of the equations
14
. You can think of this extra
constant term as representing an external perturbation to
the Lorenz system. It is as close as one can get, in a system
as simple as the Lorenz system, to a forcing, such as a
doubling 
of 
the 
carbon 
dioxide 
concentration 
in 
the
atmosphere.
The irregular oscillations in the lower time series are
still unpredictable and depend sensitively on the starting
conditions, as before. However, in the lower time series, it
is now more likely on average that the 
X
variable has a
positive value than a negative value. The external forcing
has 
changed 
the 
symmetry 
of 
the 
attractor 
geometry,
making one lobe of the attractor bigger and the other lobe
smaller. Let’s think of the lobes of the Lorenz attractor as
corresponding to opposite types of weather, such as warm
weather or cold weather, or wet weather or dry weather.
The 
added 
forcing 
to 
the 
equations 
changes 
in 
a
predictable way the likelihood of having warm or cold
weather, or wet or dry weather, even though the detailed
way in which the weather evolves between these states is
unpredictable. This is an important conceptual point when
thinking about climate change.
« »
THERE ARE SOME REMARKABLE and profound connections
between the geometry of fractal attractors—the geometry
of chaos—and some of the deepest mathematical theorems
of 
twentieth- 
and 
twenty-ﬁrst-century 
mathematics.
Because of this, Lorenz’s geometry really does connect
Newtonian mathematics with some of the most modern and
exciting mathematical developments of recent years. When
I learned about these connections, they convinced me of
the fundamental importance of fractal geometry, not only
for mathematics but also for physics. I leave this latter
point for Part III of the book but discuss some of the
mathematical connections here. You can skip to the next
chapter if this is not to your taste.
Let’s go back to the line, the simplest type of Euclidean
geometry. As mentioned, if you draw a line on a sheet of
paper, 
you 
can 
think 
of 
each 
point 
of 
the 
line 
as
representing a number, such as 1, √2 or π. There will also
be points on the line representing numbers like 1 + √2, and
√2 + π, or even √2 × π. Indeed, take any two points on the
line and convert them to their corresponding real numbers.
Now 
either 
add 
or 
multiply 
these 
two 
real 
numbers
together to get a third real number. Finally, convert this
third number back to a point on the line. In this way, we
can add or multiply two points on the line and get a third
point on the line. We can do arithmetic on the real line
using real numbers. This reﬂects the fact that there is a
deep 
connection 
between 
real 
numbers 
and 
Euclidean
geometry.
Can we do arithmetic on the points of the Cantor set
using real numbers? No! If we take two points on the
Cantor set and convert them into real numbers, and then
either add or multiply the numbers together, the third
number typically won’t correspond to a third point on the
Cantor set. However, the reason is not because we can’t do
arithmetic on the Cantor set. Instead, it’s because the real
numbers are not good numbers to use to describe points on
the Cantor set. As we have discussed, fractal geometry is
fundamentally diﬀerent to Euclidean geometry. We should
therefore not be surprised that we need a diﬀerent type of
number system to do arithmetic on fractals.
It turns out that there is a number system—called the 
p
-
adic 
numbers—which 
allows 
you 
to 
do 
arithmetic 
on
fractals. Here 
p
itself stands for a whole number
15
. Now, it
is probably diﬀicult to imagine that there exist numbers
that are not described by real numbers. To get a glimpse at
the diﬀerence between real numbers and 
p
-adic numbers, a
real number such as √2 can be written in decimal format as
1.41421356237… 
whilst 
a 
possible 
10-adic 
number 
is
written instead as… 739620285.643, where the dots go oﬀ
to inﬁnity towards the left (corresponding to smaller and
smaller pieces of the associated fractal). It’s best to think of
a 
p
-adic number simply as a new type of number, better
suited to Cantor sets than lines. The rules for adding and
multiplying 
p
-adic numbers are very similar to those for
adding and multiplying real numbers. For the Cantor set
shown in Fig. 7, 
p
= 2. In this way, each point on a Cantor
set can be represented by a 2-adic number
16
. If you add or
multiply two such 2-adic numbers, the sum or the product
is a 2-adic number and corresponds to a third point on the
Cantor set.
We have to generalise the notion of a Cantor set in order
for it to be a geometric representation of 3-adic, or 5-adic,
or more generally 
p
-adic numbers. For example, Fig. 12
shows a generalised Cantor set where we start with a 2-
dimensional disk and then in the ﬁrst iteration shrink it
down to create 5 smaller disks inside the original disk. We
repeat this iteration over and over and the generalised
Cantor 
set 
comprises 
the 
points 
common 
to 
all 
the
iterations. 
This 
generalised 
Cantor 
set 
can 
then 
be
described 
mathematically 
by 
the 
5-adic 
numbers
17
. 
As
before, if we take two points on this fractal set, convert
them into 5-adic numbers, and add or multiply the numbers
together, 
the 
resulting 
5-adic 
number 
corresponds 
to
another point on the generalised Cantor set. It’s easy to
generalise this further to imagine what a 
p
-adic fractal
looks like for an arbitrary 
p
.
FIG. 12. The ﬁgure describes a fractal based on an
iteration which takes a 2-dimensional disk and maps it
to 5 smaller disks. It generalises the Cantor set with
two iterated line segments. Just as the real line is a
geometric representation of real numbers, so this
fractal disk is a geometric representation of 5-adic
numbers. Such numbers play a vital role in modern
number theory.
The 
p
-adic 
numbers 
are 
bread 
and 
butter 
for 
pure
mathematicians, 
and 
number 
theorists 
in 
particular. 
A
number 
theorist 
is 
someone 
primarily 
interested 
in
understanding the properties of the whole numbers {1, 2,
3… }. Whole numbers are in some sense the “quanta” of all
numbers. 
The 
greatest 
mathematician 
of 
all 
time, 
the
nineteenth-century 
German 
Carl 
Friedrich 
Gauss,
described 
mathematics 
as 
the 
queen 
of 
sciences 
and
number theory as the queen of mathematics. His view
would have been that number theory lies at the heart of
everything quantitative.
The importance of 
p
-adic numbers was clear to Andrew
Wiles, who in 1995 famously proved Fermat’s last theorem.
Pierre de Fermat was a brilliant French mathematician of
the seventeenth century. His so-called last theorem was in
fact a conjectured property
18
of the whole numbers that
Fermat was himself never able to prove. This conjecture
captivated 
mathematicians 
over 
the 
centuries, 
and 
had
become, until Wiles, one of the most famous unsolved
problems in mathematics.
What is amazing about Wiles’s proof is the variety of
mathematical 
ideas 
and 
techniques 
needed 
to 
crack
Fermat’s 
last 
theorem. 
However, 
one 
of 
the 
really
important 
techniques 
Wiles 
used 
revolves 
around 
the
theory of 
p
-adic numbers.
Moving into the twenty-ﬁrst century, one of the greatest
contemporary 
number 
theorists 
is 
the 
German
mathematician Peter Scholze. In 2018 Scholze won the
Fields Medal, the most prestigious mathematical prize for
mathematicians under the age of forty. The oﬀicial citation
was for “transforming arithmetic algebraic geometry over
p
-adic ﬁelds through his introduction of perfectoid spaces”.
These perfectoids are examples of fractal geometry. Given
what 
I 
have 
written, 
it 
is 
perhaps 
not 
surprising 
that
Scholze uses 
p
-adic numbers to describe his perfectoid
spaces. In an interview, Scholze said
19
:
“
Now
I ﬁnd real numbers much, much more confusing
than 
p
-adic numbers. I’ve gotten so used to them that now
real numbers feel very strange.”
Perhaps one might imagine that there are many diﬀerent
types of geometry in addition to locally Euclidean and
fractal 
geometry. 
However, 
an 
important 
theorem 
in
number 
theory, 
Ostrowski’s 
theorem, 
says 
not. 
If 
your
geometry has an associated distance function, called a
metric, then, according to Ostrowski’s theorem, the metric
is essentially locally Euclidean or fractal, represented by
the real numbers or the 
p
-adic numbers, respectively.
I 
believe 
number 
theory, 
and 
p
-adic 
numbers 
in
particular, will play a central role in physics in years to
come, reﬂecting the importance of the geometry of chaos.
A 
mathematical 
theorem 
of 
the 
twentieth 
century
rivalling Wiles’s proof of Fermat’s last theorem in terms of
fame is Gödel’s incompleteness theorem. Here again there
is a link to fractal geometry.
The logician, mathematician and philosopher Kurt Gödel
was born in part of the Austro-Hungarian Empire (now in
the Czech Republic) in 1906 and did his famous work while
in Vienna, before immigrating to the United States and
becoming a colleague and friend of Einstein. The key result
at the heart of Gödel’s theorem is a formula which can be
constructed from the rules of any suﬀiciently powerful
logical system. However, it is a strange, self-referential
formula. It states that if this formula could be proved, the
formula would be false
20
. We must assume that consistent
application of the rules of any logical system will yield only
true 
results. 
The 
only 
way 
out 
of 
the 
dilemma 
is 
to
acknowledge that Gödel’s formula, whilst true, cannot be
proved.
In the 1930s, the British mathematician Alan Turing,
who subsequently went on to lead the code-breaking work
at Bletchley Park during World War II, realised that Gödel’s
theorem 
implies 
that 
certain 
mathematical 
problems
cannot be solved by algorithm: they are “uncomputable”.
As 
an 
example, 
Turing 
demonstrated 
that 
a 
universal
computer—a 
concept 
that 
Turing 
deﬁned—would, 
in
principle, be unable to solve a mathematical problem posed
by the German mathematician David Hilbert some years
earlier. Hilbert’s “halting problem” was to construct an
algorithm 
which, 
on 
inputting 
some 
mathematical
proposition, would halt if the proposition was true. When
he posed the halting problem, Hilbert believed that there
was 
no 
such 
thing 
as 
an 
unsolvable 
problem. 
Turing
showed that no algorithm can solve the halting problem.
Roger 
Penrose 
has 
speculated 
that 
Gödel/Turing
undecidability 
will 
underpin 
the 
theory 
of 
quantum
gravity
21
, the as-yet undiscovered theory that synthesises
quantum and gravitational physics. Penrose himself won a
Nobel 
Prize 
for 
his 
pioneering 
work 
on 
space-time
singularities 
in 
Einstein’s 
geometric 
theory 
of 
gravity,
general relativity. Indeed, by studying properties of the
famous fractal Mandelbrot set, Penrose speculated on the
link 
between 
undecidability 
and 
fractal 
geometry, 
and
hence between fractals and quantum gravity
22
.
In 1993, the computer scientist Simant Dube proved the
link between undecidability and fractal geometry
23
. He
showed this by constructing Turing’s universal computer
using fractal geometry. In this way, undecidable problems
such as Hilbert’s halting problem can be expressed as
problems 
in 
fractal 
geometry. 
Dube 
did 
this 
using
dynamical 
systems 
called 
iterated 
function 
systems
24
,
which are even simpler than the Lorenz equations
25
. An
iterated function system can produce fractal ﬁgures like
the 
Sierpinski 
triangle, 
shown 
in 
Fig. 
13. 
This 
fractal
triangle is to the iterated function system what the Lorenz
attractor is to Lorenz’s three equations. That is to say, no
matter what point in the plane one starts from, the iterated
function system ends up operating only on the points of the
Sierpinski triangle. The triangle is a fractal attractor of the
iterated function system.
Dube showed that a problem similar to Hilbert’s halting
problem was equivalent to asking whether a given line
intersects 
the 
fractal 
attractor 
of 
the 
iterated 
function
system. If it could be determined by algorithm whether a
given line intersects a fractal, then a point of intersection,
or 
lack 
of 
it, 
could 
be 
used 
to 
determine 
if 
the
corresponding Turing machine would halt (at the state
corresponding to the point of intersection). We know that
we 
can’t 
decide 
by 
algorithm 
whether 
such 
a 
Turing
machine 
will 
halt, 
ergo 
we 
can’t 
decide 
by 
algorithm
whether the line will intersect the fractal.
The 
idea 
that 
fractal 
geometry 
provides 
a 
way 
to
understand 
the 
mysteries 
of 
quantum 
physics 
is 
a
discussion point in Part III of this book.
FIG. 13. The Sierpinski triangle, the fractal attractor of
an iterated function system.
...
3
Noisy, Million-Dollar Butterﬂies
W
ATCHING WATER FLOW DOWN A WATERFALL CAN BE
MESMERISING. The ﬂow at the very top is quite smooth
—“laminar”, as it is sometimes called. But as it starts its
journey down, the water breaks into a myriad of pieces. We
might try to follow the path of one small parcel of ﬂuid, but
it breaks into yet smaller pieces which soon become too
small to follow individually. Is the science of chaos for such
complex systems fundamentally similar or fundamentally
diﬀerent to what Lorenz had found with his three-variable
model? We will ﬁnd that the growth of uncertainty in such
complex systems is like chaos on steroids, so much that our
deterministic mathematical models seem to fail us. This has
important consequences whose implications extend well
beyond waterfalls.
« »
THE UBIQUITOUS PHENOMENON OF turbulence has both
fascinated and perplexed scientists. Werner Heisenberg,
one 
of 
the 
founding 
fathers 
of 
quantum 
mechanics,
famously remarked: “When I meet God, I am going to ask
him two questions. Why [Einstein’s theory of] relativity?
And 
why 
turbulence? 
I 
really 
believe 
he 
will 
have 
an
answer for the ﬁrst!”
As 
we 
will 
see, 
turbulence 
continues 
to 
fox
mathematicians today. One of the most important unsolved
mathematical problems relates to the question of just how
predictable 
turbulent 
ﬂow 
really 
is. 
How 
far 
down 
a
waterfall could Laplace’s daemon predict the motion of an
individual small piece of ﬂuid? All the way down? A tenth of
the way down? Big prizes (and considerable kudos) await
the person who can advance our knowledge of such a
problem. As I will discuss in this chapter, the phenomenon
of chaos as described in the last two chapters may not be
unpredictable enough to describe turbulence adequately.
In 
the 
1940s, 
the 
Russian 
mathematician 
Andrey
Kolmogorov 
developed 
a 
mathematical 
model 
of
turbulence. In his model, a turbulent ﬂuid is characterised
by a spectrum of interacting eddies or whirls, some big,
some medium sized, some small. Energy is injected on the
largest scale, e.g., by stirring a tank of water with a big
paddle or, in the case of the atmosphere, by the sun, which
preferentially warms the equatorial regions of the Earth.
The energy then cascades
1
down to smaller and smaller
scales, where eventually it dissipates by increasing the
irregular motion of individual molecules, manifest by a
general warming of the ﬂuid. As we have discussed, this
dissipation eﬀect is characterised by an internal frictional
property 
of 
a 
ﬂuid 
known 
as 
its 
viscosity. 
A 
pithy
description of Kolmogorov’s cascade of energy was given
by the polymath Lewis Fry Richardson (whom we shall
meet several times in this book)
2
:
Big whirls have little whirls
That feed on their velocity,
And little whirls have lesser whirls
And so on to viscosity.
An example of turbulent ﬂuid ﬂow with multiple whirls of
diﬀerent scales is shown in Fig. 14.
The atmosphere itself is a turbulent ﬂuid system. The
very largest-scale whirls in the atmosphere are the jet
streams
3
, rivers of fast-moving air in the high atmosphere.
Aircraft avoid jet streams when ﬂying from east to west,
and try to catch them when ﬂying west to east. The location
of the jet streams is not ﬁxed, but varies from day to day,
month to month and season to season. The jet streams do
not always ﬂow directly from west to east, but can have
large 
meanders 
towards 
higher 
and 
lower 
latitudes
(perhaps reminiscent of the meandering of a river of water
as it wends its way to the sea). These meanders have scales
of 10,000 km (6,213 miles) or so. They can cause long
spells of unusual weather. In February 2021, a meander in
the jet stream caused Texas to experience record-breaking
cold weather for around ten days. In July 2021, another
meander caused parts of British Columbia to experience
record-breaking hot temperatures, reaching almost 50°C
(122°F).
FIG. 14. A computer simulation of turbulence, characterised by a spectrum
of eddies or whirls, some big, some small. We shall be referring to this
simulation again.
The jet streams, in turn, determine the evolution of low-
pressure weather systems—or midlatitude cyclones, as they
are often called—that routinely bring rain and strong winds
to 
those 
of 
us 
who 
live 
in 
midlatitude 
regions. 
These
midlatitude cyclones have scales around 1,000 km (620
miles), 
and 
so 
are 
smaller 
in 
spatial 
scale 
than 
the
meanders of the jet streams. Inside a cyclone, regions of
warm and cold air are separated by features known as
fronts, close to which many of the rain-bearing clouds are
clustered. 
Big 
thunderstorm 
clouds 
can 
have 
scales
between 
10 
and 
100 
km 
(6.2 
and 
62 
miles). 
Inside
individual clouds, the air can be particularly turbulent,
which is why ﬂying through clouds can be an unpleasant
experience. In extremis, such turbulent eddies can cause a
plane to crash. And embedded in one of these sub-cloud
eddies 
are 
even 
smaller-scale 
whirls 
that 
may 
not 
be
energetic enough to buﬀet an aircraft but can be detected
by small balloons. And so on to viscosity.
FIG. 15. The Navier-Stokes equation as a work of art.
Fluid motion is described mathematically by an equation
discovered independently in the nineteenth century by the
French 
mathematician 
Claude 
Navier 
and 
the 
Irish
mathematician George Stokes. Based on Newton’s laws of
motion, the nonlinear Navier-Stokes equation
4
describes,
with twenty-three symbols, the motion of every whirl in the
atmosphere, from the smallest to the largest (see Fig. 15).
Being based on Newton’s laws, the Navier-Stokes equation
is deterministic; it contains no indeterminate or random
terms.
This 
equation 
appears 
to 
generate 
a 
type 
of
unpredictability 
and 
uncertainty 
which 
is 
much 
more
radical than the type of chaos we have met so far: not only
can Laplace’s daemon not predict arbitrarily far into the
future, it cannot predict beyond a ﬁxed ﬁnite time horizon.
This remarkable result was set out in a paper published by
Ed Lorenz (yes, him again!) in 1969
5
, six years after his
famous paper on the geometry of chaos was published.
Here is part of the abstract of Lorenz’s 1969 paper:
“It is proposed that certain formally deterministic ﬂuid
systems 
which 
possess 
many 
scales 
of 
motion 
are
observationally 
indistinguishable 
from 
indeterministic
systems; speciﬁcally, that two states of the system diﬀering
initially by a small ‘observational error’ will evolve into two
states diﬀering as greatly as randomly chosen states of the
system 
within 
a 
ﬁnite 
time 
interval, 
which 
cannot 
be
lengthened by reducing the amplitude of the initial error
.”
The last clause (my italics) is the killer. In the type of
chaos described in the last chapter, the more we can
reduce the uncertainty in the initial state, the farther ahead
we can predict. Put another way, you tell me how far ahead
you want to predict and I will tell you how accurately you
need to know the initial conditions. However, according to
Lorenz’s 1969 paper, in a complex system that contains
many scales of motion which interact with each other, you
will not be able to predict beyond some ﬁxed predictability
horizon no matter how well you know the initial conditions.
In this picture, the limit where the uncertainty in the initial
conditions is strictly zero is what’s called a singular limit.
According 
to 
Lorenz, 
even 
if 
the 
uncertainty 
was
inﬁnitesimally diﬀerent to zero, we would not be able to
predict 
beyond 
a 
ﬁnite 
horizon 
in 
time. 
We 
will 
meet
singular limits again when we discuss quantum physics.
Lorenz gave a popular account of his 1969 paper at a
meeting 
in 
1971 
of 
the 
American 
Academy 
for 
the
Advancement 
of 
Science. 
This 
1971 
talk 
has 
become
famous because of the title: “Does the Flap of a Butterﬂy’s
Wings in Brazil Lead to a Tornado in Texas?”; it is the origin
of the phrase “the butterﬂy eﬀect”. What I am going to
describe 
is 
the 
original 
meaning 
of 
the 
phrase 
“the
butterﬂy eﬀect”
6
. It is more radical than the meaning that
is usually attributed to it today, described in Chapter 1.
To solve the Navier-Stokes equation, we will need a
computer, indeed a very powerful computer. To make use of
the computer, we must ﬁrst transform the Navier-Stokes
equation into a form that the computer can understand. To
do this we create a ﬁnite mathematical representation of
the atmosphere by partitioning it into a number of small
but ﬁnite volumes which are called “gridboxes” (see Fig.
16). In this mathematical representation, the atmosphere is
assumed 
to 
be 
homogeneous 
and 
hence 
unvarying 
on
scales smaller than the gridbox scale. That is to say, in this
ﬁnite mathematical representation, all the turbulent scales
of motion that are smaller than the gridbox scale are
assumed to be unimportant and are therefore removed
from the computer’s representation of the Navier-Stokes
equation. Hence, the representation of the Navier-Stokes
equation 
that 
the 
computer 
sees 
is 
not 
the 
original
equation, 
but 
the 
mathematical 
model 
based 
on 
this
simpliﬁed atmosphere.
The smaller the size of the gridbox, the more accurate
the 
computer’s 
representation 
of 
the 
Navier-Stokes
equation. 
However, 
the 
smaller 
the 
gridbox, 
the 
more
powerful the computer we will need to solve the equation.
Let’s suppose that the gridboxes in our model have a
horizontal dimension of 100 km
7
, which might describe a
weather forecast model from a few decades ago.
To estimate the future weather, we will need a set of
initial conditions corresponding to today’s weather
8
. These
initial conditions—of temperature, pressure, wind velocity,
humidity and so on around the world—are determined by
observations 
of 
today’s 
weather. 
These 
come 
from 
a
combination of remote satellite measurements and in situ
instruments on weather balloons.
We will assume that we have plentiful perfectly accurate
observations of the atmosphere everywhere around the
globe. This doesn’t mean that the initial conditions for our
weather forecast model are perfect and without error. As
mentioned, all of the whirls in the atmosphere on scales
smaller than 100 km are necessarily missing from the
model’s 
initial 
state, 
even 
though 
we 
may 
have
observations of these small-scale whirls. Because of the
ﬁnite 
size 
of 
the 
model 
gridboxes, 
we 
are 
unable 
to
“assimilate”
9
all the sub-100-km information provided by
the observations into the model’s initial state.
Now 
let’s 
suppose 
we 
are 
interested 
in 
predicting
weather systems only on scales of, say, 1,000 km or more.
We might think that, as a result, we don’t have to worry
that scales smaller than 100 km are missing in the model’s
initial conditions. However, we would be wrong to think
this. Because of the nonlinear nature of turbulence, these
initial sub-100-km-scale errors will grow in amplitude and
in spatial scale as the forecast proceeds. They will after a
few days become large, 1,000-km-scale forecast errors.
Eventually, even forecasts of the 10,000-km-scale meanders
in the jet stream will be in error because of the missing
sub-100-km scales.
FIG. 16. A schematic of gridboxes in an atmospheric model.
With 
a 
100-km 
gridbox 
model, 
we 
can, 
on 
average,
forecast 1,000-km weather patterns to about a week ahead.
Not bad, but suppose we really want to predict the day-to-
day evolution of the weather three weeks ahead. A way to
improve the forecast would be to decrease the size of the
gridbox from, say, 100 km to 50 km. This increases the
number of gridboxes in our model, which means we will
need a more powerful computer to make the forecasts. OK,
suppose we have a new computer to do this. With the new
model, errors in the computer model’s initial conditions will
now be conﬁned to scales smaller than 50 km, rather than
100 km as before. This will reduce the initial-condition
error and will allow us to predict the weather for longer
than seven days. But how much longer?
Lorenz in his 1969 paper concluded that such a doubling
of model resolution would not lead to an extra seven days
of skilful forecast time. Using the theory developed by
Kolmogorov, he estimated that a doubling of resolution
would give us only an extra 7 / 2 = 3.5 days of prediction
time. 
The 
fundamental 
reason 
for 
this 
is 
that 
in
Kolmogorov’s theory, errors grow faster on smaller scales.
For example, a small error in the structure of a 1,000-km-
scale weather system will get twice as big after a day or
two of forecast time, whilst a small error in the structure of
a 1-km-scale cloud will get twice as big after only an hour
or two.
Continuing in this way, Lorenz argued that each doubling
of the resolution of the model will increase our prediction
time by only half of what the previous doubling achieved.
So, if we decrease the size of a gridbox in our model in a
series of steps from 100 km to 50 km, to 25 km to 12.5 km,
our prediction time will increase from 7 days to 7(1 + ½) =
10.5 days, to 7(1 + ½ + ¼) = 12.25 days, to 7(1 + ½ + ¼ +
⅛) = 13.125 days. This is clearly a law of diminishing
returns, since each doubling of model resolution provides a
smaller and smaller gain in prediction time
10
.
Can you see a problem here? If we keep increasing the
resolution of the model again and again and again until the
gridboxes become inﬁnitesimally small—in which case, with
our perfect observations, the initial error will also become
inﬁnitesimally small—we are not going to be able to predict
forever, as we might think we should be able, given the
determinism of the Navier-Stokes equation. Instead, we will
be able on average to predict for only a maximum of 7(1 +
½ + ¼ + ⅛ + 1⁄16… ) days ahead, where there are now an
inﬁnite number of fractions inside the brackets, each a half
of the one before. In this limit, the predictability timescale
sums to 7 × 2 = 14 days, because 1 + ½ + ¼ + ⅛ + 1⁄16…
= 2. That is to say, even with perfect observations of the
weather, we can predict ahead for only 14 days. In this
picture, the idea of predicting ahead 3 weeks would be an
in-principle impossibility, even for an omniscient being like
Laplace’s daemon! This is the real meaning behind the
butterﬂy eﬀect.
But is the butterﬂy eﬀect literally true? Well, the Navier-
Stokes equation does not take account of quantum physics,
so going to the limit of inﬁnitesimal-scale errors is artiﬁcial.
A better question would be this: Does the Navier-Stokes
equation exhibit a ﬁnite predictability horizon? The truth is,
we don’t know for sure. In his 1969 paper, Lorenz was fully
aware that he hadn’t rigorously 
proved
that the initial-value
problem 
for 
the 
Navier-Stokes 
equation 
had 
a 
ﬁnite
predictability horizon; his arguments were plausible but did
not 
come 
up 
to 
the 
rigorous 
standard 
of 
proof 
in
mathematics. That’s why he says in the abstract quoted
earlier “it is 
proposed
that”, not “it is 
proved
that”. Indeed,
over ﬁfty years later, we still don’t know whether the
butterﬂy eﬀect is literally true or not. Understanding this
property of the Navier-Stokes equation remains one of the
most important unsolved problems in mathematics: it is one
of 
the 
Clay 
Mathematics 
Institute’s 
Millennium 
Prize
problems, set at the beginning of the twenty-ﬁrst century
11
.
This 
list 
of 
problems 
is 
a 
modern 
update 
of 
a 
set 
of
mathematical problems posed by German mathematician
David Hilbert at the beginning of the twentieth century
12
.
Solving any of the Millennium Prize problems will win you a
million-dollar prize—hence the title of this chapter. The
only Millennium Prize problem to have been solved at the
time of writing was one posed by Poincaré in the ﬁeld of
topology.
On 
the 
other 
hand, 
evidence 
from 
computer 
models
suggests the butterﬂy eﬀect is true in practice. We don’t
have computers powerful enough for us to reduce the size
of the gridboxes to the scale of butterﬂies. However, if we
reduce the gridbox size to the size of individual clouds, we
do indeed ﬁnd that forecasts of large-scale weather can be
very sensitive to uncertainties in these small-scale cloud
structures. 
Not 
only 
that, 
fourteen 
days 
does 
seem 
to
represent 
well 
the 
limit 
to 
which 
we 
can 
predict 
the
detailed motion of weather systems, on average.
This takes us back to the discussion in Chapter 1 as to
why 
weather 
is 
unpredictable. 
Back 
in 
the 
1950s, 
the
experts would have said it is unpredictable because the
atmosphere is turbulent and contains many whirls and
eddies 
of 
diﬀerent 
scales. 
I 
mentioned 
that 
Lorenz’s
intuition at the time was that the atmosphere would be
unpredictable even if the atmosphere had as few as three
degrees of freedom.
Both views are correct. In 1963, Lorenz showed that a
model 
with 
just 
three 
variables 
could 
be 
chaotically
unpredictable. However, in 1969 Lorenz showed that the
experts were right too—a system with a very large number
of variables (big whirls and small whirls) could be 
much
more 
unpredictable 
than 
a 
system 
with 
a 
very 
small
number of variables. In practice, the latter is actually the
key reason why the atmosphere is unpredictable.
For this reason, we must distinguish between what is
called 
low-order 
chaos 
(or 
low-dimensional 
chaos) 
and
high-order chaos (or high-dimensional chaos). Here the
word “dimension” refers to the dimension of state space,
i.e., the number of variables needed to describe the system
being studied. Recall trouser state space as an example.
The number of variables in a system describable by low-
order 
chaos 
is 
suﬀiciently 
small 
that 
they 
can 
all 
be
represented in a computer. By contrast, the number of
variables in a system describable by high-order chaos is so
large that not all can be represented in a computer. The
Lorenz model described in Chapters 1 and 2 is an example
of low-order chaos. Turbulence, as described by the Navier-
Stokes equation, is an example of high-order chaos.
However, we are now on the horns of a dilemma. On the
one hand, we must limit the number of whirls in order to be
able to solve the Navier-Stokes equation. Essentially, we
must 
reduce 
the 
dimension 
of 
weather 
state 
space 
to
something a powerful supercomputer can manage, a billion
or 
so. 
However, 
in 
so 
doing, 
the 
model 
has 
lost 
all
knowledge 
of 
the 
butterﬂies 
(i.e., 
tiny 
whirls) 
in 
the
atmosphere. As we have seen, these butterﬂies can and
indeed will cause the weather forecast to go completely oﬀ
the rails after some ﬁnite time. A model without these
butterﬂies could produce detailed forecasts that may in
reality be complete nonsense. And if that’s the case, the
forecasts will lead us up the garden path in predicting
things that may never happen, or, in Michael Fish’s case, in
not predicting things that do happen.
We solve this problem by representing the unresolved
ﬂapping butterﬂies by random numbers. That is to say, we
deliberately make our models noisy
13
. A fancy word for
randomness is “stochasticity”. In this way, modern weather
forecast 
models 
(and, 
increasingly, 
climate 
models—see
Chapter 6) are stochastic in nature. If we run an ensemble
of forecasts using our stochastic weather model, even when
each 
ensemble 
member 
has 
absolutely 
identical 
initial
conditions, the individual members of the ensemble will
still spread apart from each other, because each ensemble
member has diﬀerent realisations of the random noise in
the model equations. Put another way, to represent a high-
order chaotic system on a computer, we ﬁrst truncate the
system so it is a low-order chaotic system and then add
noise to represent the scales of motion (the tiny whirls) that
have been excised by the truncation.
The consequences of using noise to model high-order
chaotic systems extend well beyond weather prediction, as
we will see.
« »
IF WE SAY THAT noise is created by random numbers, then
this raises the question: What are random numbers? That’s
a 
diﬀicult 
question 
to 
answer. 
It’s 
easier 
to 
say 
what
randomness isn’t. The sequence of numbers (0, 1, 2, 3, 4,
5… ) is not random because there is an obvious pattern to
the sequence: the next number is one more than the last.
So, certainly, a sequence of random numbers should not
exhibit any pattern like this.
In computer science, we create random numbers using
“pseudorandom-number generators”, or PRNGs for short.
PRNGs are in fact deterministic pieces of computer code
that produce sequences of numbers without any obvious
patterns. Now, this might seem like a contradiction in
terms: 
How 
can 
you 
generate 
randomness 
from
determinism? 
Indeed, 
it 
is 
a 
contradiction, 
and 
the
contradiction has consequences. A computationally cheap
PRNG will spit out numbers that may superﬁcially look
random, 
but 
when 
analysed 
in 
more 
detail 
will 
reveal
patterns. Worst of all, the numbers from a PRNG will
eventually start to repeat themselves, a sure-ﬁre guarantee
that the PRNG isn’t truly random. The cure is to increase
the 
complexity 
of 
the 
computer 
code 
for 
the 
PRNG.
However, 
if 
we 
keep 
increasing 
the 
complexity 
of 
the
PRNG, it will eventually be as computationally expensive to
run as representing the butterﬂies that we truncated away
in the ﬁrst place! We therefore have to make a judgement.
We need a PRNG that is suﬀiciently complex to emulate
randomness well enough, but still computationally cheap
compared with simulating the processes the randomness
represents.
However, 
there 
may 
be 
a 
fascinating 
alternative 
to
PRNGs in future-generation computers: using noise created
directly 
by 
the 
computer 
hardware. 
For 
example, 
as
mentioned in the introduction, if we turn the voltage down
across a transistor in a computer, it will start to become
rather “noisy” in the way it operates. The transistor will
become sensitive to the seemingly random motion of atoms
and 
molecules 
associated 
with 
their 
thermal 
energy.
Indeed, as discussed, if the transistors are small enough,
then the noise will start to become directly associated with
quantum physics (for example, through a process called
quantum tunnelling). In this way, the ultimate source of
such hardware-based noise is quantum noise, believed to
be the purest form of randomness possible in the universe.
In 
this 
way, 
it 
may 
be 
possible 
to 
generate 
almost
perfectly 
random 
noise 
for 
truncating 
our 
high-order
chaotic models, not at some small computational cost as
would 
be 
the 
case 
with 
a 
PRNG, 
but 
at 
negative
computational cost. That is to say, by turning the voltages
down, we can run the computer using less energy than we
did when the computer was operating deterministically. I
hope 
this 
is 
the 
way 
some 
future-generation
supercomputers will work
14
—many of their chips being
low-energy indeterministic processors.
But hang on. Why are we trying to generate noise? We
normally think of noise as a nuisance, something to be
eliminated or at least minimised. In signal detection theory
we often speak about a “signal to noise” ratio, and try hard
to maximise this ratio by boosting the signal or reducing
the noise as much as possible.
However, in chaotic systems, noise can be a positive and
useful 
resource 
and 
not 
one 
we 
should 
be 
trying 
to
eliminate 
or 
minimise. 
This 
is 
perhaps 
a 
paradoxical
message, but one that lies at the heart of the nonlinear
philosophy 
that 
underpins 
this 
book. 
This 
is 
such 
an
important point that I want to give some examples of when
noise can play an important role, not in making a signal
harder to see—the intuitive picture we have of noise—but
in 
making 
it 
easier 
to 
see. 
In 
all 
the 
examples 
next,
nonlinearity plays a crucial role.
Look at the percentage ﬁgures on the left of Fig. 17. The
pixels in the ﬁgures are made from diﬀerent shades of grey
(where solid black would correspond to 100 percent and
plain white would correspond to 0 percent).
Let’s suppose we want to transmit the greyness of one of
the ﬁgures, but we can transmit only one bit of information
per pixel: 0 (white) or 1 (black). For the percentages in the
middle of Fig. 17, the lighter shades of grey are simply
truncated to 0, and the darker shades truncated to 1. We
have clearly lost a lot of information. In fact, we now can’t
see half of the ﬁgures, because they have been truncated to
the same whiteness as that of the page.
However, there is an alternative way to truncate a shade
of grey to 1 bit, and that is to use noise. The results are
almost magical—see the right-hand ﬁgures in Fig. 17. As an
example, consider a pixel element in the “30 percent”
ﬁgure. Instead of deterministically truncating to 0, we ﬁrst
randomly choose a fraction between 0 and 1 (e.g., using a
PRNG) assuming all fractions are equally likely. If the
randomly chosen number is greater than 0.3, then we
truncate 
the 
shading 
of 
the 
pixel 
to 
0 
(white). 
If 
the
randomly chosen number is less than (or equal to) 0.3, then
we truncate the value of the pixel to 1 (black). There is a 70
percent chance that the randomly chosen number will be
greater than 0.3. Hence it is more likely than not that the
pixel will be truncated to white. By contrast, consider a
pixel element in the “70 percent” ﬁgure. We now truncate
to white if the randomly chosen number is greater than 0.7.
It is more likely than not that the pixel will be truncated to
black.
FIG. 17. Stochastic rounding. On the left are percentage ﬁgures shown in
diﬀerent shades of grey. In the middle, the shade of grey is rounded to one
bit (black or white) in a systematic way. On the right, the shade of grey is
also rounded to one bit (black or white) but in a more stochastic way, i.e.,
using random noise. There is clearly more information on the right-hand
side than in the middle.
If 
we 
use 
a 
similar 
procedure, 
performing 
this
“stochastic 
rounding” 
to 
all 
the 
pixels 
and 
choosing
independent random numbers for each pixel, we end up
with something looking like the right-hand side of Fig. 17.
Hey presto, the missing percentages have reappeared! The
noise has ampliﬁed the signal!
Stochastic rounding is beginning to play an important
role in weather modelling
15
. Fig. 18 is a simulation of
turbulence, 
like 
that 
in 
Fig. 14, 
but 
where 
each 
ﬂuid
variable is represented by 16 bits instead of the default
numerical “precision” in scientiﬁc computation, which is 64
bits. There is a good reason to try to use as few bits as
possible when doing a computation. Most of the energy of a
modern 
supercomputer 
is 
used 
transporting 
bits 
of
information inside the computer, e.g., from memory to a
processing unit. If you can condense the information into
packages of 16 bits rather than 64 bits, then you will save
about 
three-quarters 
of 
the 
energy 
otherwise 
used 
for
moving data. The problem is that the 16-bit computation is
deﬁnitely not as accurate as the 64-bit computation. It
looks 
like 
we 
have 
lost 
information 
in 
the 
16-bit
computation. However, when applying stochastic rounding
to the 16-bit representations of variables, as shown in the
bottom panel in Fig. 18, the result is almost identical to the
original 64-bit computation.
I strongly believe that future electronic computers must
develop 
this 
type 
of 
stochastic 
rounding 
capability 
in
hardware
16
. With several billion variables in a weather
model, 
tens 
of 
billions 
of 
bits 
are 
being 
transported
unnecessarily inside the computer when each variable is
represented with 64 bits. This is energetically ineﬀicient
and constrains the speed at which the computer can do
calculations.
FIG. 18. Top: a copy of the simulation of the ﬂuid
turbulence shown in Fig. 14. Here, the individual ﬂuid
variables in the simulation are represented by 64-bit
numbers. This representation is the default in scientiﬁc
computation but uses a lot of computer resources.
Middle: where the number of binary digits representing
the individual ﬂuid variables in the simulation has been
reduced from 64 to 16. The turbulent whirls in the
simulation are degraded in realism compared with the
top ﬁgure. Bottom: the same simulation but where the
stochastic rounding technique has been used when
truncating to 16 bits. Now the simulation is virtually
identical to the top ﬁgure. Adding noise has made the
simulation more accurate. From Paxton et al. (2022).
FIG. 19. Simulated annealing is an algorithm which uses noise to ﬁnd the
highest point of a complicated set of peaks and troughs. Here the algorithm
is shown progressing to the highest peak in nine steps.
These are not the only examples where noise can be
shown to be a valuable resource.
Suppose we want to ﬁnd the highest point of some
complicated curve, such as the one shown in Fig. 19. A
deterministic algorithm for ﬁnding this highest point would
typically start at some arbitrarily chosen initial point on the
curve and then simply move one step to the left or one step
to the right depending on which direction is up. However,
such an algorithm will get stuck at a local peak. Once at
this 
local 
peak, 
both 
directions 
are 
“down”, 
and 
the
algorithm will wrongly think it has reached the highest
point and stop.
There 
is, 
however, 
an 
algorithm 
(called 
“simulated
annealing”) which uses random numbers to ﬁnd the highest
point. Let’s imagine the algorithm has been running for a
while and has reached a point on the curve which is not yet
the highest point. Let’s see what happens in the next step
of the algorithm.
First 
of 
all, 
the 
algorithm 
consults 
a 
PRNG, 
which
randomly 
suggests 
a 
new 
point 
on 
the 
graph 
for 
the
algorithm to consider. The algorithm ﬁrst looks to see if the
suggested point is higher up the curve than the old point. If
it 
is 
higher, 
then 
the 
algorithm 
deﬁnitely 
accepts 
the
PRNG’s suggestion and we move on to the next step of the
algorithm. If, on the other hand, the new point is lower
than the original point, the algorithm doesn’t automatically
reject the PRNG’s suggestion. However, as the algorithm
proceeds, it becomes less and less likely that a point which
is 
substantially 
lower 
than 
the 
current 
point 
will 
be
accepted. That is to say, in the early stages, the algorithm
won’t be too fussy about accepting a substantially lower
point, but later on it becomes more discriminating about
accepting 
such 
a 
degradation. 
This 
latter 
feature 
is
reminiscent of annealing metals, hence the name of the
algorithm—when a metal is hot, in the early phases the
metal 
is 
quite 
pliable; 
however, 
as 
the 
metal 
cools, 
it
becomes more rigid.
Fig. 19 
shows 
the 
simulated 
annealing 
algorithm 
at
work. 
The 
steps 
from 
1 
to 
9 
correspond 
to 
the 
steps
suggested by the PRNG and accepted by the algorithm. The
rejected steps are not shown. The step from 1 to 2 is
accepted because 2 is higher than 1. The step from 2 to 3 is
accepted because, although 3 is lower than 2, we are in the
early phases of the algorithm; as mentioned, there is a fair
probability that lower steps will be accepted early on. The
step from 4 to 5 is accepted because 5 is only very slightly
lower than 4. The likelihood of actually ﬁnding the highest
point of the graph depends on the length of time the
algorithm is run. However, for a given length of time, the
algorithm appears very eﬀicient at estimating the highest
point compared with more deterministic algorithms.
In the ﬁnal example, we add noise (not too weak, not too
strong) to the chaotic Lorenz model. The result, shown in
Fig. 20, is startling. Instead of washing out the structure of
the two lobes of the Lorenz model, the noise exaggerates
these lobes. The system typically spends longer in one lobe
before making an unpredictable transition to the other
lobe. This is a rather profound example of the way in which
nonlinearity 
can 
partially 
stabilise 
a 
nonlinear 
system,
making it more predictable.
In conclusion, for nonlinear systems, noise can be your
friend. It’s not necessarily the nuisance we often think it to
be. Perhaps nature makes use of this fact much closer to
home than in weather models. I will be discussing this in
Part III of the book.
FIG. 20. Top: a time series of the 
X
-variable of the standard Lorenz model.
Bottom: a time series of the 
X
-variable of the Lorenz model where noise has
been added to the equations. The presence of noise makes the “regime”
structure of the model much more pronounced, with the system spending
longer in one of the lobes
17
. The transitions between the positive and
negative 
X
regimes remain completely unpredictable.
...
4
Quantum Uncertainty
Reality Lost?
T
HE 
BUTTERFLY 
EFFECT 
IS 
PART 
OF 
WHAT 
IS 
CALLED
“CLASSICAL” physics. Classical physics describes everything
that predates quantum mechanics, as formulated in the
mid-1920s 
by 
Niels 
Bohr, 
Werner 
Heisenberg, 
Erwin
Schrödinger and others (Fig. 21). Hence, Einstein’s theory
of relativity is considered “classical” even though it was
formulated 
in 
the 
twentieth 
century. 
Because 
Lorenz’s
equations of chaos are ultimately based on Newton’s laws
of motion, these equations are also described as “classical”.
Should we describe the algorithmically undecidable fractal
geometry of chaotic systems as “classical”? I would say not
—the 
discovery 
of 
this 
geometry 
postdates 
quantum
mechanics. But we are jumping the gun.
Uncertainty lies at the heart of quantum physics. Indeed,
it 
is 
elevated 
to 
a 
principle: 
Heisenberg’s 
uncertainty
principle. According to the uncertainty principle, there are
pairs of properties of a quantum particle, say its position
and its momentum, and if you know one of these properties
perfectly, then you know nothing about the other property.
There’s the old (and not very good) joke about the traﬀic
cop pulling Heisenberg over and asking whether he knows
how fast he was going. “No,” replies Heisenberg, “but I
know where I am.” “Well, you were doing exactly one
hundred miles per hour,” says the cop. “Oh great,” says
Heisenberg, “now I’m completely lost!”
FIG. 21. The Schrödinger equation lies at the heart of quantum mechanics.
Like Newton’s laws of motion, it makes use of Newton and Leibniz’s
calculus. Unlike Newton’s laws, it describes a picture of physical reality
which physicists and philosophers argue about to the present day.
According to Heisenberg’s uncertainty principle, if the
precise 
position 
of 
the 
particle 
is 
mathematically
determined, 
its 
precise 
momentum 
is 
mathematically
undetermined. What on earth does this mean? I think it’s
fair 
to 
say 
no 
one 
really 
understands 
what 
it 
means.
However, it has sparked a debate that originated with the
birth of quantum mechanics, and continues to the present
day. Is quantum uncertainty “epistemic” or “ontological” in
nature? We can deﬁne these words as follows. If it is
epistemic, then quantum uncertainty merely describes 
our
uncertainty about an otherwise precisely deﬁned system. If
it is ontological, then quantum uncertainty describes some
inherent property of a quantum system, independent of
whether we are trying to study it or not. Most physicists
believe that quantum uncertainty is ontological for reasons
I will discuss in this chapter. Since what we call reality is
comprised of myriads of elementary particles, this means
that the very concept of reality—which most of us take for
granted—is itself uncertain.
The debate about the nature of quantum uncertainty
goes back a long way. In his 1930 book, 
The Physical
Principles of the Quantum Theory
, Werner Heisenberg tried
to explain his eponymous principle by imagining locating a
particle 
(we’ll 
call 
it 
the 
“object-particle”) 
by 
looking
through a microscope. He argued that the smaller the
wavelength of the light used by the microscope, the better
the position of the object-particle can be determined. In
fact, modern microscopes use electrons with wavelengths
up to 100,000 times smaller than visible light to see small
objects with ultra-high resolution. However, the smaller
this wavelength, the more energetic are the photons (or
electrons) 
and 
hence 
the 
more 
they 
will 
recoil
unpredictably 
with 
the 
object-particle 
being 
measured,
making it increasingly diﬀicult to determine the momentum
of the object-particle.
This seems like a nice explanation of the uncertainty
principle, except it isn’t right, at least according to Niels
Bohr, Heisenberg’s mentor. Bohr argued that variables like
position and momentum are inherently “complementary”—
an experiment to measure one necessarily excludes the
possibility 
of 
measuring 
the 
other. 
Heisenberg’s
explanation suggests that the quantum uncertainty in the
momentum 
of 
the 
particle 
describes 
our

uncertainty,
necessarily arising when we try to measure the quantum
properties of the object-particle by shining light, or other
particles, on it. Bohr complained that quantum uncertainty
is more profound than this, and that it somehow refers to
the particle’s 
own
uncertainty.
Bohr came to this point of view because of so-called
wave-particle duality: the notion that to explain quantum
reality we must sometimes use the language of waves,
whilst other times we must use the language of particles. A
good example is the so-called double-slit experiment, as
shown in Fig. 22. In the nineteenth century, the British
polymath Thomas Young demonstrated the wavelike nature
of light by passing light waves through a screen with two
closely spaced slits. At the other side of the screen, the
waves 
spread 
out 
from 
the 
two 
individual 
slits 
and
interfered with each other. In some regions the intensity of
the light was increased by this interference eﬀect; in other
regions 
the 
intensity 
was 
decreased. 
In 
the 
twentieth
century we were able to pass single photons through the
same double-slit screen. Providing you don’t try to observe
which slit a photon goes through, the individual photons
tend 
to 
be 
detected 
in 
places 
where 
the 
light 
waves
interfered 
positively 
to 
create 
regions 
of 
enhanced
brightness, and tend not to be detected in regions where
the 
waves 
interfered 
negatively 
to 
create 
regions 
of
darkness. On the other hand, if you try to observe which
slit a photon goes through, then the interference pattern
disappears and the photons behave like small classical
particles. In this way, we can describe the quantum double-
slit experiment using both wave and particle descriptions of
light. 
But 
neither 
description 
alone 
is 
suﬀicient. 
Bohr
characterised these wave and particle descriptions as being
complementary to each other. Why quantum physics should
be like this is a mystery
1
.
FIG. 22. Individual photons passing through two slits produce a wavelike
interference pattern on a screen behind the screen—unless we try to
observe which slit a photon goes through. How this happens remains a
deep mystery and is illustrative of wave-particle duality in quantum
physics.
Einstein famously battled with Bohr about this issue. He
believed 
that 
the 
standard 
interpretation 
of 
quantum
mechanics, in which the laws of physics contain some
inherent uncertainties, made no sense. At the famous 1927
Solvay Conference in Brussels, Einstein posed a simple
thought 
experiment 
to 
challenge 
Bohr 
and 
the 
other
proponents of quantum mechanics. It illustrates his two
major objections to quantum mechanics. The ﬁrst is its
inherent indeterminism, made famous by Einstein’s phrase
“Does God play dice?” The second refers to what he called
“spooky 
action 
at 
a 
distance”. 
Einstein’s 
thought
experiment still poses a profound challenge for today’s
advocates of quantum mechanics.
Consider a single photon emitted by a light source (Fig.
23). The particle passes through a small hole in a screen
and 
is 
sent 
to 
a 
point 
on 
a 
hemisphere 
coated 
with
phosphorescent material. As suggested in Fig. 23, it seems
reasonable 
to 
imagine 
that 
the 
photon 
travels 
to 
the
hemisphere in some particular direction, which 
we
don’t
know, but the photon does, until the photon hits the point 
q
on the hemisphere. Then both 
we
and the photon know
where the photon is, and hence which path it took from the
source. Surely that’s the sensible way to imagine what’s
going on.
But 
no! 
According 
to 
quantum 
mechanics, 
before 
it
excites the photoluminescent material and reveals itself,
the photon is described by a mathematical quantity called a
“wavefunction”. The wavefunction is represented by the
Greek 
letter 
ψ 
in 
the 
art 
gallery 
in 
Fig. 
21. 
The
wavefunction 
describes 
mathematically 
the 
wavelike
features 
of 
quantum 
systems. 
It 
spreads 
out 
in 
all
directions 
from 
the 
hole 
in 
the 
screen 
like 
a 
wave
propagating 
out 
from 
a 
source. 
In 
popular 
books, 
the
wavefunction is sometimes called a “probability wave”. But
that’s a dumbed-down description which does not do justice
to 
the 
incomprehensibility 
of 
quantum 
mechanics.
According to quantum mechanics, the wavefunction does
not assert that there is (say) a 40 percent chance of the
photon being here and a 60 percent chance of it being
there. Rather, the wavefunction describes a photon which is
somehow simultaneously here and there, with the degree of
“hereness” and “thereness” being weighted by quantities
that 
resemble
2

probabilities 
(like 
40 
percent 
and 
60
percent) but aren’t. This is usually called a “superposition”.
For example, in the quantum two-slit experiment, we don’t
say that there is a 50-50 chance of a photon going through
one slit or the other; instead the wavefunction appears to
require us to say that the photon went, equally, through
both slits at once. Confused? Join the club. But that’s only
the half of it!
FIG. 23. An example Einstein raised at the Solvay Conference in 1927. A
photon hits a point on a hemisphere coated with phosphorescent material.
According to quantum mechanics, the wavefunction associated with the
particle collapses randomly at 
q
to reveal a particle. At all other points,
such as 
r
, the wavefunction collapses to not reveal a particle. How the
wavefunction achieves this without “spooky action at a distance” is a
mystery.
When 
the 
photon 
hits 
the 
sphere 
and 
causes 
the
photoluminescent material to glow at 
q
, then suddenly the
photon 
exists 
only 
at 
q
, 
and 
nowhere 
else. 
The
superposition 
no 
longer 
applies. 
In 
standard 
quantum
mechanics, this process is described by the “collapse of the
wavefunction” 
and 
is 
associated 
with 
what 
is 
called
“measurement”: the process by which we get to know
about quantum information. During collapse, the photon
suddenly (and mysteriously) goes from being everywhere at
once, describable by a wavefunction, to existing only at 
q
.
Put another way, the wavefunction mysteriously goes from
looking like a wave to looking like a particle. According to
standard quantum mechanics, the position of the point 
q
,
where 
the 
wavefunction 
collapses 
to 
reveal 
a 
deﬁnite
particle, is inherently random.
Maybe we might be prepared to accept such randomness
in the laws of physics. But before so doing, we should be
aware that this does not resolve the conceptual dilemma
associated with this thought experiment. If the place 
q
,
where the wavefunction collapses to reveal the particle, is
random, so too are the other places on the sphere where
the 
wavefunction 
collapses 
to 
reveal 
no 
particle. 
This
means 
that 
all 
these 
random 
processes 
on 
the 
sphere
cannot be operating independently of one another; if the
processes were operating independently, then there is a
chance 
we 
would 
see 
more 
than 
one 
ﬂash 
on 
the
photoluminescent screen. But we don’t. So the random
processes on the hemisphere have to be correlated with
one another. How could this correlation arise in practice? Is
it that, when the wavefunction randomly collapses at 
q
to
reveal a photon, quantum information is somehow sent to
all the other points on the sphere telling the wavefunction
at these other points not to collapse to reveal a photon, i.e.,
to instead collapse to reveal no photon? If so, how quickly
does this apparent information propagate? Well, it would
have to be instantaneous. Otherwise, there would be a
chance that at the precise instant that the wavefunction at
q
collapses to reveal a photon, at some other point 
r
on the
sphere 
the 
wavefunction 
would 
collapse 
to 
produce 
a
second photon before the information from 
q
arrives telling
the wavefunction at 
r
not to collapse in this way. Again, this
can’t happen because we know that the source produced
only a single photon—and indeed we see only a single
photon. And so, even if the hemisphere were light years in
radius, 
the 
apparent 
quantum 
information 
telling 
the
wavefunction at 
r
not to collapse to reveal a photon would
have to have been propagated instantaneously from 
q
.
If quantum uncertainty was epistemic, i.e., it was our
uncertainty, then this process would not be conceptually
problematic. The instant we know that the particle is at 
q
,
then we also know it cannot be anywhere else. If we hear
that we have won the lottery and that the lottery has only
one winner, then we know instantly that no one else has
won the top prize. But if the wavefunction really does
describe the evolving nature of quantum reality at diﬀerent
places 
in 
space-time—and 
that 
is 
the 
standard 
way 
of
thinking—then this instantaneous propagation of quantum
information poses severe conceptual problems. Indeed, if
you think this just sounds completely crazy—then you are
in very good company; this is what Einstein thought too.
Newton’s theory of gravity (which is, on Earth at least, a
very accurate theory of physics) also shows this type of
instantaneous propagation, called “action at a distance”.
Imagine in a distant part of the universe, two neutron stars
colliding 
with 
each 
other. 
How 
long 
does 
it 
take 
the
information about the changing gravitational ﬁeld of the
two stars to arrive here on Earth? According to Newton’s
theory, it takes no time at all; the information propagates
instantaneously. Newton himself realised this couldn’t be
right, but it took Einstein to solve the problem. In general
relativity theory, gravitational information propagates at
the speed of light, in the form of gravitational waves
3
.
Having 
found 
action-at-a-distance 
again 
appearing 
in
quantum mechanics, Einstein concluded that there must be
something 
wrong 
with 
the 
theory, 
just 
as 
there 
was
something 
wrong 
with 
Newton’s 
theory 
of 
gravity.
Einstein’s 
thought 
example 
is 
important 
because 
many
physicists 
think 
that 
quantum 
action-at-a-distance—or
nonlocality, as it is now called—is a feature of having two or
more interacting particles
4
. However, this is not the case. If
we 
can 
understand 
what 
is 
happening 
with 
a 
single
quantum particle, we will likely be able to understand what
happens with more than one.
Here is what Einstein said about quantum mechanics
5
:
“The 
attempt 
to 
conceive 
the 
quantum-theoretical
description as the complete description of the individual
systems 
leads 
to 
unnatural 
theoretical 
interpretations,
which become immediately unnecessary if one accepts the
interpretation that the description refers to 
ensembles
of
systems and not to individual systems” (emphasis mine).
The question of whether it is possible to interpret the
quantum wavefunction as a description of an ensemble of
systems is central to the debate about whether quantum
uncertainty 
is 
epistemic 
or 
ontological. 
According 
to
Einstein, there exists a deeper theory of physics in which
the photon does indeed just go from the source to 
q
. From
the point of view of this deeper theory, the wavefunction
simply 
approximates 
a 
probabilistic 
description 
of 
an
ensemble of possible but hypothetical worlds, in one of
which the photon travels to 
q
, in another it travels to 
r
and
in yet other worlds the photon travels to other points on the
sphere. Until the photon hits the sphere, 
we humans
cannot
be sure which of the ensemble members corresponds to
reality. In Einstein’s picture, the photon itself knows where
it is and to which point on the sphere it is heading. Reality
is something deﬁnite, it corresponds to one of the ensemble
members, and the photon knows which ensemble member
corresponds to reality. In this interpretation, uncertainty in
the particle’s movement is fundamentally no diﬀerent to
uncertainty in how the weather will evolve: we don’t know
how the weather will evolve, even though the weather itself
(or, at least, the set of equations describing the weather)
does. 
According 
to 
Einstein’s 
ensemble 
interpretation,
quantum uncertainty is all ours (epistemic uncertainty), not
the particle’s (ontological uncertainty).
So, there you are—straight from the horse’s mouth—
ensembles are the solution and quantum uncertainty is
epistemic. Now you see why quantum physics ﬁts so easily
into a book focussing on ensemble forecasting for weather
and climate prediction.
If Einstein was right, that quantum uncertainty reﬂects
merely our uncertainty, then as far as the particles are
concerned, 
their 
properties 
and 
behaviour 
must 
be
determined by precise rules and laws. It may be that the
precise forms of these rules are hidden from us, but they
are not hidden from the particles themselves. A theory
which assumes such a set of rules is called a “hidden-
variable theory”.
The ﬁrst attempt at such a model was made by one of
the 
quantum 
pioneers, 
the 
French 
physicist 
Louis 
de
Broglie 
in 
the 
1920s. 
Einstein 
himself 
had 
a 
go 
at
developing a hidden-variable model, but abandoned the
attempt when he found it was inconsistent with his theory
of 
relativity—more 
about 
that 
later. 
The 
physicist 
best
known for developing hidden-variable models of quantum
physics (and in so doing, rediscovering the earlier work of
de Broglie) is the American physicist David Bohm. Bohm
was one of the leading theoretical physicists of the 1950s.
But, having earlier written one of the standard textbooks
on quantum mechanics, he became disenchanted with the
theory. Bohm suﬀered badly at the hands of the McCarthy
witch hunts of the time and was forced out of the country,
eventually settling in England. However, he also suﬀered at
the hands of his physics colleagues, who considered him as
no better than an amateur crank for questioning quantum
mechanics.
As I will describe, physicists typically do not believe
quantum 
physics 
can 
be 
described 
by 
hidden-variable
theories. 
As 
a 
result, 
Einstein 
is 
almost

universally
considered 
to 
have 
been 
misguided 
in 
his 
ensemble
interpretation of quantum mechanics. To understand why,
we need to probe a little more deeply into the world of
quantum physics. To do this, I will introduce a quantum
experiment using what is called a Stern-Gerlach device, or
SG for short, named after German physicists Otto Stern
and Walther Gerlach. I’ll be using SG devices for two
diﬀerent (but ultimately related) purposes ahead.
Again, imagine a source of particles. Instead of spitting
out photons, the source spits out electrons
6
. An electron
has a property usually referred to as “spin”. The spin can
be measured by passing the electron through a magnet
whose north and south poles are aligned in a direction
chosen by the experimenter. When it passes through the
magnet, the electron will be deﬂected either upwards or
downwards relative to this chosen direction. If the electron
is deﬂected up, we say it is “spin up” relative to the chosen
direction. If it is deﬂected down, we say that the electron is
“spin down”. In the following, SGz denotes an SG device
whose magnet is oriented in the 
z
direction (as in Fig. 24),
and SGx denotes an SG device whose magnet is oriented at
right angles in the 
x
direction. We will suppose that the
electrons 
are 
moving 
in 
the 
y

direction—the 
third
orthogonal direction in physical space.
We can link two or more of these SG devices together, as
in Fig. 25. These are called “sequential SG experiments”.
Let’s ﬁrst analyse the double-SG experiment in Fig. 25a.
The ﬁrst SG device in Fig. 25a is an SGz. We take the “spin
up” output from this device and feed it into a second SG
device oriented in the orthogonal 
x
direction—an SGx.
FIG. 24. Top: the Stern-Gerlach device. Here a beam of electrons passes
through a magnet (with north and south poles showing) oriented in a
certain direction, 
z
, and is split into two beams, traditionally referred to as
“spin up” and “spin down”. Bottom: the same Stern-Gerlach device
represented more schematically.
According 
to 
quantum 
mechanics, 
it 
is 
completely
uncertain whether an electron will be deﬂected to the spin-
up or the spin-down output channels of the second SGx
device. All we can say is that there is a 50 percent chance
of an electron being output to the spin-up output channel
and a 50 percent chance of it being output to the spin-down
output channel.
A hidden-variable theory of quantum physics can easily
explain this behaviour. In such a theory, it is predetermined
how each particle will react to being sent through magnets
aligned in diﬀerent directions. This implies a hypothetical
“look-up table” which says, for example, that a particular
particle 
will 
have 
an 
up 
spin 
when 
passed 
through 
a
magnet oriented in the 
z
direction, and a down spin when
passed through a magnet oriented in the 
x
direction. Table
1, which has two rows and twelve columns, is an example
of such a look-up table, where each column corresponds to
a diﬀerent electron. The ﬁrst row (+ for “spin up”, − for
“spin down”) shows the spin of the twelve electrons when
measured in the 
z
direction. The second row shows the spin
of the twelve electrons when measured in the 
x
direction,
as 
implied 
by 
the 
hidden-variable 
theory. 
The 
table
accounts for the results shown in Fig. 25a.
FIG. 25. Two sequential SG experiments performed on an ensemble of
particles: a) SGz—SGx and b) SGz—SGx—SGz. The fraction of particles in
each of the two output channels of the ﬁnal device is shown. The second of
