these is not straightforward to explain: Since the ﬁrst SGz ﬁlters out all
spin-down particles, how come they reemerge from the second SGz?
Although the hidden-variable model is able to describe
the results of SG experiments with two sequential SG
devices, problems appear if we couple three SG devices
together. In Fig. 25b we have added a third SG device,
oriented again in the 
z
direction—an SGz. What would we
expect to see coming out from this second SGz? Well, since
the ﬁrst SGz device has eﬀectively ﬁltered out all the −
particles, 
i.e., 
particles 
which 
are 
spin 
down 
in 
the 
z
direction, 
we 
would 
surely 
expect 
to 
see 
only 
spin-up
particles emerging from the second SGz. But that’s not
what we see. In reality, half the particles coming out of the
second SGz are spin up, and half are spin down. So where
on earth have these spin-down electrons come from, if the
ﬁrst SGz supposedly ﬁltered them all out beforehand?
TABLE 1. The twelve columns refer to twelve diﬀerent electrons, showing
part of a hypothetical “look-up” table giving the spins of these twelve
particles for two diﬀerent possible orientations of the SG magnet. The table
is a consequence of assuming that quantum physics can be explained by a
deterministic hidden-variable theory. For each particle, the ﬁrst row gives
the result of a possible SG measurement in the 
z
direction. The second row
represents the result of a possible SG measurement in the 
x
direction. The
table is consistent with the probabilities shown in Fig. 25a. In particular,
taking all columns which are + in the ﬁrst row, half are + in the second row
and half are −.
Explaining the triple-SG experiment is problematic for a
hidden-variable model, though it’s possible to fudge some
sort of explanation
7
. However, there is another experiment,
which can also be described using SG devices, that is
generally considered to provide the killer blow to hidden-
variable 
theories 
and, 
with 
it, 
Einstein’s 
ensemble
interpretation of quantum physics. This is the so-called Bell
experiment. I am going to describe the Bell experiment
next and return to it in Chapter 11. In this experiment, the
notion of nonlocality rears its ugly head in a way which
seems even more unavoidable than in Einstein’s thought
experiment shown in Fig. 23. By contrast, the triple-SG
experiment shown in Fig. 25b seems to have nothing to do
with nonlocality. Despite this, in Chapter 11, I will try to
explain how both the Bell and triple-SG experiments can be
understood using ensembles in 
exactly the same way
.
The Bell experiment makes use of the phenomenon of
“entanglement”, a word coined by Schrödinger to describe
interacting 
quantum 
systems. 
The 
importance 
of
entanglement 
for 
testing 
hidden-variable 
theories
experimentally was realised in the 1960s by Irish physicist
John Bell
8
, whose day job was as a high-energy physicist at
CERN
9
. Bell showed that under seemingly unexceptionable
assumptions, 
hidden-variable 
theories 
that 
do 
not 
have
action-at-a-distance 
must 
satisfy 
a 
certain 
statistical
inequality that is violated in quantum mechanics. At the
time Bell published his paper, no one had tested whether
the inequality was violated experimentally. However, there
was very little doubt that if quantum mechanics predicted
that the inequality was violated, then it would indeed be
violated in reality.
To understand Bell’s inequality, we construct another
table like Table 1, made of +s and −s. This time, however,
we have three rows instead of two (see Table 2). As with
Table 1, I have used twelve columns by way of illustration,
though in principle we can have as many columns as we
like. There is nothing special about the way I have ﬁlled the
boxes of Table 2 with +s and −s. You can choose your own
entries if you want. In fact, for the time being, just imagine
the table as something without any particular connection to
physics.
Now count the number of columns where there is a + in
the ﬁrst row and a + in the second row, and call this 
A
.
From Table 2, 
A
= 2 (corresponding to the fourth and
eighth columns). Now count the number of columns where
there is a − in the second row and a + in the third row, and
call this 
B
. From Table 2, 
B
= 6. Finally, count the number
of columns where there is a + in the ﬁrst row and a + in
the third row, and call this 
C
. From Table 2, 
C
= 5. In this
example, 
A
+
B
= 8 is clearly bigger than 
C
= 5.
As Bell showed in 1964
10
, 
A
+
B
will always be bigger
than or equal to 
C
, no matter how we ﬁll in the entries to
this table with +s and −s. This result is known as Bell’s
inequality. 
I 
give 
a 
proof 
of 
Bell’s 
inequality 
using
elementary mathematics in the endnotes
11
.
TABLE 2. We ﬁll +s and −s into a table with three rows and as many
columns as we like (here twelve). The statistics of these +s and −s
necessarily satisfy a mathematical inequality, known as Bell’s inequality,
described in the text.
FIG. 26. The famous Bell experiment, using pairs of entangled particles
moving in opposite directions. In this experiment, Alice’s and Bob’s SG
devices can be oriented arbitrarily. However, if they are oriented in the
same direction and Alice measures her particle to be spin up, Bob will
measure his particle to be spin down. In practice, the experiment is
typically performed with photons and polarisers instead of electrons and SG
devices.
Bell 
applied 
his 
eponymous 
inequality 
to 
a 
situation
where a source emits pairs of entangled particles, say
electrons
12
, moving in opposite directions, as shown in Fig.
26. Here the particles are sent to two SG devices which
measure the particles’ spins. Each SG device is oriented by
the experimenter. Traditionally, “Alice” sets the left-hand
SG device and hence measures the spin of the left-moving
particle, and “Bob” sets the right-hand SG device and
measures the spin of the right-moving particle. As before,
we will denote spin up by a + symbol and spin down by a −
symbol. But now we imagine that each SG device can be
oriented in any direction Alice or Bob chooses and not just
the 
z
and 
x
directions.
A crucially important property of such entangled particle
pairs is that if Alice’s and Bob’s SG devices are oriented in
the same direction, and if one device outputs a +, then the
other device, measuring the spin of the other entangled
particle, will output a −, or vice versa. That itself is not a
surprising fact and can easily be explained with hidden
variables
13
. It relates to the fact that the combined spin of
the two particles leaving the source must always be zero.
If 
the 
particles’ 
spins 
are 
determined 
by 
hidden
variables, then we can imagine Alice’s and Bob’s two sets
of particles each having a look-up table. An example of such
a look-up table was in fact shown in Table 2. We can
imagine it’s a look-up table for twelve of Alice’s particles,
where the “1”, “2” and “3” that label the rows of the table
correspond to three possible orientations for Alice’s SG
device. Bob’s look-up table for the twelve particles that are
entangled with Alice’s, and for the same three orientations,
would be the same as Alice’s look-up table except that each
+ in Alice’s look-up table would be a − in Bob’s, and each −
a +. In this way, the mathematical inequality—that 
A
+ 
B
is
greater than or equal to 
C
—becomes an intrinsic property
of this type of hidden-variable theory of quantum physics.
The question that Bell asked himself was this: Can we
test experimentally the hidden-variable model prediction
that 
A
+ 
B
is greater than or equal to 
C
, in Alice’s (or
Bob’s) look-up table? It turns out that there is a way, using
real-world 
entangled 
particle 
pairs. 
For 
example, 
as
mentioned, the quantity 
A
describes the number of columns
where there is a + in the ﬁrst row and a + in the second
row 
of 
Alice’s 
look-up 
table. 
We 
can 
estimate 
A
experimentally by measuring the spins of an ensemble of
entangled particles, where for each pair, Alice measures
her particle in the 1 direction and Bob measures his in the
2 direction. We now use the “zero-spin” property to infer
that if Bob’s particle was measured to be a +, then Alice’s
particle, if it had been measured in the 2 direction, would
necessarily have given a − outcome, and vice versa. In this
way we can estimate the quantity 
A
in Alice’s look-up table
from an experiment on an ensemble of entangled particle
pairs 
using 
measurements 
that 
Alice 
and 
Bob 
actually
make. We can similarly estimate 
B
and 
C
on separate
ensembles of entangled particle pairs.
So which theory is correct in reality: the conceptually
simple 
deterministic 
ensemble-based 
hidden-variable
model, where 
A
+ 
B
is necessarily greater than or equal to
C
, or quantum theory, where the inequality can be violated?
The exact experiment that is performed is a little more
complex than the one suggested and tests a variation of
Bell’s inequality known as the CHSH
14
inequality. However,
the answer is not good for hidden variables, and hence for
Einstein’s 
ensemble 
interpretation 
of 
quantum 
physics.
Bell’s 
inequality 
is 
violated 
in 
practice
15
, 
exactly 
as
predicted by quantum mechanics.
So, what is going wrong with our hidden-variable model
in failing to predict the experimental violation of Bell’s
inequality? There’s something wrong with this assumption
that Alice and Bob’s particles each have their own separate
look-up tables. But what is wrong?
One conclusion is that the concept of the look-up table
doesn’t work because there’s no such thing as deﬁnite
reality in quantum physics. That’s what most physicists
believe (even though it’s really hard to know what this
means 
physically), 
and 
that’s 
the 
reason 
why 
most
physicists believe Einstein was misguided to think that
uncertainty 
in 
quantum 
physics 
can 
be 
explained 
by
ensembles.
It’s important to understand that this is not the same as
saying that quantum physics contains some randomness. As
discussed in Einstein’s thought experiment, one needs also
to posit some kind of action-at-a-distance to ensure that the
randomness is properly correlated over space. It’s the same
with 
Bell’s 
inequality. 
One 
can 
posit 
that 
the 
hidden-
variable model contains some stochastic terms which make
the spin outcomes deﬁned in only a probabilistic sense.
However, such a stochastic hidden-variable model is still
constrained by Bell’s inequality (essentially because the
quantities 
A
, 
B
and 
C
are statistical quantities deﬁned from
the statistics of the look-up tables). Randomness alone does
not explain the experimental violation of Bell’s inequality.
A diﬀerent way to invalidate the existence of separate
look-up tables for Alice’s and Bob’s particles would be to
assume that reality is deﬁnite, but that the spins of Alice’s
electrons are determined not only by the orientation of
Alice’s SG device but also by the orientation of Bob’s SG
device. This would mean giving up on the idea that Alice’s
and Bob’s particles have their own separate look-up tables,
as in Table 2. This is what physicists typically mean by the
word “nonlocality”. But it is a truly horrible idea, in my
view. If Bob made up his mind how to set his SG device only
just as his electron was arriving, his decision would have to
somehow be communicated instantaneously to Alice. It’s
the same old action-at-a-distance idea which we saw earlier
and which Einstein—rightly in my view—hated so much.
On the other hand, nonlocality is a subtle matter. As with
Einstein’s thought experiment, we cannot use nonlocality to
send classical information instantaneously from Alice to
Bob. For example, Bob cannot communicate the result of
some 
horse 
race 
superluminally 
to 
Alice 
using 
these
entangled particles. If he could do this, then the hidden-
variable theory would explicitly violate a basic property of
causality in relativity theory: that causes always precede
eﬀects. If this idea is violated, then Alice and Bob could
make certain money at the bookies.
Because it’s not possible to signal superluminally with
these types of experiments, most physicists take the view
that understanding what is going on in Bell’s experiment is
not something to lose sleep over, e.g., when doing low-
temperature experiments or quantum computing.
But for fundamental physics I believe this is something
we should lose sleep over. Even if quantum nonlocality does
not allow superluminal signalling of classical information,
this superluminal communication of quantum information
goes against the spirit of relativity theory. Perhaps our
failure to understand quantum nonlocality is the primary
reason 
why 
we 
are 
still 
struggling 
to 
unify 
quantum
mechanics 
and 
general 
relativity 
theory 
into 
a 
single
uniﬁed theory of physics—as the Penrose quote at the
beginning of Part III of this book suggests.
So, does the experimental violation of Bell’s inequality
require us to either abandon our intuitive concept of reality
or 
accept 
spooky 
action-at-a-distance? 
If 
pressed 
hard,
most physicists will admit that there is likely something
deeper going on, which we have yet to understand. In
Chapter 11, I will suggest there may be another way to
explain both Bell’s experiment of Fig. 26 and the sequential
SG experiment of Fig. 25b. The explanation is consistent
with Penrose’s speculation that Gödel/Turing undecidability
may play a key role in any future theory of quantum gravity.
In particular, the explanation requires us to exploit the
geometry of chaos. If the explanation is correct—without
clear-cut experimental tests I cannot be certain—then it
will 
be 
possible 
to 
describe 
the 
mysteries 
of 
quantum
physics 
using 
Einstein’s 
ensemble 
interpretation. 
In
particular, God will not play dice, there will be no action at
a 
distance, 
quantum 
uncertainty 
will 
be 
epistemic 
and
reality 
something 
deﬁnite. 
In 
claiming 
this, 
I 
am 
not
proposing a return to pre-quantum classical physics: as
mentioned at the beginning of the chapter, we should not
think of the algorithmically undecidable geometry of chaos
as classical.
However, before developing these ideas, we have more
practical matters to discuss.

PART II
Predicting Our Chaotic World
I will give you a talisman. Whenever you are in doubt,
or when the self becomes too much with you, apply the
following test. Recall the face of the poorest and the
weakest man whom you may have seen, and ask
yourself if the step you contemplate is going to be of
any use to him. Will he gain anything by it? Will it
restore him to a control over his own life and destiny?
ONE OF THE LAST NOTES LEFT BY MAHATMA GANDHI
1
The guidelines, which May described as “the most
important thing I did”, set out three principles for
providing scientiﬁc advice to government: fully
acknowledge uncertainties (sometimes the answer is
“we don’t know”); seek a wide range of views (it is
rare that the scientiﬁc and other communities totally
agree on the evidence); be totally transparent about
the process and outcome of advice. These principles
are simple, but as salient today as when they were ﬁrst
published.
FROM THE ROYAL SOCIETY’S BIOGRAPHICAL MEMOIR OF
LORD ROBERT MAY, PIONEER OF CHAOS THEORY AND UK
GOVERNMENT CHIEF SCIENTIFIC ADVISER
2
We apply the ideas discussed in Part I to develop practical
tools for predicting complex, inherently uncertain systems.
The key idea here is ensemble prediction: running our
models 
multiple 
times 
while 
varying 
uncertain 
initial
conditions and model equations. When the spread of a
reliable ensemble system is small, we can make fairly
precise forecasts with conﬁdence. By contrast, when the
spread is large, we can make forecasts only using the
language of probability. In this way, the geometry of chaos
is 
manifest 
in 
the 
variable 
spread 
of 
the 
ensemble.
Ensemble prediction methods are applied to weather and
climate (where the techniques are reasonably mature and
well validated), and to disease, economics and conﬂict
(where techniques are still in development). Since we are
dealing with extremely complex systems, the best models
used for making ensemble predictions are, consistent with
ideas discussed in Part I, inherently noisy. We show that the
value for a forecast user of a reliable noisy ensemble
prediction system can greatly exceed that of a more precise
but less reliable best-guess deterministic forecast.

5
The Two Roads to Monte Carlo
T
HE 
PREVIOUS 
FOUR 
CHAPTERS 
HAVE 
BEEN 
QUITE
INTENSE, 
developing 
concepts 
which 
underpin 
our
understanding of uncertainty in the world around us. It’s
now 
time 
to 
apply 
this 
understanding 
to 
matters 
of
practical concern—to eﬀorts to predict our chaotic world.
I need to issue a caveat. I am not an expert in some of
the 
topics 
discussed 
in 
this 
part 
of 
the 
book, 
notably
disease, economic and conﬂict prediction. Nevertheless, I
have taken the liberty of expressing my views about what I
see as the state of play in these areas, based on my
experience in the areas of predictive science where I do
have some in-depth technical knowledge. I expect some
experts will be rather exasperated with my meddling in this
way. I can fully understand this feeling as I myself have
been at the butt end of criticism from those expressing
their views to me about the inadequacy of climate science.
In truth, it is good to have multiple views from diﬀerent
ﬁelds about a topic, and in my experience progress is often
made by taking existing ideas from one ﬁeld and applying
them to another.
« »
THE 
EMERGENCE 
OF 
MODERN 
science, 
sparked 
by 
the
scientiﬁc renaissance, seemed to have passed by the dark
art of weather prediction. The rather desperate state of
aﬀairs 
coming 
into 
the 
twentieth 
century 
was 
well
described 
in 
Thomas 
Hardy’s 
novel 
The 
Mayor 
of
Casterbridge
, published in 1886. The novel’s protagonist, a
hay and grain merchant, has fallen on hard times. In an
attempt to regain some of his lost wealth, he decides to
consult a “weather prophet” about the harvest weather.
The self-proclaimed prophet tells him:
“By the sun, moon and stars, by the clouds, the winds,
the trees, and grass, the candle-ﬂame and swallows, the
smell of the herbs; likewise by the cats’ eyes, the ravens,
the 
leeches, 
the 
spiders, 
and 
the 
dungmixen, 
the 
last
fortnight in August will be—rain and tempest.”
Unfortunately for the grain merchant, the forecast turns
out wrong, and he is ruined.
In 
fact, 
just 
a 
few 
years 
before 
Hardy’s 
book 
was
published, the ﬁrst attempts to make weather prediction
more scientiﬁc had begun. This development was spurred
by a tragic event. On October 25, 1859, the steam clipper
the
Royal Charter
was on the last leg of its sixty-day voyage
from Melbourne to Liverpool, having crossed the Paciﬁc
and navigated the infamous Cape Horn. There were four
hundred passengers aboard, many bringing the fortunes
they had made in Australia back home to Britain. As they
passed Anglesey, an exceptionally violent storm developed.
The captain of the 
Royal Charter
could not control her and
she was thrown against the rocks of an Anglesey headland.
Only forty-one passengers survived; none were women or
children. Victorian Britain was traumatised.
As others were wringing their hands, one man thought
he could do something to prevent such a loss happening
again. Robert Fitzroy was a naval oﬀicer who had made a
name for himself captaining the ship, the HMS
Beagle
, that
carried 
Charles 
Darwin 
on 
his 
epic 
voyage 
to 
the
Galápagos, inspiring him to the theory of evolution by
natural selection that would revolutionise biology.
Four years before the 
Royal Charter
disaster, Fitzroy had
been put in charge of the Meteorological Oﬀice. In those
days, the Meteorological Oﬀice did not make forecasts.
Indeed, the word “forecast” did not exist—Fitzroy would
later invent the word. But Fitzroy had a brilliant idea.
Using the electric telegraph invented a few years earlier,
he realised that if all of the coastal stations taking weather
readings could relay their information to a central oﬀice,
then Fitzroy, with his expert meteorological knowledge,
could analyse the information to create a spatial picture of
the current weather patterns, and with that picture make a
prediction of the expected weather for the coming days.
The ﬁrst storm warning was made on February 6, 1861.
It was a success and 
The Times
congratulated Fitzroy for
his 
pioneering 
work. 
For 
the 
next 
few 
years, 
things
generally went well. The number of shipwrecks dropped
and the media and public alike were impressed by Fitzroy’s
forecasts. However, a number of inaccurate predictions
began to be made. As a result, questions were asked about
the expense of Fitzroy’s telegraphic network. And ﬁnally,
some of his scientiﬁc colleagues began complaining that
there was no scientiﬁc basis to the method Fitzroy used to
make 
his 
predictions. 
By 
1865 
Fitzroy’s 
health 
was
beginning 
to 
fail 
him 
and 
he 
ran 
out 
of 
energy 
and
enthusiasm. He sank into one of the black moods he would
suﬀer from time to time. On Sunday, April 30, 1865, just
before he was due to go to church, he pulled out a razor
and cut his throat.
Fitzroy had correctly identiﬁed one key aspect of how to
make 
skilful 
weather 
forecasts: 
the 
need 
for 
plentiful
observations, 
not 
just 
at 
one 
location 
but 
across 
a
geographical domain. In modern parlance, he realised the
importance 
of 
accurate 
“initial 
conditions”. 
However,
knowing the initial conditions was only part of the solution.
« »
AROUND THE SAME TIME, in a part of the British Empire far
from the stormy coasts of Britain, meteorology was taking
another step forward.
As winter turns to spring, the sun melts much of the
snow that has fallen over the Asian land mass, the Tibetan
plateau in particular. When the snows have largely melted,
the land warms and surface temperatures begin to rise. To
the south, the Indian Ocean also feels the changing energy
of the sun, but here the sun’s energy evaporates moisture
from the sea surface and the sea warms less than does the
land
1
. As a result, a strong surface temperature gradient
develops between the Indian Ocean and the land mass to
the north. The gradient is the reverse of what one normally
ﬁnds, 
where 
low 
latitudes 
are 
warmer 
than 
higher
latitudes. In turn, a vast current of moist air starts to ﬂow
north 
over 
the 
Indian 
subcontinent—a 
sea 
breeze 
of
massive 
proportions. 
As 
the 
air 
is 
forced 
up 
over 
the
Western 
Ghats 
mountains 
of 
India, 
the 
air 
cools 
and
releases its moisture through torrents of rain. The summer
monsoon has arrived. Many give thanks as the stultifying
heat and humidity of the early summer are ﬁnally broken.
Under the plentiful rainfall, the crops grow, and the food
sustains the inhabitants of the Indian subcontinent for
another year.
And yet, the chaos in the climate system ensures that the
strength of the monsoon is not the same year in, year out.
Some years the monsoon fails altogether, bringing drought
and misery to the population.
In the 1870s, there were a series of monsoon failures.
Exacerbated by British imperial policy, the drought led to
famine and death. The British authorities looked to their
scientists 
to 
see 
if 
these 
monsoon 
failures 
could 
be
predicted. In 1882, the India Meteorological Department,
established a few years earlier and under the control of its
ﬁrst director, Henry Blanford, began to make the ﬁrst
monsoon forecasts for India. Unlike a weather forecast for
a few days ahead, this was an attempt at a long-range
seasonal prediction for rainfall over all of India averaged
over the summer months of June through September, and
made before the summer monsoon actually broke.
Blanford 
was 
convinced 
that 
the 
key 
to 
successful
prediction of the monsoon lay in estimating the amount of
snowfall 
that 
fell 
over 
the 
Tibetan 
Plateau 
during 
the
preceding winter: the less snow that falls during the winter,
the less there is to melt in the spring, and the more the sun
can warm the plateau. From this, the larger will be the
temperature diﬀerence between the continental land mass
and the more constant ocean temperatures to the south,
and the stronger will be the Asian summer monsoon.
Blanford used observations of the amount of snow on the
Himalayan mountain peaks as an indicator of more general
snow cover over the Tibetan Plateau to the north. Like
Robert Fitzroy’s early predictions back in Great Britain,
Blanford’s 
ﬁrst 
predictions 
were 
reasonably 
successful.
However, after a series of forecast failures, it became
apparent that there must be other things going on.
Things took a big step forward when in 1904 Gilbert
Walker 
became 
the 
third 
director 
of 
the 
India
Meteorological Department.
Walker attempted to improve the long-range forecasting
techniques 
of 
his 
predecessors 
by 
correlating 
monsoon
strength with observations of atmospheric surface pressure
from weather stations around the world. He discovered
that the strength of the Indian monsoon tended to be
correlated 
with 
the 
diﬀerence 
in 
surface-pressure
observations taken at Tahiti and Darwin, Australia, some
months earlier. A barometer eﬀectively measures the mass
of 
air 
in 
a 
column 
above 
the 
instrument. 
Systematic
variations in mass between one location and another are an
indication 
of 
some 
large-scale 
circulation 
in 
the
atmosphere. From this, Walker deduced that the strength
of the Indian monsoon was correlated with, and could be
predicted by, indicators of some large-scale atmospheric
circulation 
over 
the 
tropical 
Paciﬁc 
and 
Indian 
Ocean
basins—a circulation we now call the Walker circulation.
Walker 
referred 
to 
the 
variations 
in 
the 
pressure
diﬀerence between Tahiti and Darwin as the “Southern
Oscillation”—in contrast with another pressure oscillation
he had discovered involving stations in the North Atlantic
region, which he called the “North Atlantic Oscillation”.
But what caused these so-called oscillations? Walker had no
idea.
From Fitzroy to Walker, Victorian meteorologists had
identiﬁed that observations of the weather are vital to
making 
good 
forecasts. 
But 
by 
themselves, 
they 
aren’t
enough.
« »
AROUND 
THE 
TURN 
OF 
the 
twentieth 
century, 
two
meteorologists, 
the 
American 
Cleveland 
Abbe 
and 
the
Norwegian 
Vilhelm 
Bjerknes, 
who 
incidentally 
studied
under Poincaré, independently suggested that we could
make much better forecasts if we put the laws of physics to
work on these observations. Speciﬁcally, they suggested
that we should try to solve the Navier-Stokes equation
(amongst others) as discussed in Chapter 3. They proposed
that weather forecasting should be seen as a scientiﬁc
initial-value 
problem: 
we 
use 
the 
laws 
of 
physics 
to
determine the future state of the atmosphere, given the
initial conditions from today.
The ﬁrst person generally credited with trying to make a
forecast using these ideas was Lewis Fry Richardson
2
.
Richardson was born in 1881 into a Quaker family, and this
instilled in him a deep belief in paciﬁsm that strongly
shaped his life. He graduated from Cambridge in 1903, and
worked at a number of places including, just before the
outbreak of World War I, the Meteorological Oﬀice (now
called the Met Oﬀice). When war broke out, he refused to
ﬁght, resigned from the Met Oﬀice and instead served in
the Friends’ Ambulance Unit near the front. In free time
during this period, he performed his pioneering weather-
forecast calculations.
In practice, Richardson wasn’t making a forecast at all,
because the computations of how the weather would evolve
over a few hours took him many months of hard work.
However, the method he developed to solve the Navier-
Stokes equation is still, broadly speaking, the way it is done
today. Richardson’s method was described in Chapter 3,
and is based on dividing the atmosphere up into a number
of gridboxes, inside which one assumes the atmosphere is
uniform 
and 
unchanging. 
After 
weeks 
of 
tedious
computations, Richardson ﬁnally reached the endpoint of
his 
forecast 
calculation: 
an 
estimate 
of 
the 
change 
in
surface pressure over six hours for one of the gridboxes. It
was a disaster. His estimate was over one hundred times
bigger than occurred in reality.
The error Richardson introduced, which led to such a
disastrously wrong forecast, was in fact a subtle one. The
problem wasn’t the way the forecast calculations were
made. Rather it lay in how the initial conditions were
created from the limited observations available. Relatively
recently, 
Irish 
meteorologist 
Peter 
Lynch 
developed 
an
algorithm 
to 
improve 
the 
way 
in 
which 
the 
available
observations could be used to create initial conditions for
the model gridboxes. After applying this algorithm, Lynch
showed that Richardson’s retrospective forecast became
remarkably accurate after all
3
.
The more serious problem with Richardson’s method
was 
that 
the 
calculations 
were 
proceeding 
much 
more
slowly than the weather was itself advancing. Hence in
practice the method was useless. Richardson dreamt of a
future weather forecast factory where thousands of human
“computers” 
toiled 
away 
doing 
mindless 
arithmetic
calculations at breakneck speed, in an attempt to beat the
weather 
to 
it. 
This 
vision 
was 
remarkably 
prescient 
if
instead of a human computer, we think of an individual
processing 
unit 
inside 
a 
massively 
parallel 
electronic
supercomputer.
It took the end of World War II to realise Richardson’s
dream, 
thanks 
indeed 
to 
the 
development 
of 
the 
ﬁrst
electronic 
digital 
computers. 
The 
mathematician 
and
physicist John von Neumann, based at Princeton University,
put together a team of meteorologists led by Jule Charney.
Since these models are based on laws of physics such as
the Navier-Stokes equation, I will henceforth call them
“physics 
based”, 
to 
distinguish 
them 
from 
statistical-
empirical models (sometimes called data-driven models) of
the type that Fitzroy, Blanford and Walker developed. To
run their physics-based models, Charney’s team used the
ﬁrst fully programmable electronic digital computer, known
as ENIAC, which stood for “electronic numerical integrator
and computer”.
Weather forecasting wasn’t ENIAC’s main use. Although
it 
was 
originally 
conceived 
as 
a 
tool 
to 
help 
in 
the
computation of artillery look-up tables, one of ENIAC’s
main uses was for hydrogen bomb computations
4
. Here von
Neumann and his colleague Stanislaw Ulam were major
users of the computer.
These bomb calculations turned out to be hard because
the hydrogen bomb is in part a classical device, describable
using classical laws of physics, and partly a quantum device
—after all, the basic release of energy is due to the fusion
of nuclei of the hydrogen atom. As we saw in Chapter 4,
quantum 
mechanics 
deals 
in 
abstract 
quantities, 
which
under 
the 
right 
circumstances 
can 
be 
interpreted 
as
probabilities.
Ulam was only too aware how hard it was to make
calculations 
when 
some 
of 
the 
basic 
variables 
were
probabilistic and others not. When recuperating from an
illness, he began playing a game of solitaire, which he lost
many more times than he won. He began to think about
how 
one 
might 
calculate 
from 
ﬁrst 
principles 
the
probability 
of 
winning 
this 
game. 
Like 
his 
H-bomb
calculations, this was a diﬀicult mathematical problem.
However, in the case of solitaire there was an alternative—
you could simply play the game enough times and count the
fraction 
of 
times 
that 
you 
win. 
That 
gives 
you 
the
probability you are looking for.
This was a eureka moment for Ulam. What he realised
was that instead of treating the diﬀusion of neutrons in
terms of probabilities, he could use pseudorandom-number
generators 
(PRNGs) 
to 
create 
an 
ensemble 
of 
possible
deterministic paths for the neutrons, get ENIAC to do the
computations separately for each of these deterministic
paths, and then ﬁnally average the results from all these
computations. This was so much simpler than dealing with
probabilities explicitly. Essentially the idea was to let the
computer 
do 
the 
hard 
work—ENIAC 
wouldn’t 
mind
repeating, over and over, calculations for how the neutrons
might diﬀuse.
This new method was considered so important that Ulam
and von Neumann decided they needed to give it some kind
of 
secret 
name, 
and 
the 
code 
name 
“Monte 
Carlo”
emerged, based on the casino in Monaco where Ulam’s
uncle had gambled.
Here is a simple example of the Monte Carlo method at
work. Suppose you want to estimate the value of p / 4
without using a calculator. The area of a circle is (π / 4) ×
D
2

where 
D

is 
its 
diameter. 
The 
Monte 
Carlo 
way 
to
estimate π would be to take a sheet of paper and draw on it
a square (of area 
D
2
) which just contains the circle (see
Fig. 27). You then randomly scatter a number of small
seeds on the sheet of paper. It is the random scattering of
small 
seeds 
that 
is 
the 
repeated 
operation 
mentioned
earlier. Now count the total number, 
N
1
, of seeds that lie
inside the square. Of these count the number, 
N
2
, that also
lie within the circle. If indeed the seeds are randomly
distributed inside the square, then the ratio 
N
2
/ 
N
1
will get
closer and closer to p / 4 the greater the number of seeds
that are scattered on the sheet of paper.
The circumstances of Ulam’s eureka moment are similar
to those of many other eminent scientists’. The moments
occurred 
when 
they 
were 
relaxing—playing 
a 
game 
of
solitaire in Ulam’s case. Why do eureka moments seem to
occur during moments of relaxation? I will have some
things to say about this later in the book.
The methods of Ulam and von Neumann would have
been known to Chuck Leith, an American physicist and
mathematician who also worked on the hydrogen bomb
after the war. However, in the 1950s, Leith decided to
change direction and work in the newly developing ﬁeld of
climate modelling. With his knowledge of Ulam and von
Neumann’s work, Leith wrote a paper in 1974 suggesting
that the Monte Carlo method could also be applied to
weather forecasting. Leith proposed that instead of running
a weather forecast model once, you run it several times
from 
slightly 
diﬀerent 
initial 
conditions, 
each 
initial
condition 
being 
consistent 
with 
the 
slightly 
imperfect
observations you have at the initial time. Like the Monte
Carlo simulations for the bomb, you then average all the
forecasts together.
FIG. 27. An illustration of the Monte Carlo method. Here we randomly
scatter small seeds on a sheet of paper on which a square and the largest
circle which ﬁts inside the square have been drawn. The ratio of the total
number of seeds lying inside the circle to those lying inside the square
provides an estimate of the (irrational) number π / 4.
Suppose a particular low-pressure system is especially
unpredictable. Then whilst one member of a Monte Carlo
forecast 
might 
predict 
a 
drop 
in 
the 
pressure 
system,
another member might predict an increase in pressure in
the same spot. By averaging the two forecasts together, the
averaged prediction would be for no change in pressure at
all, neither positive nor negative. In this way the averaged
Monte Carlo forecast would ﬁlter out the unpredictable
parts of the weather, leaving only the predictable part: the
part common to all Monte Carlo members. Leith estimated
that in order to get the most beneﬁt from such a Monte
Carlo forecast, you would only need to average together
about ten forecasts.
The 
idea 
that 
averaging 
together 
many 
individually
uncertain forecasts can lead to improved predictions wasn’t
itself 
new. 
In 
1906, 
British 
statistician 
Francis 
Galton
observed a phenomenon now referred to as the “wisdom of
the crowd”. At a country fair, eight hundred people took
part in a contest to guess the weight of an ox. Galton noted
that the average estimate from the eight hundred uncertain
guesses was within 1 percent of the true weight. In another
example, 
in 
the 
1980s, 
ﬁnance 
professor 
Jack 
Treynor
famously asked his ﬁfty-six students to guess the number of
jellybeans in a jar. The actual number was 850. Only one
student did better than the average guess of 871, an error
of 2 percent.
If only von Neumann and Ulam had talked more to
Charney and his team at Princeton, Monte Carlo weather
prediction could have ﬁrst happened on ENIAC. But the
Monte Carlo methods were secret and so they didn’t talk. It
would take another forty years for this synthesis to occur.
Instead, the early weather forecast models attempted to
forecast 
tomorrow’s 
weather 
based 
on 
a 
single
deterministic run of the forecast model. It would take
several decades before the operational weather forecasters
came to rely fully on these forecast models and completely
abandon their own empirical rules of thumb for weather
prediction.
As these computer forecasts using physics-based models
started 
to 
become 
more 
and 
more 
successful,
meteorologists began to wonder how far ahead they could
forecast the day-to-day evolution of weather. Certainly, the
1963 paper of Ed Lorenz, discussed in Chapter 1, had made
it 
clear 
one 
could 
not 
predict 
indeﬁnitely 
far 
ahead.
However, by the end of the 1960s, there was a general
feeling, backed up by some research, that the day-to-day
evolution of weather should be predictable to about two
weeks ahead, consistent with the analysis I described in
Chapter 3. Two weeks became known as the “limit of
deterministic predictability”.
In 1975, the countries of Europe got together to pool
their 
human 
and 
computing 
resources 
and 
create 
the
European 
Centre 
for 
Medium-Range 
Weather 
Forecasts
(ECMWF). The primary purpose of this new organisation,
located in Reading in southern England, was to realise a
forecast system that could predict the detailed evolution of
weather 
up 
to 
the 
two-week 
limit 
of 
deterministic
predictability.
In the early days of ENIAC, when forecasts were made
only one day ahead and for a speciﬁc region of the world—
the United States—it was not necessary to model weather
in the tropics or in the southern hemisphere. Hence the
early 
physics-based 
models 
had 
regional 
domains 
with
lateral boundaries. However, when forecasting two weeks
ahead, the weather anywhere on the globe can aﬀect the
weather anywhere else. It became clear that to realise the
goal 
of 
forecasting 
to 
two 
weeks 
ahead, 
physics-based
models with a global domain had to be developed.
In setting up ECMWF, the European countries rightly
realised that there was no point in each member state
developing its own global model, and so the eﬀort focussed
on developing a single global forecast system which would
beneﬁt all the citizens of Europe equally. From the day it
started producing forecasts, ECMWF became de facto the
most skilful centre in the world at so-called medium-range
weather prediction. This forecast system now beneﬁts all
the citizens of the world, especially those at risk of the
most extreme forms of weather.
« »
IN THE 1960S, WILHELM Bjerknes’s son, Jacob, started to
ponder Gilbert Walker’s ﬁnding that the strength of the
Indian monsoon could be forecast knowing the Southern
Oscillation index (the surface pressure diﬀerence between
Tahiti 
and 
Darwin, 
Australia). 
Bjerknes 
junior 
had
emigrated 
in 
1940 
to 
the 
US, 
where 
he 
founded 
the
Department of Meteorology at the University of California,
Los Angeles. There, he asked himself a question: What
makes the Southern Oscillation vary from year to year? He
began to realise that the answer depended on an oceanic
phenomenon 
known 
for 
many 
centuries 
to 
Peruvian
ﬁshermen.
Under normal circumstances, sea temperatures along
the equatorial Paciﬁc Ocean are relatively warm in the west
and relatively cold in the east. This is a consequence of the
trade 
winds 
that 
blow 
from 
east 
to 
west 
across 
the
equatorial Paciﬁc Ocean. The winds blowing over the ocean
near the South American coast cause cold water to upwell
from the deeper parts of the ocean. However, the ﬁshermen
knew that in some years this normal state of aﬀairs is
completely 
disrupted—again 
the 
chaotic 
variability 
of
climate at work. When these disruptions occur, the trade
winds and the cold upwelling in the eastern Paciﬁc weaken
and the water warms. This is bad news for the ﬁshermen,
since the upwelled waters carry nutrients such as nitrates
and 
phosphates, 
the 
product 
of 
the 
decomposition 
of
sinking organic matter. So when the upwelling ceases, the
source of nutrients is shut oﬀ and the ﬁsh either die or
move elsewhere. When these disruptions occurred, they
tended to be ﬁrst noticed around Christmastime and so
were referred to as El Niño events—“El Niño” meaning
“the Christ Child”
5
.
Jacob Bjerknes realised that these occasional disruptions
to sea temperatures in the equatorial eastern Paciﬁc are
intimately 
related 
to 
variations 
in 
Walker’s 
Southern
Oscillation. 
Bjerknes 
postulated 
a 
dynamical 
process
involving feedbacks between the tropical Paciﬁc Ocean and
the atmosphere above it to explain both the El Niño event
and the Southern Oscillation. Thanks to Bjerknes’s work,
we now inexorably link El Niño to the Southern Oscillation
through 
the 
joint 
acronym 
ENSO 
(El 
Niño/Southern
Oscillation). Indeed, in part due to Bjerknes, we now think
of the atmosphere and oceans as components of a single,
dynamically coupled entity.
In the 1970s, it began to be realised that when an ENSO
event occurs, the eﬀects not only inﬂuence the Indian
monsoon, they can also be broadcast around the globe
through 
what 
are 
called 
atmospheric 
teleconnections
6
.
Indeed, it turns out that through these teleconnections, El
Niño can also inﬂuence Gilbert Walker’s North Atlantic
Oscillation and hence the weather of Europe
7
.
In this way, the interaction between the atmosphere and
the underlying ocean (and to a lesser extent land) provides
a source of predictability for the atmosphere on timescales
much 
longer 
than 
the 
limit 
of 
deterministic 
weather
predictability of two weeks.
Does 
the 
possible 
existence 
of 
such 
long-range
predictability conﬂict with the butterﬂy eﬀect discussed in
Chapter 
3? 
No. 
ENSO 
changes 
the 
statistical
characteristics of weather during a season. For example,
knowing ENSO, we can make a prediction of the likely
number of Atlantic hurricanes in a season, and their typical
intensity and track, even though the intensity and track of
any single Atlantic hurricane may be rather unpredictable
more than a few days ahead.
By the end of the 1970s, a kind of meteorological schism
had 
arisen. 
Complex 
physics-based 
models 
had 
been
developed to forecast the weather within the two-week
limit 
of 
deterministic 
predictability, 
whilst 
simple
statistical-empirical models were used to predict weather
on month-to-seasonal timescales. The forecasts from the
physics-based models were precise and deterministic (“it
will be sunny” or “there will be no hurricane”), whilst the
forecasts from the statistical-empirical models were based
on probabilities and hence uncertain in nature.
The 
two 
groups 
of 
meteorologists 
developing 
these
diﬀerent forecast systems hardly talked to each other—
their methodologies had next to nothing in common.
« »
I 
JOINED 
THE 
UK’S 
Met 
Oﬀice 
in 
the 
1970s 
having
completed 
a 
PhD 
in 
general 
relativity 
theory. 
After
spending a few years working on the nonlinear dynamics of
the 
upper 
region 
of 
the 
atmosphere 
known 
as 
the
stratosphere
8
, in 1982 I was posted to the branch making
long-range weather forecasts. At the time, I wasn’t super
keen about this posting, but in the scientiﬁc civil service
you do not always get to choose. As it turned out, it was
probably 
the 
best 
thing 
that 
happened 
to 
me 
in 
my
scientiﬁc career.
Pioneering work on monthly prediction using physics-
based 
models 
of 
the 
atmosphere 
had 
begun 
to 
be
performed 
by 
Indian 
American 
meteorologist 
Jagadish
Shukla, from NASA, and Japanese American meteorologist
Kiku Miyakoda, from Princeton. In Shukla’s studies, the
observed slowly varying sea-surface temperatures provided
what 
are 
called 
“lower 
boundary 
conditions” 
for 
the
atmospheric 
models, 
and 
these 
boundary 
conditions
generated predictable atmospheric circulations inside the
models on the monthly timescales
9
. These studies made me
realise that physics-based models could play a valuable and
powerful role in long-range weather forecasting, alongside
the 
more 
traditional 
statistical-empirical 
models. 
My
colleagues Chris Folland and David Parker had developed
statistical-empirical models for monthly-averaged weather
prediction over the UK. These statistical models predicted
probabilities 
of 
diﬀerent 
types 
of 
time-averaged
atmospheric circulation patterns, and the forecasts were
sold to utility companies—water, electricity and gas—to
help their planning for the weeks and months ahead.
We began wondering, Could the physics-based models be
used to strengthen the skill of these statistical-empirical
models for long-range predictions over the UK? Maybe—
but 
in 
order 
to 
blend 
the 
statistical-empirical 
model
approach with the physics-based model approach, the latter
would need to be probabilistic somehow.
It was clear that some kind of Monte Carlo method of the
type Leith had been advocating some years earlier was
needed. But instead of averaging the forecasts together as
Leith had suggested, we would need to estimate forecast
probabilities of diﬀerent types of weather patterns from the
members of the ensemble. In this way, in November 1985,
my colleague James Murphy and I implemented the world’s
ﬁrst ensemble weather forecast system based on the latest
generation of physics-based models
10
. It was a very crude
system by present-day standards.
I recall thinking at the time: Why are we making these
ensemble forecasts only for the monthly timescales? Why
don’t we also run these ensemble forecasts for predictions
on shorter timescales, i.e., within the two-week limit of
deterministic predictability? I joined ECMWF in 1986 with
the aim of trying to do something about this. I wanted to
take the ensemble system that James Murphy and I had
recently developed and implement something similar within
the two-week forecast range, the focus of work at ECMWF.
This idea met some resistance both from my research
colleagues and from the weather forecasters who made
forecasts for the media. They saw the two-week limit of
deterministic 
predictability 
as 
a 
natural 
dividing 
line
between 
deterministic 
prediction 
and 
probabilistic
prediction. My colleagues’ view was that when predicting
weather on timescales shorter than two weeks, you just
make a single best-guess forecast from your single best-
guess 
initial 
state 
using 
your 
single 
best-guess 
model.
Hence, these colleagues argued that when new computer
resources 
became 
available—this 
was 
the 
era 
when
computer speed was increasing exponentially with time—
they should be focussed on improving the best-guess model
and best-guess initial state, and not on making multiple
ensemble 
forecasts 
to 
estimate 
forecast 
uncertainty. 
In
addition, the forecasters didn’t like the idea of ensembles
because they felt that by explicitly estimating uncertainty,
these 
predictions 
were 
somehow 
undermining 
their
professionalism. Their job, they felt, was to tell it like it was
—and not how it might or might not be.
I did not agree. Because the atmosphere is nonlinear, its
predictability will vary from day to day. This variation in
predictability can be seen in simple chaotic models—see
Fig. 10. On many days, the atmosphere might indeed be
predictable enough so that deterministic predictions would
be quite accurate for a long time ahead (like the top left of
Fig. 10). However, there will be days when the atmosphere
is suﬀiciently chaotic that the deterministic predictions
could become inaccurate even after a few days of forecast
time (like the bottom of Fig. 10). And yet we were releasing
these 
deterministic 
forecasts 
to 
the 
public 
every 
day,
without 
any 
idea 
whether 
the 
atmosphere 
is 
in 
a
predictable state or in an especially chaotic state. If the
latter, then the best-guess forecasts could be worse than
useless if people used them to make important decisions.
What 
is 
it, 
after 
all, 
that 
distinguishes 
science 
from
pseudoscience? Surely one key feature is an ability to
handle 
uncertainty, 
to 
estimate 
error 
bars. 
Weather
forecasting inside the limit of deterministic predictability
had no reliable means of estimating these error bars.
However, to estimate these error bars reliably, ensemble
systems would need to have many more members than
Leith’s estimate of ten. This method really was going to
take a lot of computer time. My deterministic colleagues
were not happy.
The 
1987 
storm, 
discussed 
in 
the 
introduction, 
was
something of a godsend. We ran our ensemble system
retrospectively on this storm. In the computer simulation,
we initialised the ensemble just over two days before the
storm hit the UK. The ﬁfty ensemble members started with
virtually, but not quite, identical initial conditions—ﬂaps of
butterﬂies’ wings if you like. Just two days later the ﬁfty
ensemble members had diverged more than I would have
believed possible (see Fig. 28). Some ensemble members
showed 
intense 
storms. 
Others 
showed 
rather 
benign
weather. 
The 
atmosphere 
was 
in 
an 
exceptionally
unpredictable and chaotic state during this period and the
diversity of possible solutions was remarkably large. This
was the equivalent in the real world of the explosive growth
of uncertainty shown at the bottom in Fig. 10 for the
Lorenz system.
What can one do with these ﬁfty forecasts? The last
thing 
you 
would 
want 
to 
do 
is 
average 
the 
forecasts
together in the way Leith was suggesting. If you did that,
the storm solutions would just get smoothed away and
there would be no chance of predicting the possibility of
hurricane-force winds.
Instead, one can simply count the number of ensemble
members 
with 
exceptional 
storms 
and 
treat 
this 
as 
a
frequentist-based 
estimate 
of 
the 
probability 
for 
an
exceptional storm. Fig. 29 is a map showing just this. It is a
forecast of the probability of hurricane-force winds. From
Fig. 29 you can see that such winds are predicted with a
probability 
of 
around 
30 
percent 
or 
so 
over 
southern
England. Given that “in Hertford, Hereford and Hampshire,
hurricanes hardly ever happen
11
”, a 30 percent probability
is enormously large compared with one’s prior expectations
of hurricanes over southern England. At the very least,
knowing such a probability, it might be prudent to park
your expensive new car in the garage and not under a big
oak tree.
FIG. 28. An ensemble forecast for the morning of October 16, 1987, from
initial conditions from midday on October 13. Illustrated are ﬁfty “stamp
maps” of forecast surface pressure. The uncertainties in the initial
conditions and in the model equations have led to enormous divergence in
possible forecast states for the sixteenth. Some ensemble members have
severe storms, others benign high-pressure weather.
FIG. 29. A map showing the probability of hurricane-strength winds on
October 16, 1987, based on the ensemble forecast shown in Fig. 28.
If ever there was a slam-dunk example of the need for
ensemble 
forecasts, 
this 
was 
it. 
The 
arguments 
and
discussions 
were 
over. 
The 
ECMWF 
ensemble 
forecast
system
12
went operational late in 1992. At the same time,
colleagues Eugenia Kalnay and Zoltan Toth implemented a
similar scheme at the US National Weather Service. Over
the coming years, all operational weather forecast centres
around the world would implement ensemble prediction
systems of one sort or another.
Of course, an ensemble system like this would be useless
if it predicted hurricanes over England all the time. Fig. 30
illustrates a set of ensemble “stamp maps” for a rather
typical situation (a three-day ensemble forecast at the time
of writing). Compared with Fig. 28, the ﬁfty pressure maps
look fairly similar to one another and suggest a rather high
probability of a cool northerly air stream across parts of
central 
and 
western 
Europe. 
There 
is 
no 
chance 
of 
a
hurricane over England.
Of course, it’s not just midlatitude storms that beneﬁt
from ensembles. Fig. 31 shows weeklong ensemble forecast
tracks for three tropical-cyclone/hurricane forecasts. The
top shows the ﬁfty forecast tracks for Cyclone Sidr, which
hit Bangladesh in 2007. This is an example where the path
of 
the 
storm 
was 
fairly 
predictable. 
The 
Bangladesh
authorities sent out clear warnings of the storm and few
lives were lost. The middle shows the forecast tracks of the
infamous Hurricane Katrina ﬁve days before it hit New
Orleans. 
Here 
the 
path 
of 
the 
storm 
was 
much 
less
predictable. At this range the most likely path was actually
up 
over 
Florida. 
However, 
there 
was 
a 
second, 
weak
maximum in the probability of predicted tracks over the
Gulf of Mexico and on to New Orleans. This path became
more and more likely as landfall time approached. Katrina
is an example of an only partially predictable event. The
bottom shows an ensemble forecast for Hurricane Nadine
in the Atlantic in 2012. In this case, the direction Nadine
would take was completely uncertain.
The 
widely 
familiar 
probability 
forecasts 
on 
weather
apps today give ensemble-based probabilistic predictions
right down to postcode or zip code scales. To do this, some
additional “downscaling” software is applied to the physics-
based model output. This is an area where AI techniques
are becoming more and more important.
But what does a probability forecast mean? It’s worth
pausing to think about this. After all, it’s easy to make a
probability forecast, and it seems you can’t go wrong. Just
say, 
“There 
is 
an 
eighty 
percent 
chance 
of 
it 
raining
tomorrow”. If it rains you say, “Well, I did say it would very
likely rain!” and if it doesn’t rain you say, “Well, I did say it
might not rain!” So, if we are putting a lot of computer
resources into estimating these probabilities, we’d better
be sure we know what a forecast of 80 percent probability
actually means and whether our forecast system captures
this properly.
FIG. 30. An ensemble of surface pressure “stamp maps” over Europe for a
three-day weather forecast starting November 26, 2021. Compared with
Fig. 28, the ensemble forecasts have not diverged much. This is a more
typical situation in weather prediction.
FIG. 31. Ensembles of week-long forecast tropical-cyclone/hurricane
tracks, made using the ECMWF ensemble forecast system.
Top: Sidr—fairly predictable.
Middle: Katrina—fairly unpredictable.
Bottom: Nadine—totally unpredictable!
Suppose my weather app says that the probability of rain
between 6 and 7 p.m. over my town on Tuesday is 80
percent. This 
doesn’t
mean that it will rain over 80 percent
of my town on Tuesday between 6 and 7 p.m. Neither does
it mean that it will rain for 80 percent of the time between
6 and 7 p.m. over my town on Tuesday. And ﬁnally, it
doesn’t mean that only 80 percent of weather forecast
providers think it will rain over my town between 6 and 7
p.m. on Tuesday. So what does it mean?
Let’s suppose that we have access to all the ensemble
weather forecasts that have been made over the last year
(from a particular weather provider, like ECMWF). That’s 2
ensemble 
forecasts 
per 
day 
for 
365 
days, 
making 
730
ensemble forecasts altogether. Now let’s extract from all
these 
ensemble 
forecasts 
the 
ones 
that 
predicted 
rain
between 6 and 7 p.m. over my town with 80 percent
probability. 
That 
is 
to 
say, 
with 
an 
ensemble 
forecast
comprising 50 individual forecast members, we are looking
for ensembles where 40 out of the 50 ensemble members
(i.e., 80 percent of them) predicted rain over my town
between 6 and 7 p.m.
Let’s suppose there were 35 days over the last year
when the forecast probability of rain was 80 percent. Now,
if these forecast probabilities are reliable, we should expect
that in reality it actually rained on 80 percent of these 35
forecast dates; that is to say, it actually rained on 28
occasions. If, for example, we found it rained only on 3 of
the 
dates 
when 
an 
80 
percent 
probability 
had 
been
forecast, it is likely
13
that our ensemble forecast system is
not reliable and we would need to go back to the research
and development drawing board to try to ﬁnd out why we
are not representing forecast uncertainties properly in our
ensemble system.
More generally and more succinctly, if the ensemble
system produces reliable forecast probabilities, then, from
all occasions that a weather event is predicted with a
probability of 
p
percent, the weather event should actually
occur on 
p
percent of these occasions. What we are doing
here is taking a probability from the ensemble forecast and
comparing it with a frequency of occurrence from the real
world. The ﬁrst time I referred to “
p
percent”, it was as a
forecast probability. The second time it was as a frequency
of occurrence in the real world. It is this link between
estimated 
probabilities 
of 
occurrence 
and 
actual
frequencies of occurrence that makes ensemble forecasting
useful.
It turns out that developing an ensemble forecast system
whose probabilities are reliable in this way (i.e., precisely
calibrated 
with 
frequencies 
of 
occurrence) 
is 
very
challenging, 
especially 
for 
the 
most 
extreme 
types 
of
weather. Merely adding random perturbations to the initial
conditions doesn’t lead to reliable ensembles at all: with
such perturbations, the ensemble members do not typically
diverge from one another nearly as rapidly as they should.
As 
a 
result, 
such 
ensembles 
underestimate 
forecast
uncertainty, and the corresponding forecast probabilities
are overconﬁdent. Decisions on when to evacuate a city
based on unreliable probabilities of hurricane-force winds
could 
be 
disastrous. 
In 
order 
to 
forecast 
uncertainty
reliably, you 
really
have to understand the nature of the
uncertainties you are dealing with—it’s the primacy of
doubt again.
Trying to represent the butterﬂy eﬀect properly in an
operational ensemble weather prediction system took many
years 
of 
hard 
work, 
not 
least 
because 
the 
error
characteristics of many processes relevant in determining
the forecast initial state are diﬀicult to quantify (e.g., how
exactly 
information 
in 
observations 
is 
lost 
when
assimilating these observations into truncated models of
the 
atmosphere). 
To 
compensate 
for 
such 
“uncertainty
about uncertainty”, we implemented techniques from what
is called generalised instability theory. Essentially the idea
is to focus initial ensemble perturbations along state-space
directions in which the atmospheric circulations are most
unstable. 
Such 
perturbations 
are 
known 
as 
“singular
vectors”. Without such singular-vector perturbations, the
ensemble members tend to be too tightly clustered and the
ensemble 
is 
overconﬁdent 
and 
generates 
unreliable
probabilities. We will return to these singular vectors when
discussing 
ensemble 
conﬂict 
prediction, 
because 
the
concept has relevance more generally. Because of these
special 
types 
of 
perturbations, 
operational 
ensemble
forecast systems are not the simple Monte Carlo forecast
systems originally envisaged by Leith, and so instead we
now use the more general phrase “ensemble prediction” to
describe them.
From the time of implementation in 1992 to the time of
writing, the ﬁfty-member ECMWF ensemble has been run
in parallel with a more traditional single-member, best-
guess deterministic forecast. The latter is made with a
model whose spatial resolution is around twice that of the
ensemble 
model. 
With 
its 
higher 
resolution 
model, 
the
deterministic 
forecast 
provides 
more 
detail 
than 
the
ensemble model. However, there is no way of knowing
whether this extra detail is trustworthy or not. As I write
these words, ECMWF has just announced that in 2023 the
horizontal resolution of the ensemble prediction system will
be 
increased 
to 
9 
km 
(5.6 
miles), 
the 
same 
as 
the
deterministic 
forecast 
model. 
Essentially, 
in 
2023, 
the
ensemble system will be ECMWF’s only forecast system.
This event will mark the end of a very long road of research
and development to introduce ensemble prediction into
weather forecasting.
« »
TO FINISH THE STORY, let’s brieﬂy return to the month-to-
seasonal 
forecast 
timescale—beyond 
the 
so-called 
two-
week limit of deterministic predictability. A major advance
in our ability to predict ENSO using physics-based models
of 
the 
coupled 
ocean-atmosphere 
system 
came 
in 
the
1980s. This is an area where scientists in the United States
led the way, notably Mark Cane and Steve Zebiak, from
Columbia University, who developed a physics-based model
of the tropical Paciﬁc Ocean system and atmosphere above,
which 
encoded 
the 
basic 
coupled 
ocean-atmosphere
dynamical processes that Jacob Bjerknes had uncovered
back in the 1960s. Based on Cane and Zebiak’s work, it
seemed ENSO was predictable over a year ahead
14
. I still
recall with great excitement the time I ﬁrst heard Cane talk
about his model at a meeting at the Royal Society in
London in 1986. At the end of the talk, he showed his
prediction of the 1986/87 ENSO event. It happened just as
predicted. Like with Fitzroy and Blanford before him, this
might have been beginner’s luck. But in this case, it wasn’t.
Nevertheless, none of this could be done reliably with
single deterministic forecasts, and ensemble prediction is
now a vital part of any seasonal prediction system. Such
predictions can be used to warn of the risk of prolonged
droughts in parts of the world, or of exceptional hurricane
seasons, or of the likelihood of cold and snowy winters.
Indeed, 
with 
many 
operational 
centres 
now 
producing
seasonal 
forecasts, 
and 
with 
so 
much 
of 
the 
forecast
uncertainty arising from shortcomings in the resolution of
these 
ocean-atmosphere 
models 
(as 
I 
will 
discuss 
in
Chapter 6), it makes sense to combine diﬀerent model
outputs from diﬀerent forecast institutes into a so-called
multi-model ensemble system. I myself led an international
project 
to 
develop 
one 
of 
the 
ﬁrst 
such 
multi-model
seasonal 
ensemble 
forecasts
15
. 
We 
used 
it 
to
probabilistically 
predict 
crop 
yields 
in 
Europe 
and
outbreaks of epidemic malaria in Africa, two of the many
applications of seasonal predictions.
This multi-model ensemble technique is also used by the
Intergovernmental 
Panel 
on 
Climate 
Change 
for 
their
projections of climate change (Chapter 6). And multi-model
ensembles have also been used to predict the spread of
COVID-19 (Chapter 7).
...
6
Climate Change
Catastrophe or Just Lukewarm?
C
LIMATE CHANGE HAS VARIOUSLY BEEN DESCRIBED AS A
CATASTROPHE in waiting or a lukewarm perturbation to our
climate system of no great signiﬁcance. That’s quite a
diﬀerence of opinion. Which is scientiﬁcally correct? How
should 
we 
view 
climate 
change 
from 
a 
perspective
consistent with the “primacy of doubt”?
Let’s 
start 
by 
reviewing 
two 
opposing 
sides 
of 
the
argument. On the one hand:
We are in a climate crisis. We have reached this state by
burning fossil fuels such as coal, oil and gas. This started in
earnest with the Industrial Revolution and continues to the
present day. Over this time, we have released back into the
atmosphere hundreds of billions of tons of carbon, most of
which had been stored in the ground for hundreds of
millions of years. In this way, we have suddenly increased
the concentration of carbon dioxide in the atmosphere to
levels not seen for millions of years.
These concentrations will continue to increase into the
future unless we stop burning fossil fuels. Because carbon
dioxide is a greenhouse gas, our carbon emissions will
warm the atmosphere, leading to catastrophic changes to
our 
climate, 
including 
deadly 
heatwaves, 
storms 
of
unprecedented 
intensity, 
and 
metres 
of 
sea-level 
rise
caused by ocean warming and the disintegration of ice
sheets.
And the carbon we release into the atmosphere will stay
there for hundreds if not thousands of years. We must stop
our emissions now.
On the other hand:
Such 
alarmist 
warnings 
are 
completely 
overblown.
Carbon 
dioxide 
is 
what 
is 
called 
a 
“trace 
gas” 
in 
the
atmosphere. Its concentration is measured in parts per
million. Since the start of the Industrial Revolution, the
concentration 
has 
increased 
by 
just 
one 
part 
in 
ten
thousand. That’s a tiny amount. Indeed, if you calculate the
amount of warming due to a doubling of carbon dioxide
concentration, it is barely more than 1°C (1.8°F). Not only
is 
this 
not 
a 
catastrophe, 
it’s 
barely 
noticeable.
Temperatures change by more than 1°C from one day to
the next.
And of course, Ed Lorenz’s great insight was to realise
that a chaotic climate never repeats itself. On this basis, a
chaotic system like climate is always changing and hence
we can never be sure that some unusual warm spell in the
atmosphere is not just the eﬀect of internal variability, even
if the warm spell is record breaking. In any case, climate-
change prediction is just a long-range weather forecast,
and, again, we know from chaos theory that such long-
range predictions are impossible. Hence, these forecasts of
deadly 
heatwaves 
and 
substantial 
sea-level 
rise 
are
completely bogus.
On 
top 
of 
this, 
the 
climate 
models 
are 
ﬂawed 
and
unreliable. If we look at the predictions of climate change
made thirty years ago, the models predicted too much
warming. Indeed, when has a climate model ever made a
prediction that has actually turned out to be correct?
And 
in 
any 
case, 
this 
increase 
in 
carbon 
dioxide
concentration is something we should be happy about—
having those extra molecules of carbon dioxide in the
atmosphere will help plants grow more strongly, thereby
greening the planet.
There—I have summarised both sides of the argument,
hopefully in an accurate and disinterested way. Perhaps the
arguments on both sides sound plausible.
Before diving into the science of climate change, I need
to ﬁnd some words that describe these two points of view.
Sometimes the phrase “climate sceptic” is used to describe
the second point of view. However, all scientists, including
climate scientists, are inherently sceptical people. Bob May,
whose 
work 
on 
chaos 
and 
ecology 
was 
described 
in
Chapter 
1, 
characterised 
science 
as 
“organised
scepticism”. 
Another 
phrase 
that 
is 
sometimes 
used 
is
“climate 
denier”. 
However, 
the 
overtones 
of 
Holocaust
denial make this phrase oﬀensive to many people. In an
attempt to be neutral, I propose to call the ﬁrst a “climate
maximalist” 
position 
and 
the 
second 
the 
“climate
minimalist” position
1
. An important question is this: Where,
on a spectrum in which these positions lie at the extremes,
does climate science lie? Near one end, halfway along?
The 
maximalists’ 
certainty 
that 
we 
are 
headed 
for
disaster seems inconsistent with the “primacy of doubt”
mantra of this book. Perhaps, therefore, we should heed
the minimalists who emphasise the need to be cautious and
who 
stress 
uncertainties 
in 
our 
understanding 
of 
the
climate system.
However, as stressed in the previous chapter, estimates
of 
uncertainty 
are 
useful 
for 
making 
decisions—e.g.,
whether to cut carbon emissions or not—only if they are
reliable, 
i.e., 
neither 
overconﬁdent 
nor 
underconﬁdent.
Exaggerating 
uncertainty 
can 
be 
just 
as 
damaging 
as
underestimating 
it. 
If 
Michael 
Fish 
had 
warned 
of 
a
possible hurricane hitting southern England 
every
evening
he appeared on TV, no one would have paid attention on the
day when the hurricane did actually hit.
In their inﬂuential book 
Merchants of Doubt
2
, Naomi
Oreskes 
and 
Erik 
Conway 
describe 
how 
a 
group 
of
scientists and scientiﬁc advisers exploited the inevitable
uncertainties in research to obscure scientiﬁc knowledge
on 
a 
variety 
of 
issues 
from 
tobacco 
smoke 
to 
global
warming. Oreskes and Conway’s book appears to give 
The
Primacy of Doubt
a bad name! However, the authors focus
on the deliberate inﬂation of uncertainty for ideological or
commercial reasons. Clearly, we should be just as wary of
inﬂation of uncertainty as of attempts to make predictions
more certain than can be justiﬁed.
In short, in trying to estimate what climate change holds
in store for us, it is vital that our estimates of uncertainty
are reliable. How do we achieve this? We typically estimate
uncertainty 
using 
ensemble 
techniques 
of 
the 
type
described in the previous chapter for weather prediction.
Indeed, it’s simply not possible to describe climate change
in an objective, scientiﬁc way without explaining the three
roles of ensembles: for estimating the uncertain feedback
eﬀects in climate science; for estimating the impact of
climate policy on climate change; and for separating the
eﬀects of natural chaotic variability from human-induced
eﬀects.
From the ﬁrst way of using ensembles, we can address
how big an impact our emissions of greenhouse gases are
having on global temperatures. With the second set of
ensembles, we can assess whether mitigating actions can
be eﬀective or not. And from the third set of ensembles, we
can not only assess the extent to which observed changes
in weather and climate are natural (“climate is always
changing”), we can try to attribute speciﬁc weather events
to climate change, at least in a probabilistic way.
But let’s start at the beginning. What is a greenhouse
gas? 
We 
say 
that 
carbon 
dioxide 
is 
a 
greenhouse 
gas
because it is relatively transparent to the predominantly
visible and ultraviolet photons emitted from the sun, but is
relatively opaque to the less energetic infrared photons
emitted 
back 
to 
space 
by 
the 
cooler 
Earth
3
. 
Imagine
looking 
down 
on 
Earth 
from 
space, 
wearing 
infrared
goggles
4
. Then instead of seeing Earth’s surface, the blue
of the ocean, for example, we will see a fuzz of photons
emitted from the atmosphere a few kilometres above the
Earth’s surface. These are the photons emitted by the
greenhouse gases in the atmosphere. We would not be able
to make out the Earth’s surface at all from this fog of
infrared photons.
Suppose we could suddenly double the amount of carbon
dioxide in the atmosphere. Then the Earth’s surface would
become even more invisible. The greenhouse blanket is
now stronger than before. Carbon dioxide is what is called
a “well-mixed gas”, which means that if we double the
amount 
of 
carbon 
dioxide 
in 
the 
atmosphere, 
its
concentration will double everywhere in the atmosphere.
As a result, the photons that we see with our infrared
goggles 
will 
on 
average

have 
been 
emitted 
from
greenhouse 
gas 
molecules 
a 
little 
higher 
up 
in 
the
atmosphere—the original layer we were seeing with our
goggles is now partially obscured by extra greenhouse
molecules above this layer. But higher up in this part of the
atmosphere, the air is a little cooler. This means that the
energy of the infrared photons that are emitted out to
space is less than it was before. The Earth is now in an
energetic imbalance: the sun is giving the Earth more
energy than the Earth is re-radiating back to space. The
only way to restore this balance is for the Earth to warm. It
does this at the level where the photons are radiating
energy back to space, and, as a consequence, it warms at
the surface too
5
. Directly from this eﬀect, a doubling of
carbon dioxide from pre-industrial times will warm the
surface of the Earth by a little more than 1°C—perhaps not
something to make a big deal of. Here the minimalists are
correct.
However, carbon dioxide is not the only greenhouse gas
in the atmosphere. Indeed, it is not even the strongest
greenhouse gas, molecule for molecule. As discovered by
the Irish physicist John Tyndall in the nineteenth century,
the most important greenhouse gas in the atmosphere is
actually water vapour—the gaseous form of water. When
the sky is blue and cloudless, there is still plenty of water
vapour in the air. This water vapour is largely transparent
to visible light, which is why we don’t see it. However, it is
opaque to light at infrared wavelengths. Hence, like carbon
dioxide, it can trap the outgoing energy from the Earth.
We often think of the important role that liquid water
plays in sustaining life on Earth. However, Tyndall realised
that, as a greenhouse gas, the gaseous water vapour plays
a vital role too. In a wonderfully eloquent turn of phrase,
Tyndall wrote,
“This aqueous vapour is a blanket more necessary to the
vegetable life of England than clothing is to man. Remove
for a single summer night the aqueous vapour from the air
that overspreads this country, and you would assuredly
destroy 
every 
plant 
capable 
of 
being 
destroyed 
by 
a
freezing 
temperature. 
The 
warmth 
of 
our 
ﬁelds 
and
gardens would pour itself unrequited into space, and the
sun would rise upon an island held fast in the iron grip of
frost.”
With this in mind, you may well wonder why we are so
bothered by an increase in carbon dioxide concentration.
Surely, we should instead be worried about the eﬀect we
might be having on the amount of water vapour in the air.
The answer is that we 
are
worried about the eﬀect we
are having on the amount of water vapour in the air. But we
aren’t worried so much by any direct emissions of water
vapour as by the knock-on eﬀect on water vapour from our
emissions 
of 
carbon 
dioxide. 
Speciﬁcally, 
if 
the 
global
atmosphere warms a little from our emissions of carbon
dioxide, 
this 
warming 
will 
be 
ampliﬁed 
by 
a 
knock-on
increase in the amount of water vapour in the air. The
process is perhaps more familiar in reverse. During cold
autumn nights, the warm, moist air of the day cools and the
water vapour condenses into small droplets we call fog (or
a low-level cloud). The cooler nighttime air contains fewer
water vapour molecules than the warmer daytime air. And
then, as the next day breaks, the air warms again and the
opaque 
water 
droplets 
slowly 
re-evaporate 
back 
into
optically transparent water vapour.
The amplifying knock-on eﬀect is an example of what is
called a positive feedback process. We emit carbon dioxide
into 
the 
air. 
The 
air 
warms 
globally 
a 
small 
amount.
Because of evaporation (e.g., from the oceans and land
surface), 
the 
warmer 
air 
becomes 
more 
moist. 
The
greenhouse eﬀect from this extra water vapour increases
the warming of the air from carbon dioxide alone. The
direct warming due to a doubling of carbon dioxide is a
little 
over 
1°C. 
However, 
if 
we 
add 
this 
water 
vapour
feedback, the warming doubles to just over 2°C (3.6°F). If
we also take into account the fact that reﬂective ice and
snow cover on the Earth’s surface start to disappear as the
Earth gets warmer, so that more of the sun’s energy is
absorbed at the surface, the warming increases to about
2.5°C 
(4.5°F). 
Now 
climate 
change 
starts 
to 
become
something to worry about.
We 
understand 
these 
feedback 
processes 
reasonably
well. 
However, 
there 
is 
another 
feedback 
process
associated with water that we understand rather poorly.
This is the cloud feedback process. Clouds occur when the
water 
vapour 
in 
moist 
air 
condenses 
into 
small 
water
droplets or small ice crystals. Compared with water vapour,
very little of the water in the atmosphere is in the form of
clouds
6
(less than one hundredth). However, clouds have a
big eﬀect on climate. They punch well above their weight,
so to speak.
There are two key questions: How will clouds adjust to
the increasing levels of carbon dioxide, and will this lead to
an ampliﬁcation of the warming or an oﬀ-setting cooling
eﬀect? These are especially diﬀicult questions to answer.
This is partly because, depending on its type, a cloud might
warm or cool the Earth’s surface. For example, a layer of
cloud just above the Earth’s surface will predominantly
scatter photons from the sun back to space. Earth’s surface
will never see these photons, and so the surface will be
cooler than it would be if the layer of cloud weren’t there.
However, 
a 
thin, 
wispy 
cirrus 
layer 
of 
cloud, 
mainly
comprising ice crystals high in the atmosphere, would have
an 
opposite 
eﬀect. 
These 
clouds 
let 
sunshine 
through.
However, like carbon dioxide in the atmosphere, they trap
infrared photons emitted from the atmosphere below. The
Earth’s surface is warmer as a result.
How do clouds respond to, say, a doubling of carbon
dioxide? If low-level clouds increase and high-level clouds
decrease, then clouds will likely save us from catastrophe
7
.
They will oﬀset the warming due to the increase in carbon
dioxide. In this scenario, clouds will feed back negatively
on climate change. It won’t take us all the way back to the
minimalist description of climate change, but perhaps to
somewhere in the middle ground between minimalism and
maximalism. By contrast, if it’s the other way around—low-
level clouds decrease and high-level clouds increase—then
clouds will exacerbate the warming due to the increase in
carbon dioxide. In this scenario, clouds provide a positive
feedback on climate change. Another positive feedback,
compounding the water vapour feedback, would be very
bad news for humanity. In this case we will be facing
climate maximalism.
The question of whether clouds act as a positive or
negative feedback on climate change can’t at present be
answered unambiguously: indeed, I would say it is the
biggest 
unsolved 
problem 
in 
physical 
climate-change
science. There are two reasons why it is a diﬀicult problem.
Firstly, the physics of the small droplets and ice crystals
in a cloud—what is called cloud microphysics—is itself
rather complicated. For example, the balance between ice
crystals and water droplets determines how reﬂective a
cloud is to incoming solar photons, and this reﬂectivity is
crucial 
in 
determining 
the 
eﬀect 
of 
the 
cloud 
on 
the
temperature at the surface. Now, it isn’t the case that cloud
droplets 
simply 
freeze 
into 
ice 
crystals 
once 
the
temperature 
of 
the 
cloud 
falls 
below 
0°C 
(32°F). 
In
particular, cloud droplets frequently comprise supercooled
liquid 
water 
at 
temperatures 
well 
below 
0°C. 
The
conversion of supercooled water droplets into ice crystals
depends not only on temperature but also on impurities
(natural 
and 
manmade) 
in 
the 
air—what 
are 
called
“aerosols”. 
Indeed, 
these 
aerosols 
are 
important 
for
determining how much water vapour in the cloud turns into
water 
droplets 
and 
ice 
crystals 
in 
the 
ﬁrst 
place. 
In
addition, once the ice crystals do freeze, their precise
shape and size can be important in determining things like
cloud reﬂectivity. Hence, trying to estimate how individual
clouds will change as the atmosphere warms depends on
quite complicated cloud microphysical processes.
The second problem is that clouds also depend on the
larger-scale environment in which they exist. There are
many types of clouds in the atmosphere, and assessing how
each of these changes in response to increasing levels of
carbon dioxide is a complicated business. One type which is
believed 
to 
be 
important 
is 
what 
is 
called 
“marine
stratocumulus”. Over the Paciﬁc Ocean, for example, the
extent of these types of clouds depends on the strength of
the 
Walker 
circulation, 
the 
large-scale 
atmospheric
circulation 
associated 
with 
Gilbert 
Walker’s 
Southern
Oscillation, 
as 
described 
in 
Chapter 
5. 
The 
marine
