stratocumulus clouds broadly occur in the sinking branch
of the Walker circulation, over the tropical eastern Paciﬁc
8
.
By 
contrast, 
deep 
thunderstorm 
clouds 
form 
in 
the
ascending 
branch 
of 
the 
Walker 
circulation, 
over 
the
tropical western Paciﬁc, bringing rain to these regions. The
conversion 
of 
water 
vapour 
to 
liquid 
water 
in 
these
thunderstorm clouds plays a crucial role in determining the
strength of the Walker circulation. And so if we want to
know what will happen to the marine stratocumulus clouds
under climate change, it’s not enough to simply study the
microphysics of the stratocumulus clouds in the eastern
Paciﬁc; it is also important to understand what will happen
to the thunderstorm clouds in the remote western Paciﬁc
9
.
And this is just one small part of the cloud feedback jigsaw.
It’s all consistent with the complicated picture of high-
order chaos described in Chapter 3. It is why climate-
change science is unquestionably complicated and why
clouds are the most complicated and uncertain part of it all.
At present, there is no observational evidence that global
cloud cover is systematically changing. On the other hand,
neither is there evidence that it is not changing. The record
we 
have 
of 
high-quality 
global 
cloud 
observations 
is
relatively short, and the changes we are looking for are
small 
compared 
with 
natural 
year-to-year 
variations 
in
cloud cover. The problem of detecting changes in clouds is
compounded 
by 
the 
fact 
that 
individual 
satellite
instruments 
do 
not 
produce 
data 
over 
multidecadal
timescales and the precise calibration of one instrument
relative to its successor or predecessor is delicate and
diﬀicult. However, if we do one day start to see systematic
changes, and they are in the direction of reducing global
low-level cloud cover, and/or increasing high-level cloud
cover, indicating a positive cloud feedback, this will be a
bleak day indeed for humanity. Here the word “positive”
does not denote something good.
Precisely because observations of global cloud cover
have yet to change in a systematic way, it is impossible to
estimate how much warming we can expect over the rest of
this 
century 
by 
simply 
looking 
back 
at 
the 
amount 
of
warming that has happened over the past ﬁfty years or so
and extrapolating into the future. These cloud feedbacks
are highly nonlinear processes.
So, we are faced with a problem. We cannot model
clouds using the proper laws of physics (i.e., using the
Navier-Stokes 
equation 
of 
Chapter 
3) 
because 
the
gridboxes in current global climate models are too coarse
in spatial scale, in turn because the computers climate
scientists use aren’t powerful enough. Instead, clouds have
to 
be 
represented 
by 
simpliﬁed 
formulae 
known 
as
parametrisations. 
These 
parametrisations 
are, 
by 
their
nature, approximate and uncertain. The real problem is
that we are trying to compress the complex fractal-like
multiscale 
structure 
of 
clouds 
into 
a 
highly 
simpliﬁed
formulaic representation. Just as Poincaré found there is no
simple formula to describe the motion of planets, so climate
scientists realise there is no simple formula to describe the
structure of clouds. As discussed in Chapter 3, a way to
incorporate the inherent uncertainty in cloud and other
small-scale turbulent processes is through stochastic noise.
Such 
noisy 
parametrisations 
are 
beginning 
to 
be
introduced into the current generation of climate models.
The ﬁrst physics-based climate model was developed in
the 
1950s 
by 
Norman 
Phillips, 
a 
meteorologist 
at 
the
Institute for Advanced Study in Princeton. Phillips showed
that many of the properties of climate, such as the jet
streams and cyclonic weather disturbances, could emerge
naturally 
from 
the 
Navier-Stokes 
equation 
and 
other
primitive equations of classical physics. However, Phillips’s
model 
was 
crude. 
The 
model 
knew 
nothing 
about 
the
continentality of the Earth or the seasons, and had no
representation of clouds or rain. In addition, the model had
very coarse-scale gridboxes.
In 1958, one of the pioneers of climate science, Nobel
laureate Syukuro Manabe
10
(known to his colleagues as
“Suki”), moved from the University of Tokyo, where he had
just completed his PhD, to the Geophysical Fluid Dynamics
Laboratory at Princeton University, then being led by Jo
Smagorinsky, 
another 
of 
the 
early 
ENIAC 
pioneers.
Together Smagorinsky and Manabe began to develop the
ﬁrst comprehensive global climate models with realistic
geography and seasonal cycles and with hydrological cycles
and hence (parametrised) clouds.
Whilst these models were important for understanding
the emergence of Earth’s climate from the laws of physics,
Manabe 
realised 
that 
they 
could 
also 
be 
useful 
in
estimating the impact of human emissions of greenhouse
gases, a matter which was already of concern in the 1960s
in scientiﬁc and environmental circles. In the mid-1970s,
Suki 
Manabe 
and 
his 
colleague 
Richard 
Wetherald
11
performed the ﬁrst climate-change experiment with the
three-dimensional climate model they had developed. In
this model they estimated the eﬀect on climate of doubling
the 
carbon 
dioxide 
concentration 
from 
pre-industrial
values, as discussed earlier. Fig. 32 shows the temperature
response of the model to such a doubling, as a function of
latitude and height.
Let’s take a careful look at this early physics-based
climate model simulation of climate change. You can see
two clear predictions, over and above the prediction that
the surface of the Earth will warm due to the doubling of
carbon 
dioxide 
concentration. 
The 
ﬁrst 
is 
that 
in 
the
stratosphere, the region of the atmosphere above about 15
km (9.3 miles), the doubling of carbon dioxide will actually
cool the air. The second is that the surface warming will be
particularly marked in the Arctic.
The stratosphere is a region of the atmosphere where
temperatures increase with height due to the absorption of
sunlight by stratospheric ozone, a special molecule made of
three oxygen atoms. The absorption of ultraviolet light by
stratospheric ozone is important in preventing us from
getting skin cancer. So why, then, does the stratosphere
cool when the carbon dioxide concentration increases? The
ﬁrst point to make is that carbon dioxide is a well-mixed
gas. So, when we emit carbon dioxide into the atmosphere,
it 
mixes 
throughout 
the 
atmosphere, 
including 
the
stratosphere, fairly quickly. The second point is that the
temperature 
in 
the 
stratosphere 
arises 
from 
a 
balance
between a warming from the absorption of solar radiation
by ozone and a cooling from the emission back to space of
infrared radiation by carbon dioxide. If the amount of
carbon 
dioxide 
in 
the 
stratosphere 
increases, 
then 
the
emission to space dominates over the ozone absorption,
and the stratosphere cools. As the stratosphere cools, it
emits less infrared radiation to space, and in this way the
stratosphere 
gets 
back 
into 
equilibrium 
at 
a 
reduced
temperature.
FIG. 32. A simulation of climate change from Manabe and Wetherald’s
seminal 1975 study. It shows the simulated change in temperature,
averaged around a line of constant latitude, as a function of latitude and
height in the northern hemisphere, with the North Pole on the left and the
equator on the right. Note the cooling in the stratosphere (at a height of
about 15 km [9.3 miles]) and the “hot spot” warming over the surface
Arctic. Both stratospheric cooling and enhanced Arctic warming have
occurred in reality. Published in 1975 by the American Meteorological
Society.
This diﬀerential heating eﬀect—a warming of the surface
and a cooling in the stratosphere—would not be expected if
the warming of the Earth’s surface was due to an increase
in energy from the sun. In that case, the stratosphere
should 
warm. 
Observations 
show 
that 
the 
Earth’s
stratosphere 
is 
indeed 
cooling 
as 
the 
Earth’s 
surface
warms. 
This 
is 
a 
clear 
prediction 
of 
Manabe 
and
Wetherald’s model that has been veriﬁed by observations.
The second prediction from Manabe and Wetherald’s
study, the enhanced warming in the Arctic, has also come
to pass—large parts of the Arctic are now ice-free in the
summer. This is in part (there are other reasons) due to a
positive feedback eﬀect associated with water. The sea ice
over the Arctic reﬂects sunlight straight back to space.
However, 
as 
the 
Arctic 
warms, 
the 
sea 
ice 
melts 
and
exposes darker water underneath, which absorbs these
solar photons, which then adds further to the warming of
the Earth.
After the success of Manabe and Wetherald’s climate-
change papers, many institutes around the world began to
develop 
their 
own 
climate 
models. 
The 
number 
has
continued to grow to the present day and is approaching
one hundred. What was technically innovative has become
relatively straightforward for individual climate institutes.
These models are all based on the same principles: a
“dynamical 
core” 
where 
the 
Navier-Stokes 
equation
(amongst others) is solved up to some imposed spatial
resolution, and a set of simpliﬁed parametrisation formulae
for unresolved subgrid scale processes like clouds. The
models contain representations not only of the atmosphere
but also of the oceans, the land and the cryosphere (the
regions of Earth covered in ice), and more recently the
biosphere.
This provides a natural “ensemble of opportunity” to
study climate change. It is an example of the so-called
multi-model ensemble mentioned in Chapter 5. Each model
diﬀers 
from 
the 
others 
in 
the 
precise 
computational
techniques used to solve the Navier-Stokes equation, and,
more 
importantly, 
in 
the 
parametrisation 
formulae 
for
unresolved processes. Every few years, the World Climate
Research Programme, partly under UN auspices, oversees
the production of a set of highly coordinated multi-model
ensembles to study climate change. Results from these
multi-model ensembles are fed into the Intergovernmental
Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) assessment reports.
Let’s use these ensembles of opportunity to estimate
how much global warming would arise if we were to double
the amount of carbon dioxide in the atmosphere—from
roughly 300 parts per million (pre-industrial values) to 600
parts per million (which we may reach later this century)
12
.
In Fig. 33 I have shown these estimates in the form of a
histogram 
combining 
together 
output
13

from 
climate
models which contributed to either the ﬁfth or the sixth
IPCC assessment report. It can be seen that the most likely
warming is around 3°C (5.4°F) though a number of models
predict 
increases 
of 
more 
than 
5°C 
(9°F). 
No 
models
predict the Earth will warm less than about 2°C (3.6°F).
The Earth’s surface has already warmed by over a degree
since pre-industrial times.
FIG. 33. A histogram of estimates of climate sensitivity—the global
warming that arises from a doubling of carbon dioxide concentration—from
models which contributed to either the ﬁfth or sixth IPCC assessment report
(Coupled Model Intercomparison Project Phase 5 [CMIP5] or CMIP6). The
dashed line is a probabilistic estimate of such warming. As expected from
theory, it is skewed towards large values of warming.
Motivated by expectations from theory
14
, I have ﬁtted a
simple curve to this histogram. The higher the curve, the
more likely the corresponding increase in global mean
surface temperature shown on the horizontal axis. The
distribution has a characteristic shape that statisticians call
“skewed”, which means the distribution is not symmetric
about its peak, i.e., about its most likely value. It has a
long, fat
15
tail going out to large warming values
16
. By
contrast, it goes to zero for global warming values less than
one 
degree. 
We 
will 
see 
other 
examples 
of 
skewed
distribution 
when 
we 
study 
pandemics 
and 
ﬁnancial
crashes. 
Skewed 
distributions 
with 
long, 
fat 
tails 
are
indicative of an underlying nonlinear system
17
.
One consequence of such a skewed distribution is that
the warming we can expect to see will be greater than the
most 
likely 
warming 
predicted 
by 
a 
single 
model. 
To
illustrate this, suppose we have 10 hypothetical models
which diﬀer by the value of some uncertain parameters,
and suppose the 10 predictions from the 10 models are
described by the highly skewed sequence of numbers {1; 1;
1; 1; 1; 1; 1; 1; 1; 100,000}. If each set of parameter values
is equally likely, the most likely single-model prediction is
the value 1. However, if we average the predictions over
the 10 models, then the average or “expected” prediction is
close to 10,000—much larger than the most likely single-
model prediction. Based purely on the ensemble data in
Fig. 
33, 
the 
most 
likely 
warming 
from 
a 
single-model
prediction is between 2.5° and 3°C, whilst the expected
warming from the ensemble is about 3.6°C (6.5°F). If this is
the warming we can expect, it is the level of warming we
should be prepared for.
The skewed shape of the climate sensitivity curve can be
largely understood from the feedbacks discussed earlier
18
.
We have already mentioned that if climate change were
associated only with an increase in carbon dioxide, then we
would expect the Earth’s surface to warm by a little over
1°C. 
As 
mentioned, 
with 
water 
vapour 
feedback 
this
increases to just over 2°C. The fact that we have this rather
broad, skewed distribution is largely due to uncertainty in
the cloud feedback eﬀect
19
. Whilst a few models have
negative 
cloud 
feedback 
eﬀects, 
most 
climate 
models
predict 
positive 
cloud 
feedbacks 
but 
with 
diﬀerent
strengths.
Although climate-model experiments in which carbon
dioxide levels are doubled (such as shown in Fig. 33) are
useful in quantifying the possible changes in weather due
to climate change, they are in one particular way too
idealised to be useful for policy-making. These experiments
don’t tell us when we will reach a given level of climate
change. If our emissions reduce substantially, but not to
zero, it could take many hundreds of years before carbon
dioxide 
concentrations 
in 
the 
atmosphere 
will 
double.
Politicians tend to lose interest about things too far in the
future.
This brings us to the second important use of ensembles
in climate prediction. If we are to estimate the future
climate for, say, the year 2100, we need to know how our
emissions of carbon dioxide will change with time over the
twenty-ﬁrst century. This of course depends on how we
humans 
respond 
to 
the 
model 
simulations 
of 
climate
change. Climate models are like economic models in this
respect—just as output from economic models can aﬀect
the economy, so output from climate models can aﬀect the
climate. Earth’s climate could turn out very diﬀerently if,
on the one hand, we ignore the simulations or, on the other
hand, we heed the simulations. In principle one could
imagine 
incorporating 
these 
diﬀerent 
human 
responses
into a single coupled human-climate ensemble—in which
humanity 
ignores 
the 
predictions 
in 
some 
ensemble
members and cuts emissions in others.
However, this may not be especially useful for policy-
making. 
Policymakers 
need 
to 
know 
how 
climate 
will
change both without regulatory policy and with regulatory
policy. From such information they can assess the value of
such regulatory policy.
To do that, separate ensembles are run for diﬀerent
emissions “scenarios”, as they are called. For example, one
emissions 
scenario 
could 
be 
considered 
a 
worst-case
situation in which we burn fossil fuels as rapidly as we
can
20
. We appear to have moved away from this scenario
because we are not burning coal as much as we might be.
In another emissions scenario, we try to decarbonise at a
leisurely pace. In a third emissions scenario we not only
decarbonise rapidly, we ﬁnd ways of sucking carbon dioxide
back out of the air—e.g., by planting more trees or by more
technological 
means—through 
what 
are 
called 
negative
emissions.
In this way, climate scientists tend to talk not about
climate predictions but climate “projections”. A projection
is a prediction conditioned on an assumed scenario for
carbon emissions (and other related processes). We create
an 
ensemble 
of 
climate 
projection 
for 
each 
emission
scenario.
Being 
aware 
of 
this 
dependence 
on 
scenario 
is
particularly 
important 
when 
examining 
the 
accuracy 
of
climate projections made in past decades. For example, one
of the best-known historical sets of projections was made in
1988 by one of the leading climate scientists: Jim Hansen
from 
NASA. 
Hansen 
presented 
to 
a 
US 
congressional
committee results based on the NASA climate model, run
using three diﬀerent emissions scenarios. In retrospect we
can see that his Scenario A overestimated the emissions
that 
have 
actually 
occurred. 
This 
does 
not 
mean 
that
Hansen’s model was wrong with this projection—Hansen
could not have been sure how our emissions would evolve
with time. Indeed, his somewhat less aggressive Scenario B
has turned out to be a more accurate representation of our
emissions. Some climate minimalists use Hansen’s Scenario
A projection to suggest that the models overpredict global
warming. However, in terms of Scenario B, the observed
and simulated changes in global temperature are quite
close. 
Indeed, 
looking 
more 
broadly 
at 
those 
climate
projections whose emissions scenarios were close to what
actually happened, estimates of increases in global mean
surface temperature were found to be broadly consistent
with observations
21
.
So far, we have focussed on global mean temperature.
Scientists describe climate change in terms of increases in
global 
mean 
surface 
temperature, 
not 
because 
it’s 
a
societally 
important 
variable 
but 
because 
the 
chaotic
internal 
variability 
of 
climate 
aﬀects 
global 
mean
temperatures much less than more regional temperatures.
The temperature over London can change by 1°C from one
hour to the next. However, the temperature averaged over
the 
whole 
surface 
of 
the 
Earth 
is 
much 
more 
stable.
Changing it by 1°C is a big deal.
Using 
global 
mean 
surface 
temperature 
to 
describe
climate change comes with a major downside—it conveys
no real sense of what climate change means to us humans.
We, after all, are aﬀected by local weather, not by global
mean temperatures. Perhaps the best book I know that
gives a plausible sense of what a warmer world would be
like 
at 
the 
regional 
scale 
is 
Mark 
Lynas’s 
Our 
Final
Warning: Six Degrees of Climate Emergency
.
What I want to do now is ﬁx on a speciﬁc level of 4°C
(7.2°F) of warming. According to Fig. 33, it’s a little more
than the expected warming given a doubling of carbon
dioxide but certainly one that is plausible in the future. This
is what Lynas says about a 4°C warmer world:
“At four degrees a whole new process has begun to
unfold, making substantial areas of the planet biologically
uninhabitable for humans. Whilst the increased mortality
risk in today’s heatwaves tends to be among older, very
young and other vulnerable people, temperatures at four
degrees of global heating are high enough to reach a
critical threshold that will kill anyone, however ﬁt and
healthy, because of the laws of thermodynamics.”
Here Lynas is referring to the fact that the human body
loses excess heat (e.g., when air temperature exceeds body
temperature) 
by 
sweating, 
i.e., 
by 
evaporative 
cooling.
However, once temperature and humidity pass a certain
threshold, we are unable to lose heat by sweating, and,
without going into an air-conditioned building, death will
result. It is no good sitting in the shade for respite. The
critical threshold occurs when a thermometer whose bulb
is encased in a wet piece of cloth—the temperature of a so-
called wet-bulb thermometer—reaches 35°C (95°F).
We 
have 
not 
yet 
experienced 
such 
wet-bulb
temperatures, though in 2015 the wet-bulb temperature in
Bandar Mahshahr in Iran reached 34.6°C (94°F)
22
. Lynas
recounts 
how, 
in 
a 
4°C 
warmer 
world, 
such 
deadly
heatwaves will become commonplace in large parts of the
Middle East and South Asia, including large parts of India
and 
China. 
The 
consequence 
would 
likely 
be 
mass
migration either polewards or to higher altitudes.
But this won’t necessarily bring complete relief because,
according 
to 
the 
models, 
subtropical 
regions 
like 
the
Mediterranean, whilst not as hot as the Middle East, will
have 
turned 
into 
deserts 
as 
the 
rain-bearing 
storms
themselves shift polewards
23
. The desertiﬁcation of large
parts of the world will impact the crops we grow for food,
and simultaneous drought in the main breadbaskets of the
world will not be uncommon.
On top of that, the more humid conditions will make
extreme storms more intense. The latent heat released
when water vapour condenses into liquid water plays an
important role in determining the intensity of such storms.
More water vapour in the air allows for greater latent heat
release. With sea-level rise (caused by a combination of
thermal expansion of water and disintegrating ice sheets),
the storm surges associated with some of these extreme
storms will be devastating for many coastal cities.
From 
Lynas’s 
description, 
a 
4°C 
or 
warmer 
world
describes a vision of hell. I cannot imagine anyone would
want to live in such a world, even if it were possible to ﬁnd
ways of adapting to it in part.
However, we should not have to take Lynas’s word for it.
Since 
climate 
models 
are 
very 
similar 
in 
structure 
to
weather forecast models—historically, they evolved from
the weather models of the 1950s—they not only simulate
changes in global temperature, they also simulate changes
in regional weather patterns.
Indeed, because of this, we can in principle use climate
models to study whether some actual observed weather
event, let’s say a heatwave in Canada or a ﬂooding event in
Germany, is somehow caused by climate change. This is a
contentious issue, as these days climate maximalists tend
to associate any observed extreme weather event with
climate change, whilst minimalists dismiss them as part of
the natural chaotic variability of climate.
This 
brings 
us 
to 
the 
third 
use 
of 
ensembles. 
Two
separate 
ensembles 
of 
runs 
of 
physics-based 
climate
models are made. In the ﬁrst ensemble, the climate model
is run for, say, one hundred years with an atmospheric
carbon dioxide concentration that is ﬁxed at values from
just before the beginning of the Industrial Revolution. In
the second ensemble, the carbon dioxide concentration is
doubled. 
Let’s 
suppose 
we 
are 
interested 
in 
whether
climate change will aﬀect the probability of a heatwave
over California. We simply count the number of times such
heatwaves occur in each of the two ensembles. If the model
is 
realistic 
enough, 
Californian 
heatwaves 
will
spontaneously occur in the ﬁrst ensemble since they are
part of the natural variability of climate. Comparing the
two 
ensembles’ 
heatwave 
frequencies 
will 
give 
us 
an
estimate 
of 
the 
change 
in 
likelihood 
of 
a 
Californian
heatwave due to our carbon emissions
24
. The technique of
quantitatively 
attributing 
real-world 
weather 
events 
to
climate change was pioneered by my Oxford colleague
Myles Allen
25
in 2003, and the application of his ideas
features 
strongly 
in 
the 
most 
recent 
IPCC 
assessment
report.
It’s certainly true that because a chaotic system never
repeats itself, we can never be 100 percent certain that
some real-life weather event was caused by our carbon
emissions. It’s true but pretty much irrelevant. What is
relevant 
is 
knowing 
that, 
under 
climate 
change, 
the
likelihood of this type of event has changed from 1 in 1,000
years to, say, 1 in 10 years. From such a statistic, we can
decide whether it’s worth trying to mitigate the eﬀects of
such weather events, for example by changing forestry
management practices or by building better ﬂood defences.
This methodology works if the weather events we study
are not too extreme. However, there is a problem if the
weather 
events 
are 
particularly 
extreme. 
If 
we 
try 
to
perform attribution studies on some of the recent extreme
events (e.g., from 2021, the extreme summer temperatures
in British Columbia or the extreme rainfall in the Eifel
region of Europe, Henan Province in China or New York),
we ﬁnd that current-generation models are not capable of
simulating events of this intensity. This is simply because
the climate model gridboxes aren’t small enough to allow
the model equations to generate the intensity of rainfall or
temperature or wind associated with such extremes. The
probability of such events in both pre-industrial times and
in 
the 
twenty-ﬁrst 
century 
is 
0, 
according 
to 
current-
generation models. Hence, according to the recipe I’ve
described, the eﬀect of climate change is equal to 0 / 0,
which 
is 
an 
undeﬁned 
number—it 
could 
literally 
be
anything. And yet it is these extreme events in which we
are most interested. This very much suggests that if we try
to use current climate models to estimate the nature of
weather extremes in a 4°C warmer world, we will likely
underestimate them.
The conclusion is not that climate change isn’t aﬀecting
the likelihood of such extreme events. Rather, it means we
need to put more resources into climate modelling
26
to
decrease the size of model gridboxes enough to allow
climate models to simulate such extremes. I return to this
point further on.
« »
MAXIMALIST 
OR 
MINIMALIST: 
WHICH 
is 
scientiﬁcally
correct?
Let’s start by pointing out a few scientiﬁcally incorrect
statements on both sides. Clearly what is wrong with the
maximalist position (as stated earlier) is the assertion that
climate change will be catastrophic. This is not supported
by 
the 
science. 
Because 
of 
uncertainties 
in 
climate
feedbacks, most importantly with clouds, the science is not
that certain.
However, the minimalist position (as stated earlier) is
scientiﬁcally incorrect in a number of ways.
For example, one cannot dismiss century-long climate
projections just because a deterministic weather forecast
goes wrong after a few days. Climate-change prediction is
not 
an 
initial-value 
problem 
of 
the 
type 
suggested 
by
Cleveland 
Abbe 
and 
Vilhelm 
Bjerknes, 
as 
we 
saw 
in
Chapter 5. 
In 
weather 
forecasting, 
we 
try 
to 
estimate
where we will be on the climate attractor as accurately as
possible at some point in the future. In climate forecasting,
by contrast, we try to estimate how an external forcing
(e.g., 
coming 
from 
our 
carbon 
emissions) 
changes 
the
shape of the climate attractor as a whole. Fig. 11 from
Lorenz’s model illustrates the point well: we can predict a
change in the shape of the attractor with much more
conﬁdence than we can predict where the state will be on
the attractor at any time.
A 
second 
factually 
incorrect 
point 
in 
the 
minimalist
argument as stated is that models from thirty years ago
predicted too much warming and that these climate models
made no other veriﬁable predictions. As mentioned, given
the correct emissions scenario, the early models did predict
the correct amount of warming. In addition, the earliest
climate 
models 
correctly 
predicted 
the 
cooling 
of 
the
stratosphere 
and 
the 
fact 
that 
the 
Arctic 
would 
be
especially prone to warming.
Then there are statements in the minimalist position that
are true but misleading. It is true that we can never be sure
that some record-breaking event is not part of natural
climate variability. However, as I have explained, this is a
red herring. What we want to know is whether such types
of events are becoming more likely, and by how much, as a
result of climate change.
Another 
example 
of 
a 
misleading 
statement 
is 
that
increases in carbon dioxide will help to green the planet.
Such beneﬁcial impacts will occur only as long as the
weather doesn’t become unconducive to plant growth. It’s
no good having that extra plant material if there is too little
or too much rain for it to grow or it is so hot that it wilts.
Perhaps there is an analogy with addictive recreational
drugs. Initially they seem beneﬁcial to your sense of well-
being, but ultimately, they can destroy you.
Insofar as the science of climate change is concerned
with 
estimating 
the 
probabilities 
of 
diﬀerent 
levels 
of
climate change, it is unscientiﬁc to assert that we are
heading 
towards 
climate 
maximalism 
or 
climate
minimalism, or indeed any speciﬁc point in between.
It may appear from what I have written that I am more
sympathetic with the climate maximalist position than the
climate minimalist position. However, there is one element
of 
the 
climate 
minimalist 
statement 
that 
I 
cannot
completely dismiss: the possibility that the climate models
are, in part, unreliable. To be clear, I would ﬁnd it very hard
to 
believe 
that 
the 
probabilistic 
projections 
of 
global
warming are completely unreliable, for the simple reason
that 
these 
estimates 
are 
entirely 
consistent 
with 
basic
physics in a moist atmosphere. On top of which, it is hard
to think of any external forcing (like solar variability), or an
internal dynamical mechanism, that would generate the
observed changes in global temperatures in both the lower
and upper atmospheres on a multidecadal timescale.
Nevertheless, there are good reasons to suppose, for
example, that we are not estimating the cloud feedback
term as accurately as we might. For example, no cloud
parametrisation scheme can properly represent what is
called the “mesoscale” structure of clouds: structures that
are larger than individual clouds but smaller than the grid
spacing of the models. For example, holes can appear in
sheets of stratus cloud, like in a regular honeycomb
27
. Such
structures can’t be properly represented in current climate
models because the gridboxes—typically 100 km (62 miles)
or more in horizontal length—aren’t small enough. Another
type of mesoscale structure which can’t be represented in
climate models is associated with the way thunderstorm
clouds sometimes aggregate together to form superstorms.
When they do aggregate, the surrounding air is clear and
relatively dry. Such structures change the radiative balance
of the Earth. If the mesoscale structure of clouds evolves
under climate change, this will contribute to the cloud
feedback term.
Indeed, because of such inherent shortcomings, current-
generation 
climate 
models 
depart 
systematically 
from
observations in quantities like regional seasonally averaged
rainfall. Indeed, these systematic departures (indicating a
bias 
of 
the 
models 
compared 
with 
observations) 
are
somewhat larger than the climate-change signals we are
asking the models to simulate
28
. In a nonlinear system like
climate, this is an indication of unreliability on the regional
scale.
We can imagine a climate version of the “Turing test” for
AI. In the original Turing test—the imitation game
29
—we
ask the AI system questions and, based on the answers, try
to tell whether we are talking to a human or a computer.
Current AI systems cannot pass the Turing test. Similarly,
current climate models cannot pass the climate Turing test;
looking at output from these models, we can tell that it is
coming from a computer model rather than from the real
world
30
.
Despite these shortcomings, we cannot simply ignore
climate models. These models are our only tool to try to
understand and estimate the future. It’s not like other
areas of science where we can simply do a laboratory
experiment. There are no lab experiments that can emulate
climate change.
Climate 
models 
are 
central 
in 
determining 
the 
best
strategy on carbon emissions. One particularly important
issue is whether we will pass “tipping points”—changes to
ice sheets, rain forests or ocean circulations which cannot
be undone by negative carbon emissions at a later time. We
can plant as many trees as we like, but if they are planted
after one or more of these tipping points have been passed,
they 
will 
be 
ineﬀective 
in 
returning 
us 
to 
current
conditions. Current climate models are not able to assess
reliably whether such tipping points will occur, as the
models’ resolution is too coarse.
Output from climate models is also vital for assessing
societal vulnerability to climate change on the regional
scale, and so for determining the investments needed to
make 
society 
resilient 
to 
changes 
in 
climate. 
This 
is
particularly 
important 
in 
the 
developing 
world 
where
extremes 
of 
weather 
are 
likely 
to 
become 
especially
extreme 
(e.g., 
wet-bulb 
temperature 
exceeding 
35°C).
Prioritisation 
of 
possible 
adaptation 
strategies 
will 
be
crucial for such countries: Is preparing for drought and
heatwaves more important than preparing for exceptional
storms and deluges of rain, or vice versa? Again, this
requires high-resolution simulations of climate.
Climate models are also vital if we are ever to consider a
so-called Plan B in which we try to actively control the
climate, e.g., by spraying aerosols (or their precursors) into
the stratosphere to reﬂect sunlight back to space
31
. The
danger 
with 
such 
alternatives 
is 
that 
they 
may 
have
unexpected consequences, such as diverting the monsoons
or 
shutting 
oﬀ 
the 
moisture 
supply 
to 
the 
rainforests.
Reliable climate models are the only tools we have to
quantify these risks.
It is for all these reasons that I am not happy with the
status quo in climate modelling—i.e., with the multi-model
ensembles of relatively low-resolution models, as used in
the IPCC reports. They have done their job of warning the
world of the dangers of climate change, but they are not
well suited for estimating climate change at the regional
level, particularly for estimating changes in the likelihood
of extreme events. The problem is that individual institutes
often 
lack 
the 
necessary 
human 
and 
computational
resources. They simply can’t aﬀord to run their models at a
higher resolution. As a result, we do not have the capability
to produce high-resolution ensemble projections of climate.
My own view is that we need a new framework for
developing next-generation climate models if we are to
reduce 
some 
of 
these 
key 
uncertainties 
about 
climate
change. 
Instead 
of 
a 
large 
number 
of 
subcritical
institutional 
eﬀorts, 
we 
need 
to 
pool 
resources 
into 
a
“CERN 
for 
climate 
change”
32
, 
CERN 
being 
the
international institute which found the Higgs boson. In this
way, scientists should work more collaboratively to develop
next-generation, 
high-resolution 
climate 
models 
for 
the
beneﬁt of society worldwide. There is a relevant African
proverb: if you want to go fast, go alone—if you want to go
far, go together. Perhaps philanthropy can play a role in
supporting such an institute. The cost per year need not be
much more than that of launching a few craft into space.
With such an institute—or better, a federated hub of
institutes 
around 
the 
world—we 
can 
envisage 
applying
ensemble prediction in a fourth sense, one which connects
with its use in weather prediction as described in Chapter
5. 
As 
we 
discussed, 
it 
doesn’t 
matter 
that 
weather is
chaotic 
and 
unpredictable 
when 
making 
projections 
of
future climate. As such we do not have to try hard to ﬁnd
precise initial conditions for the projections, as we would in
weather forecasting. On the other hand, there is some
evidence that decadal-timescale variability in the oceans
may be partially predictable. For example, as mentioned
right 
at 
the 
beginning 
of 
this 
book, 
decadal-timescale
drought in the Sahel region of Africa may be linked to
ocean circulations in the Atlantic. There is evidence that
these circulations may be inherently predictable, that is to
say, can be predicted given an ensemble of forecasts in
which the ocean circulations are accurately initialised from
observations. This raises the prospect of making regional
climate 
projections 
over 
multidecadal-timescales 
using
ensembles 
which 
are 
both 
carefully 
initialised 
with
observations 
and 
in 
which 
scenarios 
for 
our 
carbon
emissions are also included
33
. Such an endeavour would
need high-resolution climate ensembles. It would be an
ideal 
goal 
for 
an 
international 
federated 
institute 
with
pooled human and computing resources.
Here I am not arguing for a single, monolithic climate
model. However, I am arguing for a rationalisation of eﬀort
around a few modelling systems (perhaps one or two per
continent) which individually have suﬀicient resolution to
pass the climate Turing test. I hope this is something both
climate minimalists and climate maximalists will support.
The better we can represent our climate system with the
primitive laws of physics, the more conﬁdence we can have
in the output of these models.
Let’s summarise. Taking a speciﬁc position—minimalist,
maximalist or indeed any speciﬁc point in between—is
simply inconsistent with the science. The key message of
this chapter is that one’s attitude to climate change, like to
weather prediction, should be framed in terms of risk: Is
the risk of undesirable changes to climate high enough to
warrant taking precautionary action now? Consider the
possibility of the Earth spiralling oﬀ to inﬁnity, as discussed
in Chapter 1. If this were to occur, it would wipe out
humanity. We could take precautions of a sort by diverting
massive resources into populating Mars (assuming Mars
won’t itself be ejected at the same time). However, the risk
is 
so 
small—recall 
that 
not 
one 
single 
member 
of 
the
ensemble of solar system forecasts predicted this would
happen—that 
it 
is 
clearly 
not 
worth 
taking 
such
precautions. However, as we have seen, the risk of a hellish
4°C or warmer world is far from negligible—it is only a few
tenths of a degree warmer than what we would expect from
a doubling of carbon dioxide concentration.
If we think the risk is worth the cost, then manifestly the
uncertainties discussed in this chapter should not deter us
from 
taking 
precautionary 
action. 
That 
is, 
uncertainty
should not be a cause for inaction. However, given the
importance of the problem, if we do decide to act, we must
surely continue to fund the research that will help reduce
uncertainties, or will at least increase conﬁdence in the
reliability of our estimates of uncertainty. Whether the risk
really is worth the cost is something I will address in
Chapter 10.
...
7
Pandemics
C
OVID-19 HAS KILLED MILLIONS AROUND THE WORLD.
AND YET, IF we can leave aside the misery it has brought,
the disease provides a fascinating example of the role of
ensemble prediction in practice. COVID-19 also illustrates
an extraordinary number of parallels with the climate-
change problem. What can we learn from this?
Early in the days of the pandemic, governments around
the world faced diﬀicult decisions about which most had no
prior experience. Indeed, although some, like the UK, had
made plans for a major outbreak in disease and illness, the
plans were not really appropriate for COVID-19, where
many in the population were asymptomatic carriers. In any
case, the crucial question was whether to impose strict
lockdowns to try to minimise the spread of the virus, and
hence minimise the risk that the countries’ health services
would collapse under the weight of COVID infections, but
with the consequence that the countries’ economies would
suﬀer badly. The alternative was to simply let economies
continue 
as 
unimpeded 
as 
possible, 
suﬀer 
the 
health
consequences and hope for some eventual herd immunity.
Many policies were based on the former approach, though
some governments took a more laissez-faire attitude.
This strikingly paralleled strategies on tackling climate
change: try to cut emissions and get to net-zero as quickly
as 
possible, 
or 
do 
little 
that 
might 
threaten 
economic
growth. As far as I could tell, those who advocated minimal
restriction to the economy during the pandemic tended to
also advocate minimal restrictions on the use of fossil fuels.
In the UK at least, the politicians repeatedly said that we
must “follow the science” in dealing with the pandemic.
However, as with climate change, science does not itself
advocate a particular policy. If you want to reduce the risk
of dangerous levels of climate change, then, according to
climate 
science, 
you 
should

reduce 
carbon 
emissions.
Climate-change science does not say that you 
must
reduce
emissions—that 
involves 
making 
value 
judgements.
Similarly, if you want to reduce the risk that health services
get overrun, then, according to COVID-prediction science,
you 
should 
restrict 
the 
way 
people 
interact 
with 
one
another. The science does not say that you therefore must
restrict 
such 
interactions. 
That 
too 
involves 
value
judgements.
To invoke measures that minimise the risk of hospitals
being overrun, governments need ways of predicting the
number of people needing hospitalisation as a function of
diﬀerent possible policy measures. The predictions will
therefore be contingent on these policy measures. This is
exactly the same as occurs in estimating climate change.
Because of this, the COVID-modelling community uses the
same 
terminology 
as 
the 
climate-modelling 
community:
predictions 
contingent 
on 
particular 
policies 
are 
called
“projections”. This is important when trying to assess the
accuracy of a particular projection. It’s no good comparing
real-world data with a projection of hospital admissions
that 
assumes 
a 
policy 
of 
normal, 
unrestricted 
societal
interaction 
if 
in 
the 
real 
world 
the 
government
implemented a policy to restrict interaction. This is like
comparing the observed global warming rate with climate
projections 
based 
on 
bigger 
carbon 
emissions 
than
occurred in reality.
The core of typical COVID prediction models is quite
simple. The population at any time is divided into three
classes: individuals who have not yet been in contact with
the virus but are susceptible to infection; individuals who
have the virus and can infect others; and individuals who
have been infected and have either recovered or died, but
in either case cannot infect others. This is often referred to
as an SIR (susceptible/infected/removed) model.
The 
development 
of 
eﬀective 
vaccines 
does 
not
invalidate the SIR model. Now vaccinated individuals are
less susceptible to infection (and are much less likely to
need hospitalisation and much less likely to die). This
eﬀectively increases the number of “removed” individuals
in the SIR model.
The diﬀiculty in predicting the spread of the disease is
less to do with uncertainty in classifying the stages of
infection, and more to do with uncertainty in the way we
humans interact with each other, and hence uncertainty
into which of these three classes an individual will belong.
At one extreme, one can imagine a hypothetical population
of individuals which is fully homogeneous in the sense that
each individual has the same chance of interacting with,
and hence of passing the infection to, any other individual.
This is described by the R
0
parameter: R
0
describes how
many people each infected person will infect on average.
With R
0
= 2, on average 1 infected person will infect 2
people, these 2 people will infect 4 people, these 4 people
will infect 8 and so on. This is called exponential growth.
More generally, if R
0
is greater than 1, the disease grows. If
R
0
is less than 1, the virus will eventually die out on its
own.
Clearly no population is homogeneous in this way. We
each live in our own regions, our own cities, towns and
villages. Living in the south of England, I am much less
likely to come into contact with someone living in Scotland
than with someone living in, say, London. We can try to
account 
for 
this 
by 
estimating 
the 
R
0

parameter 
for
diﬀerent regions. On the other hand, if this heterogeneity
extends down to the smallest scales—as well it might—then
R
0
ceases to be a simple parameter, and instead becomes a
highly dynamic variable which ﬂuctuates wildly from one
grouping of people to another, and from time to time.
The most obvious manifestation of the heterogeneity of
society is the potential existence of “superspreaders”—
individuals who infect many more people than would be
implied 
by 
a 
single 
R
0

parameter—or 
“superspreader
events”, where many more people would get infected than
would be expected from a more homogeneous society. An
important question is whether overall infection rates can be
signiﬁcantly inﬂuenced by a small number of superspreader
events. To answer this, the assumption of homogeneity
must be replaced with one which is not overly complex and
intractable, and yet takes account of the way in which
individuals 
interact 
as 
a 
function 
of 
age, 
geographic
location and other factors.
FIG. 34. An example of a network of people. Individuals correspond to
nodes of the network, and they interact if they are joined or linked together.
One way to model the more heterogeneous aspects of
society is through what are known as networks
1
. From a
mathematical point of view, a network is easy to describe: it
is a set of points, called “nodes”, and lines joining some of
these points, called “links”. A simple example of a network
is shown in Fig. 34. Here nodes reﬂect individuals, and
they are joined by links when the individuals interact.
For example, imagine a cocktail party where initially no
one knows anyone. The nodes of the associated network
would correspond to the individuals at the party. If two
people talk to each other during the party, we draw a link
between the two corresponding nodes. Since it is fairly
unpredictable who will talk to whom, we can model the
interactions between individuals in a stochastic way, i.e.,
using 
random 
numbers. 
That 
is 
to 
say, 
we 
assign 
a
probability 
p
that these individuals will interact. If all the
individuals are introverts, then 
p
will be quite small; if they
are all extroverts, 
p
will be larger. Nodes are then joined
randomly, consistent with the probability 
p.
This is sometimes called a random network. One of the
interesting 
properties 
of 
a 
random 
network 
is 
that 
it
exhibits 
what 
is 
sometimes 
called 
the 
“small-world”
property: if you choose any two individuals on Earth, you
will ﬁnd that one of them knows someone, who knows
someone, who knows someone, who knows someone, who
knows someone, who knows someone, who knows the other
person, i.e., that any two people are linked by at most six
intermediate acquaintances. For many years after network
theory was developed, it was thought that such random
networks 
described 
societal 
and 
many 
other 
types 
of
interaction quite accurately.
In random networks of the type I’ve described, it is very
unlikely that nodes with a large number of links will exist if
p
is small. For example, with 100 nodes and with 
p
= 0.02,
then we would expect each node to have about 2 links
emanating from it on average. The likelihood that a node
has, say, 8 links emanating from it is virtually zero. This is
because 
of 
the 
assumption 
that 
if 
the 
probability 
that
individual A interacts with individual B is equal to 
p
, then
the probability that individual A will interact with both B
and C is equal to 
p
× 
p
= 
p
2
. If 
p
is a small number (e.g.,
0.02), then 
p
2
is a very small number (0.0004). Because of
this, the number of nodes with 
k
links to other nodes
decreases exponentially the larger is 
k
. Just as exponential
growth represents very rapid growth, so exponential decay
represents very rapid decay.
However, in the real world, this assumption frequently
turns out to be incorrect. Imagine that at our cocktail party
we had invited a few celebrities. Then, likely, we would ﬁnd
that 
many 
people 
would 
try 
to 
interact 
with 
those
particular 
individuals. 
In 
terms 
of 
the 
corresponding
network, there would be many more links emanating from
the celebrity nodes compared with the non-celebrity nodes.
If 
we 
analyse 
the 
World 
Wide 
Web, 
where 
the 
URLs
(websites) are represented by nodes, then we will ﬁnd
numerous nodes with a small number of links coexisting
with a few nodes with a very large number of links—we call
these “hubs”. If we look at an air-traﬀic network, we ﬁnd a
few hubs coexisting with many nodes of small degree.
These 
hubs 
correspond 
to 
superspreaders 
in 
COVID
transmission.
In networks with hubs, the number of nodes with 
k
links
does not decrease exponentially as 
k
increases. Instead, the
probability that a node has 
k
links is described by what is
called a “power-law” distribution, where the probability
decays with 
k
much more slowly than exponential decay.
Such power-law distributions also describe how energy,
in the spectrum of whirls in a turbulent ﬂuid, varies with
the size of the whirl. Indeed, fractal geometries can also be
described 
by 
such 
power-law 
distributions. 
The
mathematician Benoit Mandelbrot—the one who coined the
word “fractal”—drew attention to these connections in his
book 
The (Mis)behaviour of Markets
2
. He made the point
that extreme events, like ﬁnancial crashes, which might
superﬁcially be assessed as extremely unlikely, are not
unlikely 
at 
all 
in 
models 
with 
this 
type 
of 
power-law
behaviour. The existence of power-law behaviour is telling
us about a type of nonlinearity which underpins multiscale
systems, which is common to the spread of disease, the
economy, the weather and maybe even the whole universe.
Using power-law networks of ten thousand nodes, where
nodes represent individuals who are either “susceptible”,
“infected” or “recovered”, it was indeed found that the best
strategy for curbing the spread of the infection was to
reduce the extent to which the superspreader hubs spread
the infection
3
. Here the spread of the disease when the
number 
of 
links 
emanating 
from 
a 
hub 
is 
reduced 
is
compared with a strategy in which the number of links
emanating from randomly chosen nodes is cut.
Whilst 
such 
network 
models 
are 
useful 
in 
drawing
attention 
to 
the 
importance 
of 
heterogeneity 
of 
the
population, 
they 
are 
still 
too 
idealised 
to 
be 
useful 
in
actually 
estimating 
infection 
and 
hence 
hospitalisation
numbers. For a country like the UK, one would need to
increase the number of nodes from ten thousand to about
67 million if one were to capture the population using one
of these networks (and model the way in which all such
individuals interact with each other). And that would not
include the infections coming from people travelling to the
UK 
from 
abroad. 
Although 
this 
is 
not 
completely
inconceivable—as 
I 
discuss 
later 
on—with 
present
supercomputers it is not feasible.
Instead, state-of-the-art epidemiological models—such as
the 
Imperial 
College 
COVIDSim 
model
4
—work 
in 
a
somewhat diﬀerent way. COVIDSim is an important model
as 
its 
projections 
provided 
one 
of 
the 
key 
pieces 
of
information 
that 
led 
the 
UK 
government 
to 
order 
a
nationwide lockdown in March 2020 to try to contain the
virus. In COVIDSim, the country is tiled with gridsquares,
in much the same way a weather or climate model tiles the
Earth with gridboxes. Each of the gridsquares contains
information about the population within the gridsquare—
for 
example, 
population 
density, 
age 
structure 
and
household structure, along with information about schools,
universities 
and 
workplaces. 
This 
information 
is
represented in the form of parameters—some 940 of them
in total—numbers which are ﬁxed and don’t change with
time during a model forecast.
A 
key 
question 
for 
any 
government 
is 
whether 
the
projections from a model like COVIDSim are reliable. Can
the projections be trusted to give a plausible picture of the
future? 
Clearly 
this 
depends 
on 
the 
accuracy 
of 
the
parameter values. It turns out that there is real uncertainty
in about sixty of the parameter values and the model output
is especially sensitive to the value of about nineteen of
these uncertain parameters.
Is it important to know how uncertain the values of these
sensitive parameters are? Suppose we wanted to know only
the expected numbers of hospitalisations and deaths from
COVID. Then we might argue that all we need to do is run
the model with the most likely values of the parameters. As
such, the uncertainty in these parameter values wouldn’t
be judged to be particularly important.
However, this argument is valid only if the system we are
dealing with is fundamentally linear. The emergence of
power-law 
network 
scaling 
suggests 
that 
we 
are 
not
dealing 
with 
linear 
systems. 
Here 
we 
can 
get 
some
guidance from climate-change projections. In Chapter 6 we
saw that if we vary the uncertain subgrid parametrisations
in 
a 
climate 
model, 
the 
global 
warming 
caused 
by 
a
doubling 
of 
carbon 
dioxide 
appears 
to 
have 
a 
rather
asymmetric, skewed probability distribution with a long, fat
tail 
going 
out 
to 
large 
warming 
values 
(and 
no
corresponding tail at low warming values). For such a
distribution, the expected warming—the average over an
ensemble 
of 
models 
with 
diﬀerent 
parametrisations—is
larger than the warming obtained by setting the parameter
values 
at 
their 
most 
likely 
values. 
The 
same 
situation
occurs here.
In 2021, a study
5
was published of ensemble projections
with COVIDSim in which these uncertain parameters were
varied according to plausible estimates of their uncertainty.
These estimates were obtained by what is called “expert
solicitation”, which means asking the experts how much
they felt a parameter value could be in error: 10 percent,
20 percent, 50 percent? Ensembles were then run in which
the parameter values were randomly perturbed by the
experts’ estimates of parameter uncertainty. One of the key
results from the study is shown in Fig. 35. It illustrates
probability distributions of projected COVID deaths for two
diﬀerent policy scenarios.
Interestingly, 
the 
ensemble-based 
probability
distribution of projected deaths, particularly for the ﬁrst
policy scenario, is remarkably similar to the probability
distribution of global warming due to a doubling of carbon
dioxide—a skewed distribution with a long, fat tail going
out to large numbers of deaths.
As with global warming, this means that the expected
number of deaths cannot be reliably estimated using a
model simulation run with the most likely values of the
uncertain parameters. This is shown explicitly in Fig. 35.
The expected number of deaths is higher than the number
predicted using the most likely values of the parameters. In
the 
case 
of 
the 
second, 
more 
laissez-faire 
policy, 
the
estimated 
cumulative 
number 
of 
deaths 
at 
day 
three
hundred using the most likely value of the parameters is
around 25,000. However, the expected number of deaths
based on the full ensemble with perturbed parameters is
signiﬁcantly larger than this, around 40,000.
FIG. 35. 
(Facing Page)
The grey shading denotes estimates of uncertainty
in COVID deaths based on ensembles of Imperial College COVIDSim model
projections. The darker the shading, the more likely is the estimated
number of deaths. The diﬀerence between the top and lower illustrations
reﬂects diﬀerent policy assumptions (e.g., whether social gatherings are
allowed). The thin solid lines show the average of the ensemble
distributions. The dashed lines show the estimated number of COVID
deaths projected by a model with its parameters ﬁxed at their most likely
values. The former is higher than the latter, providing an important reason
to make an ensemble of projections. The squares show real data. The fact
that reality does not lie within the ensemble estimates of uncertainty
suggests that the ensemble does not fully capture the true uncertainty in
these projections (especially initial infections). From Edeling et al. (2021).
Estimates 
of 
the 
expected 
numbers 
of 
deaths 
and
hospitalisations may not be the most relevant information
for policy decisions. Having a health system that essentially
collapses because it is overwhelmed with new patients is
something a government may not wish to risk at all. As
such, 
plausible 
worst-case 
outcomes 
can 
be 
just 
as
important, if not more so, as expected outcomes. This is
similar 
to 
the 
situation 
in 
weather 
forecasting—the
averaged 
forecast 
can 
hide 
the 
possibility 
of 
some
catastrophic weather event. Hence, for example, if we take,
as an estimate of a plausible worst-case scenario, the value
given by the distribution at the edge of the heavier shading
(the region where, according to the ensemble, there is a
two-thirds probability of occurrence), then the number of
deaths rises to 60,000, well over double the prediction with
best-guess parameter values.
However, 
Fig. 
35 
also 
illustrates 
a 
problem. 
As
discussed, an ensemble prediction is useful only if the
probabilities it generates are reliable. For the ensemble
predictions I have helped develop in weather and climate
science, far and away the largest part of research time was
spent 
on 
developing 
initial-condition 
and 
model
perturbations which would produce reliable probabilities.
We can see in Fig. 35 that in the ﬁrst hundred days or so,
the actual numbers of deaths rose much more sharply than
the ensemble suggested could occur. This means that the
ensemble 
probabilities, 
in 
this 
case, 
weren’t 
reliable.
Clearly some other sources of uncertainty are important. In
this case, the missing source of uncertainty is associated
with the date the disease started to grow—in reality it must
have been growing sooner than was thought possible. That
is 
to 
say, 
the 
projections 
used 
unduly 
certain 
initial
conditions. 
This 
result 
is 
consistent 
with 
weather
prediction, 
where 
uncertainty 
in 
the 
forecast 
initial
conditions is typically more important than uncertainty in
the values of model parameters.
However, there is a second type of uncertainty in these
projections 
which 
is 
not 
well 
represented 
by 
simple
parameter perturbations. For example, COVIDSim is based
on 
a 
tiling 
of 
the 
country. 
Are 
the 
assumptions 
of
homogeneity within a tiled region accurate? How small
should the tiles be for this assumption to be correct? Is it
ever 
correct? 
More 
generally, 
there 
may 
be 
many
assumptions 
in 
the 
models 
where 
uncertainty 
goes 
far
beyond simple parameter uncertainty. Such uncertainties
are often referred to as “structural model uncertainties”.
This 
is 
perhaps 
a 
good 
time 
to 
dust 
oﬀ 
the 
much-
repeated saying from Donald Rumsfeld, US secretary of
defense at the time of the invasion of Iraq. Speaking about
reports 
of 
the 
lack 
of 
evidence 
for 
weapons 
of 
mass
destruction, he famously said in 2002
6
:
“Reports that say that something hasn’t happened are
always interesting to me, because as we know, there are
known knowns; there are things we know we know. We also
know there are known unknowns; that is to say we know
there are some things we do not know. But there are also
unknown unknowns—the ones we don’t know we don’t
know. 
And 
if 
one 
looks 
throughout 
the 
history 
of 
our
country and other free countries, it is the latter category
that tends to be the diﬀicult ones.”
In the context of modelling, and this could be COVID
modelling or weather and climate modelling, the known
knowns could refer to a subset of parameter values which
are known to great certainty. In a weather or climate
model, this could be the value of the gravitational constant
or the amount of energy the Earth receives from the sun. In
COVIDSim, this could be some of the demographic data.
Continuing, 
the 
known 
unknowns 
are 
the 
parameters
where we can quantify the range of uncertainty in the
parameter 
values. 
In 
weather 
and 
climate 
models, 
the
values 
of 
some 
of 
the 
parameters 
relating 
to 
the
microphysical properties of clouds are uncertain, but the
range of uncertainty can be quantiﬁed.
In this context, Rumsfeld’s unknown unknowns refer to
structural model uncertainties—to uncertainties in the way
in which the computational equations are formulated in the
ﬁrst place. Here it is not easy to quantify the range of
uncertainty of a parameter value, because the process in
question may not be representable by a ﬁxed parameter
value at all. In a weather or climate model, the structural
form of the parametrisation formulae used to represent
unresolved 
processes 
such 
as 
clouds 
are 
profoundly
uncertain; there is no good reason to suppose that one can
represent clouds accurately with such formulae. Hence,
some of the parameters in such a parametrisation are
ontologically rather than epistemically uncertain, to borrow
from 
the 
philosophical 
language 
used 
in 
Chapter 
4.
Similarly, in COVIDSim, the basic assumptions around the
way in which humans interact with one another, and indeed
respond 
to 
governmental 
restrictions, 
are 
themselves
ontologically uncertain.
If we are to aspire to truly reliable predictions and
projections, we have to ﬁnd some way of handling these
structural model uncertainties. However, they can be some
of 
the 
hardest 
uncertainties 
to 
represent 
and 
quantify.
There’s an old North English saying: “There’s nowt so
queer as folk!” For sure, there’s nowt as uncertain as folk.
In Chapter 3, I discussed the representation of unresolved
scales in models of ﬂuid turbulence using random noise.
Such noise can in principle also provide a representation of
some of the uncertainty associated with structural model
error—even with the uncertainty of folk. Now whilst models
like COVIDSim do have some inherent stochasticity in their
equations, the associated noise processes do not contribute
much 
to 
the 
ensemble 
spread
7
. 
As 
such, 
the 
use 
of
stochasticity as a means to represent inherent structural
uncertainty 
in 
these 
COVID 
models 
is 
likely 
being
underdone. One argument I heard in this regard is that if
we were to use stochasticity to represent structural model
uncertainty, then the spread from the ensembles would be
unusably huge. But of course, that is the whole point of
ensembles! When ensemble spread is truly huge, as it was
for the retrospective ensembles for the weather for October
1987, then the ensembles tell you, on this occasion at least,
not to trust any of the outcomes and to be wary of the
possibility of extreme outcomes. The worst thing, under
such circumstances, is to act on the basis of unreliably
conﬁdent, underspread ensembles.
In 
Chapter 
5 
I 
discussed 
another 
approach 
to 
the
problem 
of 
structural 
model 
error: 
the 
multi-model
ensemble. For seasonal-timescale climate predictions of
ENSO, the multi-model ensemble was more skilful than
were single-model ensembles. On a very pragmatic level,
the rather diﬀicult issue of the ontological uncertainty of
structural 
model 
error 
was 
addressed 
by 
constructing
ensembles using multiple models developed in diﬀerent
institutes. In some ways, this is no diﬀerent to the “wisdom
of the crowd” concept, where here the “crowd” is not the
views of members of the public, but the views of diﬀerently
constructed 
climate 
models 
developed 
in 
diﬀerent
institutes.
A limited form of multi-model ensemble was used by the
UK 
government’s 
Scientiﬁc 
Advisory 
Group 
for
Emergencies when making COVID projections
8
. However,
the multi-model ensemble approach has been developed
more extensively in the United States, where there are
many more individual groups developing COVID models.
Here, the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention
(CDC) partnered with an academic research lab at the
University of Massachusetts Amherst to create the COVID-
19 Forecast Hub, launched in April 2020. Predictions focus
on 
the 
one-to-two-month 
timescale, 
which 
can 
inform
operational 
decisions 
about 
allocation 
of 
healthcare
supplies, staﬀing needs or such matters as school closures.
Essentially, any team in the US was permitted to submit a
forecast model to the multi-model ensemble as long as data
was provided in a speciﬁed format and a full description of
the 
methods 
used 
to 
generate 
a 
forecast 
was 
given.
According to a paper describing this technique
9
, some
twenty-three models had met the eligibility criteria and
were used in the evaluation of the multi-model ensemble.
Separate forecasts were submitted for all ﬁfty states in the
US, and a national forecast for the whole of the US.
The results were entirely in line with what we found for
seasonal 
climate 
prediction. 
Although 
for 
any 
one
particular forecast it was possible to ﬁnd a model that
performed 
better 
than 
the 
ensemble, 
the 
ensemble
performed better on average, both in terms of an ensemble-
averaged prediction and as a probability forecast. Clearly,
consistency in skill is important when making decisions,
indicating that the multi-model ensemble was preferable to
any individual model forecast.
Since the US COVID-19 Forecast Hub went live, a similar
multi-model ensemble system has been implemented in
Europe. There is perhaps one further step to go: a global
multi-model 
ensemble 
forecast 
system 
for 
disease
prediction, to which all nations of the Earth can contribute
their models in a coordinated way. There is a precedent for
this.
In 
the 
mid-1990s, 
the 
World 
Climate 
Research
Programme—an 
international 
body 
of 
climate 
scientists
sponsored by the World Meteorological Organisation and
the International Oceanographic Commission (both United
Nations 
bodies) 
as 
well 
as 
the 
International 
Science
Council began to organise a set of coordinated multi-model
ensemble 
experiments 
that 
would 
feed 
into 
the
Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change assessment
reports. The project which organised these coordinated
experiments became known as CMIP—the Coupled Model
Intercomparison 
Project. 
In 
the 
ﬁrst 
multi-model 
CMIP
experiment, 
climate 
modelling 
groups 
from 
around 
the
world ran experiments with their models with a prescribed
1 percent increase in carbon dioxide concentration per
year. 
In 
the 
latest 
CMIP 
experiment, 
the 
prescribed
increases 
in 
carbon 
dioxide 
are 
based 
on 
diﬀerent
socioeconomic scenarios, ranging from what is sometimes
called “business as usual”, to net-zero by 2050. There are
now some thirty diﬀerent modelling groups contributing to
the CMIP multi-model ensemble.
It 
seems 
to 
me 
that 
there 
is 
a 
need 
for 
global
collaboration like this for disease prediction in general—not
just for COVID but for other diseases such as malaria,
dengue, inﬂuenza and Ebola. Perhaps the World Health
Organization could take the lead.
But there are downsides to the multi-model ensemble.
The “wisdom of the crowd” works only if estimates from
members of the crowd really are independent. For example,
if there is a rumour going round that Fred guessed ﬁve
hundred jellybeans, and that Fred is quite a smart guy, then
the possibility that other people’s guesses are inﬂuenced by
Fred’s estimate can completely negate the beneﬁt of the
ensemble approach to guessing the number of jellybeans in
the jar.
In practice, individual modelling groups 
are
inﬂuenced
by 
other 
groups’ 
assumptions, 
and 
so 
the 
models 
are
typically not independent of one another. They are at best
quasi-independent. 
This 
is 
certainly 
true 
in 
climate
modelling, and I would ﬁnd it hard to believe it is not also
true 
in 
disease 
modelling. 
The 
types 
of 
model
representations 
and 
parametrisations 
that 
diﬀerent
institutes use can be strongly inﬂuenced by one dominant
player 
in 
the 
group. 
That 
is 
to 
say, 
in 
a 
multi-model
ensemble, the models may be less independent of one
another, and hence less useful, than an outsider might
imagine them to be.
There 
is 
another 
problem. 
Each 
modelling 
group 
is
typically 
not 
composed 
of 
a 
team 
of 
one 
hundred
researchers, say. It may be only a half a dozen. Indeed, the
team may comprise just one person. The question then
arises—Which is a better use of resources: funding the
development 
of 
a 
large 
number 
of 
quasi-independent
models, or focussing human and computing resources to
produce a much smaller number of (necessarily stochastic)
models? 
In 
terms 
of 
climate, 
I 
believe 
the 
latter 
is 
a
preferable 
route. 
I 
am 
not 
expert 
enough 
in 
disease
modelling to know which strategy is better. However, it
strikes me as an important topic for discussion.
There is one aspect of these COVID ensembles that
hasn’t been discussed. If we study, say, the one-to-two-
month 
multi-model 
ensembles 
across 
a 
range 
of 
initial
conditions, 
is 
there 
evidence 
that 
the 
spread 
of 
the
ensembles varies from one initial condition to another (as
illustrated in Fig. 10)? If so, can the spread of the ensemble
be used to predict the likely skill of, say, the ensemble-
average 
forecast? 
For 
example, 
if 
the 
spread 
of 
an
ensemble 
is 
especially 
small, 
we 
would 
expect 
each
ensemble member to be close to the truth. By contrast,
when the spread is especially large, many of the ensemble
members will turn out not to be skilful at all. This is only
now starting to be studied. Results will have to await a new
edition of this book.
At the time of writing, COVID-19 isn’t over. The highly
transmissible Omicron variant has spread exponentially.
The 
primary 
uncertainty 
about 
Omicron 
when 
it 
ﬁrst
appeared was its virulence—how many hospitalisations and
deaths would it cause? Fortunately, it seems not to be as
virulent as some of the previous variants. In the UK, some
politicians to the right of centre have complained that
scientists 
have 
somehow 
exaggerated 
the 
Omicron
predictions, giving politicians only worst-case scenarios
10
.
Of course, this is not so. As we have discussed, the job of
the scientists is to provide information about the whole
range 
of 
possible 
future 
hospitalisations 
and 
deaths,
including the expected as well as plausible worst-case and
best-case scenarios. In the earlier phases of development,
when little is known, the predictions are necessarily broad.
As with climate change, it’s up to the politicians to decide
on policy implications given such forecast information—
further lockdowns, lighter measures to try to restrict social
interactions or a complete relaxation of such measures. Of
course, it isn’t easy to decide when predictions are highly
uncertain. 
However, 
making 
politicians’ 
lives 
easier 
by
giving them forecasts which are more precise, but less
reliable, is not the right way to go—though more precise
forecasts would provide politicians with a scapegoat (the
scientists) if policies turn out badly.
In the meantime it seems we need a new generation of
ensemble systems which, coupled to these SIR models, can
probabilistically 
predict 
the 
spread 
of 
COVID 
virus
mutations. Some ﬁrst steps in this direction have already
been taken with models trained using the many millions of
genome sequences of COVID viruses (mutations of the
SARS-CoV-2 virus to be more speciﬁc). In one paper
11
, the
authors develop a model for predicting virus mutations that
will spread across the population for diﬀerent phases of the
pandemic. The key information for making such predictions
is 
an 
analysis 
of 
the 
recent 
patterns 
of 
spread 
of 
a
particular 
mutation/variant, 
and 
whether 
particular
mutations appear in diﬀerent viral subtypes (a criterion
called “convergence” in biology). Based on observations
outside the training data, the authors show that the model
has predictive power out to about four months.
Ultimately, one wonders whether it might be possible to
predict the spread of virulent virus mutations from ﬁrst
principles, i.e., directly from the basic genomic structure of
the virus. However, there are so many potential mutations
to consider that predicting viral evolution in a general way
would 
be 
completely 
intractable 
with 
present-day
computational technology. On the other hand, this is the
sort of problem that might conceivably be solvable with
some 
future-generation 
quantum 
computing 
technology,
discussed later in the book.
...
8
Financial Crashes
A
CCORDING TO ANDY HALDANE IN 2017, THEN THE BANK
OF 
England’s 
chief 
economist, 
economists 
had 
their
“Michael Fish moment” when the global ﬁnancial markets
collapsed without warning in 2008
1
. The economic models
were simply unable to function properly when “the world
was tipped upside down”.
When 
I 
read 
Haldane’s 
comments, 
his 
reference 
to
Michael 
Fish 
brought 
to 
mind 
something 
that 
I 
had
wondered from time to time: whether the same ensemble
techniques 
that 
have 
transformed 
weather 
and 
climate
prediction could also play a role in economic prediction. If
it was possible to create a reliable ensemble prediction
system, 
would 
the 
2008 
crash 
turn 
out 
to 
have 
been
something inevitable, insensitive to the precise economic
starting 
conditions 
and 
economic 
model 
equations, 
or
would it look like the actual Michael Fish storm, something
highly 
unpredictable 
and 
only 
forecastable 
in 
a
probabilistic 
sense? 
Even 
if 
the 
latter, 
a 
probabilistic
ensemble 
prediction 
would 
still 
be 
useful. 
Just 
as
forecasting the October 1987 storm in a probabilistic way
is 
enough 
to 
make 
simple 
planning 
decisions, 
so 
too
presumably 
if 
an 
imminent 
global 
ﬁnancial 
crash 
is
predictable only probabilistically.
However, 
responses 
from 
economists 
about 
the
possibility of creating such an ensemble forecast system
were 
mostly 
disappointing. 
There 
were 
a 
number 
of
diﬀerent reactions, all of which were broadly negative in
one 
way 
or 
another. 
The 
ﬁrst 
response 
was 
that 
in
economics, more than in weather and climate prediction,
there is an element of uncertainty that is itself unknowable.
This is often called “radical uncertainty”
2
. It’s related to
the type of ontological uncertainty discussed in Chapter 7.
For example, no one could have predicted the 9/11 attack
on the Twin Towers, and yet this had a major impact on the
state of the world, including its economies, resonating to
the present day. The view was that such radical uncertainty
would make the prediction of ﬁnancial crashes impossible.
Another point raised by the economists was that whilst
weather 
follows 
along 
the 
“tramlines” 
provided 
by 
the
Navier-Stokes equation, there is no known equation which
governs the state of the economy. Economists are in the
dark, so the argument went, about the equations which
govern ﬁnancial shocks and crises.
Then 
there 
was 
the 
“self-referential” 
argument. 
A
weather forecast doesn’t change the weather, whilst an
economic forecast can change the economy. Hence the two
systems are completely diﬀerent from each other and not
much can be learned comparing one with the other. Finally,
I met economists who, although somewhat sympathetic to
what I was suggesting, said that economists had not yet
developed 
techniques 
to 
assimilate 
economic 
data 
into
models with the same mathematical sophistication that
meteorologists use to assimilate weather observations into
their models.
Dispirited, I let the idea drop. But in planning this book,
I decided to revisit these issues. Perhaps things had moved
on. Alas, writing to some economists I knew, I got pretty
much the same responses as the ﬁrst time around. Are they
right? 
Is 
the 
idea 
of 
creating 
some 
kind 
of 
ensemble
prediction 
system 
for 
predicting 
economic 
shocks 
a
complete non-starter? Of course, as an outsider in this
ﬁeld, it would be arrogant of me to claim that the experts
are categorically wrong. However, I was not convinced by
their 
objections. 
For 
example, 
radical 
ontological
uncertainty 
does 
certainly 
play 
a 
role 
in 
weather 
and
climate prediction. Indeed, I might make a probabilistic
seasonal forecast for six months from now, only for it to be
completely 
undone 
by 
an 
unpredicted 
volcano 
whose
aerosols spread around the atmosphere and dim the sun’s
radiation. In principle, one could include a volcano in one
or two ensemble members. However, that is not what we
do. We just accept that, occasionally, even probabilistic
forecasts 
can 
go 
wrong 
as 
a 
result 
of 
the 
radical
uncertainty of a volcano. I don’t think that makes the
probabilistic El Niño forecasts any the less useful.
However, as discussed in Chapter 3, we do include a
certain 
amount 
of 
radical 
uncertainty 
in 
our 
weather
forecast models by introducing noise into the truncated
version of the Navier-Stokes equation. It really isn’t the
case that the Navier-Stokes equation provides hard-and-
fast tramlines on which the forecasts evolve. Now it may be
that to model the radical uncertainties that exist when
modelling the economy, the amplitude of the stochastic
noise 
would 
have 
to 
be 
much 
larger 
than 
it 
is 
when
modelling the weather. If realistic amounts of noise mean
that the ensembles are always so highly spread that no
useful signal can be extracted, then so be it. However, as
with forecasts of weather, it may be that sometimes the
spread 
isn’t 
that 
large, 
meaning 
that 
the 
state 
of 
the
economy 
is 
able 
to 
absorb 
the 
inherent 
radical
uncertainties that exist. On other occasions, the ensembles
may tell us that the state of the economy is like the
kingdom 
whose 
existence 
is 
so 
fragile 
that 
it 
can 
be
compromised by a missing horseshoe nail.
Regarding the self-referential issue, although it is the
case that weather forecasts don’t change the weather, it
certainly is the case that climate forecasts can change the
climate, as discussed in Chapters 6 and 7. For many years,
climate scientists have known that they are not really
making 
predictions
of climate change but 
projections
; they
are “merely” estimating how the climate will respond to
certain emissions scenarios for greenhouse gases. These
projections can be veriﬁed once the emissions scenarios
are known. In this sense, the diﬀerence between climate
and 
economic 
projections 
need 
not 
necessarily 
be
fundamental.
Economists 
do 
in 
fact 
make 
eﬀorts 
to 
present 
their
projections, such as the inﬂation rate or the gross domestic
product, with estimates of uncertainty. For example, the
Bank of England’s projections are in the form of what are
known as “fan charts”
3
. These essentially give error bars
on 
predictions 
and 
were 
inspired 
by 
discussions 
with
meteorologists back in the 1990s. They are called fan
charts because the uncertainties get larger, as one would
expect, for longer forecast times. However, these fan charts
are not themselves based on ensembles. Instead, they are
based on a statistical analysis of errors from past forecasts.
However, there are times when subjective judgements are
added to widen or narrow the fan charts from one forecast
to the next, based on whether the outlook is deemed to be
more or less uncertain.
This was the key question for me. As a nonlinear system,
the predictability of the economy should itself be variable,
perhaps similar to what was shown in Fig. 10 for the
Lorenz model. Instead of thinking of the two lobes of the
Lorenz system as, say, fair weather and foul weather, let’s
imagine them as representing a vibrant economy and a
depressed 
economy. 
Fig. 
10 
showed 
that 
there 
were
situations where the transition from one lobe to the other
was quite predictable, and hence rather inevitable, and yet
others where it was very unpredictable, and hence not at
all 
inevitable. 
Is 
there 
a 
way 
to 
characterise 
the
predictability of the economic system under capitalism as it
moves between boom and bust in this way?
