FIG. 36. A Bank of England “fan chart” predicting the rate of inﬂation (from
November 2019). The shaded regions denote the likelihood that the true
rate of inﬂation lies within certain values. The heavier the shading, the
more likely lies the true rate of inﬂation. However, these fan charts are
based not on ensemble forecasts but on a statistical analysis of past error,
together with subjective judgements about future uncertainty. From the
Bank of England Monetary Policy Report, November 2021. With permission
from the Bank of England.
To try to quantify such predictability, we need good
models of the economy. What is a “good” economic model?
Based on discussions with economists, the authors of a
paper entitled “The Seven Properties of Good [Economic]
Models
4
” 
identify 
goodness 
in 
terms 
of 
parsimony,
tractability, 
conceptual 
insightfulness, 
generalisability,
falsiﬁability, empirical consistency and predictive precision.
The authors then go on to comment that only the ﬁrst four
are well accepted by economists, the last three not so.
This struck me as very odd. If I were to ask a group of
my meteorologist colleagues to characterise the principal
diﬀerence between a good weather forecast model and a
bad weather forecast model, the answer would hardly be
surprising: a good weather forecast model makes accurate
weather forecasts and a bad one makes inaccurate weather
forecasts. There may be some discussion about how one
might 
measure 
the 
accuracy 
or 
inaccuracy 
of 
weather
forecasts (e.g., how much emphasis one should give to
forecasting 
extreme 
weather 
situations 
rather 
than
common or garden situations) but that is a matter of detail.
In other words, the properties of falsiﬁability, empirical
consistency 
and 
predictive 
precision, 
which 
are 
at 
the
bottom of these economists’ list, would be at the top of the
list for meteorologists, and vice versa. Indeed, I have never
heard 
a 
meteorologist 
use 
the 
word 
“parsimony” 
as 
a
desirable trait for a weather forecast model. This does not
mean 
they 
put 
processes 
into 
their 
models 
that 
are
irrelevant, like the weather on Jupiter. However, there is no
conscious eﬀort to pare things down to the bare minimum
—rather the philosophy is to add as much detail into the
model as the computer resources allow.
However, 
there 
are 
circumstances 
where 
parsimony,
tractability 
and 
conceptual 
insightfulness 
are
important
attributes 
of 
meteorological 
models. 
In 
their 
research,
meteorologists develop hierarchies of models. At one end of
the hierarchy, there are the models which are good at
forecasting 
the 
weather. 
Because 
these 
models 
are 
so
complex, with millions upon millions of lines of code, and
billions of individual variables, they are not at all tractable
and are diﬀicult to generalise, the things that economists
hold dear. However, at the other end of the hierarchy are
the 
simple 
conceptual 
models, 
like 
Lorenz’s 
prototype
model 
of 
chaos. 
The 
conceptual 
models 
are 
better 
for
understanding why the weather is the way it is, but they
are typically useless at forecasting it.
It was a simple conceptual model of the atmosphere that
drew me into a career in meteorology. It was based on the
assumption 
that 
the 
behaviour 
of 
weather 
could 
be
understood 
using 
the 
so-called 
principle 
of 
maximum
entropy production, an idea drawn from the physics of
thermodynamic systems (like steam engines). This principle
can’t be rigorously shown to apply to large-scale weather
systems. But it is an interesting concept, and it’s certainly
interesting to explore what properties of the weather can
be 
derived 
from 
it. 
From 
this 
perspective, 
midlatitude
weather systems can be thought of as nature’s way of
transporting heat from hot tropical latitudes to colder polar
latitudes as eﬀiciently as possible on a rotating planet.
Such a model predicts how the temperature of the Earth,
as well as more complex variables like mean cloud cover
and poleward ﬂuxes of heat, varies between the equator
and the poles on average.
These simple models certainly are parsimonious, in the
sense that they are stripped to the bone of irrelevant
complexity, and can sometimes be solved with pencil and
paper, 
or 
at 
worst 
with 
a 
laptop 
computer. 
They 
are
certainly 
tractable 
and 
conceptually 
insightful. 
During
periods 
when 
the 
weather 
seems 
relentlessly 
wet 
and
windy, 
it 
is 
somehow 
reassuring 
to 
know 
that 
the
atmosphere is being a ruthlessly eﬀicient thermodynamic
machine—if it weren’t like this, then lower latitude regions
would become unbearably hot, and higher latitude regions
unbearably 
cold. 
So, 
the 
rainy, 
stormy 
weather 
we
frequently experience here in the UK is at least making life
more equable for others on the planet, and that provides
some modicum of comfort when the weather is especially
bleak!
On the other hand, it is important to understand the
limitations 
of 
these 
parsimonious 
conceptual 
weather
models. They are useless for actually predicting weather.
And they are utterly useless in predicting the sorts of
extreme weather that occurred over southern England in
October 1987. I am not aware of any way that the principle
of maximum entropy production could have been used to
predict the October storm.
What conclusion can we draw from this? Could it be that
the economists are trying to have their cake and eat it too?
They 
can 
have 
highly 
parsimonious 
models, 
good 
for
understanding how the economy works in general terms, or
they 
can 
have 
predictive 
models, 
good 
for 
actually
forecasting the economy. However, perhaps one size does
not ﬁt all.
So, 
what 
are 
these 
economic 
models 
and 
why 
did
Haldane 
paint 
such 
a 
bleak 
picture 
of 
their 
ability 
to
predict 
the 
2008 
crash? 
Well, 
it 
seems 
there 
are 
two
traditional types of models.
The 
ﬁrst 
are 
so-called 
econometric 
models. 
An
econometric model is really the same type of statistical-
empirical analogue model which Ed Lorenz’s statistical
colleagues said could be used to predict the weather weeks
or months ahead. That is to say, an econometric model
predicts the future based on patterns it ﬁnds in past data.
Essentially, we try to ﬁnd an analogue of the current state
of the economy and base our prediction on what happened
in the months and seasons following the analogue state.
These days, one might use sophisticated AI to do the
forecasting, 
but 
the 
underpinning 
methodology 
is
essentially the same.
Lorenz had shown that this method doesn’t work for a
nonlinear system like the weather. As such, perhaps it’s not
surprising that it doesn’t work well for a nonlinear system
like the economy, especially during periods of extreme
volatility.
The second traditional type of economic model is based
on what is called neoclassical economics. The fundamental
idea 
in 
neoclassical 
economics 
is 
the 
notion 
of 
an
equilibrium arising from an agent maximisation problem. A
simple 
example 
arises 
in 
determining 
the 
price 
of
something like a car. The more expensively a car is priced,
the more proﬁt the seller can potentially make. However,
the more expensive the car, the less likely a potential buyer
will want to buy it. An “equilibrium” is reached when the
seller’s and buyer’s desires (to make as much proﬁt as
possible and to buy as cheaply as possible) are jointly
optimised.
The value of something is encoded into a concept called
a 
utility 
function. 
This 
originated 
in 
the 
theory 
of
utilitarianism 
by 
nineteenth-century 
moral 
philosophers
Jeremy 
Bentham 
and 
John 
Stuart 
Mill 
as 
a 
way 
of
measuring pleasure or happiness. In this way, the utility
function can be used to discriminate quantitatively between
diﬀerent possible choices. For example, I typically get more
pleasure eating chocolate ice cream than, say, strawberry-
ﬂavoured, and would in principle pay (a little) more for a
chocolate ice cream than another ﬂavour.
Neoclassical 
economists 
treat 
agents 
as 
idealised
rational 
beings 
who 
make 
decisions 
to 
maximise 
some
relevant utility function. In the neoclassical framework, an
agent 
is 
really 
an 
idealised 
representative 
of 
a 
set 
of
individuals or businesses. As the idealised agent behaves,
so, it is assumed, the set of individuals represented by the
agent 
behaves. 
However, 
we 
have 
seen 
(e.g., 
in 
both
climate 
and 
pandemic 
prediction) 
that 
in 
a 
nonlinear
system, this assumption can break down. The prediction
made by a “representative” prediction model with its most
likely set of model parameters can diﬀer greatly from the
expected prediction when we vary the model parameters
according to their uncertainties.
Why do economies crash, as they sometimes do? The
standard neoclassical view is that it is because of factors
which are external to this world of utility maximisation.
This can be in the form of a shock—some kind of noisy
input from the outside which would make the system non-
deterministic—or some “externality”, an outside factor that
the capitalist system of price setting does not take into
account. 
For 
example, 
environmental 
pollution 
or
degradation is traditionally considered an externality in
neoclassical economics (despite the fact that it is us, the
agents, who cause it in the ﬁrst place). One of the mantras
of environmental economics is the need to internalise the
externalities—the 
price 
of 
goods 
should 
reﬂect 
their
damage to the environment, e.g., in shipping them from
one part of the world to another.
The more I thought about it, the more the “maximisation
of utility” philosophy of neoclassical economics sounded
like 
the 
“maximisation 
of 
entropy 
production” 
used 
in
meteorology to understand why weather behaves the way it
does. In both cases, we are taking a principle which has
some relevance under certain speciﬁc circumstances, and
applying it at a scale where it no longer rigorously holds.
For example, the principle of maximum entropy production
holds for systems which are close to being in what’s called
thermodynamic equilibrium. However, the climate system
is not like this—the energy the atmosphere receives from
the sun every day means it is far from thermodynamic
equilibrium. In a similar way, the idea that we can model
the economy based on the actions of representative agents
who are maximising some speciﬁc utility function may not
be a good assumption for a highly heterogeneous nonlinear
system in which the individuals may be acting according to
some individually tailored optimisation principles. In both
cases we can form some understanding of the systems of
interest—the weather and the economy, respectively—from
these heuristic optimisation principles, but equally it is
important to understand their shortcomings when making
actual predictions of systems far from equilibrium.
Pursuing this analogy, and given that we do not use the
principle of maximum entropy production to forecast the
weather, is there an alternative to the principle of utility
maximisation 
by 
representative 
agents 
to 
forecast 
the
economy? In Chapter 7 I discussed how we can model the
spread of disease using networks in which nodes represent
individuals. Could such a framework also be relevant in
economic modelling? Indeed, it seems it can. In 2009, the
year after the crash, economists Doyne Farmer and Duncan
Foley published an inﬂuential paper in 
Nature
explaining
why 
neoclassical 
economic 
models 
were 
fatally 
ﬂawed
when it came to predicting the crash. In the paper they
claimed that so-called agent-based models will stand a
much better chance of predicting the next big crash than
neoclassical models ever could.
I knew Farmer’s name when I ﬁrst read this paper, as he
features 
prominently 
in 
James 
Gleick’s 
masterful 
1988
book,
5

Chaos: 
Making 
a 
New 
Science
, 
the 
book 
that
brought Ed Lorenz’s work to the public’s attention. In
Gleick’s book, Farmer was portrayed as one of the new
generation of “complexity theorists”—scientists steeped in
the concepts and methods of chaos theory. In his youth,
Farmer perfected a way of beating the casinos at roulette
by embedding a small computer in the sole of his shoe. The
computer was fed with data on the position of the roulette
ball as it went around the wheel, in the form of taps of the
foot. From this, the computer would calculate where the
roulette ball would ﬁnally land. Farmer had great success
until the casinos discovered what was going on and banned
him 
from 
entering 
again! 
Farmer 
went 
on 
to 
found
Prediction Company and made a small fortune. He now
uses 
his 
expertise 
in 
complexity 
theory 
to 
understand
macroeconomic 
cycles 
and 
crashes; 
he 
is 
director 
of
complexity economics at the Institute for New Economic
Thinking at the University of Oxford, where he is now a
close colleague and friend of mine.
Farmer has remarked on the sociology of mathematical
sophistication in economics
6
. He makes the point that if
your model isn’t based on the standard mantra—about
representative agents maximising their utility functions—
all wrapped up in elegant mathematics, then you won’t get
your paper published in one of the top economics journals.
If you are a young academic seeking tenure, you will be at
a disadvantage if your paper deviates from this mantra.
When I ﬁrst read this, I have to confess, I wondered
whether 
there 
was 
an 
element 
of 
hyperbole 
in 
this
statement, or whether, if it was once true, it is no longer so.
But it seems there is some truth in Farmer’s views today, as
we shall see.
In an agent-based model, an agent represents an actual
economic unit. It could be an actual individual, or it could
be an actual ﬁrm or a bank. In an agent-based model, one
tries to represent as many of these microeconomic agents
as 
possible—thousands, 
millions 
(or 
potentially 
billions,
which we’ll see ahead). Agents are perceived as acting as a
unit from the outside. They have an ability to act and to
react to external stimuli, and they can interact with each
other. 
A 
key 
question 
is 
what 
sort 
of 
macroeconomic
behaviour is emergent when studying such large numbers
of 
microeconomic 
agents. 
An 
example 
of 
emergent
behaviour—though nothing directly to do with economics—
can be seen in studying the ﬂow of traﬀic on a road
7
.
Suppose we think of each car or truck as an agent who
merely responds to the agent in front. When the density of
vehicles is small, the traﬀic ﬂows well. However, as the
density increases, we start to see the familiar shock waves
as the traﬀic becomes congested. These shock waves are
examples of macro-scale emergent behaviour.
In fact, a relatively simple agent-based model may be
able to describe some of the essentials of the 2008 crash
8
.
In this model, there are three types of agents. The ﬁrst are
described 
as 
“noise 
traders”. 
They 
more 
or 
less 
trade
stocks and shares at random. However, they believe that
these shares have some intrinsic fundamental value. The
second type of agent is a hedge fund. It holds stocks which
the 
hedge 
fund 
managers 
believe 
to 
be 
underpriced.
Otherwise, they hold cash. The third type of agent is the
bank that can lend money to the hedge fund, allowing them
to buy more stock.
Under 
normal 
circumstances, 
the 
presence 
of 
hedge
funds dampens volatility in the value of the stocks, since
hedge 
fund 
trading 
should 
move 
the 
price 
of 
a 
stock
towards its intrinsic worth. However, there is a problem. To
contain their risk, the banks will limit the amount of money
that they will lend to a hedge fund. In the jargon of ﬁnance,
the banks cap leverage at a predetermined maximum value,
determined by the wealth of the hedge fund. A problem
then arises if a stock’s value falls when a hedge fund is fully
leveraged. This can take the amount borrowed beyond the
predetermined cap. The hedge fund will have to sell oﬀ its
stock to pay oﬀ part of the loan, to bring it back to the
predetermined maximum value. But selling oﬀ the stock in
this way can cause the value of the stock to fall further. In
extremis, this can lead to a death spiral that it is impossible
to get out of, leading to disaster for both the hedge fund
and potentially the bank too. Of course, this is a very
simple example of an agent-based model. However, it can
relatively easily be extended, making it more realistic. For
example, the banks can themselves borrow money and be
limited by leverage caps.
Well, we seem to be getting somewhere at last. Is it
possible 
to 
perform 
a 
retrospective 
agent-based 
model
ensemble forecast of the 2008 ﬁnancial crash, much like
the retrospective ensemble forecast we had performed of
the October 1987 storm? Would it show exceptional spread,
like the bottom example in Fig. 10? Or would the crash be
shown to have been very predictable, like the top left
example 
in 
Fig. 
10—an 
inevitable 
consequence 
of 
the
exceptionally leveraged positions that many individuals,
hedge 
funds 
and 
banks 
had 
taken 
over 
subprime
mortgages?
I approached several agent-based modellers around the
world and was again disappointed by the responses. It
seemed my idea wasn’t intrinsically silly, it was just that the
agent-based models weren’t suﬀiciently sophisticated to
answer 
such 
questions. 
However, 
unexpectedly, 
I 
was
emailed by a postdoctoral researcher, Juan Sabuco, at my
own university, Oxford, who said that he was using an
agent-based model and would like to develop it to have
ensemble capability. Would I like to work with him to
explore this idea? And so, I began a small piece of research
with Juan.
In Sabuco’s highly idealised agent-based model, we can
think of the agents, of which there can be as many as
100,000, 
as 
divided 
into 
two: 
ﬁrms 
and 
unemployed
workers. The agents move randomly in a two-dimensional
space that simulates a virtual job market. Every “time step”
(which in this model has no speciﬁc linkage to real time),
the workers move around looking for a job opportunity.
Firms also move around in this marketplace looking for
new workers. When a ﬁrm hires workers, these workers are
removed from the unemployed pool of workers. Now ﬁrms
lose staﬀ for any number of reasons, and so for a particular
ﬁrm to be viable, it must be able to attract new labour. If it
doesn’t attract new labour over a predeﬁned number of
time steps, it will go out of business. In addition, it is
assumed that unemployed workers appear every time step,
either 
due 
to 
workers 
being 
ﬁred 
or 
because 
of 
new
workers 
entering 
the 
market. 
These 
new 
workers 
are
placed randomly in the marketplace, but close to the other
unemployed workers already present in the simulation. It is
also assumed that new ﬁrms are created in the market
every time step. We also assume that the newly created
ﬁrms are placed in regions close to other existing ﬁrms
9
.
Fig. 37 shows a long run of Sabuco’s agent-based model
over a quarter of a million time steps. Shown on the
vertical axis is the employment rate of the workers, with
100 
percent 
representing 
full 
employment. 
The 
agent-
based model shows considerable internal variability in this
employment 
rate 
with 
a 
number 
of 
“crashes” 
in
employment 
rate. 
Economists 
would 
call 
such 
internal
variations “endogenous”, to contrast with externally forced
variations, which would be called “exogenous”. We will call
this the “truth” run, i.e., we will assume this is what
actually happens in Sabuco’s agent-based model world. The
question 
is 
whether 
we 
can 
predict 
the 
endogenous
ﬂuctuations with ensemble forecasts.
Like 
weather 
prediction 
models 
and 
COVID 
models,
Sabuco’s 
agent-based 
model 
has 
a 
number 
of 
free
parameters, 
some 
of 
which 
are 
represented 
using
stochastic formulae (hence involving random numbers). It
is straightforward to generate large ensembles by using
diﬀerent 
draws 
from 
the 
pseudorandom-number
generators. 
Fig. 
38 
shows 
some 
results 
from 
these
ensembles. 
The 
top 
left 
ﬁgure 
shows 
an 
economically
healthy 
situation 
where, 
according 
to 
the 
truth 
run,
employment rates are stable and stay high throughout the
forecast period. Some ensemble members predict drops in
employment, but the bulk of the ensemble suggests that
this will be a period of stability. The top right ﬁgure shows
a situation where, later in the forecast period, the truth run
does show a dip in employment. Some of the ensemble
members predict this (i.e., the ensemble predicts the dip
with some non-zero probability), but most do not—at this
range, the dip in employment is not really predictable,
according to the ensemble. The bottom two ﬁgures show
situations where there is an employment crash relatively
early on in the forecast, where one would hope for some
predictability. 
One 
crash 
(bottom 
left) 
appears 
very
predictable according to the ensemble, whilst the other
(bottom right) appears less so. If one tried to make a single
best-guess deterministic forecast for this last situation, it
seems quite likely that the forecast would underestimate
the true drop in employment (and indeed predict a faster
recovery than actually occurred). In this case the ensemble
provides an alert of the chance of a deeper and longer-
lasting crash. If this were to occur, then with the aid of the
ensemble, 
the 
crash 
would 
not 
come 
as 
a 
complete
surprise.
FIG. 37. Output from a long truth run of Sabuco’s agent-based model. In
the next ﬁgure, ensemble forecasts are made from the four points marked
in the ﬁgure.
This 
was 
all 
quite 
encouraging. 
However, 
we 
were
looking at very idealised situations.
And then, when I had all but given up on real agent-
based model forecasts, Doyne Farmer pointed me to a new
paper in which a group of economists led by Sebastian
Poledna from Austria had used an agent-based model to
make retrospective predictions of the Austrian economy
10
.
They had developed a simple data assimilation system for
inputting the observed economic data into their model.
There is nothing special about Austria except that two of
the authors’ home institutes are based there. The authors
say that their model could be applied to make predictions
for the UK or the US, for example.
A 
pure 
mathematician 
looking 
at 
the 
equations 
in
Poledna’s 
paper 
would 
not 
ﬁnd 
the 
mathematics 
very
endearing. It’s engineering mathematics. It’s the sort of
mathematics used in comprehensive climate models. But
precisely because of this, I personally found myself drawn
to the paper. It seemed to me to be exactly the type of
inelegant mathematics you need to represent the messy
realities 
of 
the 
real-world 
economy. 
In 
Poledna’s
predictions, the agent-based model is run ﬁve hundred
times in ensemble mode, varying uncertain aspects of the
initial conditions and sampling the pseudorandom-number
generators 
that 
drove 
the 
stochastic 
agent-to-agent
interactions in the model.
The 
proof 
of 
the 
pudding 
is 
in 
the 
eating, 
and 
the
authors put their model to the test with some retrospective
“out 
of 
sample” 
predictions. 
This 
means 
that 
the
predictions were for years which weren’t used to set the
values 
of 
parameters. 
The 
authors 
report 
that 
the
ensemble-mean forecasts are better than those from both
econometric 
models 
and 
the 
neoclassical 
equilibrium
models.
I was excited reading this paper. I emailed Poledna and
asked if he had looked at retrospective forecasts for 2008.
He answered that he had literally just produced some
results for 2008, but hadn’t started to write up the results.
This was too good to be true! I had to speak to him. We
arranged to chat over the internet.
Fig. 39 shows the spread in retrospective forecasts of
Eurozone gross domestic product (GDP) growth in two ﬁve-
hundred-member 
ensembles 
from 
Poledna’s 
model. 
The
ﬁrst is for a normal year. The second is for the ﬁnancial
crash year of 2008. The most likely predictions don’t really
show any diﬀerences between the two years. However, the
spread of the ensemble in 2008 is substantially larger and
the 
tail 
of 
the 
distribution 
extends 
to 
values 
twice 
as
negative 
as 
from 
the 
normal 
year. 
Like 
the 
ensemble
predictions of climate change and of COVID, the ensemble
distribution 
is 
skewed 
towards 
the 
dangerous 
end.
Nonlinearity is at work again.
FIG. 38. Ensemble “forecasts” from four diﬀerent initial states chosen from
the truth run in Fig. 37. The darker line itself shows the truth run.
Impressive as the results were, the ensemble for 2008
did not actually capture the observed downturn for one
quarter, which was around −0.03. I suggested to Poledna
that it was because the ensemble was not large enough;
perhaps 
he 
should 
try 
running 
ﬁve-thousand-member
ensembles 
instead 
of 
ﬁve-hundred-member 
ensembles.
However, his explanation was more plausible. He did not
feed into his model the economic downturn from the world
outside 
the 
Eurozone. 
He 
did 
not 
do 
this 
because 
he
wanted to see what would happen from purely endogenous
dynamical processes.
But this raises an extremely important question. Since
the world is, of course, globally connected, we really need
to extend these agent-based models to cover the whole
world. How many individual agents can an agent-based
model handle? Indeed, Poledna has been involved in the
development of an agent-based economic model with 331
million individual agents
11
, implemented and run eﬀiciently
on a supercomputer. Is that the limit? Certainly not. As
discussed, a modern weather forecast model has several
billion variables, about the same number as there are
people on the planet. It is therefore perhaps not completely
ridiculous to imagine an agent-based model with one agent
per human on the planet. I look forward to seeing some
ensemble results for 2008 when the agent-based model is
truly global in extent.
Sadly, 
Poledna 
conﬁrmed 
Farmer’s 
conclusions
concerning barriers to progress in economics. He believed
it would be impossible to get his paper considered by the
top economics journals. Indeed, he said that traditional
economics colleagues had largely ignored his paper. He
was not at all optimistic that his work in this area would
help him get a tenure-track position in a top economics
department.
Just as ensemble weather prediction means we should
never 
again 
get 
these 
Michael 
Fish 
weather-forecast
moments, so perhaps once these ensemble-based agent-
based 
prediction 
models 
and 
their 
associated 
data
assimilation schemes have been developed on the global
scale, we will no longer have the Andy Haldane/Michael
Fish economic-forecast moments either
12
.
FIG. 39. Histograms of predicted quarterly growth in Eurozone gross
domestic product for a “normal” year and for 2008, containing the ﬁnancial
crash.
Or is this wishful thinking? As I complete this book,
inﬂationary pressures are increasing strongly and there is a
danger of a global economic recession. Was this predictable
a year ago? These economic impacts can be linked in part
to the fact that Russian forces have been shelling Ukrainian
cities, something most of us would hardly have believed a
year 
ago. 
These 
actions 
have 
led 
the 
West 
to 
impose
signiﬁcant sanctions on Russia, pushing up (already high)
energy prices and depressing stock markets worldwide.
Hence 
the 
answer 
to 
the 
question 
above 
depends 
on
whether conﬂicts are predictable. We will look at this in the
next chapter.
Indeed, such events make it clear that the development
of 
ensemble 
economic 
systems 
based 
on 
agent-based
models are really just the tip of the iceberg. We need to
develop ensemble systems in which human and physical
systems (such as the climate system) are fully integrated on
a global scale. Welcome to the digital twin of planet Earth,
another topic for the next chapter.
...
9
Deadly Conﬂict and the Digital
Ensemble of Spaceship Earth
W
HO’S TO SAY WHY GENERAL OSKAR POTIOREK, AUSTRO-
HUNGARIAN governor of Bosnia and Herzegovina, didn’t
tell the driver that there had been a change to the planned
route 
for 
the 
archduke’s 
car. 
There 
had 
been 
an
assassination attempt on Franz Ferdinand earlier in the
day, 
and 
changing 
the 
route 
seemed 
like 
a 
sensible
precautionary step. Perhaps Potiorek was about to tell the
driver, 
but 
was 
momentarily 
distracted 
by 
a 
butterﬂy
passing in front of him—we’ll never know.
But in any case, he didn’t tell the driver, and he and the
car’s 
occupants, 
including 
Potiorek, 
Archduke 
Franz
Ferdinand and his wife, turned right near the Latin Bridge
in Sarajevo. Realising his mistake, Potiorek ﬁnally shouted
to the driver to stop and reverse. In so doing, the car
stalled. Gavrilo Princip, still a teenager, thought he had
missed his chance to assassinate the archduke earlier that
day. And then, to his amazement, the stalled car was right
in front of him. Princip shot the archduke dead.
If only the butterﬂy had not passed by, the archduke
might not have been killed. And perhaps the First World
War would not have happened. And perhaps the Second
World War would not have happened either. That is, if it’s
permissible to argue in this counterfactual way.
This 
is 
a 
real-world 
example 
of 
the 
parable 
of 
the
missing horseshoe nail. Because of a ﬂapping butterﬂy
(perhaps), the driver was not told to change his route.
Because the driver was not told to change his route, the
archduke 
was 
assassinated. 
Because 
the 
archduke 
was
assassinated, 
Austria-Hungary 
declared 
war 
on 
Serbia.
Because of this, Russia, an ally of Serbia, mobilised its
army against Austria-Hungary. Because of this, Germany
declared 
support 
for 
Austria-Hungary 
and 
mobilised
against Russia and its ally France. Because of this, and as a
pre-emptive 
strike, 
Germany 
attacked 
France 
through
neutral Belgian territory. Because Britain had a treaty to
defend 
Belgium, 
it 
went 
to 
war 
against 
Germany 
and
Austria-Hungary. Because of this, the United States in 1917
joined the war, and Germany and Austria-Hungary (and the
Ottoman Empire, which had joined the war on the Austro-
Hungarian side) lost their empires—all for the want of a
horseshoe nail. Indeed, Britain came out of the war so
much poorer that this marked the beginning of the end for
its empire too.
However, 
as 
many 
historians 
have 
written 
over 
the
years, the fact that the countries of Europe were bound by
these 
various 
treaties 
and 
alliances, 
and 
that 
military
spending was generally on the increase, meant that the
transition to war was in fact quite predictable. Perhaps if
the 
archduke 
had 
not 
been 
assassinated, 
some 
other
relatively minor incident could have triggered a similar
sequence of events. Like with weather, pandemics and the
economy, 
it 
seems 
that 
the 
dynamics 
of 
conﬂict 
are
similarly nonlinear, varying from periods when its evolution
is 
stable 
to 
small 
perturbations, 
to 
periods 
when 
its
evolution is highly unstable.
Can we think about world wars in terms of the geometry
of chaos as shown in Fig. 10? Let’s now imagine that the
left-hand lobe of the Lorenz attractor corresponds to states
of the world where the nations are at peace with one
another, and the right-hand lobe corresponds to states of
the world where the nations are at war. Is World War I a
situation 
like 
the 
top 
left—inevitable—or 
the 
bottom—
highly 
unpredictable? 
Does 
it 
make 
sense 
to 
imagine
ensemble forecasting being applied to conﬂict prediction?
If ensembles are to be part of any serious attempt to
predict conﬂict in the world, one needs to ask ﬁrst whether
conﬂict 
prediction 
is 
amenable 
to 
the 
methods 
of
mathematical modelling. This question fascinated Lewis
Fry Richardson, whose seminal contributions to numerical
weather 
prediction 
were 
described 
in 
Chapter 
5.
Richardson felt strongly that war could be predicted using
mathematical equations. Coming from a Quaker family, he
felt that trying to develop a mathematical theory of conﬂict
could be his most important contribution to humanity.
We left Richardson in Chapter 5 as an ambulance driver
at the front in World War I. He had resigned his position at
the 
Meteorological 
Oﬀice, 
and 
during 
rest 
periods 
he
worked not only on performing the ﬁrst weather forecast
by numerical methods, but also on developing what he
called “the mathematical psychology of war”.
After the war, Richardson rejoined the Meteorological
Oﬀice. However, his paciﬁst principles caused him to resign
again, on grounds of conscience, when the oﬀice joined the
Air Ministry in 1920. Instead, he focussed on the two things
that fascinated him, turbulence and the causes of war. To
help with the latter he enrolled in psychology courses.
However, 
Richardson 
realised 
that 
to 
make 
real
progress, he needed data that would measure things like a
country’s preparedness for war—e.g., how much it spends
on 
armaments, 
its 
war 
weariness 
and 
its 
degree 
of
internationality (which could be gauged from the extent of
its international trading). And he grouped conﬂicts using
the same logarithmic (Richter) scale that we use to group
earthquake events. In this way, gang conﬂict and world
wars are merely points on a continuous spectrum of deadly
conﬂict.
When he did this, Richardson found a phenomenon that
we 
have 
commented 
on 
in 
the 
context 
of 
weather,
turbulence, networks, economics, pandemics and perhaps
even the universe as a whole—power-law structure. As we
noted 
in 
earlier 
chapters, 
power-law 
behaviour 
is 
an
indication of some underlying nonlinearity. Here was an
indication that some rationality and nonlinear order could
be brought to the seemingly irrational world of conﬂict.
Based 
on 
his 
experience 
with 
weather, 
Richardson
sought a system of diﬀerential equations (equations using
calculus which included rates of change of key quantities
with 
time) 
similar 
in 
many 
respects 
to 
the 
types 
of
equations 
Lorenz 
sought 
years 
later. 
For 
example, 
he
posited that the rate of change of a nation’s armament
build-up is directly proportional to the number of arms its
rival has and also to the grievances felt towards the rival,
and inversely proportional to the number of arms it already
has itself.
One of the most curious spin-oﬀs from his work came
when he tried to include in his equations a term which
described the propensity of two countries to go to war as a
result of having a common border. Richardson reasoned
that the longer the length of this common border, the
greater the propensity. In trying to ﬁnd data that would
support 
his 
hypothesis, 
he 
found 
that 
the 
books 
he
consulted gave wildly diﬀerent estimates of the length of
borders, e.g., between Spain and Portugal or between the
Netherlands and Belgium.
Richardson eventually realised that the estimated length
of a border depends strongly on the resolution of the
measuring system used to determine its length. Imagine
trying to measure the length of the coastline of Britain with
a 200-km ruler. It gives us some sort of estimate. But now
cut the ruler in half and measure with a ﬁner-scale 100-km
ruler. The estimate increases. Cut the ruler in half again
and remeasure—the estimate increases yet again. We cut
again and again and the estimate increases and increases.
Richardson 
imagined 
a 
mathematical 
coastline—
indistinguishable 
from 
Britain’s 
coastline 
on 
any
perceivable scale—whose length was literally inﬁnite.
What is going on here is a generalisation of the fractal
process we discussed in Chapter 2. There we discussed the
Cantor set, an inﬁnite set of points characterised by a
fractional dimension between 0 and 1. Richardson had
found—in the idealised coastline of Britain—an example of
an 
inﬁnite 
set 
of 
points 
characterised 
by 
a 
fractional
dimension between 1 and 2. A simpler example of such a
fractal is the Koch snowﬂake, illustrated in Fig. 40. We
start with an equilateral triangle and iterate by replacing
the 
middle 
third 
of 
each 
side 
with 
two 
sides 
of 
a
corresponding equilateral triangle.
Richardson’s 
work 
on 
fractal 
coastlines 
and 
borders
inspired the mathematician Benoit Mandelbrot. Recall that
it 
was 
Mandelbrot 
who 
coined 
the 
word 
“fractal”,
essentially meaning some fractionally dimensioned object,
and in so doing discovered one of the most intriguing
fractals known: the eponymous Mandelbrot set.
Lewis 
Fry 
Richardson 
died 
in 
1953. 
His 
book 
The
Statistics of Deadly Quarrels
was published posthumously
in 1960. In some ways, he reminds me of Alan Turing.
Whilst Richardson is best known for his work on turbulence
and weather prediction, his later work became the basis of
much that followed in conﬂict prediction. Similarly, whilst
Turing 
is 
best 
known 
for 
his 
work 
on 
computing 
and
cryptography, 
his 
later 
work 
forms 
the 
basis 
of 
many
studies 
in 
mathematical 
biology. 
Interestingly, 
the
mathematics 
behind 
Richardson’s 
work 
on 
conﬂict 
and
Turing’s work on biology are very similar. Both involve the
mathematics of nonlinear equations based on calculus—the
equations on which the geometry of chaos is based. I am
not aware that Turing and Richardson ever met. If they
had, I expect they would have got on like a proverbial
house on ﬁre.
FIG. 40. The Koch snowﬂake at various levels of iteration. The length of the
edge of the snowﬂake increases with iteration number. Lewis Fry
Richardson found that a similar phenomenon made the length of nation
borders uncertain—complicating his theory of deadly conﬂict, but making
him aware of fractal geometry.
Today, 
appropriately 
perhaps, 
some 
of 
the 
important
work on conﬂict prediction has been taking place at the
Alan Turing Institute, the UK institute for data science and
artiﬁcial intelligence (perhaps they need a Richardson wing
for their institute). Their work on deadly conﬂicts started in
2015 when team leader Weisi Guo began studying a map of
where 
ﬁghters 
of 
the 
so-called 
Islamic 
State 
were
operating. He found that there was some overlap with
places along the old Silk Road. He started to notice that
many of the areas prone to repeat violence were along
ancient 
trade 
routes 
more 
generally. 
He 
realised 
that
regions which are geographical bottlenecks, i.e., routes
travellers 
have 
no 
choice 
but 
to 
use, 
are 
inherently
unstable and prone to violence.
Guo likened this to a chessboard, whose central four
squares are contested by almost every signiﬁcant piece at
some point. Guo created a human connection network and
found 
the 
geographical 
equivalents 
of 
those 
key 
chess
squares for various locations around the world.
With colleagues at the Turing Institute, Guo created a
prediction system based on such networks. It is called
GUARD, 
which 
stands 
for 
“global 
urban 
analytics 
for
resilient defence.” In this model, states of war and peace
are represented by what are called “potential wells”, a little
like the two lobes of the Lorenz attractor as described
earlier in this chapter. In between these are states of
instability, which Guo translates as meaning that countries
can’t be on the brink of war for long. Running the model in
ensemble mode, in order to try to estimate uncertainties in
its predictions, will I hope be a focus of future work at the
Turing Institute.
A project that has made progress with ensemble conﬂict
predictions 
is 
called 
ViEWS
1
: 
Violence 
Early-Warning
System (it seems to be a fact of modern-day science that all
projects have to have acronyms). The project is run from
the Department of Peace and Conﬂict Research at the
University 
of 
Uppsala 
in 
Sweden. 
Currently, 
ViEWS
provides 
early 
warnings 
for 
three 
forms 
of 
political
violence: armed conﬂict involving states and rebel groups,
armed 
conﬂict 
between 
non-state 
actors, 
and 
violence
against civilians. The outputs from ViEWS are probabilistic
assessments of the risk and likely severity of the three
types of conﬂict events, at three diﬀerent spatial scales:
national, subnational and individual actors. Like GUARD,
the prediction model makes use of key data that aﬀects the
likelihood of conﬂict in a given place: a history of conﬂict,
political 
institutions, 
timing 
of 
elections, 
economic
development, 
natural 
resources, 
demography 
and
geographical proximity to conﬂict. However, the ViEWS
researchers have developed a kind of multi-model ensemble
from which to make probabilistic forecasts. On the ViEWS
website they have analysed the skill of their forecasts from
the period of 2011 to 2013. One thing is completely in line
with everything written so far: the skill scores for the
ensemble forecasts are much improved compared with any
of the single-model forecasts. Having said that, I haven’t
yet seen a convincing account of whether the spread from
the ViEWS ensemble is a good predictor of the skill of the
ensemble-averaged predictions. As with other applications
of ensemble prediction, this will provide a good indication
of whether the project’s ensemble predictions are reliable.
One of the consequences of regional conﬂicts is the
displacement of local populations who then seek refugee
sanctuaries. 
Derek 
Groen 
from 
Brunel 
University 
has
developed a network-based model which predicts conﬂict
migration based on expected local conﬂicts. His predictions
are fed to the Save the Children charity. The network nodes
mark potential conﬂict regions, refugee camps and other
settlements.
In Groen’s model, a conﬂict can be generated randomly
at 
a 
node, 
and 
the 
model 
then 
predicts 
the 
resulting
movement of the displaced population along a simpliﬁed
travel network. In this way, the numbers arriving at the
refugee camps can be estimated. Alternatively, a conﬂict
can be speciﬁed deterministically and numbers of displaced
people 
arriving 
at 
the 
diﬀerent 
camps 
estimated 
as 
a
consequence of such a conﬂict. The diﬀerence between
these two uses of the model is similar to the “prediction vs.
projection” distinction discussed in the context of climate
and pandemics. Groen makes these predictions using an
ensemble of estimates, where uncertain parameters are
varied.
As mentioned in Chapter 8, at the time of completing
this book, Russia invaded Ukraine. On timescales of months
this 
was 
predictable, 
at 
least 
in 
a 
probabilistic 
sense,
simply because Russia had begun amassing troops on the
Russia-Ukraine border a few months prior to invasion. But
more than that, Richardson’s conditions for conﬂict were
met: a long common border, perceived grievances that the
Russian president felt about Ukraine after the demise of
the 
Soviet 
Union, 
and 
a 
general 
build-up 
of 
Russian
armaments in recent years. Weisi Guo conﬁrmed to me that
conﬂict 
models 
had 
identiﬁed 
Ukraine 
as 
a 
region 
of
concern for a number of years.
In thinking about the development of ensemble systems
for 
conﬂict 
prediction, 
perhaps 
some 
ideas 
can 
be
borrowed 
from 
the 
way 
in 
which 
operational 
weather
prediction 
ensembles 
are 
initialized. 
In 
particular, 
as
described 
in 
Chapter 
5, 
the 
initial 
perturbations 
of 
a
weather 
ensemble 
deliberately 
target 
the 
dominant
instabilities 
in 
the 
atmospheric 
circulations, 
using
mathematical quantities known as singular vectors. These
are perturbations in state-space directions which can be
expected to have a large eﬀect on the forecast. Without
these initial singular-vector perturbations, the ensemble
members would be too tightly clustered and the predictions
too conﬁdent. An analysis of the corresponding “singular
vectors” of the global geopolitical system would surely
identify Vladimir Putin’s actions and decisions (rather than
mine, say) as being critical to global peace and security.
That is to say, in developing a global ensemble conﬂict
model, uncertainty in what people like me—and billions like
me—think and do can be represented by general stochastic
noise. 
But 
Putin’s 
actions 
and 
decisions 
represent
geopolitical singular vectors, and need to be considered
explicitly. Any ensemble system which purports to predict
conﬂict probabilities reliably must surely focus on such
geopolitical singular-vector perturbations.
« »
WHAT 
HAVE 
WE 
LEARNED 
from 
the 
diﬀerent 
contexts
discussed in Chapters 5 through 9? First, for complex
nonlinear systems, ensemble prediction is vital if we are to
make 
reliable 
predictions 
which 
include 
estimates 
of
uncertainty. Such ensemble prediction systems can help
avoid the Michael Fish moments when a prediction of some
disastrous event fails because the prediction is especially
sensitive to the ﬂap of a butterﬂy’s wings, or a missing
horseshoe 
nail. 
These 
ensemble 
forecasts 
provide
quantitative estimates of the probability of such events. As
discussed 
in 
the 
next 
chapter, 
prediction 
probabilities
provide 
the 
basis 
for 
making 
rational 
decisions 
about
whether to take anticipatory action to mitigate the worst
consequences of these possible disasters.
On the other hand, we have treated the various topics in
this 
part 
of 
the 
book 
as 
if 
they 
were 
completely
independent 
of 
each 
other. 
Clearly, 
they 
are 
not. 
For
example, one of the biggest drivers of forced migration and
conﬂict in future years is likely to be climate change. If
heatwaves exceed the level at which humans can maintain
their body temperature at safe limits, people may have no
alternative other than to move polewards. In migrating
polewards, the migrants are likely to pass one or more of
the hotspots identiﬁed by Guo as places where conﬂicts will
emerge and develop.
So how do we go about bringing all these elements
together in a more uniﬁed way? My answer to this question
is to ﬁrst step back in time to April 1970.
One of the most famous phrases uttered during the
Apollo 
space 
programme 
was 
from 
the 
commander 
of
Apollo 13
2
. “Houston”, Jim Lovell said to ground control,
“we have a problem.”
This isn’t exactly what he said, but that doesn’t matter.
Essentially, he reported, in a matter-of-fact way, that he had
heard a thud, apparently linked to a computer message of a
“Main B Bus Undervolt”. What did matter at the time was
that, unknown to both the astronauts and ground control,
an explosion in the spacecraft’s oxygen tanks had critically
damaged their main engine and left the oxygen supplies
leaking 
into 
space. 
With 
the 
explosion, 
the 
Apollo 
13
service 
module 
had 
become 
a 
dangerously 
crippled
spacecraft moving farther from Mother Earth with each
passing hour. On their current trajectory, if the astronauts
were unable to perform any further engine burns, they
would stay on an elliptical orbit for the best part of eternity,
never coming closer to the Earth than 70,000 km (43,500
miles). The key question was whether the spacecraft was
fatally crippled. How was mission control going to bring the
astronauts home, knowing that trying possible solutions
could well make matters worse?
Fortunately, NASA had created a number of simulators of
the 
Apollo 
spacecraft. 
These 
had 
been 
used 
to 
train
astronauts 
and 
mission 
controllers 
ahead 
of 
the 
space
missions. As Gene Kranz, the chief ﬂight director for Apollo
13, said, “These simulators were some of the most complex
technologies of the entire space programme: the only real
things in the simulation training were the crew, cockpit and
the mission control consoles. Everything else was make-
believe, created by a bunch of computers, lots of formulas
and skilled technicians.”
These simulators had already proved their worth for the
Apollo 11 mission, which landed the ﬁrst men on the moon.
In 
the 
ﬁnal 
simulation 
of 
the 
Apollo 
11 
mission, 
the
guidance computer issued a “1201 alarm code”, which had
not 
been 
seen 
before. 
Thinking 
this 
meant 
that 
the
computers were somehow getting overloaded, the mission
controllers 
called 
for 
an 
abort. 
However, 
having 
later
consulted the team at MIT that had written the computer
software code, they realised that a “1201 alarm code” was
only a warning and did not indicate a critical error. If it
were not for that ﬁnal simulation, it is likely that the
landing of the Apollo 11 Eagle, during which 1201 and
1202 alarms occurred in the minutes before touchdown,
would similarly have been aborted.
In the case of Apollo 13, the simulators proved vital to
the safe return of the crew. The back-up crews had to
develop and practise the three manual engine burns—the
computers had not been programmed to do these burns
because it was assumed that the lunar module would not
still be attached on the return journey. Perhaps one of the
most complex problems occurred after the astronauts had
powered the command module down to save power and
used the lunar module as a life raft. Now they had to power
the command module up again so that they could re-enter
Earth’s atmosphere. The simulator teams worked around
the clock to deﬁne a power-up sequence using the tiny
amounts 
of 
energy 
left 
in 
the 
batteries. 
This 
involved
operating 
hundreds 
of 
switches, 
in 
precisely 
the 
right
order. Any mistake could instantly deplete the remaining
electrical energy and thus be fatal.
What’s the point of this story? Well, like Apollo 13, we
can think of planet Earth as a damaged spacecraft hurtling
through the emptiness of space. Unlike Apollo 13 with its
three 
astronauts, 
planet 
Earth 
holds 
many 
billions 
of
human astronauts. Unlike Apollo 13, there is no external
Houston for us to call for advice.
Planet Earth has not been damaged because of some
design ﬂaw, as was the case for Apollo 13; it has been
damaged by its astronauts. As Partha Dasgupta’s inﬂuential
report
3
to the UK government put it, we have failed to
manage our global portfolio of national assets sustainably.
Between 
1992 
and 
2014, 
produced 
capital 
per 
person
doubled. However, the stock of natural capital per person
declined by nearly 40 percent. It is as if the Apollo crew
squandered 
the 
few 
precious 
joules 
of 
energy 
in 
the
spacecraft’s 
batteries 
needed 
for 
re-entry 
so 
that 
they
could play music and watch videos to make their journey
back to Earth more comfortable. The problem with climate
change is one manifestation of this more general problem.
And yet at the same time, we have been developing
weapons of unimaginable destructive power. We astronauts
have the power to annihilate ourselves almost overnight, if
we should so wish. Those that need reminding might wish,
as I did just a year or two ago, to read Nevil Shute’s 
On the
Beach
, written in 1957. It’s a grim reminder of what could
be in store for us if we reach another point of instability.
Indeed, could climate change trigger such an instability?
Let’s indulge in some speculation about sometime mid-
century.
We’ll suppose international climate-change talks fail and
carbon emissions keep rising. Attempts to cut emissions in
the developed world are half-hearted and developing-world
countries 
say 
they 
won’t 
seriously 
contemplate 
cutting
emissions until the standards of living of people in their
countries have reached levels comparable with those in the
richer countries. Temperatures keep rising, but none more
so than in the western half of the United States, where
wildﬁres 
become 
a 
yearly 
fact 
of 
life. 
Temperatures
exceeding 
50°C 
(122°F) 
are 
commonplace 
around 
the
world, 
including 
high-latitude 
regions, 
where 
such
temperatures were previously unimaginable. Lake Mead
has dried up and the Hoover Dam no longer produces
electricity 
for 
large 
parts 
of 
the 
year. 
Wheat 
yields
plummet, not just one year in ten, but pretty much every
year. European countries are suﬀering similar problems
with ﬂooding events, which also destroy crops.
A 
joint 
grouping 
of 
European 
and 
United 
States
ministers comes to a conclusion: something has to be done.
They resort to Plan B. In this plan, military aircraft from
these countries ﬂy continuously around the clock spraying
sulphuric acid vapour into the lower stratosphere. From
this, sulphate aerosols are formed which reﬂect sunlight
back to space. The atmosphere now has a haze of aerosols
which, it is hoped, will oﬀset the eﬀects of global warming
and cool the atmosphere down again. They justify this
action on the basis it will help humanity as a whole.
However, 
the 
impact 
of 
such 
“geoengineering” 
on
climate isn’t as simple as it might seem at ﬁrst sight. As
discussed in Chapter 6, the global warming problem is
caused because we are trapping electromagnetic radiation
in the infrared part of the spectrum. Sulphate aerosols in
the stratosphere increase the reﬂection of sunlight in the
visible parts of the spectrum. The one is not precisely
oﬀsetting the other. What could be the consequences of
this? 
This 
is 
an 
area 
where 
estimates 
from 
current-
generation models are unreliable.
So, back to the story. After several years of spraying,
both Russia and India ﬁnd that the atmospheric circulation
patterns over their countries have changed in such a way
that rain-bearing weather systems no longer track across
the main agricultural regions. The monsoons start to fail,
and as a result the crops fail. India and Russia blame these
failures on the US/Europe policy of spraying aerosols into
the stratosphere and tell these countries to stop spraying
immediately.
There 
is 
a 
big 
international 
study 
which 
tries 
to
determine whether the Russian and Indian crop failures are
caused 
by 
the 
stratospheric 
spraying. 
The 
results 
are
inconclusive because the models’ resolution is too coarse to
give 
clear-cut 
results 
(the 
“CERN 
for 
climate 
change”
advocated for in Chapter 6 hasn’t been built because the
nations of the world could not agree to pool resources on
this project). The US and Europe claim that climate models
show that the monsoon failures are just part of the natural
variability of climate and not caused by the spraying. India
and Russia claim that the model results are unreliable and
cite as evidence the fact that they typically fail to simulate
the occurrence of extreme climatic and weather events
with anything like the frequency observed.
Eventually, Russia and India tell the US and Europe that
if they do not stop spraying, they will shoot down the
planes doing the spraying. The US responds that this would
constitute an act of war. Russia and India respond by
saying that the destruction of their food supplies is already
an act of war.
The 
ﬁrst 
plane 
is 
shot 
down, 
leading 
to 
massive
economic sanctions by the US. A second plane is shot
down, leading to airﬁelds in India being bombed. The US
and Europe issue a ﬁnal ultimatum: desist or face the very
serious consequences. A third plane is shot down, and the
consequences, both immediate and knock-on, ensue.
Within a short time, a radioactive cloud spreads across
the world, cooling the planet by several degrees, reducing
the population of Earth to one-tenth of what it was, and
making climate change, as we currently understand it, a
thing of the past.
Oh 
Lord! 
How 
can 
we 
prevent 
such 
a 
situation
developing in the ﬁrst place?
Like Apollo 13, we need credible simulators with which
these problems can be played out and addressed at the
international level before they spiral out of control.
The simulator system that Apollo 13 had would now be
called a “digital twin”. Even though not all aspects of the
Apollo simulators were digital, they performed the tasks
that today we would expect a digital twin to perform,
notably of providing information about a physical system
that for various reasons can’t be directly studied by human
intervention. As with Apollo 13, it is too risky to try these
Plan B options directly. But we need the tools with which
we can reliably simulate the consequences of a Plan B.
Here we need not so much a digital twin as a “digital
ensemble twin”. Clearly the climate models discussed in
Chapter 6 would be a critical part of a digital ensemble
twin of planet Earth. However, they must be integrated
with the other models discussed in this book: economic,
health and conﬂict models, as well as agronomic models,
hydrological models, population growth models and so on.
In 
discussing 
these 
impact 
models, 
the 
unrealistic,
equilibrium-based models are no good at all. We need to
move 
towards 
more 
agent-based 
models 
in 
which
individuals 
are 
represented 
and 
connected 
through
networks of interactions.
Could we extend the concept of an agent-based model of
global society with our 8 billion inhabitants? It’s not a crazy
idea. As we have discussed, a weather forecast model has
some billions of degrees of freedom, so doubling this to
incorporate 
degrees 
of 
freedom 
of 
individuals 
is 
not
completely out of the question. Of course, individual agents
will 
have 
to 
be 
treated 
as 
having 
some 
inherent
stochasticity. But as I have explained, we have to do this
anyway for weather variables. AI will play a central role in
such an endeavor.
Of course, such a digital ensemble twin would not only
be able to tackle the socioeconomic problems of climate
geoengineering, 
it 
should 
be 
able 
to 
provide 
credible
estimates of future migration, future conﬂict, future health
risks, future food supplies, the future health of the oceans
and so on.
Clearly, such a project is not one that could or, I would
argue, should be developed at a national level. Its output
would have to have the imprimatur of the international
community, likely the United Nations. It would be run using
literally the most powerful supercomputers in the world.
We will very soon be in the era of the exascale computer,
which will be able to perform a billion billion calculations in
each second of operation. Completely dedicated exascale
computing would be needed to even contemplate building a
digital ensemble of planet Earth, and perhaps it won’t
really 
be 
a 
serious 
option 
until 
we 
have 
zettascale
computers, 
capable 
of 
performing 
one 
billion 
trillion
computations a second. Perhaps we will have these in the
2030s. 
Perhaps 
by 
then 
we 
will 
in 
part 
use 
quantum
computers, 
or 
photonic 
processors 
as 
well 
as 
silicon
processors, 
and 
high-performance 
low-power 
imprecise
chips that produce noise in hardware.
This 
would 
be 
an 
extraordinarily 
interdisciplinary
exercise; perhaps a more interdisciplinary exercise than
the scientiﬁc community has ever contemplated. And since
handling uncertainty underpins all sciences, the project to
build a digital ensemble of global society would bring
together 
the 
various 
approaches 
to 
representing
uncertainty 
that 
have 
developed 
in 
the 
diﬀerent
subdisciplines. 
This 
project 
really 
would 
recognise 
the
primacy of doubt.
A ﬁrst step in this direction is the European Union’s
Destination 
Earth 
(DestinE) 
project
4
. 
Evolving 
from 
an
earlier EU ﬂagship project proposal, ExtremeEarth, that
some 
colleagues 
and 
I 
developed 
a 
few 
years 
ago,
Destination Earth will see not only the construction of
extremely high-resolution climate models, but also a range
of 
socioeconomic-impact 
models 
to 
which 
the 
global
climate models will be coupled. DestinE is the ﬁrst step
towards building a digital ensemble of global society. Sadly,
the UK having left the EU
5
, UK scientists will not be
contributing to DestinE.
...
10
Decisions! Decisions!
A
VERAGING ONE HUNDRED INDEPENDENT GUESSES OF
THE NUMBER of jellybeans in a jar usually provides a better
estimate of the actual number than does any one guess.
More generally, ensemble-averaged forecasts are typically
better than best-guess deterministic forecasts. However,
when 
we 
have 
to 
make 
decisions, 
simply 
knowing 
the
expected outcome is often not good enough. We need to
know the likelihood of some plausible worst-case scenario.
If the consequences of a plausible worst-case scenario are
truly dire, we should try to avoid such a scenario at any
reasonable cost. For example, COVID lockdown policies
were imposed, at great economic cost, to avoid a plausible
worst-case scenario in which hospitals were overwhelmed
and unable to cope with the number of patients needing
treatment. But how plausible is “plausible”, and what do we
mean by any “reasonable” cost? In this chapter we will look
at the use of ensembles for decision-making. This is an area
where really exciting new approaches to disaster relief
management are being developed and deployed.
« »
A 
FEW 
YEARS 
AGO, 
before 
weather 
apps 
told 
us 
the
probability of rain, a friend of mine called. He was having a
garden party in ten days’ time and had an option to rent a
tent (what in British English would be called a “marquee”).
However, he had to let the owner of the tent know by
lunchtime that day whether he wanted it or not. So, he
called me and asked: Is it going to rain in his back garden
next Saturday between 2 p.m. and 6 p.m.?
I told him that I would look at the latest forecast but it
would be in the form of a probability. I could hear the groan
at the other end of the phone line.
“How on earth are probabilities going to help me?” he
complained.
“Well, who’s coming to your party?”
“What’s that got to do with it?” he replied.
“Suppose the Queen is coming. You would not want to
risk the Queen getting wet, would you? If the Queen got
wet, your chances of that knighthood would go right down
the 
drain—along 
with 
the 
rainwater. 
So, 
even 
if 
the
probability of rain was as low as ﬁve percent, say, I guess
you would likely still rent the tent. Is the Queen coming?”
“No, of course not!”
“Well, what about the town mayor?”
I sensed he was losing patience. “Look,” I continued, “if
the town mayor is coming, you would not want him to get
wet either. However, you probably wouldn’t be quite as
bothered about him getting wet as you would the Queen
getting wet. Let’s say that if the town mayor was coming,
perhaps you would rent the tent if the probability of rain
exceeded twenty percent, say. Is the town mayor coming?”
“No.”
“So, who is the most important person coming to the
party?”
He thought about it for a while and replied, “The mother-
in-law.”
“And how much does it matter if she gets wet? That is to
say, what is the smallest forecast probability that your
mother-in-law will get wet above which you would decide to
rent the tent? If you don’t care whether she gets wet or
not, the threshold is one hundred percent. If she’s as
important as the Queen, the threshold is ﬁve percent. I’m
guessing your threshold probability is somewhere between
these two.”
Again, he thought about it for a few seconds and said,
“About ﬁfty percent.”
“Good, then we have made a decision. I’ll take a look at
the forecast. If the probability of rain exceeds ﬁfty percent,
then you hire the tent. If it does not exceed ﬁfty percent,
then you don’t. OK?”
I looked at the latest forecast. The probability was only
around 
30 
percent. 
He 
didn’t 
rent 
the 
tent/marquee.
Fortunately, it didn’t rain and everyone was happy.
What’s 
the 
essential 
idea 
from 
this 
story? 
It’s 
that
probabilities aren’t some wishy-washy things that make
forecasts 
imprecise 
and 
diﬀicult 
to 
use. 
In 
fact, 
these
probabilities help you make better decisions—providing the
probabilities are reliable.
Let’s generalise the story. Imagine some weather event
which I will call 
E
.
E
could represent the occurrence of rain
or perhaps freezing temperatures or perhaps storm-force
winds. However it is deﬁned, 
E
either occurs or doesn’t
occur. In the story about the tent, 
E
occurs if it rains.
If 
E
occurs, then Jim, as we will call him, will incur a
ﬁnancial loss, 
L
, if he has taken no measure to protect
himself. 
However, 
we 
will 
assume 
L

can 
be 
avoided
altogether if Jim takes some kind of anticipatory action. But
this anticipatory action will cost him an amount, 
C
. 
L
and 
C
have values in real monetary units such as dollars or euros,
but the ratio 
C
/ 
L
is just a dimensionless fraction which we
can assume lies between 0 and 1 (if it were greater than 1,
then there will be no point taking anticipatory action, since
it would cost more than the avoidable loss). We call 
C
/
L
Jim’s “cost-loss ratio”.
If 
C
/
L
is small enough, i.e., the cost of anticipatory
action is cheap enough relative to the loss, Jim might just
as well take anticipatory action regardless of what the
weather is likely to be. Conversely, if 
C
/
L
is close enough
to 1, then Jim might just as well never take anticipatory
action, and just suﬀer the loss when the bad weather hits.
But if 
C
/
L
lies between these two extremes, then it’s
worthwhile for Jim to consult a weather forecast to decide
when to take anticipatory action.
If Jim has access to only an old-fashioned deterministic
weather 
forecast, 
then 
the 
decision 
process 
is
straightforward: take action when the forecast predicts
that 
E
will occur; otherwise don’t take action. This is all
well and good, except, as we have seen, these old-fashioned
deterministic forecasts can be unreliable. If 
E
denotes the
occurrence of hurricane-force gusts in southern England
for the morning of October 16, 1987, then on the basis of
the deterministic forecast, on that day no one will have
taken anticipatory action (moved cars, secured boats and
aircraft, 
cancelled 
travel 
plans). 
Deterministic 
forecasts
have limited value because they are unreliable.
However, if Jim has access to probabilistic forecasts
based on a reliable ensemble prediction system, then he
has a much more valuable strategy for deciding when to
take anticipatory action. Suppose an ensemble forecast
system predicts 
E
with probability 
p
. Then the best decision
Jim can make is to take action when 
p
exceeds his cost-loss
ratio, 
C
/
L
. For example, in the case where 
L
is twice the
cost of 
C
, then Jim should take action when 
E
is forecast
with a probability of at least 0.5, or 50 percent. On the
other hand, if 
L
is twenty times the cost of 
C
, then it makes
sense 
for 
Jim 
to 
take 
precautionary 
action 
when 
E

is
forecast with a probability of as low as 0.05, or 5 percent.
Fig. 41 shows an estimate of the value of a modern
weather forecast system for decisions based on whether it
will rain four days into the future. The horizontal axis
shows all possible values of 
C
/
L
between 0 and 1. The
vertical axis shows how valuable the forecast system is for
deciding when to take anticipatory action. A value of 0
means that the forecast system has no value (you can make
decisions 
that 
are 
just 
as 
valuable 
knowing 
only 
the
climatological likelihood that it will rain in your region). By
contrast, a hypothetical perfect oracle of truth has a value
of 1; nothing can be better than that. The solid line shows
the value of decisions using a deterministic forecast system
(i.e., one that predicts that it either will rain or won’t rain
with 
100 
percent 
likelihood). 
It 
can 
be 
seen 
that 
the
forecast system has value only for a limited range of cost-
loss ratios 
C
/
L
. Outside this range, it is useless. The
dashed 
line 
shows 
the 
value 
of 
an 
ensemble 
forecast
system. It has value for almost the entire range of cost-loss
ratios. Not only that, for any cost-loss ratio where there is
value, 
the 
value 
is 
greater 
than 
for 
the 
deterministic
system
1
.
This is all very well, but does it really matter if the
Queen gets wet? In any case, she will have an army of staﬀ
who, with umbrellas and the like, ensure she never actually
gets wet. Indeed. So, let’s instead think about a farmer in
Bangladesh. We’ll call him Ahmadul. In the days before
computer-based 
weather 
forecasts, 
individual 
tropical
cyclones would kill hundreds of thousands of people like
Ahmadul; as many as half a million individuals on one
occasion
2
.
FIG. 41. The potential economic value of operational forecasts from the
European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts for the
European/North African region over the period October–December 2020 for
the event of rainfall over a six-hour period, four days into the forecast. The
lines show the value of two diﬀerent forecast systems for decision-making,
where losses 
L
associated with the occurrence of rain can be mitigated at
cost 
C
. The horizontal axis denotes the user’s 
C
/ 
L
. The vertical axis shows
the value of the forecast, where 0 is useless and 1 is perfection. The solid
line is for a state-of-the-art high-resolution deterministic forecast system.
The dashed line is for a lower-resolution ensemble forecast system.
Because modern weather forecasts are so much more
skilful, far fewer people get killed by extreme weather
events today than in decades past. However, that doesn’t
mean that we are making the best use we can of these
forecasts. Disaster relief and humanitarian aid agencies
have tended to react to extreme weather events only after
they have hit. It can take many days, perhaps a week or
more sometimes, for emergency food, water, shelter and
medicines to reach a stricken region. And of course, once
an 
extreme 
weather 
event 
has 
hit, 
reaching 
aﬀected
communities is that much harder.
It would be so much better if these agencies were able to
be 
more 
proactive—to 
use 
the 
forecasts 
to 
target 
aid,
allowing action to be taken before the event hits. The
problem 
is 
that 
aid 
agencies 
aren’t 
ﬂush 
with 
cash.
Because the old-fashioned deterministic forecasts weren’t
reliable, if they acted every time such forecasts predicted
an extreme event, valuable resources would be spent on
weather events that never happened.
Because of ensemble forecasting, one can be much more
discerning about when to become proactive. Targeting aid
before the event hits is called “anticipatory action” by
humanitarian and disaster relief agencies. Like my friend
deciding whether to rent a tent based on a probability
threshold, 
aid 
agencies 
predetermine 
a 
probability
“trigger”, based on cost/loss estimates, above which it
makes good sense to take anticipatory action.
The 
story 
starts 
over 
thirty 
years 
ago. 
It’s 
not 
just
tropical cyclones that Ahmadul has to worry about. His
life’s investments are bound up in cattle. Like many in the
country, Ahmadul lives in a low-lying area and the land on
which his cattle graze is prone to ﬂooding from the nearby
Brahmaputra River. The ﬂooding need not be caused by
local heavy rainfall; indeed, the rain may have fallen many
hundreds of kilometres upstream. But if a major ﬂood
occurs without warning, Ahmadul’s cattle, and thereby his
life savings, will be lost.
My colleague Professor Peter Webster from the Georgia
Institute of Technology has had an illustrious research
career. He is one of the world’s experts on the theory of
tropical meteorology
3
. He led a number of observational
ﬁeld campaigns to improve our scientiﬁc understanding of
the climate of the tropics. However, in 1992, Webster spent
a sabbatical year working with me, and during this period
he completely changed his research direction. He became a
convert to the recently developed ensemble-based forecasts
and decided to spend the next years of his career showing
how this new way of doing weather forecasting could help
people in some of the poorest parts of the world.
Webster 
began 
by 
consulting 
with 
local 
oﬀicials 
in
regions 
in 
Bangladesh 
aﬀected 
by 
the 
Ganges 
and
Brahmaputra 
rivers. 
Whilst 
the 
national 
meteorological
services provided conventional deterministic forecasts a
couple of days ahead, the local oﬀicials wanted forecasts
with 
more 
lead 
time, 
to 
allow 
meaningful 
anticipatory
actions to be taken ahead of possible ﬂooding events. They
needed reliable predictions a week or more ahead. With
such forecasts, families could store several days’ worth of
food and safe drinking water. Cattle and poultry, crop seed
and other belongings could be secured in safer higher
locations. 
Most 
important, 
plans 
could 
be 
made 
for
complete evacuations. Of particular concern were those
who lived on river islands called “chars”. It was diﬀicult to
make such plans when you had only a day or two of
warning.
Webster 
showed 
it 
was 
possible 
to 
make 
skilful
probabilistic 
ﬂood 
forecasts 
for 
the 
Brahmaputra 
and
Ganges as much as two weeks ahead, by coupling the
ensemble predictions of rainfall from the European Centre
for 
Medium-Range 
Weather 
Forecasts 
to 
hydrological
models of these rivers. Flooding would occur when enough
rainfall fell in the catchment basins of these rivers. In this
way, the combined weather/hydrological ensemble system
would predict the probability that one or both of these
rivers would ﬂood.
However, could probabilistic predictions of ﬂooding be
used by the local communities? Would they be able to make
sense of probabilities? It wasn’t obvious. After all, when I
was developing these ensemble prediction systems, I had
been 
told 
repeatedly 
by 
weather 
forecasters 
that 
the
British 
public 
would 
never 
understand 
the 
concept 
of
probability (despite the fact that many of them bet on
horses, where the “odds of winning” are well understood).
Answering these questions would need a ﬁeld trial. With
support from the US Agency for International Development
(USAID)
4
, 
Webster 
began 
a 
pilot 
project 
to 
provide
probabilistic forecasts to local villages in some of the ﬂood-
prone districts in Bangladesh.
The project was an enormous success. It ran for two
years—2007 
through 
2008. 
There 
were 
two 
prolonged
ﬂoods in 2007 and a third in 2008. For each of these there
was a strong signal in the ensemble forecasts, allowing
anticipatory 
action 
to 
be 
taken. 
The 
Asian 
Disaster
Preparedness 
Center
5

assessed 
the 
value 
of 
these
probabilistic forecasts. Farmers with ﬁsh farms or a focus
on ﬁshing saved about $130 per household (e.g., from ﬁsh
farm 
protection). 
Mainly 
agricultural 
households 
saved
about 
$190 
from 
early 
cropping. 
Households 
with
signiﬁcant livestock beneﬁtted the most, with an average
savings of around $500 from advanced warnings to move
livestock to higher ground. More general savings of $270
per 
household 
arose 
from 
the 
protection 
of 
household
assets. At the time, the average income in Bangladesh was
$470 per year and half the population existed on less than
$1.25 
per 
day. 
As 
such, 
the 
savings 
were 
substantial
indeed.
Webster needn’t have worried that the farmers wouldn’t
understand 
probabilities. 
When 
he 
asked 
one 
farmer
whether 
he 
could 
cope 
with 
probabilities, 
the 
farmer
replied:
“That is ﬁne! Only God knows 100 percent what will
happen, and he is not telling and you are not God!”
With probabilities, the farmer realised, he had something
more 
valuable 
than 
simple 
guesswork. 
Peter 
Webster’s
groundbreaking study showed that societies faced with
environmental catastrophe are indeed ready, willing and
able to accept and act on probabilistic forecast information.
The legacy of Webster’s work is the approach referred to
earlier 
as 
anticipatory 
action. 
This 
approach 
is
transforming 
the 
way 
in 
which 
disaster 
preparedness
agencies operate. In collaboration with the International
Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies, it is
being 
geared 
up 
for 
application 
around 
the 
world.
Associated with such anticipatory action is a scheme called
“forecast-based 
ﬁnance” 
grounded 
in 
three 
essential
elements: disaster relief emergency funds, a forecast-based
probabilistic trigger to release such funds and a pre-agreed
action plan when the trigger conditions are met.
As an early example of this system in action, on July 4,
2020, fed by ECMWF ensemble forecasts, the European
Commission’s 
Global 
Flood 
Awareness 
System 
(GloFAS)
predicted 
a 
high 
probability 
of 
severe 
ﬂooding 
in
Bangladesh (which occurred). The probability, combined
with an independent assessment by the Government of
Bangladesh’s Flood Forecasting and Warning Centre, was
high 
enough 
to 
trigger 
the 
United 
Nations 
Central
Emergency Response Fund (CERF) to release $5.2 million
of funds to a number of local organisations, who then
prepared to distribute assistance including cash, livestock
feed, storage drums and hygiene kits. This was the fastest
allocation of funds since CERF was established in 2005 and
the ﬁrst time it had been done before the peak ﬂoods
occurred. Ultimately 200,000 people were able to beneﬁt
from this anticipatory action.
In 
September 
2021, 
opening 
a 
major 
conference 
on
anticipatory action, UN secretary-general António Guterres
said that CERF had invested $140 million to scale up
anticipatory 
action 
in 
twelve 
countries, 
noting 
that
“anticipatory action protects lives”, and concluded that
anticipatory action will become central to the UN’s agenda
in the humanitarian sector going forward. This is a great
legacy of Webster’s pioneering ﬁeld trial and one that
would 
have 
been 
impossible 
without 
reliable 
ensemble
predictions.
Of course, communication issues remain critical. Most of
us experience severe weather in one form or other from
time to time, and we need to know when to take notice: the
threat to life notwithstanding, such weather can lead to
property damage, to travel delays and cancellations or to
loss 
of 
electricity 
or 
water 
supplies. 
As 
a 
way 
to
communicate the risk of a forecast event, the UK Met
Oﬀice puts out “yellow”, “amber” and “red” warnings of
severe weather based on what is called a “warning impact
matrix” (see Fig. 42). The weather event 
E
is characterised
by its estimated impact and by its forecast likelihood. In the
warning impact matrix, impact varies along the horizontal
axis and likelihood varies along the vertical axis. In terms
of the cost-loss model described earlier, we can imagine
that moving along the horizontal axis, the loss, 
L
, due to 
E
gets bigger and bigger. Moving up the vertical axis, the
probability, 
p
, of 
E
gets bigger and bigger. Severe weather
warnings are based on the product 
pL.
Hence, for example,
an amber warning could refer 
either
to an event with
medium impact but high probability 
or
to an event with
high impact but only medium probability. The probabilities
that determine such warnings are taken from the ensemble
forecast systems.
Of course, even when people are well informed, they do
not always take action. It was reported that some people
did not leave their shoreline homes ahead of the well-
forecast, deadly tropical cyclone Idai that hit Mozambique
in 2019, for fear their houses would be burgled. In such
situations, anticipatory action could include the automatic
provision of insurance coverage or of guards to protect
houses.
Anticipatory 
action 
based 
on 
probabilistic 
thresholds
isn’t relevant only for ﬂooding events or storms. There is a
growing problem of developing and maintaining global food
security. In over 80 percent of the world, farming is rain-
fed, meaning that water for agriculture comes principally
from rainfall. Many farmers around the world know the
importance of planting and harvesting crops when there
will be rainfall and when it is dry, respectively. Reliable
probabilistic forecasts can help them decide on planting,
cropping 
and 
harvest 
strategies 
in 
ways 
for 
which
deterministic forecasts are useless.
FIG. 42. Risk matrix used to determine severe weather warnings in the UK.
Shown in the ﬁgure are boxes with four shades of grey, corresponding to
“no warning”, “yellow warning”, “amber warning” and “red warning”—the
latter being the most severe weather warning. The shading is determined
by the product of impact and likelihood of occurrence. For example, an
amber warning could mean either a high probability of a medium-impact
event or a medium probability of a high-impact event. The probabilities are
determined by ensemble forecast systems. The ticked box corresponds to a
“yellow warning”.
« »
CAN THE COST-LOSS MODEL help us to decide whether to
take anticipatory action in the light of the threat of climate
change?
