Let’s summarise two possible arguments for and against
taking anticipatory action.
On the one hand:
The climate crisis is a manifestation of our overindulgent
and proﬂigate way of life. We sowed the wind; nature is
now reaping the whirlwind. As written by James Lovelock
in his
Revenge of Gaia
6
,
“Like an old lady who has to share her house with a
growing and destructive group of teenagers, Gaia grows
angry, and if they do not mend their ways, she will evict
them.”
We have to rid ourselves of our dependence on fossil
fuels as quickly as possible and live a simpler life more in
harmony with nature.
On the other hand:
Decarbonising the economies of the world will stunt
their economic growth, particularly in relatively poor parts
of the developing world, preventing them from attaining
the standards of living which the developed world achieved
on the back of cheap carbon-intensive energy. And if we
hinder the economic growth of these poorer countries, we
will lock in their high human fertility rates for many years
to 
come, 
exacerbating 
problems 
of 
growing 
population
levels and further degrading the planet. All this economic
pain is simply not worth it—the impact of unmitigated
climate change on global gross domestic product will be
minimal. And if climate change means we have to adapt to
new norms of weather, so be it—we can aﬀord it, it’s not a
big deal.
Perhaps the cost-loss model I’ve described could provide
a framework for trying to address these issues objectively.
Does the likelihood 
p
of dangerous levels of climate change
exceed the cost 
C
of cutting greenhouse-gas emissions to
zero divided by the damages 
L
associated with climate
change? If the costs were small enough or the losses large
enough, it would make sense to take action even if the
probabilities were small. But are they? If we could boil it
down to a seemingly straightforward calculation like this,
we’d have a clear-cut strategy on whether and how to deal
with climate change.
This was a question addressed by probably the most
authoritative study on the impact on the global economy of
climate change: the 
Stern Review
7
, published in 2006, and
authored by the distinguished economist Lord Nick Stern.
Using 
estimated 
losses 
L

and 
costs 
C

based 
on 
gross
domestic product (GDP), Stern’s main conclusion was that
the 
probability 
p

of 
dangerous 
levels 
of 
warming 
is
suﬀiciently great that it is certainly worth taking action
now to cut our carbon emissions. But there were those who
criticised the basic assumptions of the 
Stern Review
. These
critics argued that the science of climate change was not
good 
enough 
to 
estimate 
p

reliably, 
that 
L

was
overestimated (particularly when the future is discounted,
as one has to do in any economic calculation about the
future), and that 
C
was underestimated.
Let’s look at each of these issues in turn.
Whilst I fully agree that we could do a better job in
estimating 
p
—for example, by pooling human and computer
resources to build much-higher-resolution global models
(e.g., at a “CERN for climate change” as discussed in
Chapter 6)—there is no evidence that climate models have
overestimated 
p
. 
As 
discussed 
in 
Chapter 
6, 
climate
projections from thirty or more years ago have done a good
job in predicting observed rates of global warming.
Let’s 
look 
at 
L
—the 
avoidable 
damages 
caused 
by
climate change. We need to be able to quantify 
L
in some
shape or form. If we can’t put a ﬁgure in dollars or pounds
on 
L
, then we won’t be able to determine whether the cost
C
of cutting emissions is worth it or not.
As we have seen, economists have tried to estimate the
impact of 
L
on global GDP. According to the 
Stern Review
,
without action, climate change could reduce GDP by 5
percent or more per year. Others are not so pessimistic.
The 
Nobel 
Prize–winning 
American 
economist 
William
Nordhaus has estimated that 3°C (5.4°F) of warming would
lead 
to 
only 
just 
over 
2 
percent 
reduction 
of 
GDP
altogether. These are massive diﬀerences. Who is right?
One problem is that, as we discussed in Chapter 8,
economic models tend to be rather simple. The impact 
L
in
such models is typically determined by the global-mean
temperature change predicted by the climate models. One
could argue that they should take the regional changes of
climate into account. However, as we have discussed, the
rather coarse-resolution climate models do not simulate
well the intensity of extreme weather events. On top of this,
the economic-impact assessment models do not have the
complexity of the agent-based models discussed in Chapter
8, 
whose 
form 
allows 
them 
to 
be 
driven 
by 
regional
changes 
in 
weather 
patterns. 
I 
hope 
this 
rather
unsatisfactory 
state 
of 
aﬀairs 
will 
change 
with 
the
development of a CERN for climate change, discussed in
Chapter 6.
However, there is another issue. Let’s think again about
Ahmadul, 
our 
hypothetical 
representative 
farmer 
from
Bangladesh. How do we quantify the impact of climate
change 
in 
monetary 
terms 
on 
a 
representative 
of 
a
community with a completely negligible impact on global
GDP?
We are edging towards a topic which some people might
feel is taboo. How much is a human life worth? This is
certainly a distasteful topic, but nevertheless it is one that
has to be addressed if we are to make progress in this area.
One could of course take the view that a human life is
priceless. However, there were just short of 25,000 people
killed on the UK roads in 2020. If we valued a human life as
literally priceless, then it would make sense to impose a
ten-mile-per-hour speed limit on all roads. However, most
of us probably feel that the extra time it would take to
travel is not justiﬁed, even if it does save lives. That is to
say, we implicitly put a value, in terms of our time, on a
human life.
Statisticians have known that it is important to come up
with some value of a statistical life (VSL), as it is called, in
order to argue either for or against regulatory legislation.
In his book 
Pricing Lives
8
, W. Kip Viscusi, one of the
pioneers in this ﬁeld, discusses an issue which arose during
the 
Reagan 
presidency 
in 
the 
1980s: 
Should 
ﬁrms 
be
required to label dangerous chemicals in the workplace? At
the time, government agencies would estimate the value of
life in terms of a person’s lost earnings and associated
costs if he or she died. On this basis, the US Oﬀice of
Management and Budget argued that the cost of adding the
labelling wasn’t worth it, and therefore didn’t recommend
the legislation that required companies to provide labelling.
Viscusi 
argued 
that 
the 
value 
of 
life 
was 
not 
being
calculated properly. Instead, he argued that VSL should be
estimated 
from 
evidence 
of 
how 
much 
extra 
money
individuals would require to accept an increased risk of
death (or some life-changing disability which could make
life a misery). Viscusi presented evidence from US-based
statistical data at the time that workers were prepared to
accept an annual wage premium of $300 for a job where
the extra annual worker fatality risk was 1/10,000. From
this, we can use the cost-loss model described earlier to
estimate a value of life. That is, if we write 
C
=
pL
where
now 
C
= $300 and 
p
= 1/10,000, then we have 
L
= $3
million. That was in 1982. Adjusted for inﬂation we get a
modern ﬁgure of VSL in the US as perhaps a little less than
$10 million.
However, we have to face the uncomfortable fact that
VSL in the developing world is not as large as that in the
US 
or 
other 
wealthy 
countries. 
Sadly, 
workers 
in
developing countries will accept a smaller wage premium
for this extra risk of death or disability. How can we adjust
for this, so that we can get a ﬁgure that applies to both
Ahmadul in Bangladesh and Jim in Seattle? Very roughly, a
formula that is widely used deﬁnes VSL as 100 times the
per capita
GDP in the country of interest
9
. On this basis, we
can value Ahmadul’s life at 100 times the per capita GDP of
Bangladesh.
Remember VSL is based on the monetary premium not
only of death but of some life-changing disability. Based on
the discussion in Chapter 6, I would say that living in a 4°C
(7.2°F) or greater warmer world, a hell on earth (HoE) as
far as I can judge, is not too far oﬀ the equivalent of a life-
changing disability. Simply to be conservative, if VSL is 100
times 
the 
per 
capita 
GDP, 
let’s 
estimate 
the 
value 
of
avoiding HoE at 50 times the per capita GDP.
How likely is it that we will experience at least 4°C of
warming if we do nothing to cut emissions? According to
what I have discussed, it is, I would say, around 0.3. It does
depend critically on these cloud feedbacks about which our
knowledge 
is 
currently 
rather 
limited. 
However, 
if 
we
multiply the probability, 
p
= 0.3, by a loss, 
L
, corresponding
to 50 times per capita GDP, we end up with the “risk”, 
pL
,
of HoE at 15 times per capita GDP.
What is the cost of avoiding HoE? For many years the
cost of decarbonising a country’s economy was assumed to
be a few percent of the country’s GDP. However, according
to analysis performed by the UK’s Committee on Climate
Change in its Sixth Carbon Budget Methodology report
10
, it
seems that the phenomenal drop in the cost of renewable
energy (wind and solar) means that the cost could be as
low as 1 percent of GDP
11
. But let’s assume 2 percent of
GDP in general. Hence, for each person, this is a cost, 
C,
of
1/50 of GDP, i.e., per capita.
On this basis, 
C
is 750 times smaller than the risk, 
pL
, of
HoE. It seems overwhelmingly worth it to cut our carbon
emissions.
However, this conclusion could be undermined by the
issue of discount rates. If you had a choice of receiving
$100 today or $100 in ten years’ time, you would likely
prefer 
to 
receive 
it 
today 
(inﬂationary 
devaluation
notwithstanding). For example, you might invest your $100
in some business that you hope will make you several
hundred 
dollars 
in 
ten 
years’ 
time. 
If 
one 
estimates
discount rates from the ﬁnancial markets (e.g., based on
ﬁnancial assets such as shares), then one might discount
future losses, 
L,
by as much as 6 percent per annum.
Hence, some economists argue that we should compare
C
not with 
pL
, but with 
pL
discounted by this rate of
around 6 percent per annum. On this basis, if 
L
occurs
suﬀiciently 
far 
in 
the 
future, 
pL

will 
eﬀectively 
be
discounted to zero. Then no cost paid today will be worth it.
Does the application of a ﬁnancial discount rate make
sense? As far as I can see, the concept of human suﬀering
makes a mockery of the notion of economic discounting
based on ﬁnancial assets like shares. Human suﬀering ﬁfty
years from now seems no less troubling to me than if it
occurs ﬁve years from now, or indeed tomorrow.
Some people argue that if we are wealthier, as we may
be in the future, then we will be able to cope better with
the hell on earth of a 4°C warmer world. I don’t buy this.
One should look at pictures of relatively wealthy German
couples crying on each other’s shoulders after their houses
were swept away in the Rhine Valley ﬂoods of 2021. It
makes you realise that even if in the coming hundred years
we 
brought 
the 
standards 
of 
living 
of 
most 
of 
the
developing world to those in Germany today, a 4°C warmer
world would still be a hell on earth. Raising the per capita
income in the developing world by a factor of ten, say, is
not going to help much in the face of some devastating
storm. And in any case, if the world is heading for a hell on
earth, perhaps we will not be wealthier in the future. If
that’s the case, then perhaps the discount rate should be
negative.
Since we have left the strict domain of science, we need
to ask one further question: Who should be paying to
mitigate the eﬀects of climate change? Should Ahmadul be
paying? Well, he played no role whatsoever in causing the
problem in the ﬁrst place. We in the rich, developed world
have beneﬁtted from cheap, carbon-rich energy. Perhaps it
is time for us to pay some of that back.
Indeed, ethical and altruistic issues aside, it is in the
developed world’s interest to ensure that Ahmadul’s life is
not 
completely 
unbearable. 
This 
is 
because 
he 
and
potentially billions like him have another option: rather
than 
suﬀer 
and 
potentially 
perish 
from 
existential
heatwaves, storm surges and extended drought, he and his
family can try to migrate polewards, where the weather is
likely to be more bearable. Historically, migration is how
civilisations 
coped 
with 
local 
changes 
in 
climate.
Nowadays, such mass migrations will be the source of
major conﬂict, as we have discussed. If the developed
world does not like this option, then it can try to ensure
Ahmadul and his children can continue to live fulﬁlled lives
where they are currently living. One can think back to the
Marshall Plan, when the US provided ﬁnancial help to war-
torn Europe, not necessarily for altruistic reasons, but to
prevent Europe turning to communism. We need a climate
Marshall Plan for the future
12
.
Let’s summarise.
If the local weather forecast predicts an intense storm
with some signiﬁcant probability, it’s your decision whether
to act on it or not. The underlying science of meteorology
does not tell you that you must act if hurricane-force winds
are 
predicted 
for 
tomorrow. 
It’s 
not 
for 
the 
weather
forecaster to tell you what to do. However, coupling the
meteorology to a cost-loss model can help you make a
decision, for example, about whether to avoid travelling on
a day when hurricane-force winds are predicted with some
signiﬁcant probability.
We can look at climate change in a similar way. Does
burning of fossil fuel lead to an increase in carbon dioxide
concentration in the atmosphere? According to science,
certainly 
yes. 
Is 
carbon 
dioxide 
a 
greenhouse 
gas?
According 
to 
science, 
certainly 
yes. 
Will 
emissions 
of
greenhouse gases increase the risk of dangerous levels of
climate change? According to science, certainly yes. Should
we therefore cut our emissions of greenhouse gases as
quickly as possible? Science itself is agnostic on this last
point. The activists who simply say, “Listen to the science”,
seem 
to 
have 
missed 
this 
point. 
The 
physicist 
Sabine
Hossenfelder put it in a rather graphic way (apparently
referring 
to 
something 
that 
drunken 
Germans 
do 
from
railway bridges): science does not tell you not to pee onto
high-voltage electricity lines; it tells you that urine is a
good conductor of electricity.
Just as with weather prediction, a cost-loss analysis can
help 
you 
make 
a 
decision 
about 
whether 
to 
take
anticipatory 
action 
regarding 
climate 
change. 
However,
this requires putting a value on things which do not have
an unambiguous value, like living in a future hell on earth.
Based on the way we value our own existence in other
areas 
of 
life, 
there 
does 
indeed 
seem 
to 
be 
a 
strong
argument 
that 
we 
should 
act 
now, 
uncertainties 
about
future 
climate 
change 
notwithstanding. 
But 
this 
is
ultimately a decision which each of us must make, e.g., in
deciding which politicians to vote for.

PART III
Understanding the Chaotic Universe
and Our Place in It
My own view is that to understand quantum
nonlocality we shall require a radical new theory. This
new theory will not just be a slight modiﬁcation of
quantum mechanics but something as diﬀerent from
standard quantum mechanics as general relativity is
from Newtonian gravity. It would have to be something
which has a completely diﬀerent conceptual
framework.
ROGER PENROSE
1
If a machine is expected to be infallible, it cannot also
be intelligent.
ALAN TURING
2
Two key ideas from Part I are applied to try to understand
two of the most puzzling aspects of the universe: the world
of quantum physics and ourselves. We start by assuming
that the geometry of chaos applies to the universe as a
whole. 
This 
leads 
to 
the 
crucial 
idea 
that 
certain
counterfactual 
worlds, 
where 
we 
might 
have 
done
something but didn’t, could actually be inconsistent with
the 
laws 
of 
physics. 
This 
helps 
make 
comprehensible
certain long-standing quantum mysteries. Following this I
discuss the idea that the human brain makes constructive
use of noise to model the world around us, and that this has
enabled 
us 
to 
become 
the 
creative 
species 
we 
are. 
I
speculate that the geometry of chaos can help explain two
of our most visceral but puzzling experiences: having free
will 
and 
being 
conscious. 
I 
conclude 
with 
a 
novel
suggestion 
about 
the 
nature 
of 
God. 
A 
caveat: 
the
discussion in Part III is much more speculative than Parts I
and II.
...
11
Quantum Uncertainty
Reality Regained?
W
E LEFT OUR ANALYSIS OF QUANTUM UNCERTAINTY IN
CHAPTER 4 in a state of confusion. Einstein had proposed
that the quantum wavefunction describes an ensemble of
possible worlds. As an illustration, he imagined a photon
moving towards a point on a phosphorescent hemisphere.
According 
to 
Einstein, 
in 
each 
ensemble 
member, 
the
photon takes a diﬀerent path to the hemisphere. We don’t
know which of the ensemble of possible worlds is the real
world until we make some macroscopic measurement, such
as observing a ﬂash from the phosphorescent material.
From 
Einstein’s 
perspective, 
quantum 
uncertainty 
is
epistemic—it reﬂects our own uncertainty, not the photon’s
uncertainty. Bohr, on the other hand, believed that quantum
uncertainty 
was 
inherent 
to 
a 
quantum 
system—it 
is
ontological in nature. The experiment Bell proposed and
that 
was 
later 
successfully 
performed 
using 
entangled
photons 
casts 
severe 
doubt 
on 
Einstein’s 
ensemble
interpretation and in so doing supports Bohr’s position.
Seemingly even the particles don’t know where they are
going until they get there. Whatever that means.
What to do? For years, the answer was to shrug one’s
shoulders and say, “Get over it”. Epistemic or ontological—
frankly, who cares? Such issues were considered a matter
for 
philosophers 
with 
their 
fancy 
words, 
but 
not 
for
physicists with hard equations to solve. The mantra was to
“Shut up and calculate”. And in astrophysics, condensed
matter physics, quantum computing, high-energy physics
and 
other 
cutting-edge 
areas 
of 
research, 
quantum
mechanics 
works 
well 
without 
worrying 
about 
these
seemingly esoteric issues. In climate physics, for example,
one has to think about how photons absorb and scatter in
the atmosphere. But one never worries about the nature of
uncertainty in these radiative processes. One just gets on
and solves the Schrödinger equation, or an approximation
for it, for the problem at hand.
Nowadays, physicists are ﬁnally starting to question the
mantra. There are a number of reasons for this.
Firstly, string theory, our leading candidate for unifying
quantum mechanics and gravity (and hence for providing a
so-called theory of everything) is beginning to lose its
lustre. 
String 
theory 
utilises 
a 
concept 
called
supersymmetry. 
If 
nature 
were 
supersymmetric, 
the
diﬀerence between the elementary particles which carry
forces, such as the photon, and the elementary particles
which feel these forces, such as the electron, would not be
fundamental. Supersymmetric theories predict new types
of 
elementary 
particles 
(e.g., 
so-called 
photinos 
and
selectrons). Although string theory does not predict the
masses of these supersymmetric particles, many physicists
hoped they would have been found by the Large Hadron
Collider at CERN. So far, no such particles have been
observed.
On top of this, it has become clear in recent years that
over 80 percent of the matter in the universe is in the form
of what is called dark matter: matter of which we observe
only 
its 
gravitational 
pull, 
but 
about 
which 
we 
know
nothing else. Dozens of experiments have searched for
diﬀerent types of hypothetical particles that might make up
dark matter, but none has found even a trace. Currently, no
one knows what dark matter is made of, if indeed it is made
of anything
1
.
Dark energy is another mystery. It causes the universe’s
expansion 
to 
accelerate, 
seemingly 
consistent 
with
observations. 
Quantum 
ﬁeld 
theory—the 
extension 
of
quantum mechanics needed to describe the elementary
particles—actually 
predicts 
a 
possible 
value 
for 
this
acceleration. However, the predicted value
2
is too large by
a factor of 10
120
. If the universe accelerated according to
the value predicted by quantum ﬁeld theory, then stars and
galaxies would not have had enough time to form before
the expansion of the universe ripped them apart. Hence, we
would not exist. Quantum ﬁeld theory’s prediction of dark
energy is sometimes called the worst prediction in the
history 
of 
science
3
. 
It 
makes 
Michael 
Fish’s 
weather
forecast virtually perfect by comparison!
As a result of all this, some physicists are beginning to
ask a question that few previously had dared ask: Is there
something wrong with quantum mechanics itself? Perhaps
we will not properly synthesise gravity with the other
fundamental forces of nature until we have understood
what is wrong. One world-renowned physicist who, over the
years, has dared raise this possibility is Nobel laureate
Roger Penrose—see the quote at the beginning of this part
of the book.
Emboldened, let’s return to the Bell experiment. It’s
worth recapping by showing Fig. 26 again (see Fig. 43) and
replicating the particles’ so-called look-up table in Table 2
(see Table 3).
Fig. 43 shows the Bell experiment, in which Alice and
Bob measure the spin of pairs of entangled particles. Table
3 can be considered part of a hypothetical look-up table for
Alice’s particles. The existence of such a look-up table is
implied by a standard deterministic hidden-variable model
of 
quantum 
physics 
of 
the 
type 
Einstein 
would 
have
favoured. 
The 
rows 
of 
the 
table 
correspond 
to 
three
possible orientations of the SG devices. Each column of the
table represents the outcomes of spin measurements on,
say, one of Alice’s particles. In principle the number of rows
and columns in the look-up table may be much larger than
this. However, three rows suﬀice for our purposes.
FIG. 43. The famous Bell experiment, using pairs of entangled particles
moving in opposite directions. In this experiment, Alice’s and Bob’s SG
devices can be oriented arbitrarily. However, if they are oriented in the
same direction and Alice measures her particle to be spin up, Bob will
measure his particle to be spin down.
TABLE 3. A hypothetical look-up table for the spin of 12 of Alice’s particles
in a Bell experiment, for 3 possible choices of orientation of Alice’s SG
device. The statistics of these +s and −s necessarily satisfy a mathematical
inequality known as Bell’s inequality, described in the text. The look-up
table for Bob’s 12 particles is exactly the same, except each + is replaced
with a − and vice versa. Experimentally, Bell’s inequality is violated for
certain choices of measurement orientation. This casts doubt on the
existence of such look-up tables, and hence casts doubt on hidden-variable
theories of quantum physics.
Recall the quantities 
A
, 
B
and 
C
calculated from Table 3,
described in Chapter 4. 
A
is the number of columns where
there is a + in the ﬁrst row and a + in the second row. 
B
is
the number of columns where there is a − in the second
row and a + in the third row. 
C
is the number of columns
where there is a + in the ﬁrst row and a + in the third row.
As mentioned (and proved in endnote 11 of Chapter 4), it is
simple to show mathematically that 
A
+ 
B
will always be
bigger than or equal to 
C
, no matter how we ﬁll in the
entries to this table with +s and −s. This result is known as
Bell’s inequality.
Let’s look again at how the experiment is performed to
test whether this equality is satisﬁed in nature. In practice
the experiments that are performed test a slightly more
complicated version of Bell’s inequality. However, what I
am about to draw attention to here applies equally to this
more complex version.
For each particle pair in Fig. 43, Alice and Bob have to
decide, independently, whether to measure the spin of their
particle in the “1”, “2” or “3” directions. We don’t need to
worry what exactly these directions are, except that they
are directions in which quantum mechanics predicts that
Bell’s inequality is violated.
From the sub-ensemble of measurements where Alice
chose 1 and Bob chose 2, we calculate the quantity 
A
. To do
this calculation, we must invoke the fact that the total spin
of the two entangled particles is 0, which means that if Bob
chose 2 and got +, then for this particle pair Alice would
have got − if she had measured the spin of her particle in
the 2 direction. Similarly, we can calculate 
B
from the
measurements where Alice chose 2 and Bob 3. Finally, we
can calculate 
C
from measurements where Alice chose 1
and Bob 3. I have shown some possible such results in
Table 4.
TABLE 4. A partial look-up table for Alice based on (possible) results from a
Bell experiment. For example, the ﬁrst column is constructed from a pair of
measurements where Alice measured + in the 1 direction and Bob + in the
2 direction (which means Alice would have measured − if she had chosen
the 2 direction). Similarly, the twelfth column is constructed from
measurements where Alice measured + in the 1 direction and Bob − in the
3 direction (which means Alice would have measured + if she had chosen
the 3 direction).
The ﬁrst point to notice in Table 4 is that there are
blanks. There are no columns which contain three entries,
as there are in Table 3. It is easy to violate Bell’s inequality
from incomplete look-up tables. For example, from Table 4,
A
= 0, 
B
= 0, 
C
= 4, which clearly violates Bell’s inequality
since here 
A
+ 
B
is less than 
C.
This demonstrates that estimates of 
A
+
B
and 
C
based
on experimental data which generate partial look-up tables,
like Table 4, don’t 
necessarily
invalidate hidden-variable
theories. To be able to use the results of experiments to
rule out hidden-variable theories, we need an additional
assumption about the nature of the three experimental sub-
ensembles of particle pairs from which 
A
,
B
and 
C
are
estimated experimentally. This assumption is that the three
sub-ensembles of particles are statistically equivalent to
one another. If they have this property, then we can assume
that the experimental estimates of 
A
,
B
and 
C
are the same
as if we obtained 
A
,
B
and 
C
from a full look-up table,
providing we have a big enough statistical sample (i.e.,
enough columns in the look-up table).
This assumption seems quite reasonable at ﬁrst sight.
Suppose we were performing a blind trial of a drug (e.g., a
possible COVID-19 vaccine) for possible use in society. In
the trial, one group of volunteers gets the drug and another
a placebo. If we have devised the test properly, it shouldn’t
matter which group gets the drug and which group gets the
placebo. For the drug trial to have validity, the two groups
of volunteers should be statistically equivalent in terms of
their ages, genders, ethnic origin, eye colour and other
relevant characteristics.
When it comes to particles, however, it’s not clear what
we mean by saying that the sub-ensembles are statistically
equivalent—the particles don’t have genders, ages or eye
colours to compare and contrast. In fact, the only knowable
thing that uniﬁes the members of one sub-ensemble over
another is the type of spin measurement performed on the
particles in that sub-ensemble (e.g., that Alice’s particles
were all measured in the 1 direction).
What is the key reason why physicists typically assume
that the sub-ensembles are statistically equivalent? It is
because 
of 
the 
conclusion 
that 
although 
Alice 
actually
measured a particular particle in the 1 direction, she might
just as well have measured it in the 2 or the 3 direction. For
example, suppose Alice decided to measure a particle in
the 1 direction on the basis that her grandmother was born
in the ﬁrst third of a month. Since the grandmother might
just as well have been born in the last third of the month,
so 
the 
argument 
goes, 
Alice 
might 
just 
as 
well 
have
measured that same particle in the 3 direction.
In the quantum foundations community, asserting some
physical link between the intrinsic properties of particles
and whimsical determinants of measurement settings is
viewed as implausibly conspiratorial. For this reason, it is
typically 
assumed 
that 
the 
diﬀerent 
sub-ensembles 
are
indeed statistically equivalent. If we make this assumption,
then we are faced with the uncomfortable fact that the
violation of Bell’s inequality can be explained only by giving
up the notion of reality as something deﬁnite or by the
equally ghastly notion of quantum action-at-a-distance.
Despite this entirely plausible argument in favour of
statistical equivalence, I want to argue in this chapter that,
based on the geometry of chaos, there may be an implicit
physical linkage between a particle’s hidden variables and
the measurement settings, and this linkage is not in the
least ridiculous or conspiratorial.
To understand this, let’s rewind the clock to the previous
millennium 
and 
imagine 
a 
counterfactual 
world 
where
Alice’s great-grandmother’s pregnancy was for forty-two
weeks instead of forty weeks. In this counterfactual world,
Alice’s 
grandmother 
was 
born 
in 
the 
last 
third 
of 
the
month, and as a result Alice’s particle was measured in the
3 direction, rather than the 1 direction.
A key question I want to ask is this: Is the world where
the 
great-grandmother 
had 
a 
forty-two-week 
pregnancy
necessarily consistent with the laws of physics? We can put
this a little more explicitly. Given the actual state of the
universe, consider a mathematical state seemingly close to
the 
actual 
state 
where 
Alice’s 
great-grandmother’s
pregnancy was forty-two weeks long. Is this mathematical
state 
of 
the 
universe 
necessarily 
consistent 
with 
the
mathematical laws of physics?
Suppose for some reason—which we will come to—it
isn’t consistent. Then the counterfactual world where Alice
actually 
measured 
in 
the 
3 
direction 
would 
also 
be
inconsistent with the laws of physics. If this were so, then
instead 
of 
treating 
as 
conspiratorially 
implausible 
a
physical link between the intrinsic properties of Alice’s
particle with the actual measurement setting 1, the link
would instead be wholly inevitable
4
!
Indeed, the only reason quantum foundations experts
believe 
that 
the 
three 
sub-ensembles 
of 
particles 
are
statistically 
equivalent 
is 
because 
of 
the 
implicit
assumption that Alice and Bob might just as well have
measured their particles in a diﬀerent way from how they
actually 
measured 
them. 
Take 
this 
away 
and 
the
assumption 
of 
the 
statistical 
equivalence 
of 
the 
sub-
ensembles disintegrates. And without that assumption, we
do 
not 
have 
to 
assume 
spooky 
action-at-a-distance 
or
indeﬁnite reality to explain the experimental violation of
Bell’s inequality!
We tend to think about counterfactual worlds all the
time, but, I suspect, we rarely ask the question of whether
the counterfactual worlds we envisage might actually be
consistent 
with 
the 
laws 
of 
physics. 
In 
Chapter 
9 
we
discussed 
how 
Archduke 
Franz 
Ferdinand’s 
driver
mistakenly took the wrong route. If he had taken the
correct 
route, 
the 
archduke 
would 
not 
have 
been
assassinated 
by 
Princip 
and 
the 
whole 
history 
of 
the
twentieth century could have worked out diﬀerently. A
world 
where 
the 
archduke 
was 
not 
assassinated 
is 
a
counterfactual world. Is such a world 
necessarily
consistent
with the laws of physics?
According to the laws of classical physics, it is. For
example, in Lorenz’s equations of chaos or the Navier-
Stokes equation for a ﬂuid, there is nothing preventing you
making 
an 
arbitrarily 
small 
change 
to 
the 
starting
conditions for these equations. Similarly, there is nothing in
the classical laws of physics preventing you hypothesising a
tiny change to the world—the ﬂap of a butterﬂy’s wings—
that 
would 
have 
made 
the 
archduke’s 
driver 
take 
a
diﬀerent 
route. 
As 
such, 
we 
can 
describe 
the 
laws 
of
classical physics as “counterfactually deﬁnite”. Because of
this, we frequently use the notion of counterfactuality to
infer causality
5
. If I had not thrown the stone, the window
would not have broken. Hence, because in reality I did
throw the stone, I caused the window to break.
But this does not imply that 
some
counterfactual worlds
might be inconsistent with the laws of physics in an as-yet-
to-be-discovered theory of quantum physics. Such a theory
would be counterfactually indeﬁnite and an example of
what is called a superdeterministic
6
theory. A good way to
think about the diﬀerence between a deterministic model
and a superdeterminist model—of the type I am proposing
at least—is like this. In a deterministic model, the future is
predetermined by the laws of physics from a given initial
condition. The Navier-Stokes equation is an example of a
deterministic model. Despite such determinism, we can
envisage slightly altered initial conditions (e.g., a small
change 
to 
a 
small 
eddy 
in 
the 
turbulent 
ﬂuid). 
The
deterministic evolution from the altered initial conditions,
although diﬀerent from what actually happened in the real
world, 
is 
mathematically 
allowed 
in 
this 
deterministic
model. By contrast, in a superdeterministic theory, one
cannot 
assume 
that 
such 
altered 
initial 
conditions 
are
consistent with the theory’s equations. And therefore one
cannot 
assume 
that 
evolution 
from 
this 
altered 
initial
condition is consistent with the theory’s equations.
The 
assumption 
of 
counterfactual 
deﬁniteness 
is 
not
given the prominence it deserves in quantum foundations
discussions because it’s not immediately obvious how to
formulate 
a 
model 
of 
quantum 
physics 
which 
has 
the
property 
of 
counterfactual 
indeﬁniteness. 
One 
leading
quantum expert who did understand the critical importance
of 
counterfactual 
deﬁniteness 
was 
the 
Russian 
Israeli
mathematician Boris Tsirelson, who died in 2020. Tsirelson
made a very important contribution to our understanding of
Bell’s 
inequality. 
He 
quantiﬁed 
the 
extent 
to 
which
quantum mechanics violates the Bell inequality through the
eponymous 
Tsirelson 
bound. 
It 
is 
important 
that 
any
putative theory of quantum physics does not violate Bell’s
inequality beyond the Tsirelson bound. In his pedagogical
description of Bell’s inequality, Tsirelson states clearly why
counterfactual 
deﬁniteness 
is 
a 
vital 
assumption 
if 
a
hidden-variable theory is to satisfy Bell’s inequality
7
.
It turns out that counterfactual indeﬁniteness can also
explain the puzzle of the sequential SG experiment of
Chapter 
4. 
The 
key 
issue 
is 
whether 
a 
counterfactual
experiment where we reverse the last two SG devices in
Fig. 25b—keeping the particle and its hidden variables
ﬁxed—is consistent with our theory of physics. A theory
where 
the 
counterfactual 
does 
not 
exist 
provides 
an
explanation of what in quantum mechanics is called the
“non-commutativity” of spin. In ordinary language, non-
commutativity implies that we can’t treat the sequential SG
experiment 
as 
made 
of 
completely 
independent 
SG
subcomponents—we can’t simply reverse the order of the
SG components with the same ﬁxed particle; the whole is
not simply the sum of its parts. An ensemble-based hidden-
variable 
theory 
which 
has 
this 
type 
of 
counterfactual
indeﬁniteness can explain the sequential SG experiment. I
describe one ahead.
It seems quite satisfactory that there may be a single
common explanation to both the Bell experiment and the
sequential 
SG 
experiment. 
Both 
illustrate 
an 
important
property of the quantum world: its inherent indivisibility.
This notion was emphasised in the title of David Bohm and
Basil 
Hiley’s 
inﬂuential 
book 
discussing 
hidden-variable
theories of quantum physics
8
: 
The 
Undivided 
Universe
.
This title, and the message it conveys—that the universe as
a whole is much more than the sum of its parts—provides
an important guide for the next part of the argument.
« »
WHAT FORM COULD THE laws of physics take which would
render 
inconsistent 
with 
the 
laws 
certain 
key
counterfactual quantum worlds? Here I claim that laws
based on the geometry of chaos, such as we discussed in
Part I, do this.
If we are to respect the inherent indivisibility of the
quantum world as we’ve discussed, we need to think big,
very big. We’ll take Lorenz’s insight and apply it, not just to
a ﬂuid-dynamical system on planet Earth but to the whole
universe, with all of its stars, galaxies and clusters of
galaxies. That is to say:
Conjecture 
A: 
The 
whole 
universe 
is 
a 
nonlinear
dynamical system evolving on some fractal attractor
9
in
cosmological state space.
Cosmological state space is just like trouser state space,
or 
the 
Lorenz 
model’s 
state 
space, 
but 
on 
steroids.
Cosmological 
state 
space 
describes 
all 
the 
degrees 
of
freedom of all the objects in the universe. It has a huge
number of dimensions. And like the Lorenz attractor, our
cosmological attractor will have fractal gaps on all scales.
The quantum counterfactual worlds that we have been
discussing lie in these fractal gaps.
I 
call 
conjecture 
A 
the 
“cosmological 
invariant 
set
postulate”. 
The 
phrase 
“invariant 
set” 
is 
just 
another
mathematical way of saying “attractor”
10
. The conjecture
can be reframed in a diﬀerent way:
Conjecture 
B: 
The 
laws 
of 
physics 
at 
their 
deepest
describe 
the 
geometry 
of 
a 
fractal 
invariant 
set 
in
cosmological state space.
The hypothetical counterfactual quantum worlds, if they
do 
not 
lie 
on 
the 
invariant 
set, 
are 
inconsistent 
with
conjectures 
A 
and 
B. 
This 
ﬁnally 
provides 
a 
possible
mathematical way to understand the violation of Bell’s
inequality without giving up on deﬁnite reality and without
having to accept spooky action-at-a-distance.
How would we formulate this postulate mathematically?
We 
can’t 
use 
ordinary 
“real” 
numbers 
to 
do 
this. 
As
mentioned 
in 
Chapter 
2, 
real 
numbers 
are 
linked 
to
Euclidean 
geometry, 
and 
the 
geometry 
of 
fractals 
is
fundamentally diﬀerent to Euclidean geometry. Instead, we
need the mathematics of 
p
-adic numbers—the numbers that
pure mathematicians use to understand the “quantum”
nature of mathematics—i.e., the whole numbers: 1, 2, 3…
In the theory of 
p
-adic numbers, a point which does not lie
on its corresponding fractal is necessarily distant from a
point which does, even though in terms of the familiar
Euclidean distance, such points might look close together.
That is to say, from the 
p
-adic perspective, a change which
moves a point oﬀ the cosmological invariant set is a large
change, even though it may seem small from the familiar
Euclidean perspective
11
.
In a series of papers
12
, I constructed a mathematical
model for this cosmological invariant set described in these
conjectures. I call the general framework which underpins
this 
model 
“invariant 
set 
theory”. 
What 
does 
the
cosmological invariant set look like? Is it like the Lorenz or
Rössler attractors of Chapter 2?
At present, the mathematics of invariant set theory tells
us only about the fractal structure of the trajectories on the
cosmological invariant set, not its global structure (see the
very last sentences in this book). The fractal structure of
trajectories is illustrated in Fig. 44. Here, instead of a
state-space trajectory being described by a single one-
dimensional curve as in Fig. 5, it is instead described by
something rather akin to a piece of rope (Fig. 44a). That is
to say, what looks from a distance like a single curve is
actually a helix of trajectories twisted around themselves
(cosmic DNA perhaps
13
). In turn, each of these smaller
trajectories is itself a helix of trajectories twisted around
themselves. If we take a cross-section through this rope-
like trajectory segment, it has the fractal structure shown
in Fig. 12 for 
p-
adic numbers, also shown in Fig. 44b. When
a quantum system interacts with the environment, in a
process called quantum decoherence, the rope starts to
unravel 
(Fig. 
44c). 
Finally, 
the 
decohered 
trajectories
cluster together (Fig. 44d). The clusters are associated with
the 
discrete 
measurement 
outcomes 
(e.g., 
spin 
up/spin
down) in standard quantum mechanics.
A 
key 
mathematical 
result 
is 
that 
the 
quantum
counterfactual 
worlds 
described 
earlier, 
for 
either 
the
sequential SG experiment of Fig. 25 or the Bell experiment
of Fig. 43, necessarily lie in the 
gaps
in between the
strands of rope, and therefore cannot be characterised by
p
-adic numbers. These worlds do not lie on the invariant
set. The key geometric ideas that lead to this result are
described 
in 
the 
endnotes
14
. 
They 
make 
use 
of
mathematical 
properties 
of 
spherical 
triangles 
and 
the
distinction between rational and irrational numbers
15
. The
key result means, by conjecture A, that such counterfactual
experiments are inconsistent with the laws of physics. But
this means that antecedent worlds in which the causes of
these counterfactual experiments occur similarly cannot lie
on the invariant set. This is why we can be sure that the
counterfactual 
world 
where 
Alice’s 
great-grandmother’s
pregnancy lasts two weeks longer is inconsistent with the
laws 
of 
physics—not 
directly 
because 
of 
processes
happening in the last millennium, but indirectly because
these processes were the cause of a particular quantum
measurement setting in this millennium. There is no way
that Alice’s great-grandmother could know this, not least
because the invariant set is, by the discussion in Part I of
this 
book, 
non-computable—there 
is 
no 
algorithm 
for
determining whether a point in state space lies on a fractal
set or not.
FIG. 44. a) Instead of a trajectory describing a simple curve in state space
(c.f., Fig. 5), in invariant set theory, a trajectory, on magniﬁcation,
resembles a piece of rope, comprising a helix of smaller-scale state-space
trajectories, each trajectory of this helix itself comprising a further helix and
so on. b) A cross-section through the helix is a geometric representation of
the 
p
-adic numbers, discussed in Chapter 2. c) When a system interacts
with the environment, the helix “decoheres” into its smaller strands. d)
Finally, the decohered strands evolve into discrete clusters, a nonlinear
process which we associate with the phenomenon of gravity. The clusters
correspond to measurement outcomes in quantum mechanics.
In a nutshell, failure of counterfactual deﬁniteness is
exactly what is necessary to reconcile the violation of Bell’s
inequality 
with 
Einstein’s 
ensemble 
interpretation, 
but
without giving up on the notion of a deﬁnite reality, and
without invoking the spooky action-at-a-distance that he
hated so much.
The same reasons (the ideas in note 14) explain why
when 
we 
try 
to 
determine 
which 
path 
a 
photon 
took
through the two slits in Fig. 22, the interference pattern
disappears. 
Here, 
a 
counterfactual 
world 
where 
we
measure 
which 
slit 
the 
particle 
took, 
keeping 
the
interference pattern ﬁxed, lies oﬀ the invariant set.
There is another way to understand invariant set theory.
Quantum mechanics arose from a bold speculation by the
German physicist Max Planck: that light energy does not
vary continuously, but in discrete packets which he called
“quanta”. In order to accommodate this idea, the state
space of quantum mechanics (quantum trouser space, if
you like) has to be of a particular type. We call it Hilbert
space, 
named 
after 
the 
mathematician 
David 
Hilbert,
whose 
“halting 
problem” 
was 
discussed 
in 
Chapter 
2.
Quantum trouser space is itself continuous—you can move
from one point in Hilbert space to another through a series
of 
inﬁnitesimal 
steps. 
In 
invariant 
set 
theory, 
we 
take
Planck’s idea one step further—we discretise Hilbert space.
For this to work, the discretisation has to be done in a very
speciﬁc way
16
. The gaps in the discretised Hilbert space
correspond to counterfactual measurements which might
have been performed on a quantum system but weren’t.
Not 
all 
counterfactual 
worlds 
are 
inconsistent 
in
invariant 
set 
theory—there 
are 
plenty 
of 
neighbouring
counterfactual trajectories which also lie on the invariant
set. According to conjecture B, the laws of physics describe
what happens along the real trajectory using laws built on
the 
collective 
geometric 
properties 
of 
both 
real 
and
counterfactual trajectories. From this point of view, we can
interpret the two-slit experiment of Fig. 22 as follows: even
though in reality the photon travelled through only one slit
in Fig. 22, there exist counterfactual trajectories on the
invariant set where the photon travelled through the other
slit. 
If 
the 
real 
trajectory 
is 
the 
yin
17
, 
then 
the
counterfactual 
trajectories 
are 
the 
yang. 
Like 
the
counterweight on the crankshaft in a piston engine, the
counterfactual trajectories ensure the smooth running of
the real quantum world. The ensemble of counterfactual
and real trajectories—collectively describing the geometry
of the invariant set—gives the appearance of an interfering
quantum wave in space-time.
Quantum mechanics treats the ontology of the real and
counterfactual trajectories as the same. That’s why the
notion of reality (a particle in two places at once) is so
incomprehensible in quantum mechanics. In invariant set
theory, by contrast, real and counterfactual trajectories
have a distinct but interdependent ontology: they are not
the same, but equally one cannot ignore the counterfactual
trajectories if one wants to understand evolution on the
real trajectory
18
. In the next part of this chapter, we will
conclude that the counterfactual trajectories are in fact
real trajectories, but for earlier or later epochs of the
universe. 
Hence, 
according 
to 
invariant 
set 
theory,
quantum 
physics 
ties 
together 
real-world 
evolutions 
of
multiple diﬀerent epochs of the universe. Not only is the
spatial universe undivided, so too are the epochs of the
universe.
The yin and yang of invariant set geometry can also help
explain the power of quantum computers over traditional
classical computers. The latter process information on only
our 
own 
single 
classical 
trajectory, 
whilst 
the 
former
additionally 
process 
information 
on 
the 
many
counterfactual trajectories in the neighbourhood of our
trajectory on the invariant set. Later on I will explore the
possibility that this quantum yin and yang can also explain
our visceral feeling of free will and consciousness.
Is 
there 
an 
experimental 
test 
for 
the 
cosmological
invariant 
set 
postulate? 
I 
am 
working 
on 
this 
with
physicists at the University of Bristol
19
. The key idea is that
invariant set theory is based on ﬁnite mathematics (the “
p
”
of “
p
-adic” is a ﬁnite whole number). This has a number of
consequences, one of which is that the number of ways you
can 
vary 
a 
quantum 
system 
comprising 
n

elemental
systems (called qubits) will not be as large as predicted by
quantum mechanics as 
n
approaches the logarithm of 
p
20
.
The 
quantum 
computing 
industry 
has 
indeed 
found 
it
diﬀicult to entangle a large number of qubits arbitrarily
because 
qubits 
can 
easily 
interact 
with 
the 
noisy
environment. Here I am suggesting that this obstruction
may be inevitable once a large enough number of qubits
are mutually entangled. It may be that this idea is linked to
the notion of gravitational decoherence, discussed further
on.
« »
DOES WHAT WE KNOW about the large-scale structure of
the 
universe 
support 
the 
cosmological 
invariant 
set
postulate (conjectures A and B)?
The universe evolved from a state of extremely high
density and temperature known as the Big Bang. We know
this partly for theoretical reasons. Not least, Roger Penrose
won the Nobel Prize for work (with Stephen Hawking)
showing that an initial cosmological space-time singularity,
a point in space-time where the laws of general relativity
actually break down, is a robust prediction of general
relativity theory. We also know from the observational work
of American astronomer Edwin Hubble in the 1930s that
the universe is expanding, and therefore began from a very
condensed state. The cosmological microwave background,
discovered in 1965 by American radio astronomers Arno
Penzias and Robert Wilson, is a relic of the condensed Big
Bang.
What is the fate of the universe? Will it ﬁnally collapse
into a “Big Crunch”? If it does, can we then envisage that
the current period of evolution of the universe from the Big
Bang is just one epoch of a multi-epoch universe, where
time has no beginning and no end, as in Fig. 45? The
cosmological invariant set postulate requires the universe
to evolve through these multiple epochs. Neighbouring
trajectories on the invariant set correspond to either future
or past epochs of the universe, evolving similarly but not
identically to ours.
But 
there 
is 
a 
problem 
with 
such 
a 
scenario. 
It 
is
conventionally 
believed 
from 
observations 
of 
distant
supernovae that the universe is not only expanding but
accelerating. Such acceleration is linked to the notion of
dark energy. If the universe continues to accelerate, then it
will never recollapse to a Big Crunch and hence to a multi-
epoch universe. In such a situation we are not on a fractal
invariant 
set 
but 
on 
some 
transient 
adjustment 
to 
a
profoundly uninteresting invariant set devoid of stars and
life.
FIG. 45. A multi-epoch universe evolving from repeated Big Bangs and Big
Crunches. A key question one could ask is this: What sort of geometry do
these repeated epochs trace out in cosmological state space? I argue that
such a universe could evolve on a fractal invariant set in state space,
perhaps like the Lorenz attractor but in a much-higher-dimensional state
space. If this is correct, it may explain some of the deepest mysteries of
quantum mechanics, such as quantum nonlocality. This would contradict
the notion of methodological reductionism, that to understand the
mysteries of the universe, we necessarily need to probe smaller and
smaller scales. Here, quantum nonlocality is explained by the structure of
the universe on the largest conceivable scales.
Fortunately, 
there 
are 
three 
scenarios 
where 
this
possibility does not happen. The ﬁrst is that this dark
energy will eventually reverse, aiding the ultimate collapse
of this epoch of the universe
21
. The second is linked to a
proposal by Roger Penrose that all the mass in the universe
will end up in the form of massless particles like photons.
Such 
massless 
particles 
have 
no 
concept 
of 
spatial 
or
temporal scale. As such, the asymptotic end state of the
universe can be seamlessly patched onto a condensed Big-
Bang state of a new epoch of the universe, through a
mathematical procedure known as conformal scaling
22
.
In view of the discussions in this book, I ﬁnd the third
possibility 
the 
most 
interesting. 
The 
notion 
that 
the
universe is accelerating depends on an assumption—the
cosmological principle—that the universe is homogeneous
on the very largest scales. In this book we have discussed
nonlinear 
systems 
(from 
turbulence, 
to 
networks 
of
individuals, to economic systems, to fractals) which exhibit
power-law 
behaviour. 
Such 
power-law 
behaviour 
is
indicative of structures that look the same on diﬀerent
scales. The ubiquity of such power-law behaviour might
indeed extend to the universe on the largest scales. Recent
discoveries of unexpectedly large structures in the universe
suggest that this could be the case
23
. If this is so, then
perhaps the expansion of the universe is not accelerating—
it’s just an illusion
24
. Then it certainly becomes possible
that the universe will ultimately collapse to a “Big Crunch”
singularity.
I recall as a child watching British cosmologist Fred
Hoyle 
on 
TV 
arguing 
for 
his 
steady 
state 
theory 
of
cosmology. In this theory, there is no Big Bang. Hoyle
argued that the notion of a beginning of time was a rather
inelegant concept. It somehow suggested the need for a
god to provide just the “right” initial conditions from which
the universe could start. Indeed, Hoyle coined the phrase
“Big Bang” as a pejorative way to describe what he saw as
an artiﬁcial beginning of the universe. However, Penzias
and Wilson’s observations rather killed Hoyle’s theory. On
the other hand, if the Big Bang merely represents the
beginning of our current epoch, then Hoyle’s idea that
there is no beginning to time might be right after all.
« »
LET’S DISCUSS SOME CONSEQUENCES of the cosmological
invariant set postulate.
For example, it can shed light on one deep problem in
contemporary physics: the origin of the second law of
thermodynamics. When we drop a glass onto the ﬂoor, it
smashes into small pieces. We never see the time reverse of
this. 
This 
is 
attributed 
to 
the 
second 
law 
of
thermodynamics: that entropy, a measure of disorder in the
universe, increases with time. But for entropy to increase
with time, it must have been small at the time of the Big
Bang. Why should this be?
We can see the phenomenon of increasing entropy in the
dynamics of simple chaotic systems. Fig. 10 showed how a
simple ring of points in state space evolves into a more
complex 
irregular 
banana/boomerang 
shape. 
If 
we
continued the simulation, we would see the banana shape
deform further and become less and less like the original
small 
ring. 
Eventually 
the 
distorted 
ring 
would 
spread
across the whole fractal attractor. We can deﬁne a quantity
which measures the distortion of the ring and, like the
entropy 
of 
a 
thermodynamic 
system, 
it 
would 
typically
increase with time, reaching a maximum when the ring of
points has spread right across the entire attractor.
This characterises well the increase in entropy when we
drop a glass and it smashes into a hundred small pieces.
Think 
of 
the 
initial 
ring 
in 
Fig. 
10 
as 
describing 
an
ensemble of initial conditions in each of which we have a
glass. For all practical purposes, the glass looks the same
in all initial members of the ensemble, though the detailed
arrangement of atoms in each of the ensemble of glasses is
diﬀerent. 
Because 
of 
these 
diﬀerent 
arrangements 
of
atoms, each glass will shatter in completely diﬀerent ways
and the shards of glass will spread out to form completely
diﬀerent patterns. The ensemble will have spread out in a
chaotic fashion. Chaotic and thermodynamic systems have
much in common.
However, and this is the key point, the top left of Fig. 10
shows that there are regions of state space where there is
very little change in the shape of the initial ring. Indeed, in
these regions, the initial ring can actually get smaller.
These regions denote stable parts of the attractor—where,
in this analogy, dropping a glass wouldn’t lead to noticeably
diﬀerent conﬁgurations of smashed glass because the glass
wouldn’t shatter.
Now, 
if 
the 
universe 
is 
evolving 
on 
a 
cosmological
attractor, then we would similarly imagine that there would
be speciﬁc regions of state space where an initial ring (of
universes) 
would 
get 
closer 
to 
each 
other 
as 
time
progressed. In such regions, the entropy of the universe
would be decreasing. Put another way, the heterogeneous
geometry of the invariant set leads naturally to a picture
where in most regions of state space entropy is increasing,
but in some regions, entropy is actually decreasing.
For this to be relevant to understanding the low entropy
of the universe at the time of the Big Bang, then we would
expect regions of state space where entropy is decreasing
to 
correspond 
to 
periods 
when 
the 
universe 
is
gravitationally collapsing towards a Big Crunch. If this is
so, then the entropy at the interface between one Big
Crunch and the next Big Bang would be at a minimum
25
.
What are the implications of this? Could it be that what
we call “gravity” is actually a phenomenon associated with
the heterogeneous geometry of the cosmological invariant
set? 
In 
particular, 
is 
“gravity” 
a 
manifestation 
of 
the
tendency of trajectories to cluster together on the invariant
set, as shown in Fig. 44d? If this is so, then gravity will be
associated with the quantum measurement process. This
has 
already 
been 
suggested 
independently 
by 
Roger
Penrose and Lajos Diósi
26
. Trying to establish whether
gravity really does play a role in quantum measurement is
in 
my 
view 
one 
of 
the 
most 
important 
unresolved
experiments 
in 
contemporary 
physics. 
Because 
such
clustering is preceded by a “decoherent” unravelling of the
trajectories on the invariant set (Fig. 44c), invariant set
theory supports the idea that gravity and decoherence are
inherently linked.
Whether thinking about quantum decoherence or the
entropy of the universe, we are being led to the idea that
gravity is of a 
completely diﬀerent
character to the other
forces of nature. In some sense, we know this already—in
general relativity theory, gravity is not a force at all, but a
manifestation of the curvature of space-time. If this is
correct, it suggests it is misguided to try to unify gravity
with the other forces of nature using the principles of
quantum 
ﬁeld 
theory. 
If 
the 
cosmological 
invariant 
set
postulate is correct, quantum ﬁeld theory is not the path to
uniﬁcation. Indeed, going further, I would argue that trying
to “quantise gravity”, which is the way most physicists
think about the problem of synthesising our theories of
quantum and gravitational physics, is rather putting the
cart before the horse. Instead of a quantum theory of
gravity, we might make more progress trying instead to
develop a gravitational theory of the quantum
27
.
If such an idea is correct, then there is no such particle
as a graviton—the particle associated with quantum ﬁeld
theories of gravity, like string theory. That’s a prediction,
albeit not one that will be directly testable for some time
yet.
We noted before that quantum ﬁeld theory implies, when
coupled 
to 
Einstein’s 
theory 
of 
general 
relativity, 
an
acceleration 
of 
the 
universe 
that 
is 
120 
orders 
of
magnitude bigger than what is observed. In invariant set
theory, there is no reason to suppose the eﬀects of quantum
physics would have a direct impact on the large-scale
acceleration of the universe. In this sense, invariant set
theory appears much closer to what is observed than is
quantum ﬁeld theory.
And what of dark matter? In Einstein’s theory of general
relativity, the curvature of space-time is determined by the
distribution of matter in space-time. It is possible to modify
the 
ﬁeld 
equations 
of 
general 
relativity 
so 
that 
the
curvature of our space-time is determined not only by the
distribution of matter in our space-time but also by the
distribution of matter in space-times associated with the
neighbouring trajectories on the cosmological invariant set.
If this idea is correct, then there may be no particle of dark
matter; instead, dark matter would simply be the eﬀect on
space-time curvature from matter in neighbouring space-
times on the invariant set.
And it is plausibly the case that so-called space-time
singularities which form in black holes and at Big Bangs in
standard classical general relativity would not occur if the
curvature 
of 
space-time 
is 
inﬂuenced 
by 
matter 
in
neighbouring trajectories on the invariant set. Solving the
singularity problem has long been seen as a sine qua non
for 
any 
putative 
uniﬁed 
theory 
of 
quantum 
and
gravitational physics.
One of the key philosophies that has underpinned the
development 
of 
theoretical 
physics 
over 
the 
twentieth
century and into the twenty-ﬁrst is that “smaller is more
fundamental”—sometimes 
called 
“methodological
reductionism”. Particle physicists want to build a bigger
particle collider under Lake Geneva so that particles can be
accelerated to higher speeds, and therefore to greater
energies, 
to 
allow 
the 
physics 
of 
smaller 
scales 
to 
be
probed. It’s assumed that we necessarily need to probe
these 
smaller 
scales 
to 
improve 
our 
understanding 
of
nature. My own belief is that methodological reductionism
is a ﬂawed philosophy, and that fundamental physics is
going 180° in the wrong direction because of that. See the
caption for Fig. 45.
In conclusion, we do not necessarily have to accept
either indeterminism or action-at-a-distance in describing
the 
world 
of 
quantum 
physics. 
Using 
the 
geometry 
of
chaos, Einstein’s picture of an ensemble of deterministic
worlds may be right after all. If this is so, we conclude that
we do live in a world in which elementary particles, and
indeed 
the 
notion 
of 
reality, 
are 
certain 
and 
deﬁnite.
Perhaps this picture of the world will provide a better basis
for synthesising quantum and gravitational physics. On the
other hand, before these ideas will be accepted, we need
clear-cut experiments in favour of invariant set theory over
quantum mechanics. Until then, one should of course be
quite doubtful about the ideas I have discussed in this
chapter.
