12
Our Noisy Brains
I
N CHAPTER 11, I ARGUED THAT ULTIMATELY THE LAWS OF
PHYSICS may well be deterministic, and that these laws
describe the geometry of the attractor/invariant set of the
highest-order system imaginable—the universe as a whole.
In Chapter 3, we noted that if we want to model a high-
order deterministic system with a low-order deterministic
system, we should truncate the high-order system and add
noise to represent the parts of the system we cannot model
explicitly. 
We 
also 
observed 
that 
in 
many 
nonlinear
situations, noise is a useful resource and not the nuisance
we often imagine it to be; noise can amplify weak signals,
for example. In this chapter I propose that, consistent with
this, the human brain makes constructive use of noise in
trying to create a low-order model of the high-order world
around us. I will argue that such noise is a key element in
making us the creative, innovative beings we are. However,
unlike in the earlier examples I discussed, noise is not
made by mathematical pseudorandom-number generators;
it is made in hardware. The presence of such noise appears
to be a consequence of the brain’s extraordinary energy
eﬀiciency—that it can run on just 20W of power.
« »
THE GREEK PHILOSOPHER EUCLID is commonly attributed
with giving the ﬁrst proof that there are inﬁnitely many
prime numbers. A prime number is a whole number greater
than 1 that is divisible only by itself and 1; numbers such as
2, 3, 5, 7, 11 and 13 are prime. Euclid’s proof goes as
follows:
1. Suppose there are only ﬁnitely many prime
numbers.
2. Hence there is a largest prime number. Call it 
P.
3. Form the product 2 × 3 × 5 × 7 × 11 × 13 ×… × 
P
of all the prime numbers, and add 1 to the result.
Call this number 
Q
.
4. By construction, 
Q
is not divisible by any of the
prime numbers up to 
P
, since such division leaves a
remainder of 1.
5. But all numbers, including 
Q
, must be divisible by
some prime number (possibly 
Q
itself).
6. Hence there must exist a prime greater than 
P
.
7. Hence the supposition in step 1 must be false.
8. Hence there are inﬁnitely many prime numbers.
For example, suppose we thought that 5 is the largest
prime number. Then Euclid says—think about 
Q
= 2 × 3 ×
5 + 1 = 31. Now, 31 is not divisible by 2 or 3 or 5—there is
always a remainder of 1. Hence there must be a larger
prime number than 5. In this case, 31 is itself a prime
number.
What is the creative step in this proof? Well, step 1 is
based on a commonplace technique in mathematics called
reductio ad absurdum
: assume the opposite to what you
want to prove and show that this leads to a contradiction.
Reductio ad absurdum
was, at some stage in the history of
mathematics, a creative step. It was known to be one of
Euclid’s favourite tricks. Hence, we can imagine that he
simply pulled this out of his bag of useful tricks when he
started to construct his proof. As such, it is not the key
creative step for this particular proof. Step 2 is manifestly
not a creative step—it follows immediately from step 1.
Rather, the creative step is step 3. Once Euclid had the idea
of forming the number 
Q
, steps 4–8 followed in a fairly
deductive, mechanical way.
Let’s 
try 
to 
create 
a 
computer 
that 
spontaneously
creates mathematical theorems. We’ll load up two “tricks”
taken 
from 
Euclid’s 
proof. 
The 
ﬁrst 
is 
reductio 
ad
absurdum
: assume the opposite of what you want to prove
and show that this leads to a contradiction. The second
trick will be step 3 of Euclid’s proof: if you have some
numbers, multiply them together and add 1.
So, have we made our computer creative? Well, let’s see
if 
it 
can 
come 
up 
with 
another 
classical 
mathematical
theorem from ancient Greece: Pythagoras’s proof that the
square root of 2 is an irrational number, i.e., cannot be
written as a fraction 
a
/
b
(such as 983 / 765) where 
a
and
b
are whole numbers.
The computer can reasonably start by ﬁrst using the
reductio ad absurdum
trick: assume the square root 
can
be
written as a fraction 
a
/
b
. Perhaps the computer knows the
rules of arithmetic and assumes that 
a
and 
b
have no
common factors.
It now needs to ﬁnd a contradiction. It has one more
trick up its sleeve—it’s now time to apply it: multiply 
a
and
b
together and add 1. But that’s a complete dead end. In
fact, to ﬁnd Pythagoras’s proof, the computer needs a new
trick (which is to square the numbers 
a
and 
b
)
1
. But that
trick is not in its library of tricks.
The computer hasn’t exactly got nowhere—using the
reductio ad absurdum
trick is a good ﬁrst start. But it’s as
if it has climbed a minor peak on the route to the main
goal, and then has simply got stuck not knowing where to
go from there.
We could add the new trick into the computer’s memory,
so that it now has three tricks to play with. With a little bit
of extra help, that might enable the computer to prove that
the square root of 2 is irrational. But are these tricks going
to be suﬀicient to prove some other theorem, like the
Navier-Stokes 
Millennium 
Prize 
problem 
mentioned 
in
Chapter 3?
Of course, lots more tricks will be needed up one’s
mathematical sleeves to have any chance of cracking one of
these big problems. However, the very fact that these are
unsolved 
problems 
raises 
a 
rather 
interesting 
thought.
Perhaps we will always need new tricks to prove important
new theorems: having some ﬁxed memory bank of tricks is
never going to be good enough. Perhaps there is simply no
end 
to 
the 
tricks 
needed 
to 
prove 
all 
mathematical
theorems. This is (eﬀectively) what Gödel’s incompleteness
theorem, which we met in Chapter 2, suggests. If this is so,
computers may have a hard job coming up with really
interesting new mathematical theorems.
How did Euclid get this idea of forming the number 
Q
?
He didn’t tell us. However, other mathematicians have told
us how their eureka moments have arisen, and something
very interesting emerges from them.
Here is what Henri Poincaré (whose work was discussed
in Chapter 1) said about the moment when he had a
creative ﬂash of insight
2
:
Just at this time, I left Caen, where I was living, to go
on a geologic excursion under the auspices of the
School of Mines. The incidents of the travel made me
forget 
my 
mathematical 
work. 
Having 
reached
Coutances, we entered an omnibus to go some place
or other. At the moment when I put my foot on the
step, the idea came to me, without anything in my
former thoughts seeming to have paved the way for it,
that the transformations I had used to deﬁne the
Fuchsian functions were identical with those of non-
Euclidian geometry. I did not verify the idea; I should
not have had time, as, upon taking my seat in the
omnibus, 
I 
went 
on 
with 
a 
conversation 
already
commenced, but I felt a perfect certainty. On my
return to Caen, for convenience sake, I veriﬁed the
result at my leisure.
Here 
is 
how 
the 
critical 
idea 
which 
led 
to 
Roger
Penrose’s 
Nobel 
Prize–winning 
work 
on 
space-time
singularities came to him
3
. Penrose was walking to work
with a colleague and they were deep in conversation. They
crossed the road and stopped talking to watch out for
traﬀic. When they got to the other side, they started talking
again. When the colleague had left, Penrose had a strange
feeling of elation, but couldn’t work out why. He went
through all the things he had done that day, including what
he had for breakfast. Finally, he thought about the moment
when he was crossing the road and realised that in that
moment 
he 
had 
had 
the 
crucial 
idea 
about 
how 
to
characterise extreme gravitational collapse in a generic
way. This idea led to the 1965 paper that was the basis of
his Nobel Prize.
Let me quote Andrew Wiles, perhaps the most famous
mathematician alive today, whose work was mentioned in
Chapter 
2. 
Here 
is 
what 
Wiles 
says
4

about 
creative
moments:
In particular, when you reach a real impasse, when
there’s a real problem that you want to overcome,
then the routine mathematical thinking is of no use to
you. Leading up to that kind of new idea there has to
be a long period of tremendous focus on the problem
without any distraction. You have to really think about
nothing but that problem—just concentrate on it. Then
you stop. Afterwards there seems to be a kind of
period of relaxation during which the subconscious
appears to take over and it’s during that time that
some new insight comes.
And it’s not just creativity in mathematics and physics
research. Comedian John Cleese, part of the famous Monty
Python team, makes much the same point about artistic
creativity—it 
occurs 
when 
you 
relax 
and 
let 
your
unconscious mind wander
5
.
In all these cases, the key creative insight comes during
a moment of relaxation. Now, few of us have been fortunate
enough 
to 
have 
such 
moments 
of 
creativity 
as 
those
mentioned earlier. However, perhaps an example we can all
relate to (particularly those of us of advancing years) is
when we have completely forgotten some person’s name.
We can see their face in our heads, or perhaps even in
reality. 
Perhaps 
we 
know 
them 
extremely 
well. 
Simply
trying harder and harder to remember the name doesn’t
work. Often the best thing to do is stop trying and just let
your mind relax. Then, out of nowhere, the name will
suddenly and mysteriously pop out of the ether.
But what’s going on here from a physical perspective? If
we want to create genuinely creative AI systems, should we
tell the AI program to stop working ﬂat out on some
problem 
and 
just 
idle 
for 
a 
few 
minutes, 
hoping 
for
inspiration?
That seems a silly idea, for the simple reason that digital
computers are ultimately just on-oﬀ switches. It’s possible
to express arithmetic operations such as 1 + 1 or 24 × 37
in terms of switches; this is called Boolean logic, named
after Victorian mathematician George Boole. And modern
computers perform these switching processes amazingly
quickly. However, “relaxing” and doing the switching more
slowly, for example, is clearly not itself going to make the
computer creative.
How, by contrast, do our brains add 1 + 1 and make 2?
Perhaps the answer is so ingrained that we just know that 1
+ 1 = 2. So, let’s try something harder: What’s 24 × 37?
How would I go about answering that?
At a very young, preschool age, I would go for walks in
our local woods with my father. We would take a path up to
a high point and admire the vista across the neighbouring
countryside. We would then walk down via a diﬀerent
route, one which was a little steeper and had steps cut into
the path. There were about twenty steps in all. As we went
down the steps, we would both count them out loud: one,
two, three, four… I learned to count by repeating the
numbers as we walked down the steps week after week
until they were embedded in my head.
And then, at my elementary school, the “times tables”
were 
drummed 
into 
my 
head. 
After 
a 
while 
I 
could
eﬀortlessly recall that “four sevens are twenty-eight”. So, if
I was later asked by my teacher what 4 × 7 is (on pain of
having to write out the 7 times table 100 times), I wouldn’t
set up a biological circuit of Boolean switches in my brain; I
would simply access my memory and quickly reply “twenty-
eight”. 
The 
external 
sound 
“four 
times 
seven?”
automatically 
triggered 
the 
rather 
mindless 
response
“twenty-eight!” in my head.
Later 
in 
school 
I 
learned 
the 
mechanics 
of 
long
multiplication. As an adult, it’s something I hardly ever use,
and although the technique is in my memory somewhere,
it’s not as readily accessible as it was when I was seven or
eight years old.
And so, if I were forced to multiply 24 by 37, I wouldn’t
use Boolean logic at all. I would simply try as best I could
to retrieve the relevant patterns from memory: the patterns
that describe the algorithm for doing long multiplication,
then the pattern associated with the “times tables”. Finally,
I 
would 
need 
some 
“scratchpad” 
memory 
(of 
which 
I
deﬁnitely don’t have enough these days) for storing the
intermediate steps. All in all, it’s a totally diﬀerent process
to that of a digital computer.
The eﬀort of retrieving data from memory takes energy.
If a friend insisted I do this multiplication calculation when
walking along the road with him (don’t ask me why), then I
would have to stop talking, stop walking, close my eyes,
possibly put my hands over my ears and do nothing else but
focus on the problem at hand. Presumably, I do this in
order to channel all the available energy into performing
accurately the required data transfers from memory. To do
this, I shut down all other tasks that may be consuming
energy and focus all available energy to the task in hand.
Of course, this is an unusual state to be in. Before my
friend asked me to multiply these numbers together, I was
happily multitasking: walking down the road (unthinkingly
putting one leg in front of the other), chatting about the
latest news, looking up and admiring the clouds in the sky
and the leaves on the trees, listening to the birds chirping
and wondering what I might have for lunch. In this state,
the available energy being fed to my brain was being used
for a multitude of tasks.
In 
his 
renowned 
book 
Thinking, 
Fast 
and 
Slow
, 
the
psychologist Daniel Kahneman has famously described the
brain in a binary way rather similar to this
6
. Most of the
time (when walking, chatting, looking around) it operates
in a mode he calls “System 1”. System 1 denotes a rather
fast, automatic, eﬀortless mode of operation. By contrast,
with eyes closed, focussing on one speciﬁc task, the brain is
in what Kahneman calls System 2, a much slower, more
deliberate mode of operation.
This notion of a binary mode of operation had in fact
been described some years earlier by cognitive scientist
Guy Claxton in his book 
Hare Brain, Tortoise Mind
7
.
Here
“tortoise mind” describes a more playful, leisureful and
dreamy state, somewhat akin to Kahneman’s System 1. By
contrast, “hare brain” describes the type of thinking that
requires 
reason, 
logic 
and 
deliberate 
thinking, 
as 
in
Kahneman’s System 2. The key point of Claxton’s book, one
that 
seems 
completely 
consistent 
with 
the 
experiences
described earlier, is that the leisurely tortoise mind, for all
its apparent aimlessness, is just as intelligent as the more
logical hare brain.
Here I want to think about energy as a way of describing
this bimodal operation of the brain.
Every second, the brain consumes about 20 joules of
energy. That is, it uses the same amount of power, 20W, as
an electric light bulb. This is tiny compared with the power
used by a supercomputer that runs a weather forecast
model—about 
20MW, 
six 
orders 
of 
magnitude 
more. 
It
seems from studies of blood ﬂow into the brain that there
isn’t much variation in the total amount of power consumed
whether we are in System 1 or System 2. However, the fact
that we can multitask when in System 1 suggests that, in
this mode, power is spread between many active tasks, so
that power 
per active task
is especially low. By contrast,
when operating in System 2 mode, all the available power
is perhaps focussed on a single task, making the power 
per
active task
that much higher. For this reason, I will refer to
the two modes as the low-power mode and the power-
intensive mode.
Now, there are some 80 billion neurons in the brain.
Information 
propagates 
along 
the 
slender 
axons 
of 
a
neuron (see Fig. 46) by means of spiky electric signals.
However, the axons are so long that these electric signals
would have completely dissipated long before they got to
the end of an axon, were it not for “protein transistors”
which boost the spikes at regular intervals along the axon.
These protein transistors comprise small channels in the
membrane, which otherwise act as an insulating sheath
around 
the 
axon. 
Electrically 
charged 
ions—essentially
atoms such as sodium or potassium with the electron in
their outer shell stripped oﬀ—ﬂow through these channels
in response to the voltage ﬂuctuations from the signal
propagating along the axon. By being correlated with the
signal propagating along the axon, the ﬂow of ions can
amplify the signal.
FIG. 46. The diﬀerent parts of a neuron. Electric “spike” signals are carried
along the axon. The signals are ampliﬁed by “protein transistors”
associated with ﬂows of ions across voltage-gated channels known as
“nodes of Ranvier”.
As I mentioned in Chapter 3, if we start to reduce the
voltage across regular silicon transistors, they start acting
unreliably 
and 
the 
switching 
process 
starts 
to 
become
susceptible to thermal noise. Could it be that in the low-
power mode, there is insuﬀicient energy to ensure that the
protein transistors operate completely deterministically?
The answer seems to be yes. There is evidence that
slender axons with a diameter less than about 1 micron
(10
−3
mm) 
are 
indeed 
susceptible 
to 
noise
8
. 
Now, 
the
median size of axons in the human brain is about 0.5
microns
9
. 
Hence, 
particularly 
in 
situations 
when 
the
available 20W of power is being spread around the 80
billion neurons in the brain—i.e., in the low-power mode
when the brain is multitasking—the operation of any one of
the active neurons will be susceptible to noise.
There are advantages and disadvantages to such slender
neurons. A disadvantage is that an electrical signal travels
more slowly along an axon as the axon diameter decreases.
If it is vital to have a super- fast reaction time to evade
predators, then a decrease in axon diameter may be an
evolutionary dead end. On the other hand, if one can take
advantage of social groupings to fend oﬀ predators, then
the disadvantage of slower reaction times may be more
than compensated by the extra processing power arising
from being able to cram more neurons into a given space.
If fast reaction time is not vital, could a susceptibility to
noise 
arising 
from 
neuron 
miniaturisation 
have 
an
evolutionary 
advantage? 
Let’s 
look 
at 
some 
of 
the
processes discussed in Chapter 3, in which noise was found
to be beneﬁcial, and see if these might apply to the way the
brain works.
For example, consider the process of stochastic rounding
—using noise to truncate a signal comprising many bits.
With reference to Fig. 17, if stochastic rounding was being
performed on the raw visual data that was being received
by our eyes, and the truncated data was sent along our
neurons 
to 
the 
part 
of 
the 
brain 
responsible 
for 
our
cognitive perceptions, then perhaps we would be able to
perceive the full grey shading, even though the brain was
receiving only the 1-bit black-and-white bits.
The simulated annealing algorithm appears to describe
well how we humans go about ﬁnding a solution to a
problem. We start by applying some well-known tricks (like
reductio ad absurdum
) that have helped us in the past. This
gets us some way to the solution, but then we become
completely stuck. We rack our brains for new ideas that will
help us out of the rut. If the ideas take us forward, we
accept them. However, in the initial phase we are quite
prepared to go back to step 0 if it will help us ﬁnd a better
route to the ﬁnal solution. In these early stages, we are
potentially prepared to accept any old idea, however crazy
it might seem. But when we have made a lot of progress
and feel that we are getting close to the ﬁnal solution, we’ll
be much more discriminating with the ideas that come to
us, accepting only the really promising ones for further
analysis. Remarkably, the simulated annealing algorithm
also describes quite well the process of evolution by natural
selection.
However, in order to generate random new ideas, the
brain 
must 
be 
susceptible 
to 
noise, 
and 
this 
appears
possible only when the brain is in its low-power mode. This
could explain why eureka moments occur when we are
relaxing and not concentrating hard on a problem. It is in
such a relaxation mode that the presence of noise can help
us jump out of a cognitive roadblock and in this way
advance our understanding.
By contrast, when the brain operates in the power-
intensive mode, i.e., when other units are disabled to allow
as much of the 20W of power to be focussed, then it
appears there is enough energy available to the active
neurons that they operate deterministically. This is when
we can check whether the crazy ideas the low-power mode
provides us are actually any good.
The 
theoretical 
physicist 
Michael 
Berry 
has 
dubbed
creative 
ideas 
which 
seemingly 
arise 
out 
of 
nowhere
“claritons”—mysterious particle-like units of creativity. It
would seem that the clariton is a product of the low-power
mode. However, Berry notes that more often than not, the
appearance 
of 
a 
clariton 
is, 
in 
the 
cold 
light 
of 
day,
annihilated by an “anti-clariton”. It would appear that the
anti-clariton is the product of the power-intensive mode of
thinking, triggered when you apply some logical thinking to
the idea that has popped out of the blue. It’s just as well we
have the capability to check all the crazy ideas that come
out of our head.
If 
these 
ideas 
are 
right, 
then 
the 
process 
we 
call
creativity—what 
otherwise 
might 
be 
called 
inductive
reasoning
10
—involves a synergy between the low-power
and the power-intensive modes: in other words, a close
interplay 
between 
stochasticity 
and 
determinism. 
Being
susceptible to noise, the low-power mode is producing a
stream of sometimes bizarre, sometimes useful ideas. The
more deterministic power-intensive mode then ﬁlters these,
rejecting the more bizarre ones and retaining the ones that
stand 
up 
to 
scrutiny. 
Like 
the 
simulated 
annealing
algorithm, the closer we come to the ﬁnal solution, the
more discriminating we are about the crazy ideas that we
will pursue.
In 
The Emperor’s New Mind
11
, Roger Penrose famously
asked what it is that makes us capable of understanding
Gödel’s theorem, the famous twentieth-century theorem
described 
in 
Chapter 2, 
that 
there 
are 
mathematically
sound statements that assert their unprovability. He makes
the point that if our brains worked by algorithm, we would
not 
be 
able 
to 
understand 
the 
concept 
that 
there 
are
mathematical truths that cannot be proved algorithmically.
The fact that we do understand this could perhaps be
explained if random noise, something not describable by
algorithm, is a necessary element of cognition. Of course,
you may complain that in Chapter 11 I was arguing that
even the most fundamental type of noise, quantum noise, is
not truly random, but is associated with some kind of
chaotic 
determinism 
described 
by 
the 
cosmological
invariant set postulate. However, as discussed in Chapter 2,
a fractal invariant set is itself algorithmically undecidable.
Hence, if the brain is susceptible to this most fundamental
type of noise, then it truly is not fully describable by
algorithm.
I’m not the ﬁrst to appeal to randomness in this way. In
his inﬂuential paper “The Imitation Game
12
” Alan Turing
himself 
commented, 
“It 
is 
wise 
to 
include 
a 
random
element in a learning machine”, and cited yet another
simple 
example 
where 
noise 
can 
be 
used 
to 
ﬁnd 
the
solution to a problem eﬀiciently.
In 
this 
context, 
it 
is 
interesting 
to 
note 
that 
if 
the
miniaturisation 
of 
transistors 
continued, 
quantum
tunnelling eﬀects would make the transistors unreliable as
switches. But if we take the view that noise is a necessary
resource for creativity, then taking at least some of the
transistors into this noisy quantum regime, and at the same
time 
reducing 
the 
voltage 
across 
them, 
could 
be 
an
important step in making a creative AI system capable, for
example, of coming up with interesting new mathematical
theorems.
Perhaps future AI devices will comprise two classes of
chip: the conventional power-intensive chips, performing
computations deterministically, where determinism is vital
(you don’t want a computer to send money to an incorrect
bank account because it got the last digit of the account
number wrong), and a new class of low-power noisy chips,
performing 
computations 
stochastically. 
Data 
transfer
between these chips and to memory would be performed at
low 
numerical 
precision, 
using 
the 
stochastic 
rounding
ideas we’ve discussed.
« »
LET’S LOOK AT SOME possible practical consequences of
regarding the brain as a noisy energy-eﬀicient organ.
Every day we have to make decisions, some 35,000 of
them according to some sources. Most are unimportant—
whether to watch TV this evening or read a book—and
barely 
require 
much 
eﬀort. 
A 
few 
are 
important 
and
require careful thought before we can decide what to do.
If we simply characterise decisions as important ones
and unimportant ones, then probably most of us follow a
simple strategy in making decisions: let the low-power
mode make the many unimportant decisions in our life, and
our analytic power-intensive mode make the fewer diﬀicult
decisions. Hence, we clearly don’t weigh the pros and cons
of putting the left foot forward, having just put the right
foot forward, whilst walking down the street. By contrast,
for the agonising decisions, we typically set out the pros
and cons as carefully as we can. We think hard about each
of these, and how much weight to give them. The power-
intensive mode is hard at work for these diﬀicult types of
decisions.
This partition of decisions to the low-power and power-
intensive modes makes good use of the available energy in
the 
brain. 
It 
allows 
us 
to 
process 
those 
thousands 
of
decisions we make each day rather eﬀiciently. It means that
the default mode the brain uses for making a decision is the
low-power mode. We might call this strategy “lazy”, but if
we 
dealt 
with 
each 
decision 
using 
our 
power-intensive
mode, we would hardly get to decision ﬁfty before it was
time to go to bed at the end of the day. Defaulting most
decisions to the low-power mode is manifestly a rational
strategy.
There are some consequences of this. One is that it
makes us appear irrational, even though I believe we are
not being irrational. Here is a now famous example of our
seeming irrationality. A bat and ball cost $1.10. The bat
cost one dollar more than the ball. How much does the ball
cost?
The obvious answer, the one that most people intuitively
respond with, is 10 cents. However, this is not the correct
answer. If the ball cost 10 cents, then the bat would cost
$1.10 
and 
the 
total 
cost 
would 
be 
$1.20. 
The 
correct
answer is 5 cents.
According to Kahneman, in his book 
Thinking, Fast and
Slow
, more than 50 percent of students at Harvard, MIT
and Princeton gave the incorrect answer. Kahneman found
these results shocking—seemingly proving we are not the
rational species we think we are. But this conclusion is
wrong in my view. These are the top students in the US.
They 
were 
selected 
by 
highly 
competitive 
exams 
and
interviews. There must be something deeper going on.
Some commentators feel that these examples are too
artiﬁcial for any useful conclusions to be drawn. However, I
disagree. I believe the key to resolving this dilemma is to
think about energy. The question of how much energy it is
rational to assign to making a decision, or to answering a
question, is surely going to be important when energy per
second is limited to 20W.
Suppose I came up to you in the street and asked you the
bat and ball question. How much energy would you be
willing to expend answering it? If you were a student
walking to the local examination hall to take some exam,
you might well decide not to expend any energy at all. You
don’t want to exhaust your brain before you have even
started the exam. In this circumstance the rational answer
is no answer, and you politely respond that you are sorry
but you don’t have time to answer the question.
On the other hand, suppose it’s your professor asking
the question during one of your classes: then you might
decide that you should expend a little energy answering it.
However, if she has told you that the answers will be
anonymised, and that there are no credits for answering
correctly, then perhaps you might conclude that there is no
good 
reason 
to 
expend 
a 
lot 
of 
energy 
answering 
the
question, 
not 
least 
because 
you 
have 
other 
homework
assignments to complete, for which you will get credits.
Under these conditions, your answer to the bat and ball
question may well be 10 cents. It’s the rational answer,
given the rational decision to expend only a small amount
of energy answering the problem. That is to say, it is
perfectly rational to give the wrong answer.
By contrast, if it mattered to you that the answer should
be right—for instance, if it was a question on your exam—it
would be rational to utilise all available energy to try to
answer correctly. You analyse the question carefully in
power-intensive mode. Now 5 cents is the rational answer.
The 
problem 
with 
a 
strategy 
based 
on 
maximising
energy eﬀiciency is that it can lead to a bias in the use of
the low-power mode on decisions to problems that lie on a
borderline between being important and unimportant. As a
result, we end up bitterly regretting some decisions which
we made too glibly, not giving them enough care and
attention. I can think of examples in my own life.
So, what is the solution to this type of problem? I believe
that it is to become aware that for many of us, our brain
has 
a 
tendency 
to 
systematically 
downgrade 
the
importance of decisions that lie near the interface between
being important and unimportant by sending them to the
low-power mode to sort out. If we are aware of this, then
we can potentially become more sensitive to the small
alarm bells that might ring, and not simply dismiss them
without further thought. We can then start to engage the
power-intensive 
mode 
a 
little 
more 
on 
the 
problem.
Essentially, it’s about making a little more use of that all-
important synergy between the low-power and the power-
intensive modes—the synergy that makes us the intelligent,
creative species we are.
On the other hand, there are some decisions that are
obviously important, when we know we have to engage our
power-intensive 
analytic 
mode. 
The 
problem 
here 
is 
a
diﬀerent one. It is that being overly analytic can paralyse
us with indecision. Like Buridan’s donkey, stuck midway
between a bale of hay and a pail of water, we cannot ﬁgure
out which way to turn. This was me when I was trying to
decide whether to change ﬁelds at the end of my PhD
studies.
Perhaps here we can learn from the eureka moments of
those eminent scientists. The creative steps on the route to
their breakthroughs came from a subtle interplay between
the two modes. The power-intensive mode had taken the
creative process only so far. The crucial eureka moment
actually occurred in low-power mode, it seems, precisely
because the brain was susceptible to noise.
Learning from the eureka moments suggests that we
should certainly start by listing the pros and cons and
thinking hard about them, maybe for several days, for these
diﬀicult and important decisions. Indeed, it makes sense to
try 
to 
construct 
an 
ensemble 
of 
worlds 
where 
these
diﬀerent pros and cons are realised. One doesn’t need to
actually derive probabilities from such an ensemble. Merely
constructing “storylines” portrayed in individual ensemble
members is itself an important step. In fact, the idea of
ensemble members as plausible “storylines” is becoming
important 
in 
climate 
science, 
in 
situations 
where
probabilities are diﬀicult to compute
13
.
But then we should try to get on with the rest of our
lives, 
as 
far 
as 
this 
is 
possible. 
Like 
with 
the 
eureka
moments, the hope would be that in moments of relaxation,
our instinctive side will tell us what is the right decision.
Again, the idea is to make constructive use of noise in low-
power mode to provide new perspectives which our more
analytic, deterministic power-intensive mode is failing to
give us.
The order in which we engage the two cognitive systems
is crucially important here. If we engage the low-power
mode 
ﬁrst 
and 
come 
to 
an 
instinctive 
preliminary
conclusion, this could colour and distort the way in which
we do the analytic thinking when engaging the power-
intensive 
mode. 
This 
is 
where 
we 
are 
susceptible 
to
“conﬁrmation bias”: we simply ﬁnd evidence that supports
our gut instinct and disregard the rest. The power-intensive
mode is no longer the dispassionate, disinterested analyser
of information. Rather, we should engage the low-power
mode 
after
we have got as far as we can with the power-
intensive mode. That’s the way the great eureka moments
were made.
For example, if you start with a gut feeling that climate
change is for woke tree huggers and that its importance as
a problem facing humanity is overblown, then you will
simply seek out naysayer websites and other sources of
information that support this instinctive view. Conversely, if
you come to the problem from the intuitive perspective that
we have polluted the planet with our carbon emissions and
are reaping the inevitable catastrophic consequences, then
similarly, you are likely to read only alarmist websites
about climate change. This is why I think that the IPCC
assessment 
reports 
are 
probably 
the 
best 
disinterested
source 
of 
information 
on 
climate 
science—though,
unfortunately, 
they 
are 
not 
the 
easiest 
to 
read. 
The
scientists I have worked with on IPCC are typically not
politically motivated—they merely want to present their
science as accurately and as accessibly as possible.
If there is a bottom line here, it is that in decision-
making, whether with relatively unimportant decisions or
important 
decisions, 
we 
should 
be 
aware 
of 
the
extraordinary capabilities we have when we make use of
that 
interplay 
between 
the 
low-power 
stochastic 
and
power-intensive 
deterministic 
modes 
of 
our 
brain—the
modes that appear to be central in making us the creative,
imaginative species that we are.

13
Free Will, Consciousness and
God
I
T 
SEEMS 
THE 
BRAIN 
IS 
AN 
ENERGY-EFFICIENT 
ORGAN.
LET’S TAKE this argument a bit further. Apart from noise,
what 
other 
physics 
might 
arise 
in 
the 
brain 
as 
a
consequence of its energy eﬀiciency?
Let’s return to the issue of signal propagation along the
long, slender axons of neurons. As mentioned, these signals
have to be boosted by protein transistors as they propagate
from one end of the axon to the other. These transistors are
powered by the ﬂow of ions in the neuronal membrane
walls of the axons.
Scientists from the University of Salzburg
1
conclude that
there isn’t enough available energy to explain these ion
ﬂow rates if the physics of ion transfer is entirely classical.
Instead, they have proposed a quantum process that will do
the job using much smaller amounts of energy. The idea is
to think of an ion not as a little particle but instead as an
extended 
quantum 
wavefunction. 
The 
front 
of 
the
wavefunction can then manipulate the electrical potential
in the ion channel in such a way as to allow the rest of the
wavefunction to propagate through eﬀiciently and without
hindrance. The timescale for this process to work has to be
very small indeed and is entirely consistent with the very
fast quantum decoherence timescales that operate in the
brain.
If this is correct, then we can’t think of the brain as if it
were a conventional classical computer. It must be some
kind 
of 
noisy, 
classical/quantum-hybrid 
computer. 
What
does this mean in practice? The hallmark of a quantum
computer 
is 
that 
it 
can 
perform 
certain 
types 
of
calculations 
much 
faster 
than 
a 
conventional 
classical
computer. 
This 
is 
called 
quantum 
supremacy 
and 
has
already been established for somewhat arcane and useless
types of computation
2
. In Chapter 11, I described brieﬂy
how invariant set theory could explain this speed-up: if the
laws of quantum physics describe the geometry of the
invariant set, then a quantum computer, driven by these
geometric laws, can access information in the neighbouring
trajectories on the invariant set. By contrast, a classical
computer can access information only on a single state-
space trajectory, the one that corresponds to the world of
classical physics.
As we humans don’t have the prodigious computing
capability of quantum computers, we are manifestly not
pure 
quantum 
computers. 
However, 
perhaps 
we
nevertheless 
have 
some 
residual 
capabilities 
associated
with a weak cognitive perception of these neighbouring
trajectories on the invariant set—what could be called a
“sixth” 
sense. 
This 
might 
help 
explain 
some 
of 
the
diﬀerences between us and conventional computers. This
idea is speculative. But let’s run with it for a while and see
where it gets us. How might such a sixth sense manifest
itself?
I am not the ﬁrst to propose a role for quantum physics
in the brain. Roger Penrose and Stuart Hameroﬀ
3
have
proposed microtubules inside neurons as a place where
quantum 
cognition 
takes 
place. 
Perhaps 
that’s 
right,
though I’m not enough of an expert to have a ﬁrm view on
this. In any case, I don’t think that anything I discuss ahead
is inconsistent with Penrose and Hameroﬀ’s basic proposal.
Rather, I believe what I am proposing builds on these ideas.
« »
THE TOPIC OF FREE will is one that has vexed philosophers
for millennia. The twelfth-century Persian poet Jalāl al-Dīn
Rūmī wrote
4
, “There is a disputation that will continue until
mankind 
is 
raised 
from 
the 
dead, 
between 
the
necessitarians 
and 
the 
partisans 
of 
free 
will.” 
Here 
a
necessitarian is a determinist, one, like me, who believes
that the laws of physics are ultimately deterministic in
form.
Over the centuries, debate on free will has focussed on
whether 
determinism 
is 
compatible 
with 
free 
will.
Compatibilists believe that if free will is deﬁned in terms of
an absence of constraints preventing you from doing as you
want to do, then a belief in free will is indeed compatible
with a belief in determinism. From this perspective, free
will is not a contentious topic. For some decisions—what
holiday to take, what car to drive, what house to live in—I
am not a free agent; ﬁnancial constraints limit my choice.
And of course, I am not free to break the laws of physics. I
am not free to ﬂap my arms up and down and ﬂy away from
an unpleasant situation. On the other hand, I play golf on
Sunday 
mornings 
of 
my 
own 
free 
will—there 
are 
no
constraints that I am aware of that make me so do.
However, incompatibilists believe that free will cannot
be explained in this way. For them, free will somehow
denotes an ability “to have done otherwise”. Such an ability
would appear to be incompatible with determinism since it
seems to imply that the world could evolve in multiple ways
from a given state—one way in which I did what I did, and
one or more other ways in which I did otherwise.
I don’t like the idea of indeterminacy at a fundamental
level, even though, as mentioned, noise is necessary for
modelling complex systems at a practical level. But that
doesn’t make indeterminism wrong. In fact, one of the key
arguments that incompatibilists bring to the table has, in
the past, made me question my belief in determinism. The
argument is that determinism undermines any claim that
we are moral beings. The philosopher Peter van Inwagen
has summarised the argument like this
5
:
“If 
determinism 
is 
true, 
then 
our 
acts 
are 
the
consequences of the laws of nature and events in the
remote past. But it is not up to us what went on before we
were born; and neither is it up to us what the laws of
nature are. Therefore, the consequences of these things
(including our own acts) are not up to us.”
Imagine 
a 
man, 
convicted 
of 
murder, 
about 
to 
be
sentenced. When the judge asks the man if he has any ﬁnal
comments before he passes sentence, the convict says:
“But I had no choice in whether to commit the crime. It was
already determined that I would commit the crime at the
time of the Big Bang”. Is this enough for the judge to set
him free?
You see the problem. Do we excuse Hitler for his crimes
against humanity because it was predetermined in the
cosmic 
initial 
conditions 
that 
he 
would 
be 
a 
mass
murderer? 
Compatibilist 
philosopher 
Daniel 
Dennett
argues
6
that we simply do not exempt someone from blame,
or 
indeed 
praise, 
because 
we 
think 
he 
could 
not 
do
otherwise. Dennett cites Martin Luther, who initiated the
Protestant Reformation declaring, “Here I stand, I can do
no other.” According to Dennett, Luther was taking full
responsibility for acting of his own free will, whatever free
will means.
Despite being a compatibilist like Dennett, I don’t think
the moral argument against determinism can be simply
swept away as Dennett states. This is an issue about which
there is a vast literature to which I can’t do justice here.
Instead, I am simply going to try to add something to the
discussion, based on ideas from the geometry of chaos.
Indeed, I want to focus on a question which I don’t see
addressed much in discussions of free will, but which I
believe is important. Why is it that people feel so strongly,
so viscerally, that they could have done otherwise? I feel
this every Sunday morning when I play golf. I drive home
almost 
always 
disappointed 
with 
my 
eﬀorts. 
I 
chastise
myself with thoughts like “If only I hadn’t rushed my swing
on the sixth, I wouldn’t have hit the ball out of bounds on
the left.” And yet I am an unashamed determinist, for
whom the notion that I could have done otherwise—that I
could have not rushed my swing on the sixth—makes no
sense. 
It’s 
as 
if 
my 
power-intensive 
mode, 
my 
careful
analytic mode of thinking, says, “The idea that I could have
done otherwise makes no sense.” However, my low-power
intuitive mode of thinking says, “Oh yes it does!” So, what
is going on here? Computers, I suppose, do not have these
feelings. Why do we?
This problem is not solved by indeterminism. If it were
randomness in the laws of physics that made me do what I
did, then that wouldn’t explain why I feel so strongly that I
could have done otherwise. It seems the problem rather
transcends the issue of determinism vs. indeterminism.
Perhaps 
there 
is 
a 
simple 
explanation. 
We 
have
memories of past occasions when, in similar circumstances,
we did otherwise—when we didn’t rush our golf swings, for
example. 
Does 
the 
feeling 
that 
we 
could 
have 
done
otherwise merely reﬂect such memories of previous, similar
occasions? 
Whilst 
this 
is 
a 
possible 
explanation, 
I 
am
somehow not convinced. More importantly, I don’t think it
would convince those who have a strong belief that they
could have done otherwise. As such, I don’t think this
explains the very visceral nature of a belief in free will.
Here is another possible way of looking at the issue.
Suppose our cognition is inﬂuenced by the yin and yang of
quantum physics, as described in Chapter 11. In particular,
suppose 
we 
have 
some 
cognition 
of 
neighbouring
7
counterfactual worlds on the invariant set (the strands of
rope in Fig. 44a that are close to the strand of rope
corresponding to our current reality). These neighbouring
trajectories 
correspond 
to 
worlds 
in 
which 
we 
did
otherwise 
than 
what 
we 
actually 
did. 
With 
a 
weak
perception of these neighbouring worlds, the brain would
interpret 
them 
as 
implying 
an 
ability 
to 
have 
done
otherwise. My suggestion is that it is our weak perception
of 
these 
neighbouring 
worlds—related 
to 
the 
fact 
that
energy 
eﬀiciency 
demands 
that 
quantum 
physics 
does
operate in the brain—gives us this extraordinarily visceral
feeling that we could have done otherwise, even though we
couldn’t have 
actually
done otherwise. A purely classical
computer 
could 
never 
experience 
this, 
of 
course. 
A
quantum computer experiences it much more strongly, I
would 
contend, 
every 
time 
it 
performs 
a 
nonclassical
computation.
There is a corollary to all this. It could be that our
perception 
of 
these 
neighbouring 
worlds 
is 
suﬀiciently
weak 
that 
we 
are 
unable 
to 
distinguish 
between
neighbouring trajectories that lie on the invariant set and
hypothetical trajectories that lie in the fractal gaps of the
invariant set. If this is right, our brain extends the feeling “I
could have done otherwise” to all counterfactual worlds,
irrespective 
of 
whether 
they 
do 
or 
do 
not 
satisfy 
the
invariant set postulate. As discussed in Chapter 11, in
quantum contexts, counterfactual reasoning can sometimes
be ﬂawed reasoning. However, perhaps our sixth sense
isn’t suﬀiciently well honed to distinguish neighbouring
trajectories on and oﬀ the invariant set. This is why, in my
view, 
quantum 
physics 
can 
sometimes 
seem 
intuitively
weird. Theories of counterfactual causation
8
—models of the
world 
which 
permit 
more 
or 
less 
unlimited 
use 
of
counterfactual reasoning—should therefore be treated with
extreme caution. Even though they 
seem
reasonable, they
may 
not 
be, 
especially 
when 
being 
used 
to 
describe
quantum physics.
Let’s 
return 
to 
the 
more 
important 
conundrum, 
the
diﬀiculty of explaining how, under determinism, we can
have 
a 
sense 
of 
moral 
responsibility. 
Recall 
the 
man
convicted of murder, about to be sentenced. He has just
said to the judge that he had no choice in whether or not to
commit 
the 
crime, 
simply 
because 
it 
was 
already
determined what he would do at the time of the Big Bang.
However, the judge (let’s say) has read this book and
replies to the accused:
“I’m afraid that what happened at the time of the Big
Bang isn’t relevant. Your actions are determined by the
geometry of the cosmological invariant set.”
The 
convict 
immediately 
responds, 
as 
the 
judge
suspected he would:
“All 
right 
then. 
If 
the 
cosmological 
initial 
conditions
didn’t make me do it, then the invariant set made me do it.
I don’t care, the argument is still the same.”
But now the judge can deliver the knock-out blow:
“But it’s not the same! You are part of the invariant set.
Your behaviour determines the geometry of the invariant
set 
as 
much 
as 
the 
geometry 
of 
the 
invariant 
set
determines your behaviour. That is to say, you 
do
have
some moral responsibility for what you did, determinism
notwithstanding!”
The convict is dumbfounded. What on earth does the
judge mean?
There 
is 
something 
very 
important 
here 
that 
needs
unpacking. 
In 
classical 
physics, 
e.g., 
as 
discussed 
in
Chapters 1 through 3, we treat the initial conditions as
completely separate from the dynamical laws of physics
that evolve systems in time. Compared with other states of
the universe, the Big Bang initial conditions are considered
fundamental in determining, alongside the dynamical laws,
how the universe will evolve. This is why Inwagen refers to
conditions in the remote past.
However, in invariant set theory, the initial conditions
and the dynamical laws are not independent of each other:
they are both subservient to the geometry of the fractal
invariant 
set. 
Because 
of 
this, 
the 
Big 
Bang 
initial
conditions are no more fundamental than any other state
on the invariant set. As such, the invariant set is timeless:
no point on the invariant set, corresponding to a state of
the universe at a particular time, is more fundamental than
any other point.
An aphorism for Einstein’s theory of general relativity
was 
formulated 
by 
American 
physicist 
John 
Archibald
Wheeler. He said of general relativity that “[the geometry
of] space-time tells matter how to move, and matter tells
space-time how to curve.” I like to think of the invariant set
postulate in a very similar way. That is, we can say that the
geometry of the invariant set tells matter how to evolve in
time, whilst matter tells the invariant set how to shape
itself. But 
we
are part of the matter that evolves in time on
the 
invariant 
set, 
and 
how 
we 
evolve 
in 
time 
in 
part
describes our behaviour. Hence, we can say in a self-
referential way, the invariant set geometry determines how
we behave, whilst our behaviour determines the geometry
of the invariant set.
This is the duality to which the judge is referring. The
argument “the invariant set made me do it,” whilst true, is
incomplete. The other part of the argument is that what we
do determines the geometry of the invariant set. That may
sound like we have abandoned the notion of determinism.
But in fact, all we are abandoning is the notion that our
behaviour is that of a computational automaton. We can
appeal 
to 
the 
discussion 
in 
Chapter 
2, 
that 
a 
fractal
invariant set is algorithmically undecidable. As a result, any
attempt to emulate our behaviour computationally must be
inherently stochastic. In this sense we are not mindless
automata—we do have moral responsibility. This is entirely
consistent with Penrose’s assertion that conscious minds
are not algorithmic.
This 
could 
be 
a 
plausible 
explanation 
of 
why
determinism does not absolve us from moral responsibility.
« »
LIKE FREE WILL, THE meaning of consciousness continues to
baﬄe us. And also like free will, the literature on this topic
is vast and would take a separate book to review properly.
In recent years, for example, a theory of consciousness
based on what is called “integrated information theory” has
developed
9
—the more so-called integrated information a
system has, the more the system is said to be conscious. As
in the case of free will, I do not seek to challenge these
theories, but merely ask whether the geometry of chaos
could add to them.
Here I want to focus on two aspects of this problem. The
ﬁrst 
is 
how 
the 
geometry 
of 
chaos 
might 
help 
deﬁne
consciousness in an objective way. The second is to know
how, even in principle, something like consciousness could
emerge 
from 
a 
collection 
of 
otherwise 
inanimate
elementary particles. For many people it seems implausible
that the works of Mozart, Shakespeare and Einstein could
emerge from such a collection of particles. Following ideas
formulated by Plato and Descartes, maybe there really are
two diﬀerent worlds: the physical world and the spiritual
world. As a physicist, I would ﬁnd it hard to accept such a
notion since it would imply there is a core aspect of the
world that cannot be studied by the scientiﬁc method. Of
course, this might be the case, but I am not currently
inclined to give up the idea that science can explain such
matters. Nevertheless, I completely understand why the
idea of emergence of conscious creative creatures from
elementary particles seems completely implausible. As one
scientiﬁc colleague put it, do the electrons and protons in
the 
brain 
somehow 
know 
that 
Mozart 
was 
a 
superior
composer to Mendelssohn (or the Beatles to the Rolling
Stones, in my currency)?
However, perhaps this scepticism about the emergence
of consciousness from elementary particles reﬂects not so
much the domain of applicability of the scientiﬁc method as
scepticism 
about 
methodological 
reductionism—that
“smaller” 
is 
necessarily 
“more 
fundamental”, 
which 
is
certainly part of the credo of contemporary science. In
Chapter 
11, 
I 
argued 
that 
such 
methodological
reductionism is leading us into problems with trying to
understand 
the 
nature 
of 
elementary 
particles. 
To
understand these particles, I argued, it is necessary to
consider, 
as 
equally 
fundamental, 
the 
structure 
of 
the
universe on the very largest scales of space and time. That
is a key message of the invariant set postulate.
Could the same type of top-down argument also help
explain the mystery of consciousness? Well, let’s try to be a
little 
more 
concrete. 
Rather 
than 
ask, 
“What 
is
consciousness?,” let me instead ask, “What does it mean to
be conscious of something speciﬁc?”
If I look up from my laptop screen, the ﬁrst thing I notice
is a cactus plant in a white pot which my son bought my
wife and me as a small present to mark our wedding
anniversary (how nice of him!). The plant pot sits on the
windowsill just behind my desk. Next to it is a small clock
also sitting on the windowsill, a short distance from the
cactus.
When I am focussing on my laptop screen, trying to think
what to write next, I am not at all conscious of either the
cactus or the clock, even though they are in my ﬁeld of
vision. That is to say, even though photons from the sun are
bouncing oﬀ the cactus and entering my brain, I am not
aware of the cactus. In terms of the model my brain has of
the world, when I am focussing on the laptop screen, the
world 
comprises 
simply 
the 
laptop 
screen 
and 
an
undiﬀerentiated set of other objects. As far as this model is
concerned, 
neither 
the 
cactus 
nor 
the 
clock 
has 
an
existence independent of each other, or of the rest of the
world.
On the other hand, if I stop writing and look straight
ahead, perhaps hoping for some inspiration, then, and only
then, I may become conscious of the cactus. My model of
the world now includes a cactus, and perhaps a clock, and
the rest of the external world.
What is going on? It seems to me that it is only when I
am conscious of the cactus and the clock that I sense they
have an existence independent of each other, and of the
other objects in the universe. What does this mean? Well,
as with so much else in this book, let’s think about the
cactus and the clock as two objects in “cactus-clock” state
space. If we are attributing an existence to the cactus that
is independent of the clock, it means that it is possible, in
principle at least, that the position of the cactus can be
altered independently of the clock’s in “cactus-clock” state
space. Now, when I (very occasionally) clean the windowsill
in my study, I will move the cactus relative to the clock.
Perhaps my sense that the cactus has an independent
existence from the clock comes from a memory of moving
the 
cactus 
relative 
to 
the 
clock 
during 
such 
window-
cleaning moments.
However, perhaps there is another explanation. Perhaps
my sixth sense, the one that gave me such a visceral feeling
of free will, could also explain this notion of the cactus as
an object with an independent existence. I argued that our
visceral feeling of free will arises from a perception of
trajectories 
on 
the 
invariant 
set 
that 
are 
close 
to 
the
trajectory 
on 
which 
we 
evolve. 
On 
these 
nearby
trajectories, I did indeed “do otherwise”. In a similar way,
on the nearby trajectories on the invariant set, the position
of the cactus may indeed vary relative to the clock. Perhaps
the 
diﬀerence 
in 
relative 
position 
on 
the 
neighbouring
trajectories is tiny, but nevertheless is enough for me to
sense 
that 
the 
cactus 
and 
the 
clock 
are 
independent
objects, both relative to each other and to the external
world more generally. It’s the quantum yin and yang of real
and counterfactual trajectories on the invariant set once
again.
I am reminded of a feature of smartphone cameras that
lets the camera take a tiny piece of movie—just a fraction
of a second—before ﬁxing on the still photo shot. When you
see the photo, you also see the short movie sequence
preceding 
it. 
Even 
though 
the 
movie 
lasts 
for 
only 
a
fraction of a second, it makes the objects in the static photo
seem much more lifelike. It’s a kind of augmented reality
that the photo alone does not have.
In short, I am suggesting that to be conscious of an
object is to be aware that the object has an existence
independent of the rest of the world. I am speculating that
this awareness is itself a consequence of two claims: that
for reasons of energy eﬀiciency quantum physics does play
a role in cognition, and that the laws of quantum physics at
their 
most 
fundamental 
describe 
the 
geometry 
of 
the
cosmological 
invariant 
set. 
This 
awareness 
gives 
us 
a
visceral augmented sense of reality that mere memories do
not have.
With this in mind, let us return to the question of what
distinguishes us from AI systems. We concluded in the
previous chapter that noise in the brain is a necessary part
of 
the 
answer. 
But 
is 
it 
suﬀicient? 
Is 
consciousness 
a
necessary part of understanding, as Penrose argues it must
be? I don’t know for sure, but if it is, then developing
creative AI will require a close synergy between quantum
and 
classical 
computing, 
in 
addition 
to 
the 
synergy
discussed in the previous chapter between power-intensive
and low-power classical computing.
If this is so, then we have a long way to go before we
create truly intelligent machines. “The singularity”, the day
when machines completely take over, is likely not a few
years away, as some commentators believe; it is many
decades, 
potentially 
centuries 
away. 
We 
will 
need 
our
creative brains for quite some time yet.
« »
IN THE LAST PART of this ﬁnal chapter, I want to discuss
something that would be taboo in a scientiﬁc paper: to
relate the ideas I have been discussing on uncertainty and
the geometry of chaos with the concept of spirituality and
religion.
The 
idea 
that 
there 
is 
more 
to 
us 
humans 
than 
a
collection of inanimate atoms is surely an understandable
response to the notion that the laws of physics are uncaring
mathematical 
equations 
and 
the 
universe 
vast 
and
impersonal. Perhaps if one could show how a human being
is an emergent property of the Schrödinger equation, then
one would have to admit that spirituality is merely a prop
for coping with the challenges of life, with nothing deeper
underpinning the Schrödinger equation. But science cannot
show such a thing. Not by a long way.
From spirituality comes the concept of a caring god, a
supreme and yet personal creator. Many of those who
believe in such a god do so because they seek a purpose to
life. 
The 
idea 
that 
each 
of 
us 
is 
merely 
a 
random
thermodynamic ﬂuctuation with a mean lifetime of some
three score years and ten can make our lives seem rather
pointless. Indeed, according to contemporary cosmological
models with ﬁxed dark energy, humanity is restricted to an
inﬁnitesimally 
short 
blip 
near 
the 
beginning 
of 
the
universe, after which nothing happens at all. It’s as if all
the wonders of nature, the works of Einstein, Shakespeare,
the Beatles and Beethoven, literally count for nothing—they
are 
an 
irrelevance 
for 
the 
utterly 
dilute 
state 
of 
the
universe that will dominate almost all of its existence. As a
dispassionate scientist, perhaps I should simply shrug my
shoulders and say that if that’s the way it is, then that’s the
way it is. But it’s such a depressing scenario. If we were
truly certain that this is the state to which the universe will
evolve, 
then 
why 
bother 
doing 
anything? 
Why 
bother
having children—it’s only prolonging the agony. Why bother
to write this book, other than to pass the time of day? It’s
no 
wonder 
that 
many 
people 
seek 
a 
more 
meaningful
reason for living.
The concept of a god, with whom we will spend eternity
after we die, is somewhat comforting, though the idea of
spending an inﬁnite amount of time in perfect happiness
does sound like it might get slightly boring after a while
(there are only so many rounds of golf you can play in
which you and your playing partners score twenty under
par before the game gets tedious). But leaving these details
aside, the concept of an afterlife is especially appealing as
it provides an opportunity to meet loved ones who departed
life before you do.
As scientists we can mock such views for their naivety or
for their quaint anthropomorphic character (God as a wise
old man with a white beard, for example). However, the
fact 
of 
the 
matter 
is 
that 
there 
are 
many 
profound
uncertainties about the nature of physical reality. We have
discussed some of them here. How can scientists dismiss
views 
about 
religion 
and 
spirituality 
when 
their 
own
theories are riddled with such deep uncertainties?
In science we insist on evidence and revise beliefs when
evidence 
shifts. 
In 
religion 
one 
is 
asked 
to 
have 
faith
without 
compelling 
evidence. 
From 
this 
point 
of 
view,
science and religion seem incompatible. However, it’s often
not as straightforward as this. Scientists and nonscientists
alike 
ponder 
the 
meaning 
of 
their 
lives. 
Are 
we 
just
irrelevant random ﬂuctuations in a universe destined for
some bland heat death, or do our lives have some deeper
purpose? Indeed, could we collectively play an important
role 
in 
the 
evolution 
of 
the 
universe? 
Science 
cannot
answer these questions and so people look elsewhere for
answers. With this in mind, perhaps the geometry of chaos
might also provide new insights into these questions and in
so doing might help bridge the rift that exists between
science, spirituality and religion.
For example, in the cosmological invariant set model,
there is an afterlife. We and our loved ones will be coming
back in future epochs of the universe on trajectories in
state space close to the present one, but none precisely the
same as the present one. We will have an opportunity not to
make the same mistakes again. Equally, we will likely mess
up some of the successes we have made of our lives this
time round. Perhaps it will be better next time round,
perhaps it will be worse. Who knows? That makes it all
quite exciting and something to look forward to (arguably
much more exciting than a future of perfect bliss and
happiness). For each epoch there will be such an afterlife:
there is no ending to the number of trajectories on the
cosmological invariant set.
Indeed, as someone who rejected the Catholic religion of
his childhood
10
, I tend in my more metaphysical moments
to think of the cosmological invariant set as a logical
alternative to the god of my early years. Each point in the
set is a synthesis of everything that is, has been and will be,
indeed of everything that might be, might have been and
could be in the future. It is an omniscient structure: it
knows which states of the universe are physically real and
which are not, even though we, constrained by the notion
of algorithmic decidability, cannot know this. In addition,
the invariant set lies completely outside the constraints of
time: 
as 
an 
entity 
it 
is 
literally 
timeless
11
. 
Does 
the
invariant 
set 
listen 
if 
we 
pray 
to 
it? 
Well, 
our 
hopes,
aspirations 
and 
indeed 
prayers 
are 
part 
of 
the 
self-
referential process discussed earlier in this chapter, the
process that gave us moral responsibility, so in some sense
the answer is yes.
As illustrated in Fig. 6, the Lorenz attractor looks like a
butterﬂy. This is coincidentally apposite, as by studying this
attractor, Lorenz discovered the butterﬂy eﬀect. If we could
ﬁnally construct a picture of the global fractal geometry of
the cosmological invariant set, something I have not been
able to do, and indeed something that will not be possible
to 
do 
precisely 
because 
such 
a 
set 
is 
algorithmically
undecidable, 
what 
would 
it 
look 
like? 
Well, 
as 
a
multidimensional 
object 
it 
would 
have 
many 
diﬀerent
guises depending on which dimensions in state space we
decide to probe. To what would these diﬀerent guises
correspond?
Perhaps the multifaceted face of God?
It feels like an intriguing idea. But of course I have my
doubts.

TIM PALMER is a Royal Society research professor in the
Department 
of 
Physics 
at 
the 
University 
of 
Oxford.
Following a PhD in general relativity theory, Palmer spent
much 
of 
his 
career 
working 
on 
the 
predictability 
and
dynamics of weather and climate, developing probabilistic
ensemble prediction systems across a range of weather and
climate timescales. For this work he was elected a fellow of
the Royal Society and an international member of the
United States National Academy of Sciences. He was also a
lead author for the IPCC climate assessment reports. In
addition, Palmer researches the foundations of quantum
physics and the role of noise and quantum processes in the
brain. 
For 
this 
he 
was 
elected 
a 
member 
of 
the
Foundational Questions Institute and the Association for
Mathematical Consciousness Science.

Author’s Note
1. In my ﬁrst year of graduate research, I was supervised
by one of Sciama’s earlier students, John Miller, who tried
to get me interested in solving the equations of general
relativity by computer. At the time, ironic in the light of my
later research in weather and climate, this didn’t interest
me at all—I wanted to do something more theoretical. For
the 
remaining 
years 
of 
my 
graduate 
research, 
I 
was
supervised directly by Sciama.
2. My main contribution to general relativity theory was
the formulation of some of the ﬁrst quasi-local descriptions
of gravitational energy momentum in general space-times.
See Palmer (1978a, 1978b).

Introduction
1. Who was this woman who called the BBC Weather
Centre? It seems she had a sister living in France. The
sister had been listening to the weather forecasts from the
French Weather Service, Météo-France. A few days before
the 
hurricane 
hit, 
Météo-France 
was 
warning 
of 
an
exceptional 
gale 
in 
the 
English 
Channel 
region. 
Then,
mysteriously, as the time got closer to the expected arrival
of these gales, the warnings from Météo-France stopped.
What was going on? It was the butterﬂy eﬀect at work. For
all its sophisticated use of supercomputers and satellite
technology, 
weather 
forecasting 
in 
the 
1980s 
had 
not
embraced 
the 
concept 
of 
uncertainty. 
Like 
with 
the
astrologers, there were no attempts to estimate error bars
for the forecasts.

Chapter 2: The Geometry of Chaos
1. 
However, 
if 
we 
measure 
positions 
and 
velocities
relative to one of the bodies (e.g., the sun), that reduces
the dimension of state space to 12. And it turns out that
there 
are 
various 
additional 
constraints 
implied 
by
Newton’s laws of motion which reduce the dimension of

2. 
Technically 
the 
state 
space 
of 
the 
Navier-Stokes
equation is inﬁnitely dimensional. However, this assumes
that scales can become inﬁnitesimally small. In practice the
Navier-Stokes equation would break down due to quantum
eﬀects before this limit. However, even if we assume the
equation applies to only macroscopic non-quantum scales,
the dimension of the state space of weather could be as
large as 10
50
, certainly much, much larger than could ever
be represented explicitly in a weather model.
3. 
Actually, 
there 
is 
a 
subtlety 
here. 
If 
you 
use 
a
computer to solve Lorenz’s equations, then in fact the
trajectories will be closed 1-dimensional curves. This is
because 
on 
a 
computer, 
the 
states 
X
, 
Y

and 
Z

are
represented by a ﬁnite number of binary digits, or bits, as
they are known in computer science jargon. In scientiﬁc
computation one typically represents variables like 
X
, 
Y
and
Z
with 64 bits. With this ﬁnite representation, the Lorenz
attractor will be a rather complicated closed 1-dimensional
curve. If instead you allow 
X
, 
Y
and 
Z
to be represented
with 128 bits, the 1-dimensional curve will be longer before
it again joins up to be a closed 1-dimensional curve. Go to
256 bits and the closed curve will be longer still. What
happens 
in 
the 
limit 
when 
we 
imagine 
a 
hypothetical
computer where 
X
, 
Y
and 
Z
can be represented by an
unlimited number of bits? Being based on calculus, the
mathematical 
forms 
of 
Lorenz’s 
equations 
do 
not
themselves put any restrictions on the number of bits used
to represent 
X
, 
Y
and 
Z
.

21. Penrose (1989). Some have argued that since non-
computability concerns properties of inﬁnite sets, it should
play 
no 
role 
in 
physics. 
I 
disagree 
with 
this. 
Non-
computability can be thought of as a limiting case of ﬁnite
systems 
that 
are 
called 
computationally 
irreducible.
Computational 
irreducibility 
is 
a 
concept 
proposed 
by
Wolfram (2002). The idea is that you can’t fully describe
the 
behaviour 
of 
a 
computationally 
irreducible 
system
using a computationally simpler model of that system. In
such a situation, the computationally simpler system will
always be an incomplete representation of the full system.
Non-computability can be thought of as describing the
incompleteness of a system whose simpler representation
is algorithmic but otherwise as complex as we like.

attractor,

impossible.

11. Here is a proof of Bell’s inequality, taken from Rae
(1986).
Let 
n
(+ , −, . ) denote the number of times in a table,
like Table 2, that there is a + symbol in the ﬁrst row, a −
symbol in the second row, and either symbol in the third
row. Similarly, for example, let 
n
(−, . , +) denote the
number of times there is a − symbol in the ﬁrst row, either
symbol in the second row, and a + symbol in the third row.
Generalising in an obvious way we have
A
= 
n
(+, +, . ) = 
n
(+, +, +) + 
n
(+, +, −)
B
= 
n
( . , −, +) = 
n
(+, −, +) + 
n
(−, −, +)
C
= 
n
(+, . , +) = 
n
(+, +, +) + 
n
(+, −, +)
Adding the ﬁrst two equations together we get
A
+ 
B
= 
n
(+, +, +) + 
n
(+, +, −) + 
n
(+, −, +) + 
n
(−, −,
+)
Now the ﬁrst and third terms of this equation, added
together, give 
C
. Hence,
A
+ 
B
= 
C
+ 
n
(+, +, −) + 
n
(−, −, +)
Since the last two terms are never negative
A
+ 
B
≥ 
C
QED.

11. Although the price of solar and wind power has
dropped and will likely continue to drop, these sources of
energy are subject to the vagaries of weather. Of particular
relevance 
in 
the 
UK 
are 
the 
persistent 
high-pressure
systems which can form over northern Europe and last for
weeks, or even months. At the time of writing, the wind
turbines in the North Sea have, for many months, been
producing signiﬁcantly less electricity than expected due to
a “wind drought” caused by such persistent atmospheric
high 
pressure. 
This 
has 
in 
part 
contributed 
to 
a 
big
increase in the price of gas. Although the current spell of
weak winds is surely temporary, we cannot be certain that
these wind droughts won’t get more frequent in the future
due to climate change (as part of a phenomenon known as
“global 
stilling”)—such 
uncertainty 
is 
due 
to 
the
inadequacy of current-generation climate models on the
regional scale, as discussed in Chapter 6. For this reason,
an over-reliance on wind energy is fraught with risk. Such
risk 
can 
be 
mitigated 
by 
developing 
plentiful 
energy
storage capability, and one idea being considered is storing
hydrogen in underground caverns. However, we will need
plentiful hydrogen to decarbonise transport, home heating
and 
industry 
(e.g., 
cement 
manufacture), 
and 
diverting
hydrogen to storage may end up meaning we do not have
enough capacity in total. For this reason, I am personally in
favour of a much stronger role for modern nuclear energy
in the renewable mix, even though at present nuclear
power is more expensive than wind and solar. In fact, the
high temperatures produced by nuclear reactors can be
used to coproduce hydrogen along with electricity. At the
time of writing, China is about to switch on a new type of
nuclear reactor based on the thorium cycle. Thorium-based
reactors have few of the disadvantages of the traditional
uranium 
(U
235
) 
reactors. 
Putting 
all 
this 
together, 
the
estimate that decarbonisation will cost only 1 percent of
GDP may turn out to be an underestimate. However, this
does not undermine the conclusion that, based on a cost-
loss analysis based on VSL, decarbonisation is worth it.
12. Palmer (2020a).

10. This is a mathematical way of saying that if a state
lies on the attractor now, then the evolved state will always
lie on the attractor in the future, and has always lain on the
attractor in the past. Conversely, if a putative state does not
lie on the attractor now, then it will never lie on it in the
future, and has never lain on it in the past. In this sense the
points of the attractor are invariant under the dynamical
laws which evolve states from one time to another.

14. I will describe some of the key ideas in this footnote.
Whether 
we 
are 
discussing 
Bell’s 
experiment 
or 
a
sequential SG experiment with three SG devices, we are
dealing with look-up tables with three directions which I
will write as “1”, “2” and “3”. These three directions can be
represented by three points on the celestial sphere (the
sphere on which the ancients used to think the stars all
lay). Consider the spherical triangle whose vertices are
these 
three 
points. 
In 
invariant 
set 
theory, 
the 
angle
between two arcs of such a triangle must be a rational
angle of the form (
n
/ 
p
) × 360°. In addition, the cosine of
the angular arc length of any of the sides of this triangle
must be a rational number of the form 
m
/ 
p
. Here, 
m
, 
n
and
p
are all whole numbers, and cosine is the elementary
trigonometric function taught in high school. It turns out
that, using the so-called cosine rule for spherical triangles,
together with Niven’s theorem from number theory, it is
impossible to satisfy these conditions for such triangles if 
p
is a prime number greater than 11. The situation is similar
to Penrose’s “impossible” triangle—the tribar. That is to
say, it is possible to implement the constraints of invariant
set theory for any two sides of the spherical triangle, but
not the full three.
In the case of Bell’s experiment, this has the following
implications. Suppose Alice measures her particle’s spin in
the 1 direction and Bob measures his particle’s spin in the
2 direction. By requiring that the cosine of the angular
distance between the 1 vertex and the 2 vertex of our
spherical triangle be rational—no problem with that—Alice
can deduce that had she measured her particle in Bob’s 2
direction, the spin would have been the opposite to what
Bob observed. So far so good. In addition, Bob can assert
counterfactually 
that 
although 
he 
actually 
chose 
the 
2
direction, 
he 
might 
have 
chosen 
the 
3 
direction. 
This
assertion 
requires 
the 
cosine 
of 
the 
angular 
distance
between the 2 and 3 vertices to be rational. Again, no
problem with that. But now Niven’s theorem kicks in to say
that the cosine of the angular distance between the 1 and 3
vertices—of 
the 
third 
side 
of 
the 
triangle—cannot 
be
rational. This means that Alice 
cannot
deduce that had she
measured her particle in Bob’s counterfactual 3 direction,
she would have got the opposite result to Bob’s. Following
the logic of this is a bit of a mental stretch, but it implies
that it is impossible for Alice and Bob’s particles to have
complete 
look-up 
tables 
for 
all 
three 
measurement
directions. 
And 
without 
complete 
look-up 
tables, 
it 
is
impossible to assert that invariant set theory must satisfy
Bell’s 
inequality, 
even 
though 
invariant 
set 
theory 
is
deterministic and locally causal.
In 
the 
case 
of 
the 
sequential 
SG 
experiment, 
the
“impossible 
triangle” 
rule 
means 
that 
a 
counterfactual
experiment in which we reverse the order of the last two
SG devices (keeping the particle ﬁxed) similarly cannot lie
on the invariant set. The latter situation illustrates what in
quantum mechanics is called the non-commutativity of spin.
It means that we cannot treat the individual SG devices as
single independent apparatuses.
See Palmer (2022) for more details.

20. A crucial question is, What actually is the numerical
value of 
p
? There are good reasons to suppose 
p
will
depend on the energy, 
E
, of the quantum bits (qubits) being
described. For example, if each qubit is associated with
some 
energy, 
E
, 
then, 
using 
the 
Planck-Einstein
relationship 
E
= 
h
υ, where 
h
is Planck’s constant, we can
naturally associate a timescale 
h
/ 
E
with that qubit. My
proposal is that the dimensionless number 
p
(for that qubit,
and hence for the helix in that subset of state space) is the
ratio 
h
/ 
E

to 
the 
Planck 
time 
√
hG
/
c
5

where 
G

is 
the
gravitational constant and 
c
is the speed of light. This has
two consequences: 1) The larger is 
E
the smaller is 
p
. That
is to say, the more energetic they are, the fewer the qubits
can be entangled together before they become inherently
decoherent. 2) The larger is 
G
the smaller is 
p
, i.e., the
stronger 
is 
gravity, 
the 
fewer 
the 
qubits 
that 
can 
be
entangled 
together 
before 
they 
become 
inherently
decoherent. This means that my proposal for decoherence
(when 
the 
number 
of 
entangled 
qubits 
exceeds 
the
logarithm to base 2 of 
p
) will broadly coincide with the
Penrose/Díosi 
criterion 
for 
gravitationally 
induced
decoherence. This makes sense if gravity is a manifestation
of the heterogeneity of the invariant set geometry. For
example, if heterogeneous clustering is very weak, you
would need a very large number of trajectory segments
(large 
p
) to diagnose the existence of discrete clusters.
Conversely, if clustering is very strong, you would see it
with relatively few trajectories (small 
p
).

10. I was taught by Jesuits. I still recall being taught one
of their proofs of God. “Hands up, those who have been to
Australia,” said the priest. No one put their hand up.
“Hands up, those who doubt the existence of Australia.” No
one put their hand up. “There you are,” concluded the
priest. “You do not physically need to experience something
to believe in it.” If any Jesuits read my book, even though
they won’t agree with my comments about God, they will
probably recognise the type of logic I have employed in the
book.

has 
rather
